# JOOST VAN DEN VONDEL’S
# REFLECTIONS <note>Joost van den Vondel (1587–1679) was the preeminent poet and playwright of the Dutch Golden Age. This work represents his late-career philosophical poetry.</note>

### On

## GOD and RELIGION.

### Against the atheists <note>original: *ongodiſten*, a 17th-century term for those who lack or deny God</note>, deniers
### of the Godhead or divine providence.

->From the Epistle to the Hebrews.<-

> Whoever comes to God must acknowledge that he exists,
> and that he rewards those who seek him. <note>original: *Wie Gode genaeckt moet bekennen dat hy is, en beloont die hem zuecken.* This is a translation of Hebrews 11:6.</note>

<image-desc>An ornate engraving of a stone draw-well with a wooden frame and pulley system from which a bucket is suspended. The well is decorated with a carved garland and a central lion's head mask. Below the well is a rectangular plaque containing the motto: EVERYONE IN THEIR TURN <note>original: "ELCK ZYN BEURT." This was the printer's mark of the De Wees family.</note></image-desc>

### at AMSTERDAM,

->By the widow of **ABRAHAM de WEES**, on the Middel-<-
->dam, 1662.<- <note>The date is written in the original as CIƆ IƆ C LXII, an older form of Roman numerals often used in 17th-century printing.</note>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of the 1662 edition. It begins the first book of Vondel's "Reflections," establishing the theological premise that while seeing God directly is the ultimate goal, it is impossible for mortal humans to achieve in this life.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS
## On GOD.
### The first book.

<margin>
An introduction, drawn from the highest happiness, consisting of the contemplation of the Godhead from the front,
</margin>
What is more desirable than to meet, in our reflections,
The eternally shining light, the origin of all things,
And to see Him face-to-face from the front, <note>Vondel uses the phrase "van vore" (from the front), referring to the "Beatific Vision," the direct perception of God's essence which, in Christian theology, is the ultimate state of happiness in heaven.</note>
To serve others, whether by tongue or signs,
The radiance that appears to us; if human tongue or sign
Can speak of this miraculous sight and wonder,
Or depict it in its full power before the eye!
But this is presumption: the Godhead sits too high:
We float much too low, on loose and waxy feathers, <note>A reference to the myth of Icarus, who fell into the sea when the sun melted the wax holding his wings together; a metaphor for human intellectual pride.</note>
Too weak in such a glow to know God perfectly.
More than a worm, more than a mortal man is required.
A glowing Seraphim, a great archangel recoils
Truly three times in his flight, when he shall approach
The upper roofs, the throne of the Godhead's heavenly palace,
With humility and awe. The Cherubim covers
His face with wings, so that he may bow purely,
Unblemished and worthily before the throne of the Godhead,
Where thousands upon thousands shout in witness
Of what honor and majesty—bounded by neither age nor circle <note>original: "kreits." In 17th-century cosmology, this refers to the celestial spheres or orbits of the heavens.</note>—
Radiates from God's perfections through the heavenly spheres.

<margin>
which is impossible here; therefore one must begin from below.
</margin>
One must then step backward, so as not to gallop too high.
Presumption once buried Lucifer himself,
Along with all his angels, in hellish darkness,
From where throughout eternity there is no returning.

<summary>In the opening of Book I, Vondel explains that while humans long to see God face-to-face, our mortal nature is too "weak" for such a divine light. He warns against the pride of Lucifer and suggests that one must instead study God through His works "from below."</summary>
<keywords>theology, Joost van den Vondel, Beatific Vision, angels, Lucifer, pride, celestial hierarchy, Divine Light</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening of Vondel's first book of "Reflections," following the introduction where he established that God's essence is too bright for human eyes. He continues the metaphor of Moses on Mount Sinai, who was only permitted to see God's "back." The catchword from the previous page was "Den."</meta>

No mortal yet has been granted the charter
To behold God directly once, and live.
We must, then, look at Him from behind, where He steps,
If this grace befalls us. Whoever wishes to avoid disaster, let him measure
His own incapacity, before he ventures to dare.
How many have plunged into the sand through reckless delight!
The Godhead suffers no mockery from any proud endeavor.
Let one speak humbly, or remain silent and worship Her. <note>In 17th-century Dutch, "Godtheit" (Godhead) is a feminine noun; Vondel follows this grammatical gender here, though he refers to the person of God as masculine elsewhere.</note>

<margin>
The origin and meaning of the word "God" in our language.
</margin>
If an atheist <term>ongodist: a 17th-century term for one who denies God or lives as if God does not exist</term> is to serve and honor God,
It first behooves us to strengthen and prove with reason
That God exists in essence, and this name signifies no dream—
God is not a hollow invention, painted by the sound of speech,
But is reckoned as a substance, a Being,
Just as one denotes a tree with the name of "tree."
One must then know what the name of God reveals to us
In our own language, as established by every nation—
Not in vain, but chosen with sharp judgment,
To represent either the working of this Being,
Or something that explains His nature and properties.
In Dutch, this name springs from "good," and signifies a good nature,
The origin of all good, flowing from that richness;
A name so beautiful that we yield to no other tongues
In its meaning, since it awakens the heart,
And by that sound alone draws souls toward itself,
Just as mercury <note>original: "quickzilver." Vondel uses the alchemical belief that mercury is naturally attracted to gold.</note> is drawn from gold toward gold,
The noblest metal in virtue and in capacity,
Weight, and duration, and power, and luster, and majesty,
The very imprint of God's worth, and honor, and glory.

<margin>
The inherited teaching of God is necessarily, though variously, implanted.
</margin>
The Dutchman is thus accustomed to containing the richest treasure of treasures,
Like other nations, in four letters,
And calling **GOD** that which no sign or sound
Has ever fully depicted. The infant sucks this
From the milk-fountain, the breast of its faithful mother.
This is sharpened by the nurse, and the father, guardian, and keeper.

<summary>Vondel argues that while God's essence is unknowable, His existence can be proven through reason and the "goodness" inherent in His name. He highlights how the concept of God is instilled in humans from infancy through language and upbringing.</summary>
<keywords>Godhead, atheism, etymology of God, substance vs. shadow, gold and mercury metaphor, innate knowledge of God</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues Joost van den Vondel’s "Reflections on God and Religion." On the previous page, the author discussed how the Dutch name for "God" is derived from the word for "good." He now shifts his focus to how religious belief is often not a result of personal conviction, but rather an inherited habit or social conditioning passed down from parents to children.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK.

A 3

They pour it into young children, and teach the young early
To echo that sound <note>The sound of the name of "God" or religious dogmas mentioned on the preceding page.</note> from the cradle with their tongue.
Thus the child inherits a language through social interaction and habit,
Receiving the father’s teaching along with the mother’s language and speech;
And inherited custom creates a second nature for a person,
Which is difficult to unlearn. If truth sits at the helm
Of the house and family, then the child sails happily.
But if lying speech is at the rudder, how miserably and distressingly
This shipwreck then destroys all the offspring of that lineage!
A circumspect brain therefore distrusts the mother’s milk <note>original: "mam" – used here metaphorically to represent the earliest influences and nourishment of childhood.</note>
And the traditions passed down by mother, guardian, and father;
Seeking to flee inherited deception, and wishing out of fear
To deliberate more closely with reason, to test, sharply and quietly,
What concerns God and religion, before judgment wishes to cast its vote.
This is done so as not to wander so blindly with the whole crowd,
Moved by the authority of beards <note>A reference to the "learned elders" or philosophers of the schools.</note>, pens, and schools,
Or a hired tongue—those who, stupider than cattle
Driven to the stall, follow the driver
Who fattens himself on the grease of the flock.
A circumspect spirit demands reason, which distinguishes
Humans from animals, and pays no mind to anyone’s raging,
Neither the bull’s-whip <term>bolpees: a whip made from a bull's dried penis, symbolizing physical coercion or the threat of force</term> nor the curse. In reason he seeks light.
Where this light shines and is evident, he does not deny his duty
To follow of his own accord, voluntarily, unconstrained.
He knows well the labyrinth of the world and its tongues;
The false guides <note>original: "weiſlaers" – literally "way-showers," referring to hypocrites who point the way to heaven but do not walk the path themselves.</note> who worship the belly as their God;
How worldly men only look to honor and earthly pleasure;
How religion often serves as a mask <note>original: "grijns" – literally a grimace or a theatrical mask, implying a false front.</note> for godlessness,
Varnished by a harness of lying speech and malice;
How everyone, intent on profit, sharpens the edge of their wits,
And country by country, city by city, sets up a God,
Cast in the unequal mold of their own brains,
And led to the altar by their fellow-worshippers.
If you demand from the schools proof of their foundation,
They shout all at once, from the very same mouth:

<meta>catchword: Men</meta>

<summary>Vondel argues that most religious belief is merely an inherited habit or social indoctrination. He calls for a "circumspect spirit" to use reason to investigate the truth of God and religion, rather than blindly following tradition or corrupt religious leaders.</summary>
<keywords>reason vs. tradition, religious education, social conditioning, hypocrisy, religious authority, inherited habit, free will</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues Joost van den Vondel’s "Reflections on God and Religion." Having previously discussed how children are often raised into a religion by mere habit or parental influence, Vondel now argues that a truly prudent person must use their reason to examine the foundations of their faith, rather than following blind instinct like an animal.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

One should not argue with senses that, drifting along,
Overturn the foundation of our brain and the truth.
Thus the master expels him from the school with force,
Before he has firmly established his foundation through reason.

<margin>A cautious person examines the foundation of the doctrine</margin>
Yet one praises a person who, being teachable and prudent,
Does not recklessly receive a currency of doctrine <note>original: "munt van leering" – a metaphor comparing religious teachings to coins that must be tested for their weight and purity before being accepted as "legal tender" for the soul.</note> so weighty,
Since eternal salvation or a curse hangs upon it,
In the twilight, without a test, weight, and light.
Falsehood, appearing beautiful, borders too closely in appearance to truth.
This matter of salvation demands proof, and certainty, and clarity,
At least according to its nature: for otherwise, if one here
Determines something recklessly, then man becomes a beast,
An irrational animal, deprived of the use of reason,
And compared—not absurdly—to all lack of rationality.
Whoever demands this reckless deciding, though his following may be strong,
He excludes humanity and shuts "animal-ness" into his church.
I wish, therefore, with my pen and ink,
To print upon the page of the teachable hearts of those who wander
A healing script, in which the doubter may see God,
So that he may acknowledge God, and fear, and honor, and serve Him:
For when the Deity <note>original: "Godtheit" – referring to the divine nature or the essence of God.</note> is reflected upon and found,
Then religion stands firm on immovable grounds,
Which now, through base abuse, scandal, and the stain of slander,
Extinguishes zeal and places the people at a crossroads <note>original: "drysprong" – literally a three-way junction, representing a state of spiritual indecision or confusion between different paths.</note>.

<margin>The necessity of knowing God</margin>
To pluck this desired fruit from my cultivation,
It is necessary first to impress upon the heart of the wanderer,
That he must necessarily here know the highest good,
The uncreated All, the Deity,
At least according to his power and utmost ability:
For without knowledge, no subject is moved
To serve his lord; rather, he is moved powerfully if he is
Spurred on by knowledge of his lord's worth and greatness;
Even more so to cling to Him with heart and senses,
From whom he has received body, and soul, and being.

<summary>Vondel argues that faith must be grounded in reason rather than blind habit, asserting that to accept doctrine without examination is to forfeit one's humanity. He establishes the necessity of truly "knowing" God as the essential foundation for any meaningful religious service.</summary>

<keywords>reason vs. instinct, religious foundation, doctrine, salvation, human nature, knowledge of God, Joost van den Vondel, 17th-century philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Joost van den Vondel’s philosophical poem. On the previous page, the author argued that humans must use reason rather than blind habit to understand faith. He concludes the previous sentence here, stating that a thinking person expects goodness from the Creator who prepared the world for them.</meta>

THE FIRST BOOK . 7

<meta>catchword: Seen</meta>

And expects a greater good from His benevolence,
Who prepares the court of the world for mankind.

<margin>*that here [knowledge] is imperfect,*</margin>
No man ever climbs to the peak of this knowledge
Through the light of the creatures <note>original: "ſchepſelen" – referring to all created things in the natural world.</note>, and attention, and habit.
No wise man can perfectly depict what God is,
Beside whom our mid-day is nothing but darkness.
The invisibility of God remains hidden from human senses.
The Deity <note>original: "Godtheit" – the divine essence or nature of God.</note> knows itself alone entirely from within.
There is no proportion between man and God.
Infinity is sooner mocked by our narrow understanding
Than conceived and grasped within our brain-pan.
No master of letters, however eloquent or polished,
Finds a sign or word that represents God’s perfection,
And does not, instead of giving honor, steal His glory:

<margin>*yet is somewhat reachable.*</margin>
But nonetheless, one cannot escape from this:
The possibility of knowing God and His nature,
Through the light of Lady Nature and reason, insofar as
God, through His attributes and works, gives Himself
In part to be known; even such that the willfully blind
Can find no excuse for their guilt and blindness;
Like Diagoras, Leucippus, Epicurus,
Lucretius, and Democritus <note>Vondel lists famous ancient Greek and Roman philosophers associated with atomism and atheism, who argued the world was formed by chance rather than divine design.</note>, who abandoned
Reason and nature, under the pretext of lending them an ear,
And of stepping toward the light that has appeared to everyone.
Whoever, in the middle of the day, gathers pleasure in the dark,
And sleeps through the clear sun in a night-cavern,
Let him not complain when the light, upon sinking away,
Mocks those who neglect to use such a radiance.

<margin>*the atheist denies the Deity because it is invisible.*</margin>
He who respects no proof of that which is affirmed by everyone,
Who hears no sages, and is estranged from scripture and the use of reason,
Refuses to grant that a Deity exists in being,
Or if there is one, that it obliges us to fear it.
His excuse is that the eye sees no Deity.
He concludes then: all that exists is seen; what is not

<summary>Vondel argues that while God's infinite nature is beyond full human comprehension, He reveals Himself sufficiently through nature and reason. He critiques ancient materialist philosophers like Epicurus and Lucretius for being "willfully blind" to these divine signs.</summary>
<keywords>God's nature, reason, nature, atheism, Epicurus, Lucretius, invisibility of God, divine perfection</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues Joost van den Vondel’s philosophical poem "Reflections on God and Religion." On the previous page, the author listed ancient philosophers who denied God's existence. He now addresses the argument that God cannot exist because He cannot be seen.</meta>

### REFLECTIONS.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

[Whatever is] seen by man, has neither essence nor power,
Is vanity and a dream. They rest upon lies, then,
Who build upon a nothingness, the never-seen God.
<margin>This is refuted.</margin>
But surely this conclusion hangs loose, and without a lock <note>Meaning the argument is logically unsound or "unfastened."</note>,
And contradicts itself. Nature has with five senses
Endowed man too richly, through which the understanding within
Reaches light regarding all that happens from time to time;
Therefore, there is more than each can grasp through the eyes.
The eye brings news of shape, work, and colors.
The nose receives the stench, or smells healthy scents.
The tongue passes judgment on the taste, in food and drink.
Every limb feels. The ear perceives the sound.
What is never audible is perceived with no ears;
The tasteless with no tongue. The untouchable and unborn <note>The "unborn" here likely refers to things that have not yet taken physical form.</note>
One feels with no limb. No nose ever
Could smell the scentless, and the eye never perceived the invisible:
For each of the senses has its prescribed boundaries,
And focal point <note>original: "grontpunt" – the specific object or domain a sense is designed to perceive.</note>, beyond which it rests, and cannot work.
He raves wildly then, who subjects invisibility to the light
Of the eyes, and to human sight;
And cannot find God outside the focal point of vision.
This one judges colors more ignorantly than the blind.
<margin>The air is invisible and yet a primary substance <note>original: "hooft-stof" – a fundamental element.</note>, known by its works.</margin>
Even if the eye sees no air, yet the air is known
As the neighbor of the fiery element,
And by whose favor everything that lives here must draw breath,
Or die, should it happen to wander from its nature,
And draw in a poison, and vapor, which infects the blood,
Suffocates the spirits <note>original: "geesten" – in 17th-century science, "animal spirits" were thought to be subtle fluids that moved through the nerves to animate the body.</note>, and prevents the heart, the source of life,
From sharing life with all the remaining members,
And heat, and flames, which play through vessel and nerve.
Deprive the lungs of air, and you extinguish the spirit in the body.
If a field cannon <note>original: "veltkortou" – a heavy piece of artillery.</note> with force drives a ball
Through the open air at a gate, or roof, or stone walls,
You hear two bodies <note>The cannonball and the air itself, which Vondel treats as a physical body.</note> scraping against each other most powerfully.

<summary>Vondel refutes the atheist argument that God does not exist because He is invisible, arguing that each sense has its own limits. He uses the invisibility of air—which is essential for life and physically detectable through force—as a metaphor for God's invisible but real presence.</summary>
<keywords>Senses, Empiricism, Atheism, Air, Physiology, Artillery, Joost van den Vondel, Logic</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Joost van den Vondel’s "Reflections on God and Religion." Having previously argued that the invisibility of God is no proof of His non-existence, Vondel now uses the element of air—invisible yet immensely powerful—as a physical analogy for the divine.</meta>

### THE FIRST BOOK.

<meta>catchword: That</meta>

The swift bullet howls as it tears through the air;
The sky, filled with thunder and lightning—what is that roar
But the bursting of air and clouds,
Through the clash of heat and cold, pressing against one another?
And this saltpeter-air <note>original: "salpeterlucht" – the gas produced by ignited gunpowder</note>, in the pregnant metal <note>A poetic reference to a loaded cannon</note>,
Crammed to the point of bursting; how it vomits lead and steel
Upon its enemies, in storming or in boarding <note>original: "enteren" – a naval term for boarding an enemy ship</note>!
How it demolishes locks, and sends fleets flying in splinters, grit, and shreds!
If the "red rooster" <note>A traditional Dutch idiom for fire</note> ignites a hellish mine,
How the belly of the earth bellows! How the air shrinks in pain,
Before it gives birth to a ruin of human flesh and ramparts!
How the earth thunders and quakes before it collapses,
When the swirling of the winds churns against itself
Within her bosom, struggling for breath, and feels
No relief, until the pregnant earth, in its delivery,
Bears a chaos <note>original: "baiert" – a state of confusion or primordial mass</note>, and destroys city, mountain, rock, and woods,
And wastes stream and field, and hurls everything into a heap,
So that for whole miles, the people take to their heels!
Also, this great universe <note>original: "groot begrijp"</note> cannot endure a vacuum <note>original: "ydelheit" – nature's supposed abhorrence of a void</note>:
This is proven: for when the air is sucked out of a pipe,
The pipe then grips the tongue. No wine-jug sinks to the bottom,
If the wine-jug is plunged into the water mouth-first.
Even if one submerges the jug, it will keep the water out
By the element of air. The syringe shall spray water,
And swallow it, as if from thirst or a blue dropsy,
By means of the expelled air and the inhaled air.
The wine-transferrer can thus move the wines,
Contained from full tuns into an empty vessel,
And blow Rhenish wine, or the French grape-juice,
With his bellows out of the full and into the empty vessel.
The wine-pump drinks the wine, whether it is choice or lesser,
And it does not flow out, if one, with a finger at the top,
Covers the open vent-hole <note>original: "luchtoor" – literally "air-ear," the hole used to regulate pressure</note>, and shuts and stops it tight
Against the air, after which no liquid flows or drops down,

B

<summary>Vondel illustrates the power of the invisible air through examples of gunpowder, storms, and the physical principle that "nature abhors a vacuum." These serve as a scientific and poetic argument that things do not need to be visible to possess great force or reality.</summary>
<keywords>air, vacuum, gunpowder, physics, nature, wine-pump, elements, cannons, Joost van den Vondel</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Joost van den Vondel’s "Reflections on God and Religion." While the previous page used the violent power of compressed air and gunpowder to prove the existence of invisible forces, this page begins by addressing the skeptical argument that "only what is seen is real" before systematically refuting it through the limits of the five senses and the life-giving necessity of air.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

Whatever is seen by man, has neither essence nor power,
Is vanity and a dream. They lean then upon a lie,
Those who build on a nothing, the never-seen God.
<margin>This is refuted.</margin>
But surely this conclusion hangs loose, without a lock,
And contradicts itself. Nature has with five senses
Too richly gifted man, through which the mind within
Gains light from all that happens now and then;
Therefore, there is more than each can grasp through the eyes.
The eye brings news of shape, function, and colors.
The nose receives the stench, or smells healthy scents.
The tongue passes judgment on the taste, in food and drink.
Every limb feels. The ear perceives the sound.
What is never audible is perceived with no ears;
The tasteless with no tongue. The intangible and unborn <note>The author uses "ongeboren" (unborn/uncreated) here likely to mean something that has not yet taken physical form.</note>
One feels with no limb. No nose ever
Could smell the scentless, and the eye never perceived the invisible:
For each of the senses has its prescribed boundaries,
And its focal point <term>focal point (grontpunt): the specific object or range a sense is designed to detect</term>, outside of which it rests, and cannot work.
He raves then wildly, who subjects invisibility to the light
Of the eyes, and to human sight;
And cannot find God outside the focal point of vision.
Such a person judges color more ignorantly than the blind.
<margin>The air is invisible and yet a primary element, known by its works.</margin>
Even if the eye sees no air, yet the air is recognized
As the neighbor of the fiery element,
And by whose favor everything that lives here must draw breath,
Or die, if it should happen to wander from its nature,
And draw in a poison, and vapor, which infects the blood,
Suffocates the spirits, and hinders the heart, the source of life,
From sharing life with all the remaining members,
And the heat and flames, which play through vein and nerve.
Deprive the lungs of air, and you extinguish the spirit in the body.
If a field-cannon <note>original: "veltkortou" — a heavy, short-barreled Renaissance cannon</note> drives a ball with force
Through the open air at a gate, or roof, or stone walls,
You hear two bodies <note>The "two bodies" refers to the solid cannonball and the invisible body of the air itself rubbing against it.</note> rubbing against each other with the greatest power.

<summary>Vondel refutes the atheist claim that God's invisibility proves His non-existence by explaining that each of the five senses has limited boundaries. He uses the air—invisible yet vital for life and physically powerful enough to resist a cannonball—as a primary example of an unseen reality.</summary>

<keywords>five senses, invisibility of God, nature of air, anatomy, respiration, field cannon, sensory limits, Joost van den Vondel</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that invisible things possess immense power and reality. Having previously discussed the limits of the human senses, Vondel now uses the violent effects of gunpowder and the physical properties of air and vacuums to prove that the invisible atmosphere is a potent, physical substance.</meta>

# BOOK THE FIRST.

The swift bullet howls through the tearing of the air;
The air, filled with thunders and lightning—that clamor,
What is it other than air and clouds bursting
By the strife of heat and cold, pressing against each other?
And this saltpeter-air <note>original: "salpeterlucht"; the gases produced by igniting gunpowder</note>, within the pregnant metal <note>The "pregnant metal" refers to the hollow chamber of a cannon</note>,
Crammed to the point of bursting; how it vomits lead and steel
Upon its enemies, in storming or in boarding!
How it demolishes castles, and shatters fleets into splinters, grit, and rags!
If the "red rooster" <note>An old Dutch idiom for a spark or the intentional setting of a fire</note> ignites a hellish mine,
How the belly of the earth bellows! How the air shrinks with pain,
Before she births a heap of ruins made of human flesh and ramparts!
How the earth thunders and quakes before she caves in,
When the swirling of the winds against one another
Toils within her bosom for a breath of air, and feels
No relief, until the pregnant one, in her deliverance,
Bears a chaos <term>chaos (baiert): a confused, primordial mass or a void</term>, and destroys city, mountain, rock, and woods,
And stream and field, and flings everything into a heap,
So that miles of people are set to flight!
Also, this great universe <note>original: "begrijp"; the physical world as understood by man</note> can suffer no void <note>Referring to "horror vacui," the Aristotelian belief that nature abhors a vacuum and will move to fill any empty space immediately</note>:
This is proven: for when the air is sucked out of a pipe,
The pipe then grips the tongue. No wine-jug sinks to the bottom,
If the wine-jug plunges into the water with its mouth downward.
Though one submerges the jug, it will hold back the water
With the element of air. The syringe shall spray water,
And swallow it as if from thirst, or a blue dropsy,
Through the driven-out air and the air drawn in.
The wine-siphon <term>wine-siphon (wijnverlaeter): a tool used to transfer wine between vessels without disturbing the sediment</term> can thus transfer the wines,
Contained from full tuns into an empty vessel,
And blow Rhenish wine, or the French grape-juice,
With its bellows from the full into the empty vat.
The wine-pump drinks the wine, be it choice or lesser,
And it does not flow out, if one covers the open air-vent <note>original: "luchtoor"; literally "air-ear," the small hole at the top of a pipette or pump</note>
Above with a finger, and seals and stops it tight
Against the air, after which no liquid flows away or drops down,

<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: That</meta>

<vocab>saltpeter-air, wine-siphon, wine-pump, bellows, chaos, air-vent</vocab>

<summary>Vondel illustrates the physical reality of the invisible air by describing the explosive force of cannons and mines, as well as scientific demonstrations of vacuums and siphons.</summary>
<keywords>gunpowder, saltpeter, physics, vacuum, horror vacui, wine-siphon, military engineering, Joost van den Vondel</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration of the physical power of invisible air, building on the previous discussion of vacuums and cannons. Vondel now moves from heavy weaponry to more delicate and everyday examples—juggling, bubbles, and air-guns—to bridge the gap between physical science and theological belief.</meta>

-># REFLECTIONS.<-

That would otherwise sink back again toward the center.
The juggler, to entice the crowd at the market
With his juggling art, for sale at every fair,
Swirls round and round, and rolls ten eggs steeply
Up the groove, along his stick, and lures them down,
When he turns more slowly, and walks with his steps.
The simple folk, who do not notice the drift of the air,
Gape at the rogue, as one who works wonders.
The water bubble, full of air, is driven into the air,
Until it bursts, and through the crack gives up its spirit <note>The word "spirit" (Dutch: *geest*) is used here to mean both the air trapped inside and the animating force or "ghost" of the bubble.</note>.
The air stretches out bladders, upon which the swimmer floats,
So long as no needle pricks it, and the bladder remains closed.
The air in the <term>air-gun (wintroer): an early pneumatic weapon that used highly compressed air to fire projectiles silently</term>, which, screwed and compressed together,
Bursts out, and shoots with lead an oak plank to splinters;
And the air-gun is well-suited for an attack, if one notices
That it does not reveal itself, like the fire from a musket.

From so many works a firm proof is drawn
That air has its essence, though it refuses to our eyes
To reveal itself, because of its thinness and lightness;
Which is why the name of "spirit" has also been assigned to it—
Not that it is a spirit which is felt or struck,
But by comparison to other coarser substances
And elements, its kin, which maintain the kingdom
Of the world with their power together.
Do you acknowledge this? Why then do you fear to believe in a Godhead,
Whose essence, pure spirit, transcends our senses?

<margin>The wind is invisible and is recognized by its effects.</margin>
The wind is not seen, but nonetheless felt,
And heard, when it rustles, and blows, and roars, and seethes.
The connoisseur of nature <note>A "connoisseur of nature" refers to a natural philosopher or a scientist of the 17th century.</note> remains breathless
When he must speak of the origin of the king of the winds <note>original: "wintvorst"; a poetic personification of the wind’s source or a reference to the mythological ruler of winds, Aeolus.</note>;
From where he comes; whither he secretly drives.
If a doubter now remains so stubborn here
That he denies the wind, even more foolish than the herds,
Then let him say what spirit can shake with such power?

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>Vondel uses various examples of air's physical effects—juggling tricks, bubbles, and the silent power of an air-gun—to argue that invisible things are tangibly real. He concludes that if one accepts the existence of invisible air based on its effects, it is logical to believe in an invisible God.</summary>
<keywords>air pressure, air-gun, centrifugal force, nature of spirit, existence of God, wind, physics and faith</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that invisible air possesses immense physical power. Having previously discussed smaller phenomena like air-guns and bubbles, Vondel now scales up to the sublime and terrifying power of the wind in nature and industry—from devastating hurricanes to the windmills that created the Dutch polders—to prove that invisibility does not equate to absence.</meta>

->### THE FIRST BOOK. 11<-

<sig>B 2</sig>

The tops of the forest, the oak tree in the wood,
The towers in the air, built up toward heaven;
What power helps the sea-foam to boil and well up,
How we see the Ocean swell so sky-high,
Even up to the stars; who drives the provoked sea
Into the dunes, and drowns the people and the cattle,
And all the low land, while the shepherds hide,
Having fled to mountains and rocks, from the howling
And bellowing of the monster <note>Vondel personifies the storm as a "gedroght" (monster or creature), reflecting the 17th-century view of nature's raw, chaotic power.</note>, that swallows fleet after fleet.
A dark and thick air, before the rising of the wind,
Warns the sailor, and commands him to strike the sail,
Before the storm raises itself, and storms upon dune and dikes.
The East Indian Hurricane <note>original: "Oostindiaensche Orkaen". A reference to the typhoons encountered by the Dutch East India Company (VOC) ships in the Pacific and Indian Oceans.</note>, in the finest part of the day,
With bright sun and sky, first warns the sailor
With a blow, as it flatly, [alas, who would not shudder!]
Like God’s arm and fist, to push the ship to the bottom,
Plummets down from above, and strikes the deck,
And mast, and helmsman’s cabin, and cables into a heap,
Whereupon an abyss gapes, and vomits out foam and waves,
Full of the living and the dead, buried and entombed,
While one finds neither hope nor refuge, outside of God.
How a sailor’s heart leaps when he sails before the wind!
He thanks the wind when the ship, from foreign lands,
Sails into the harbor, on long-desired shores.
The sawmill owner, when his work pleases him,
Thanks the brisk wind, which saws whole forests.
The Lord of the Purmer <note>The "Purmerheer" refers to the wealthy investors or regents of the Purmer, a large lake in North Holland that was drained and turned into a polder in 1622 using windmill power.</note> thanks the wind and watermill,
Which mills water into land, so long hidden in the wet:
And what through wheat and rye reaches a stack of years,
Thanks the wind, which breaks and grinds the grain of Poland <note>The "grain of Poland" refers to the *Moedernegotie* (Mother Trade), the backbone of the Dutch Golden Age economy, where grain was imported from the Baltic to feed Europe.</note>.
The prince of winds sweeps the air, and streams, and regions
Of foul mists and deadly plagues.
So many workings testify to us with certainty
The power of the element, although it is invisible.

<meta>catchword: Would</meta>

<summary>Vondel catalogues the dual nature of the wind, describing it both as a destructive force that sinks East Indiamen and as a benevolent utility that powers sawmills, grinds Polish grain, and drains the Dutch polders.</summary>
<keywords>wind, maritime disaster, Dutch East India Company, land reclamation, Purmer, Baltic trade, polders, natural philosophy, theology</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical proofs of invisible power (the wind) to the metaphysical proof of the invisible soul. Vondel argues that just as we cannot see the wind but witness its immense power, we cannot see the soul but recognize its existence through the "works" of the human mind—reason, memory, and will.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

Yet should anyone dare to deny the existence of the wind,
He would be either mad or foolish, and his reason quite dead.
<margin>The soul is invisible, and is known by its works.</margin>
The wisest of men stand still before the essence of the soul;
Yet one knows it by the understanding, memory, and will,
Wherever these appear to us externally in their works.
Thus invisibility allows itself to be noticed through the visible.
This is evident to us from the radiance that shines from this being.
Human knowledge goes out to graze, unbounded,
In an ocean of every science
And art; stepping wider than the celestial animals <note>original: "hemeldieren". This refers to the constellations of the Zodiac, suggesting that human thought can travel further than the stars themselves.</note>,
The spirits swarm and soar. It penetrates
Nature even to the source from which it flows and springs;
It contemplates universal and singular matters,
Separated from their matter, and those which touch no ages <note>Vondel is referring to abstract truths or eternal concepts that exist independently of time and physical substance.</note>.
It polishes and investigates the reason, cause, deed,
And property of each; it examines nature and character,
Shape and form, and method—so shrewd in its evaluation.
No experience can satisfy its desire for learning,
And the strong contemplation of every kind of good:
That it kisses, that it embraces, and flies to meet.
It knows how to weigh the unequal, to compare,
And to find the proportion, and to ground
Its conclusion upon evidence. It takes its delight and light
In virtue, godliness, and rational duty,
In fame, and praise, and honor, and the benefit of sovereignty.
It has its own inclination, and hates the slavery
And the yoke of the body, which oppresses its heavenly nature.
It shuns with zeal whatever drags the free will
Into slavish servitude, and regards neither pain nor sorrow,
Nor adversity, in order to aim with a pure heart
At the target it is meant to hit,
Even without looking back at pleasure, luxury, or profit.
Now, if anyone wishes to deny the soul and its essence,
Because it is invisible: what medicinal herb <note>original: "kruit". While it literally means "herb," it refers here to a cure or medicine for the person's ignorance.</note> can cure him?

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Vondel argues that the human soul's existence is proven by its immense intellectual and moral capacities, drawing a parallel to the invisible but undeniable power of the wind. He describes the soul as an entity that transcends the physical body, capable of exploring sciences, virtue, and eternal truths.</summary>
<keywords>soul, invisibility, reason, free will, human knowledge, philosophy, virtue, Joost van den Vondel</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous one. Having established that the soul is known by its works even if it cannot be seen, Vondel now extends this logic to the cosmos and the Creator. He challenges the reader to look beyond the visible stars and explains why the human mind, limited by its finite nature, cannot fully grasp the infinite "First Cause" that is God.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK.

<margin>
The space above the fixed stars is invisible, yet not empty.
</margin>
The beam of the eye reaches what the eyebrow’s arch overlooks,
Even the fixed stars of heaven themselves, but no higher:
Would anyone for this reason dare to firmly and surely conclude
That above the eighth sphere <note>original: "achtste ront". In the Ptolemaic geocentric model used in Vondel's time, the "eighth sphere" was the firmament containing the fixed stars, beyond which lay the higher heavens.</note>, sown full of fixed stars,
No substance nor being hovers on high,
Because it hovers higher than the powerless eye?
What madness this would be! No power, raised to its peak,
Rises higher than its mark and utmost capacity.
Nature has her design, and landmark, and path,
And her own limit, which she cannot cross:
Therefore one surely concludes that the eye cannot define
What extends wider than the sight of the eyes and their beams:
Why then does the human brain set a measure
To the being that does not allow itself to be defined by the eye?
<margin>
God is not known from before <note>original: "van vore gekent". This refers to the philosophical concept of *a priori* knowledge—knowing something through its preceding cause. Since God has no cause before Him, He cannot be known in this way.</note>.
</margin>
That God is cannot be clearly demonstrated
Through an older cause: for His being, worthy of crowning,
Has never known a cause above itself, through which
One might know the eternal being and clearly behold it from before.
Nor can one forge this proof from God’s essence,
As if from something that runs before God according to reason;
Since the essentiality of a thing is not shown by its essence,
While the dispute still hangs in court, and looks
To a knowability of this pleaded being,
And whether the being exists from which the disputes arose.
The understanding can hardly, or rather never,
Comprehend such a treasure, the uncomprehended Lord;
No tongue, no language is enough to praise and fully laud Him:
That knowledge goes far beyond understanding and reason.
One cannot scoop the rich sea into a nutshell:
The shell is much too small, the water all too great.
No human can span the heavens with his hand.
No gnat understands the spirit of the wisest men.
Who can endure the midday sun in the sight
Of the eyes, which are all too weak in that all-radiating light?

<sig>B 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: From</meta>

<summary>Vondel argues that human senses and reason have natural limits, meaning we cannot deny the existence of things beyond the stars or fully comprehend God, who as the First Cause lacks a prior cause for us to study.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, Ptolemaic spheres, First Cause, limits of reason, infinity of God, empirical vs. metaphysical, Joost van den Vondel</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues Joost van den Vondel’s philosophical argument from page 13. Having established that God cannot be known through a prior cause (since He is the First Cause), Vondel now argues that humanity must instead look "from below"—observing the physical world to understand the Creator. He utilizes the Aristotelian concept of the "Unmoved Mover" to prove God's existence through the motion of the heavens.</meta>

### REFLECTIONS.

From before <note>original: "Van voor". Referring again to *a priori* knowledge—reasoning from cause to effect. Since God is the first cause, there is no "before" to study.</note> there is no chance here; the wisest man respects the majesty of the uncreated being, and crawls in the dust along the earth.
<margin>but from his works.</margin>
One must then prove from below, through God’s handiwork, this highest, all-wise, all-good, and almighty Being, who is without limit: an incomprehensibility, the cause of all things. And although one can never penetrate into the heart of His nature—and no human wit, however sharply it is whetted, can ever fully grasp this as required—nevertheless, it is the natural duty of everyone to fear God, who shines His essence upon you through all that is created. Just as the clear sun, though enclosed in a cloud, is known to all people by the daylight it produces, so too are humans accustomed to knowing one another in two ways: through signs, speech, and writing; through images and reflections; or even in a dark mist, in night and shadows, by the light of a torch or a lamp. Created things are God’s images, His letters, His tongue, and His marks. The light of reason reveals the Master in His works so clearly that no one may consider themselves innocent if they deny this sun in the middle of the day.

<margin>The motion of things testifies that there is a single mover.</margin>
Whatever is moved, is moved by something from the outside. The sun, in its course, confirms to our eyes that something is being moved, and this movement points to a mover who either stands still himself, or does not. If he stands still, then it is evident through these principles that this Unmoved One turns so many starry spheres <note>original: "gestarnde ronden". Reference to the concentric celestial spheres of the Ptolemaic system which were believed to carry the stars and planets around the Earth.</note>. This immobility is rightly considered to be God. If this mover were to move himself, then he would be moved by a higher power, and one would have to climb upwards infinitely to find the mover who does not move. But reason does not allow one to proceed infinitely <note>A reference to the philosophical rejection of *regressus ad infinitum* (infinite regression). Logic dictates there must be a starting point.</note>; thus, one must stop before the power of the All-Mover, who was never moved himself. For in the revolving of things, no second mover can move anything else...

<vocab>bespiegelingen: reflections or contemplations, oirzaeck: cause, onbegrependom: incomprehensibility, onbeweeghde: the Unmoved (Mover), albeweger: the All-Mover (God)</vocab>

<summary>Vondel argues that while God’s essence is incomprehensible, He is revealed through His creation (a posteriori) and must exist as the "Unmoved Mover" who initiates all motion in the universe.</summary>
<keywords>a posteriori, First Cause, Unmoved Mover, divine revelation, celestial spheres, Aristotelian logic, Joost van den Vondel</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for God's existence through the observation of physical motion. The previous page established that everything in motion must be moved by something else; Vondel now follows this logic to its ultimate conclusion: the existence of a single, "Unmoved Mover" or First Cause.</meta>

### THE FIRST BOOK.

But through a first power; just as the staff moves a stone
By someone’s hand: thus one then arrives at a
Mover, who himself rests, and turns all the rings of heaven,
Which steadily, at God’s nod and will, went in a chorus, <note>Vondel uses the term "reie" (choir/chorus), personifying the planets as dancers in a choreographed performance.</note>
And dance round and round, with a harmony
That pleases the all-seeing eye, which guides their dance.
If two movers moved the moved sphere,
And this power were shared by equal capability;
Then they would both stand equal in power.
Now reason does not permit that one should strike the flag <note>A nautical metaphor for surrender; reason dictates there can only be one supreme authority.</note>
Before the other. If one then wishes to encounter the highest power,
Then whatever revolves must cast itself before its feet,
And cry out in awe: O power, who governs it all,
Moves, and drives it around, you are first before all others.

<margin>
An 
*Arabian* 
writes 
absurdly of the 
movement 
of the 
stars, 
apart from 
God, 
[ascribing it to] the 
form 
of the 
stars, or an 
indwelling 
principle.
</margin>
This solid fundamental proof of the supreme unmovingness
Of the <term>All-mover</term> <note>original: "albeweger". The Aristotelian "Primum Mobile" or Prime Mover—the source of all motion who does not move Himself.</note> nevertheless rolls and strikes against
Our shins, if one does not stop the antagonist,
Who borrows his apparent conclusion from the *Arabian*. <note>Likely referring to Averroes (Ibn Rushd), whose commentaries on Aristotle suggested the heavens moved by their own nature or "intelligence" rather than a direct act of God.</note>
He cannot deny the movement that is seen,
Whether *Copernicus*, or the honor of the *Ptolemies* <note>Vondel notes that whether the Earth moves around the Sun (Copernicus) or the Sun around the Earth (Ptolemy), the philosophical necessity of a First Cause remains the same.</note>
Turns the earth around the sun, or the sun around the earth;
The movement depends on one or the other, however one turns it.
If the first celestial ring pulls lower arcs with it,
And above the highest ring reason can perceive
No other, which drives her, and stands still at the same time,
Then one ascends, climbing into the unmoving realm,
The throne of the All-mover, neither to be shaken nor moved;
While he turns everything below him according to his laws.
To struggle free from this, the loose-minded folk devise
That an indwelling and powerful fundamental principle
In every celestial circle turns the stars, and orbits;
Or that the movement of one hangs bound to another;
Or finally that God, who was never moved,
Is not the driver of all that swirls around him.

->But<-

<summary>Vondel argues for God as the "Unmoved Mover," asserting that regardless of whether one follows a Copernican or Ptolemaic system, the chain of motion must begin with a single, stationary First Cause. He dismisses naturalistic explanations from "Arabian" philosophers who claim stars move by their own internal principles.</summary>

<keywords>First Cause, Unmoved Mover, celestial mechanics, Copernicus, Ptolemy, Averroes, cosmology, 17th-century philosophy, Aristotelianism</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument for the existence of God as the "Prime Mover." Following the previous page's critique of Averroes (the "Arabian"), Vondel now argues that even if celestial bodies have an internal "soul" or principle of motion, that principle must still have been created and granted its power by a supreme, external First Cause. He then transitions into the cosmological argument (cause and effect) and the ontological distinction between necessary and contingent existence.</meta>

->### REFLECTIONS.<-

But let the Arabian prove by whose blessing
The indwelling principle received the power of movement
Of the spheres <note>The "spheres" refer to the concentric crystalline shells in the Ptolemaic system that carried the planets and stars around the Earth.</note> apart from God, the highest power;
Since the Divinity Himself went to create that driving soul,
And God granted being to that which would move the sphere,
And transfer those movements into others.
Thus it appears that God's power, infinite in its duration,
Brings forth everything from the womb of Mother Nature,
Who must increase in fruit through God’s inward-flow <note>original: "innevloet". This refers to the concept of *influxus divinus*, the continuous divine influence required to sustain creation.</note>;
Noting that those workings begin at first from nothing,
And end in something: the objective, and the mark <note>original: "wit". Literally the white center of a target; the goal or *telos* toward which all creation moves.</note>
Of the All-mover, who rests in His own possession.
One sees movements that cry out, though we were silent:
A single unmoved one is the origin of movement.

<margin>God is the first cause of all things.</margin>
A single and most ancient cause is the worker of
All that one can grasp with understanding or the senses,
And who arises out of Himself—that is God, and the divine being,
Appointed by everyone: for all works arose
From one worker, not from themselves, since no appearance
Contends that anything could be its own beginning
And cause. Every work, whether the highest or the very lowest,
Takes its beginning from another power, and from a very first
Ground-cause, which itself knows no cause,
Unless one wishes to mount and climb without end,
From tier to tier. Thus, through this irrefutable reason,
The cause of things, God, is confessed as the supreme.

<margin>God is a necessary, not a contingent being.</margin>
No being can be shown to us, but that time
Encloses it in its circle, or it spreads itself out far
As wide as eternity, and before and after infinite;
The one is contingent, the other is necessary. <term>Contingent being</term> <note>original: "gebeurlijck wezen". In philosophy, this refers to something that exists but could potentially not exist; it depends on something else for its existence.</note> could be in existence, or not.
The <term>Necessary</term> <note>original: "nootwendige". An *ens necessarium* is a being that must exist by its very nature and cannot *not* exist; a core concept in the arguments of Thomas Aquinas.</note> by its nature, however sharply one views this,
Has no contingency of being, or it would have to conflict
With its own nature: neither right nor reason can suffer this.

<meta>catchword: Now</meta>

<summary>Vondel argues that God is the "First Cause" and "Unmoved Mover" who created the internal principles of nature. He distinguishes between God as a "necessary being" (who must exist) and the rest of creation as "contingent" (which could not exist).</summary>
<keywords>First Cause, Prime Mover, Averroes, cosmology, necessity vs contingency, divine influence, Mother Nature, Aristotelian philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument for God's existence. Having established God as the First Cause of motion on the previous page, Vondel now moves to the "Cosmological Argument" (the necessity of an eternal being to start time) and the "Argument from Gradation" (the existence of a supreme good from which all lesser goods derive).</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK.

C
<meta>catchword: Where</meta>

Now it sounds absurd that the existence
Of a being, which once began, and whose nature tells
That it knows a beginning, would start from itself.
One must necessarily then—if reason is to win the day—
Conclude that there must be a necessary being <note>original: "nootwendigh wezen". In philosophy, this refers to a being that cannot *not* exist, as opposed to "contingent" beings (like humans) that depend on something else for their existence.</note>,
Which is eternal, from whence all things in succession
Began in time. One cannot soar any higher
Than this necessity, which is attributed to God alone.

<margin>God is the supreme good of all.</margin>
The world presents us with a magnificent stage
Of every kind of good. Each thing enjoys a portion,
Either more or less, of the first and best good of all,
In beauty, virtue, and power, as their various gifts fall
From heaven’s full lap; which pours out more or less,
According to His discretion, to all that partake
In this benevolence. It can then in no way fail
That everything must draw from one source—the origin of gifts—
Their sustenance and treasure, shaken out from above,
While the fountainhead springs, which is never exhausted.
If one asks for the highest good, and climbs through all the celestial spheres;
They point higher still, to where it is found,
And where it sits enthroned at the peak, and, eternally honored,
In such a rich possession it neither diminishes nor increases,
No matter how much it bestows, or how many tongues thank God,
In a single sense, though in many different sounds,
Offered up to the Godhead like a censer <note>original: "wierockvat". An incense burner used in religious ceremonies; here, the prayers and thanks of creation are compared to sweet-smelling incense.</note>
And a pleasant fragrance, in return for that received treasure.

<margin>God's being is evident from His excellence above man, who was created by Him.</margin>
One can read the certainty of the divine being
From God’s excellence, His radiant script.
Humanity surpasses all other beings here
In knowledge; and man, the noblest creature <note>original: "dier". While this literally translates to "animal," in the 17th century it referred to any living, breathing being (from the Latin *anima*, soul). Vondel follows the Aristotelian definition of man as a "rational animal."</note>
And most worthy creation, enters the world according to the order
And the rule of nature. He did not, then, come into being
From eternity: for whatever is procreated has a beginning.
Thus, this conclusion now implies a nobler being,

<summary>Vondel argues that because the universe and humanity have a beginning, they must originate from an eternal "Necessary Being." He describes God as the exhausted fountainhead of all goodness and beauty observed in the world.</summary>
<keywords>Cosmological argument, Necessary Being, Supreme Good, hierarchy of being, human exceptionalism, divine providence</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument for God's existence. Following the previous page's discussion of the "First Cause" and "Gradation," Vondel now explores the "Teleological Argument"—the idea that the perfect order and purpose seen in nature must have an intelligent designer.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

From whom man received the spirit, and soul, and reason,
Understanding, and free will, and other excellences.
This being cannot be anything but eternal, or it excludes
An infinite chain of the working cause,
Which reason forbids us to believe.
Thus God's glory and eternal being soar above,
From whence the lesser [beings] received their inborn destiny.
This creature <note>referring to man as a created being</note> then points us to the uncreated God,
The highest excellence, from which all other excellences
Borrow their luster, both above and here below.

<margin>and from the fitness, rule, order, and the end of things.</margin>
One cannot deny that an all-wise power
Conducts and governs all, keeps everything through its strength
In order, arranges it all for some beneficial end
And purpose. This is God and the Godhead, whom one knows
Through this regularity of things, as it appears
Where each reaches its end, and deviates from no order,
In rising and setting, in procreating, and in dying.
This would be impossible for those who lack reason. <note>original: "derven" (to lack). Vondel argues that because plants and inanimate objects follow complex laws without having their own "reason," they must be guided by an external Intellect.</note>
Thus they are all guided and led by a higher hand,
A single supreme power, a supreme intellect.

<margin>The atheist <term>ungodist: an archaic Dutch term for one who denies God's existence</term> wrongly calls the world "God," or ascribes its origin to nature.</margin>
The denier of God, to give a fairer color
To his denial of God—driven by all things absurd—
Knows no Godhead but the heaven, earth, and sea,
And what one sees high and low, in length and breadth.
He melts down, to plausibly wring the proof of God from us,
Into a single name both the work and the cause of all things; <note>This is a critique of Pantheism, the belief that the universe and God are identical.</note>
Or denies the beginning and end of this universe,
And grants an eternity—to suit his dreams—
To that which has taken its origin from God alone.
To wrestle free of this, it seems to him that all things come forth
And spring, not from God, but from the essence of nature.
This [nature] sat, from eternity, according to his understanding, at the helm
Of affairs, which turn around in a circle:
Thus this "turner" <note>original: "draeier." Vondel uses a wordplay here; a "draeier" is someone who turns things, but it also implies someone who is slippery, deceptive, or whose head is spinning with delusions.</note> fancies he can satisfy himself in his dream.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>Vondel argues that the inherent order of the universe and human reason prove the existence of an eternal Designer, while critiquing atheists and pantheists who mistake the physical world for God.</summary>
<keywords>teleology, intelligent design, atheism, pantheism, nature, reason, free will, eternal being</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the origins of the world. Having previously addressed how an atheist might wrongly equate the universe with God, Vondel now moves to define the term "Nature" itself. He seeks to reclaim the word from a purely secular or materialistic definition, aligning it instead with the creative power of God.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK.

<sig>C 2</sig>

<margin>
That which is here refuted.
</margin>
But to avoid a useless battle of words,
Let us see how nature supports this praiseworthy foundation,
And what is meant by the name "natural":
Then everyone can judge who calculates correctly or poorly here.

<margin>
Various meanings of the word "nature."
</margin>
From the many uses in Greek and ancient Latin,
The word "nature" is often quite ambiguous,
And according to its use and daily occurrence,
It is adjusted, like a measure, to many different feet. <note>Vondel uses the metaphor of a measuring rod or a shoe being fitted to different sizes to show how flexible the definition of "nature" can be.</note>
In the Greek language, it briefly signifies
The procreation <note>original: "aenteelinge"</note> of all that is born;
In a less literal sense, it refers to the maker, or the end
Of something natural, and whatever was recognized as such;
Or even the essence <term>wezen: the inner nature or "being" of a thing</term> of whatever was ever produced,
Whether it be considered an accidental quality or a substance;
Or it is understood as one of the substances
Of God, or man, or beast; or collectively for the members
Of the body of the universe, and all that exists within it;
Or it looks at the order and sequence of things, their proportion,
And character, and properties, and workings, and powers;
Or at the law of things, and the rule, and the pursuit
Of each one's well-being; or it mentions man’s flawed nature,
Before supreme grace improves and reforms <note>original: "herbaert" (re-births)</note> him,
By sharing its influence more or less generously.
Thus, the same sound of one name refers to many things.
To avoid quarrel and arguments over letters, we accept
The External Beginning, the First Maker, <note>Vondel is defining God as the "First Worker" or "External Beginning" of nature.</note>
Of all that has come forth naturally,
Having taken its origin from that fountain of things:
For if one calls God "Nature," in that proper sense,
The sound defines the same, and contains no blasphemy,
But rather the Supreme Nature, the Source of all natures,
In creating, in procreating, in birthing, in governing,
And sustaining all that has been brought forth,
Where benevolence, providence, and power...

<meta>catchword: Upon the</meta>

<summary>Vondel explores the multifaceted definitions of the word "nature," tracing its roots to Greek and Latin concepts of procreation, essence, and universal order. He concludes that "nature" is a valid name for God, provided it refers to Him as the "First Maker" and "Source of all natures."</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues Vondel's philosophical exploration of the relationship between God and the physical world. On the previous page, he began defining "Nature" by looking at its etymological roots in Greek and Latin. Here, he moves toward a reconciliation, arguing that the term "Nature" is often used interchangeably with God's creative power and presence within the universe.</meta>

## REFLECTIONS.

To be seen most clearly, and to be observed,
Just as the builder's spirit is seen in his works.
<margin>The cause of the ambiguity regarding the word nature.</margin> In every language that signifies and expresses
The meaning of man, the tongue speaks either literally or not,
And uses flowery speech, taking one thing for another,
Out of common habit, and through the clever use of rhetoric
Employed to adorn speech; whether it walks 
On rhythmic feet, or measures neither sound nor meter. <note>Vondel is explaining that language—whether prose or poetry—often uses metaphors ("overbloemt" or "over-flowers"), which can lead to confusion when defining technical terms like "nature."</note>
Through this, the word "nature" has reached many levels,
And the word "nature" is used either for God's own attributes,
Or His works; seeing as God's essence here below,
And also above, shone forth in every way from His works.
Therefore the word nature shall not oppose the Godhead,
But rather confess the great God through His great work.
<margin>It is not absurd to call God and nature the same thing:</margin> That which always was and remains in essence and duration,
Can be none other than God, or God’s nature,
And the sole cause of every cause in the act of procreation.
Thus the ancients did not hesitate to pair nature and God,
To call them by the same name, to ascribe to them 
One power, one same operation, one same authority:
For the outflowings <note>original: "uitvloên." This refers to the Neoplatonic idea of emanation, where all reality flows out from the Divine source.</note> that flow from God into all things,
The rays that shoot from God into all things,
And propagate without end, witness to us with certainty
That God, or His nature, is in all things,
And grants to all things their being <term>wezen: their fundamental existence or "is-ness"</term> from His being,
Gives them their spark and life, and through the power of these,
All things arise from and out of and through and for that God,
Are moved, and born, and pass away.
No pure primary matter <term>hooftſtof: the basic elements or "prime matter" from which the world is built</term>, nor mixed, cast into one,
Excludes God, nor God’s nature and essence,
Which permeates this whole universe; therefore we hang the conclusion 
Of our proof upon this: everything is packed <note>original: "gepropt." A vivid 17th-century term meaning stuffed or entirely filled, suggesting there is no "empty" space where God is not present.</note> with God,
Packed with God’s nature. Let us shun school-room bickering
Over the sound of a name, as long as we do not differ on the matter itself.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>Vondel argues that "Nature" and "God" are often used synonymously because God's essence permeates all of creation. He suggests that linguistic ambiguity shouldn't distract from the truth that the universe is entirely sustained by and filled with the Divine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 20, where Vondel discussed the ancient practice of using "God" and "Nature" interchangeably. He now shifts to a crucial distinction: while the terms may overlap in poetic language, one must never mistake the created work (Nature) for the Creator (the Cause).</meta>

### THE FIRST BOOK.

<margin>
Yet it is absurd, if one confuses God's work and the cause of God's work with each other.
</margin>
But should an atheist, a denier of God,
Confuse God—the Cause of His work—with the work itself,
Where they stand opposed and are actually distinct;
Then the mask of nature shall not mislead me
Into departing from God, to place nature upon
God's throne, from which she took her being and beginning;
Even though she lay imagined from eternity in God's reflections,
As the sketch of things yet to be created,
Which we grasp through the five senses in the understanding,
Or, without the evidence of the senses, stand planted
In rational comprehension. The Godhead keeps its place,
And yields not to nature, as the first yields not to the second:
For the first remains and is the root of the number <note>Vondel uses a mathematical metaphor here: just as the number "1" is the source of all subsequent numbers, God is the "root" from which the multiplicity of the universe grows.</note>,
That sprouts from the first, and shall always follow.
Substance <note>original: "zelfstandigheit." In Aristotelian philosophy, "substance" is that which exists in itself, while "accident" (toeval) is a property that exists only within a substance.</note> and accident, the properties
Of the soul of things, their order, course, and regular steps
And ways of nature—which descends from God's essence—
Point toward a single God, from whom everything draws its being.
Thus we subject the daughter to her father:
Let her recognize Him as God, and shun the betrayer of God’s honor.

<margin>
The distinction between God’s external operation and the operation of nature.
</margin>
The Godhead alone creates out of the void, and nothing,
And without matter, and also—if it pleases Him—out of something:
Nature, God's daughter, gives birth from first-created matter.
God sets for her a law and a goal. She has hit God's mark
Whenever she reaches the objective set before her.
The Godhead works through her, as a king works through his law:
She obediently executes the command ingrained within her,
And wears, as a shield-bearer, God's livery <note>A "livery" is the distinctive clothing or "uniform" of a servant, signifying who their master is.</note> and arms.
She stands bound to God's rule, on her watch,
Receives the father's word, and knows and honors His power:
The Godhead binds Himself to no law, nor measure, nor seal,
And oversteps nature, her borders, bounds, and rule,
As often as it pleases His providence:
Whether His omnipotence plagues entire kingdoms,

<sig>C 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Whether</meta>

<summary>Vondel defines the hierarchy between God and Nature, arguing that while Nature is God's "daughter" and instrument, she remains subordinate to her Creator. He clarifies that God creates from nothing (ex nihilo), whereas Nature can only work with existing matter according to the laws God has set.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 21. Having established that Nature is a "daughter" or subordinate of God, Vondel now argues that miraculous events and the very existence of matter prove a "higher hand" at work, refuting both those who deify Nature and those who credit existence to blind chance or atoms.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

->REFLECTIONS<-

Whether He blesses, or shakes the world out of its order,
In matters that cannot be attributed to nature,
But, outside of her usual style, to the rational mind
They witness that this is driven and guided by a higher hand;
And the Cause, which nature can by no means reach,
Is revealed age after age through many a wonder-sign <note>Vondel refers here to miracles (wonderteiken), which by definition break the "style" or laws of nature, thereby proving a supernatural Power exists above those laws.</note>,
So clear, that the atheist <note>original: "ongodist." A 17th-century term for one who denies God's existence or providence.</note> could never deny this light,
Unless he closed his eyes to this midday sun.
The Godhead knows no beginning, nor end, nor place, nor limits:
Nature has her beginning, and place, and ceases to work,
As soon as God commands. Should matter fall away from nature,
Beneath the moonlight, or in the court of the starlight;
In what region, or place, or far-off corners
Would the atheist go to seek nature, his goddess?
But it is in vain: for nature lacks being and form,
If the foundation, all her support, falls away from her:
But God, who exists by Himself, and by His own essence,
Even before nature appeared, needs not fear as she does:
For He requires no support, unlike the atheist’s raving,
Which would rather forsake God, and fall away, all too foolishly,
To enthrone the borrowed realm of Lady Nature upon the seat,
And here, like the cattle, only fill the belly,
To die, without hope of reward, or fear of punishment.
Whoever hungers for such swill—I have no appetite for the mash of pigs. <note>Vondel uses a sharp polemical tone here, comparing a life without the soul or God to the purely physical existence of livestock.</note>

<margin>That the world did not arise by chance, or from drifting fibers.</margin>
The ancient spirit of error <note>Likely referring to the devil or the personification of Heresy/Falsehood.</note> saw his passions were not strengthened,
Nor could he work this out under the title of Nature,
To bring to light the wondrous universe;
So then he took this honor from her, and gave it to wild chance,
The running-together of indivisible fibers <term>fibers: "vezelingen." Vondel is referring to the "atoms" of the Epicurean philosophers, who believed the world was formed by the accidental collision of indivisible particles.</term>
And matter, clinging together, into so many heavenly rings
Above, and what arises below from the elements,
Mixed, and unmixed. Where does the brain sink in the end,
When it is pressed, in defense of its own inventions,
To seek prop and stay upon such mindless grounds!

<summary>Vondel argues that miracles and the dependency of matter prove God's supremacy over Nature. He mocks the "atheist" perspective that reduces human existence to animalistic consumption and rejects the Epicurean theory that the universe formed by the chance collision of atoms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the polemic against atheism and atomism. The previous page established God's transcendence over Nature; here Vondel argues that complexity and order cannot arise from "wild chance" or the random collision of atoms (associated here with Leucippus). He uses the metaphor of craftsmanship—painting, shoemaking, and weaving—to argue that a chaotic "muddle" could never produce a functional universe without a guiding Mind.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK.

<meta>catchword: Be-gin.</meta>

Nature nor wild chance ever used judgment,
Nor reason, nor understanding, nor will, nor wise guidance:
How then could they deliver this work to our eyes,
That demands wisdom at its highest, and provides it, and omnipotence?
How would the hard and the soft, the square, the sharp, and the blunt,
The angular, and the flat, and the round, gather in one lump
By chance? How would, with such an order,
So many forms, out of such a chaos <note>original: "baiert." A term for a confused mass or primordial muddle.</note>, be
Cast into their form, fitted to their last <note>original: "leest." A shoemaker's wooden model of a foot used to shape a shoe.</note>,
Stretched on their frame; if wit, and spirit,
And judgment, and guidance did not take counsel here?
We speak in human terms, in order to crown the Cause of all things
By this comparison in the universe,
And by no means "Lady Nature," or some wild chance.
Nature cannot paint a landscape without a hand and paint.
What can this wild chance do? Nothing but run wild.
Now we see every seed deliver us its fruit,
And breed its like. The earth, and the air,
And the water each give, also without resisting,
Their offspring: but no plant can breed without roots,
No animals without a mate. No bird is accustomed
To live without air, and outside the element
Of water no fish lives, nor can it spawn its eggs.
The sea, and ebb, and flow, and field, and spring, and streams,
The course of sun, and moon, and stars, precisely fitted,
Rebuke wild chance, as often as it barks,
And tires the ears, which caught nothing more absurd
Than such a noise, grown out of trifles <note>Vondel uses "beuzelingen" (trifles/bits) to mock the "atoms" of the materialists.</note>.
<margin>Absurd proof for chance, and the running together of the fibers <note>The "fibers" (vezelingen) refers to the atomist theory that the world formed by particles sticking together.</note>.</margin>
Leucippus <note>A 5th-century BC Greek philosopher and the earliest proponent of atomism; Vondel portrays him as a madman for suggesting the world formed by accident.</note> however cries out just as foolishly and madly,
From the dark madhouse, through the window of his hole:
Go hence, unlock a garden, secluded from men,
That, fifty years ago, was robbed and plundered
Of all its Royal and noblest ornament
And furniture, and stands uninhabited and idle and empty;

Contemplate this structure with a magnifying lens, <note>original: "verrekijcker." While this usually means telescope, the context of examining dust and cobwebs suggests a magnifying glass or an early microscope, which were becoming popular in the 17th century.</note>
Which enlarges the very finest things, and judge for once who is richer,

What varieties of circles and lines reveal themselves,

Toward the horse, and perceived the clever deception—
The bit naturally smeared with foam,
And such that no intellect had ever copied this.
Painterly wisdom, suddenly abandoned by art,
Now thanks wild chance for such an honor and favor.
