--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's theological argument against "blind chance" (atomism). The previous page used the example of a painter's accidental success to show that while "chance" can produce a single lucky effect, it cannot produce the sustained complexity of the universe.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK.

D

Does it not resist? Just like the air on high, covered with clouds and mists, the eye is misled by image upon image, in which giants appear, along with monsters of land and sea—shapes that vanish in an instant, or transform before our sight according to the drift of the wind, or how the sunlight happens to shine upon them. What does he <note>Likely referring to the philosopher Epicurus or a proponent of the theory that the world formed by random collisions of atoms.</note> mean to conclude now, to block <term>God’s providence</term> <note>original: "Godts voorzienigheit." The theological concept that God actively guides the universe.</note> with this "accident," like a light racket striking an iron cannonball? If understanding shines forth from here, it is no random occurrence, but the eternal <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "vernuft." In the 17th century, this implied both creative wit and systematic intelligence.</note> that can move all things, through which even the smallest speck of dust received its being, and all the sand before the wind swells into mountains and dunes, upon which the <term>Ocean</term> and all the violence of the sea breaks.

But what comparison can this draw to the act of creation! I see here something [made] from something; no mines, no metals, no flowers, no crops, no water, air, nor fire; no rock, nor mountain, no bird, fish, or beast; no man, no diamond, no pearls, no rubies, no heaven-high vault from which the stars shine and keep their pace, just as a broad army marches in square formation to the trumpet and drum of the <term>marshal</term> <note>The author uses a military metaphor to describe the "orderly" movement of the stars, suggesting a commander (God) is behind them.</note>.

The natural masterpiece and the artistic one announce their <term>cause</term> <note>original: "oirzaeck."</note>—a cause full of understanding. Chance requires the advocacy of a madman who would be better off in chains, if the dust of his "accident" grows into such a wonder that it could eclipse the glory of the <term>creator</term>, while the world comes blowing in so gloriously. If <term>blind chance</term> <note>original: "reuckeloos geval." Literally "reckless" or "senseless" accident.</note>, deprived of intellect and spirit, could surpass God's providence in its works, this would have to be evident to us through its suitability, style, and order. Yet now we see something arise by chance from the blowing dust, in which nothing but wilderness and desolation appears; and the discerning eye, however closely it seeks and strains itself,

Can perceive no proportion nor end in it,
At which the worker aims, before he begins to work.
Sprinkle a page with ink, what do you see? A shapeless mass
Of stains, small or large, according to how the spray falls;
No letters, nor a proverb, spelled out from words, names,
Prepositions, and articles, and adverbs, which together
Tell us that the pen was drawn by
A learned hand. How then would chance, with understanding,
Consult in the work for which it is so favored?
No connoisseurs ever prize the mad painter as skillful,
Who nonsensically mistakes the madhouse for a gallery.
Compare now **Rubens'** <note>Peter Paul Rubens (1577–1640), the most famous Flemish Baroque painter, renowned for his technical mastery.</note> art and painting to this,
Where drawing and color, in highlights and depths,
As if against Lady Nature, ran in competition in the arena of art;
Where shall this night-owl hide himself from the day,
In his shadows of spiderweb and cobweb?
The masters will barely restrain themselves from laughing.
Let now the <term>fiber-spinners</term> <note>original: "vezelaers." A derogatory term for atomists who believed the world was constructed from random "fibers" or particles.</note>, who built this world
Out of fibers and grit, rise up from that dream,
And refuse to give chance free rein in the wild,
Through knowledge of God’s art, for which we, amidst the dust,
Reverently draw aside a curtain of silk and purple.

<margin>Parables that refute chance.</margin>
If by chance an <term>Antipodean</term> <note>original: "Tegenvoeter." Literally "one with feet opposite us"; a person from the opposite side of the earth.</note> came

In the newly built **Town Hall** <note>The massive "Stadhuis" (now the Royal Palace) on Dam Square, completed in 1655 and famous for its thousands of foundation piles.</note>, and saw or heard no one

To behold through and through this **eighth wonder of the world**,

To rise again toward heaven, with the crown

...hung on, and melted into it? That would be a foolish argument.
One ought to forge such a head back together upon the anvil.

<margin>Weak argument of Epicurus that the world was never created by God.</margin>
He still proceeds, and mocks just as crudely:
How did the builder of the world obtain his materials,
His anvil, hammer, forge, and pincers, to forge
This fearsome masterpiece, wrought in every limb?
For does not nothing come from nothing, and must something provide something? <note>This refers to the classical philosophical principle *ex nihilo nihil fit* (out of nothing, nothing comes). The poet is parodying Epicurus's skepticism: if God made the world, where did He get the raw materials?</note>
What a massive array of tools this colossus would require, considering
Its size and weight! What screw-jacks hoisted
And propped these burdens, ensuring they did not collapse,
Or stray from the plumb line, lead, and water-level?
What power hung wheel upon wheel upon its axle—
An engine that prevents steepness from becoming too heavy, and secures
Such great circles with bands, bound all around?

<margin>Proof that God created the world out of nothing, against Lucretius, a pupil of Epicurus.</margin>
Lucretius <note>Titus Lucretius Carus (c. 99–55 BC), the Roman poet who championing Epicureanism in his work *De Rerum Natura*.</note>, now honor him as a god in your poem.
Is this man, who draws such a mist before the student’s eyes,
Appeared like a sun out of the darkness?
And he who robs the Creator of the divine luster
Of creation—is that the wise interpreter
Who lit the light in Athens for all the Greek people,
And, painting a chance occurrence with mud and swine-bristles <note>A dismissive metaphor for the Epicurean theory that the world formed by the random clashing of atoms, or "seeds."</note>,
Wishes to wrestle against the truth here in the arena?
Is this the master who outshines all the ancients,
Silences Pythagoras, and strikes Plato <note>Pythagoras and Plato are cited as the "correct" philosophers because they believed in a divine order and the immortality of the soul.</note> upon the head?
O coarse man, go now: consult with one who is wiser.
An anchor-smith needs a sledgehammer, fire, and iron;
The sculptor needs a chisel, marble-stone,
And a hammer to carve his statue—its outline and limbs—
With art, modeled after life itself, from the marble:
***The Divinity requires neither material nor tools to build:***
***Nature, established by God, works through moisture and heat,***
***She employs substances and qualities, and observes—***
***Though she herself lacks reason—which seeds she must mix,***
***To bring each thing to its proper form and to its goal,***

-># THE FIRST BOOK.<-

If God’s providence gazes from eternity,
Yet His omnipotence prepares His work out of nothing. <note>This refers to the doctrine of *creatio ex nihilo*, the belief that God did not use pre-existing matter to build the universe, but brought matter into existence by His word alone.</note>
Whatever is labored forth through procreation, or through offspring,
God’s fullness remains the same, and knows no diminution.
One should be anxious here neither for material, nor seed, nor help:
An inexhaustible fountain springs eternally in the heavens.
Whoever demands material and tools confesses his own inability
And weakness, which is only propped up from on high.
Thus the same power remains, as is fitting, the very greatest,
Which provides the material, and fashions the masterpiece from that material:
For God’s perfection and wisdom and power
Stand above and at the peak; no reason can tolerate
That God receives anything from the outside or from something lesser than Him,
Or that He must thank wild chance, or Lady Nature <note>In 17th-century philosophy, "Lady Nature" (*Vrouw Natuur*) was often personified as a creative force, but the poet here subordinates her entirely to God's will.</note>
For material, and procreation, and the power to breed,
Or for advice on how to divide this lump into limbs,
And how to bring all this unevenness into His fold;
To separate moisture and dryness, and heat and chilly cold;
To arrange light and heavy, each in its proper place;
To separate night and day; to give birth to strife and peace,
According to the nature and property of every <term>element</term> <note>The four elements—Earth, Air, Fire, and Water—which were thought to be the fundamental components of all matter.</note>,
Which follows and knows His laws with great awe,
And helps to shore up the world with its shoulders,
Like four pillars that belong to this foundation.

<margin>That the world did not appear by necessity or naturally, but voluntarily and supernaturally.</margin>
Although God’s contemplation saw it all beforehand,
And arranged it according to the model that lay in His imagination
From eternity, the world still had to be founded.
It did not radiate out of God, as rays shine from the source of lights.
This foresight from afar, as if in the distance,
Gave it neither shape, nor form, nor material, nor anything.
Foresight does not cause the things that are foreseen.
One sees them because they are in existence, or approaching.
From no necessity of God’s nature did it take
Its beginning, and place, and being; nor did it come—

D 3

Catchword: The.

While God’s providence gazes from eternity,
His omnipotence ordains His work from nothing. <note>original: "uit niet." This refers to the Christian doctrine of *creatio ex nihilo*, or creation out of nothing, contrasting with the Epicurean view that matter must be eternal.</note>
Whatever is labored into being through breeding or offspring,
God’s fullness remains the same, and knows no diminution.
Let no one here be anxious for material, seed, or help;
An inexhaustible fountain leaps eternally into the sky.
He who demands material and tools confesses his own impotence,
And a weakness that is only supported by the Most High.
Thus the same power remains the greatest, as is fitting,
Which provides the material, and from that material fashions the masterpiece:
For God’s perfection and wisdom and power
Stand above and at the summit. No reason can allow
That God should receive anything from outside or from His inferior,
Or that He should thank wild chance or Lady Nature
For material, and procreation, and the power to breed,
Or for counsel on how to divide this lump into members,
And bring all this disparity into its proper fold;
To separate wet and dry, and heat and chilly cold;
To arrange light and heavy, each in its place;
To separate night and day; to give birth to strife and peace,
According to the nature and property of every element, <term>element: the four classical elements of earth, air, fire, and water</term>
Which follows and knows His laws with great awe,
And helps to prop up the world with its shoulders,
Like four columns that belong to this foundation.

<margin>That the world appeared neither by necessity nor by nature, but voluntarily and supernaturally.</margin>
Although God’s contemplation saw everything beforehand,
And arranged it according to the pattern that lay in His imagination <note>original: "voorbeelding." This refers to the Platonic idea that the forms of all things exist in the Divine Mind before they are physically created.</note>
From eternity, the world had yet to be established.
It did not radiate from God as rays radiate from the source
Of lights. This foreseeing from afar, as if in the distance,
Gave it neither stature, nor form, nor material, nor anything.
Foreseeing does not cause the things that are foreseen.
One sees them because they exist in essence, or are approaching.
From no necessity of God’s nature did the world take
Its beginning, its place, and its being; nor did it merely come to be.

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 29, which discussed God as the master craftsman who needs no pre-existing material. This page specifically tackles the concept of "necessity"—arguing that God created the world by choice, not because His nature forced Him to (like a shadow is forced by a body), and distinguishes God's essence from His creation to refute pantheism.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

To appear from Himself, or sprung from Himself:
The will of the First Cause <note>Vondel uses *oirzaecke* (cause) to refer to God as the Prime Mover or First Cause in a philosophical sense.</note> was directed toward such a work,
That set itself above Lady Nature in this form:
Not at all like the reflection of something falling on water,
The shadow following the body after the rays are blocked.
If the world’s essence or Is-ness <term>Isheit: A term coined by Vondel to describe pure existence or "being," similar to the philosophical concept of "essence" or "being-ness."</term> came to sprout
Out of a necessity, out of an infallible need,
And compulsion from God's nature, so glorious and so great
As we see her in life; who would then with tongue or pen
Renounce and deny the world's eternity?
It would be necessary, not voluntary, brought forth:
No human ever bound to gratitude by benefit.
Who would ever need to stir his mouth and lips
To sing praise to God the creator for the creation?

<margin>That God essentially fills everything, yet remains without mixture.</margin>
The Deity pours Himself out into all beings,
In such a way that nothing excludes God's being,
And everything resembles and is drawn to the Cause of all things,
Especially humans, who received their power from God,
And are gifted with the immortality of the soul,
Which seeks its origin and runs toward the stars.
But although the works resemble God in His nature;
The works must yield to God, their source and origin.
The creator and the work are two, not one and the same:
Thus the difference remains infinite between both.
One cannot forge identity out of similar things:
And no external work necessarily comes to spring
Out of God and His nature: for if one determines this,
We remain eternally estranged from knowledge of God,
And can between God, as Cause, and His works—
The world, this universe—perceive no distinction.
What does Lucan <note>Lucan (Marcus Annaeus Lucanus) was a Roman poet. Vondel here critiques the Stoic pantheism often attributed to Lucan, which identifies God with the physical elements of the universe.</note> rave? what delirium is this?
To call the sun, the sea, the air, the earth—every limb,
A part of the divine being.
Or the body of the universe, in which God is praised,

<summary>This page argues that creation was a voluntary act of God's will rather than a natural necessity, asserting an infinite distinction between the Creator and the physical universe to refute pantheistic philosophies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological argument from the previous page, specifically addressing the distinction between God's essence and the physical world. It refutes the idea of pantheism (that God and the world are one) by arguing that a creator must be separate from the thing created, and that creation was an act of free will rather than a forced necessity of God's nature.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK.

To mix [creation] with that God, so beastly, and coarse, and clumsy,
Just as the soul—the form of man—animates and maintains
The lump of the body! One must separate the infinite
From the finite nature, the voluntary from the necessary.

<margin>That the external causes remain outside the essence of the work.</margin>
The efficient cause <note>original: *werckende oirzaeck*; the agent or force that brings something into being.</note> and also the final cause <note>original: *entlijcke oirzaeck*; the purpose or "end" for which something is created.</note> stand
And look upon their handiwork calmly from without;
While the material and form, the essential parts,
Are delivered up, and joined through harmony, do not quarrel;
Therefore one calls the first two the external [causes] alone,
And the others internal; according to how they generally
Differ in the manner of bringing a work to pass.
One can then—without infringing upon God’s majesty—
Not carve the name of the Deity (who brought all beings into the light)
Upon the forehead of the building established by the supreme power,
Nor call God’s work the cause of things.
No influence of God’s power in the work can mask this,
As long as reason keeps the "work" and the "cause" in fact
Separated, and the one stands directly over the other.
They gaze upon each other as things joined together:
But even without regard [to creation], a First remains, before all things,
In the necessity of His being, as He was;
In the voluntariness <note>The author is emphasizing that God acted out of free choice (*vrywilligheit*), not because He was forced by His own nature.</note> of His arrangement, so as to once
Turn the pointer of the stars upon the axis
Of heaven’s clockwork, without confusing time with the eternal.

<margin>Distinction between the natural and voluntary work.</margin>
The circle of the world’s ring is limited,
As is its perfection: thus He who works this must
Work voluntarily, and must understand and contain
The world and the treasures of its perfection:
For if the essence of an unlimited power
Works by necessity through compulsion, and labors with force;
It brings forth something that is grasped by no bounds,
Enclosed by no circumference, nor narrowed in.
Although the world is now God’s own handiwork,
And could be founded by no other; it is certain...

<meta>catchword: That</meta>

<summary>This page uses Aristotelian logic to distinguish God from His creation, arguing that as the "Efficient Cause," God must remain external to and independent of the universe. It further asserts that because the world is finite and limited, it must have been created by a free act of will rather than as an infinite, necessary byproduct of God's existence.</summary>
<keywords>First Cause, efficient cause, final cause, necessity, free will, clockwork universe, divine majesty, Aristotelian philosophy, cosmology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that creation is a deliberate act of God’s will rather than a natural necessity. The previous page established that the Creator remains distinct from His work, just as an architect is distinct from the building he designs. This page specifically refutes the ancient philosophical idea that matter existed eternally alongside God.</meta>

-># R E F L E C T I O N S.<-

That the Deity did not beget [the world] as rays,
Naturally shot from the sun, descend;
Nor as the heat and glow reach us from the fire,
But as our proud Town Hall <note>The author refers to the Amsterdam Town Hall (now the Royal Palace on the Dam), designed by Jacob van Campen and completed in 1655. In the 17th century, it was considered the "eighth wonder of the world" and a symbol of the city's divine favor and architectural mastery.</note> lifts its forehead bold and proud
On high, through the will and art of the great builder,
Who draws to himself the heart and the eyes of the onlooker,
As long as Amsterdam remains the market of the world,
Which strengthens her exchange and trade with a lap full of gold.

<margin>Further proof that the world emerged from nothing, and from no pre-existing matter.</margin>
As firm as this proof is, it is certainly accepted
That this world could not have come forth naturally;
Since this origin required matter, which is by no means
Sought in the workings of God, neither here nor there:
For God’s precedence <note>The concept that God must exist before anything else can exist.</note> can tolerate no matter before Him;
Because the Deity Himself must first bring forth the matter.
Many ancients <note>Referring to pre-Christian philosophers like Aristotle or the Stoics.</note>, who did not understand this, dreamed of something
That was eternal, like God. They did not deny it,
And called the first matter the *chaos* <term>original: *baiert*; a primordial state of unformed matter or "mush" before the elements were separated.</term>, never created,
Deformity, and night, and a mixture without order;
An eternal war of seeds <note>The *semina rerum* or "seeds of things," an ancient physical theory that all things are made of tiny, conflicting particles.</note> among one another,
From which the harmony of this wonderful work appeared.

<margin>The absurdity of creation from pre-existing matter.</margin>
That the first raw material came forth from God
Must stand firm against the ghosts of long-dreamed dreams:
For if it arose from itself, and not from God alone;
It would be without beginning, and God Himself: but on that ground
No rational mind will build its certainty.
One cannot behold any essence of matter with the eye,
Or grasp it in the mind, which does not have its limits,
Reveals itself through its own nature,
And willingly bows before the supreme pleasure [will],
That knows no beginning of ages nor of days,
And remains incomprehensible. How then do these two—
The matter and the Deity—both take their place in eternity?

<margin>Further proof that God the [creator]</margin>
No non-existence can give birth to itself, nor create:
It must first be an existence, if it were to move.

Already <note>This is a "catchword" (*bladwachter*), a common feature in old books where the first word of the next page is printed at the bottom of the current one to help the binder and the reader.</note>

<summary>In this passage, the poet argues that the universe was created by God's free will from nothing, rather than being a natural byproduct of His nature or formed from pre-existing, eternal matter. He uses the Amsterdam Town Hall as a metaphor for a creation that reflects the intentional "art" and "will" of its architect.</summary>

### THE FIRST BOOK.

<margin>Who created the world.</margin>
Everything that then emerges from nothing is first brought forth
By someone else, and a more ancient strength and might:
For this reason [since one cannot climb infinitely high
The tower, which consistently denies the climber
The ability to overtake this <term>working cause</term> <note>The "working cause" (Latin: *causa efficiens*) is the agent that brings something into being, like a carpenter making a chair.</note> through flight;]
The supreme cause, [not the child and fruit of another,
But the one who gave birth to the younger, and remains the oldest of all,
Also called God, or Deity, before whom we fall down,
Because He creates, governs, and sustains everything,]
Must stop us at the highest sphere, from which He beholds it all—
Whatever is sought and found within and outside of Him.
This sole cause, unfathomable, without bottom,
Nor to be overtaken; who alone exists out of Himself,
Borrows nothing from elsewhere, nor takes any support to His aid;
Was eternal, and before all. No being stood outside this,
No working cause from which this being could sprout:
For if one were to be found anywhere, it would rightfully be called
God by us; and this is, in conclusion,
Placed at the very front; for it necessarily had to remain
In being, since no cause could drive it
And cast it down from the throne of the highest choir,
From which everyone’s being came flowing, and through which
All exists and works that can work; a reasoning
Contested from of old and still today, but never defeated,
Though the sole eternity was stormed by this mud <note>The author uses "mud" or "slime" (*slijck*) as a derogatory metaphor for earthly, materialistic thinkers who reject spiritual truths.</note>,
Which sets God and world as equals in eternity,
And attempts to give an eternity to the world,
In order to deny God, and not to tremble before Him;
While it follows in the wild that lawless abuse,
Which knows no Deity, nor worships anything but the belly <note>A reference to the biblical warning in Philippians 3:19 regarding those "whose God is their belly," meaning those driven only by physical desires.</note>.

<margin>Parables that confirm this.</margin>
The largest circle encompasses the smaller ones in measurement.
The causes follow, like the links of a chain,
Each other, and all hang fast to the very first,
To whom the name of "work" <note>In this context, "work" refers to a created object or result, as opposed to the "worker" or creator.</note> was never applied,

## REFLECTIONS.

Just as others, which now become the work of a higher cause,
Then in their order become the cause of a lower one.
Finite being depends solely on the infinite,
Which was never fully grasped: however deep one descends;
How high one climbs; however many steps between them
One counts, the lowest is not separated from the highest,
But remains connected link by link and mutually.
Whoever grasped the end of the chain and sought tirelessly,
Along all the links must at last reach the first.
The temporal rises from the eternal through time.
Whoever follows a brook or stream upwards, finds the source at the end,
From which the water leaps and wells up and begins.
Although an oak spreads out its branches while blooming,
One sees them grow from one trunk, from one root.
In many movements of all that moves,
There is a very first one, which carries all others around,
And draws them and moves them. A wound-up spring,
Or sinking weight turns all the clock-wheels again
And again, and, at a steady pace,
Turns the hand, and strikes the bell and the hours precisely.
<term>Measure-of-all</term><note>The original Dutch "Almeeter" refers to God as the supreme architect who measures and defines all existence.</note>, Infinite One, First Cause, seated on high,
O highest step and <term>celestial sphere</term><note>The "trans" refers to the highest vault of heaven or the battlements of the universe.</note>, beginning of the long chain,
O source, root, trunk, mover, spring, weight
Of the heavenly clockwork, so infallible in its duty,
Who are you, what are you not, who keeps everything in order
What has become from and out of and through and for you!
Who can you be but God, the first and last alone!
Infinity, you melt at last into the only one,
The only center, which, outside all circles,
And without circumference, is also found everywhere!

The atheist <note>original: "Godtverlochenaer," literally one who denies God.</note> fights himself out of ignorance,
Every time he grants the same eternity
To the world as to God, and disturbs the entire army
Of his arguments: for if God alone is the mover

### THE FIRST BOOK.

Of everything that moves and is driven around!
<margin>things, the matter of the world is not eternal.</margin>
Who does not hear how the building of his argument collapses,
And crashes down of its own accord? Thus the weakest yields to a stronger.
If nothing can arise out of itself, and the work demands a worker,
A cause: for no thing is its own <term>cause</term><note>original: "oirzaeck". In 17th-century philosophy, the "First Cause" refers to God as the necessary starting point for all existence.</note>;
Then his foundational proof of eternity here misses the mark;
Since he can nowhere find a higher cause
Than God, whose power alone had to bind this great vault,
And build it from nothing; and who alone arose
From eternity out of himself, in the unbounded circle.
If everything must borrow its power and strength from outside itself,
And its being, and origin, and action, from the One,
The infinite God, at the peak of the endless kingdom;
Who then equates the world, which is bounded,
With God? Since no rational laws
Permit two eternal beings to be placed side by side.
The order and the succession of causes do not permit
That the supreme cause, who commands all lower ones,
Should descend, and soar with equally-ancient wings
Beside the world—which must honor God’s infinity,
And could never strike the unreachable <term>target</term><note>original: "wit". Literally a "white mark" or bullseye in archery; the author suggests human reason or the physical world cannot hit or reach the level of God's eternity.</note>
Of the light of eternity, where God reigns and sits;
How can a conceited brain, not to be reined in by reason,
Thus provide the world with untiring wings,
With which she comes striving out of the eternal eternities,
Which no one can endure, nor shall, but God alone?
How dares a <term>conceited</term><note>original: "waenwijs". This refers to someone who is "wise in their own delusion," specifically those who think their own logic is superior to divine revelation.</note> brain thus boldly step upward,
And honor eternity—the crown of the Highest’s attributes—
So arrogantly, in this round globe,
Just as the Almighty poured it so skillfully according to His design?
The errant spirit here quickly gets stuck in his proposition.
One hears these doting ones contradict themselves.
They blow hot and cold at once from a single mouth.
Ruinous is that which is not grounded on truth.

### REFLECTIONS.

<margin>
Evil consequences of eternalizing the world, and mixing it with the Godhead.
</margin>
Truth does not wish to mix with error.
One cannot bring two contradictions to an equality.
To crown more than one eternity, without end,
Has, from of old, gradually introduced this universe as a God
And a Godhead; and still, with a gloss of reason,
Through craft and misunderstanding, it has slid into the people's brains.
Men reasoned: if the mixture springs from the fertile seed
Of the <term>elements</term><note>In 17th-century science, these were earth, air, fire, and water—the building blocks of all matter.</note>, is formed, and exists;
And if it is proper for children and heirs
To serve their elders, from whom they received their origin;
It would be more proper to our duty
That each should, out of gratitude, found an altar and a temple,
And serve the elements, and honor those who give life
To our ancestors, and continuously to their blood and descent.
Thus a misunderstanding sowed the endless number
Of Godheads in the hearts of nations everywhere.
Thus we see even today these generations degenerate,
Who regard neither religion, nor God, nor idol,
As highly as office, and honor, and the profit of their state;
A longing that is never satisfied, nor sated:
For outside of God is nothing but <term>vanity</term><note>original: "ydelheit". This refers to the biblical concept from Ecclesiastes—something hollow, fleeting, or meaningless.</note> and remorse.
Infinity alone can satisfy body and soul.

<margin>
The nothingness of the world, in respect to God’s greatness, neglected by the ambitious out of unbelief.
</margin>
Is this universe anything more, in the never-measurable circle
Of God’s infinity, which never found its circumference,
Than an indivisible point; what is the globe of the earth,
Never yet won by anyone’s fist with the sword,
Than more than vanity, and a dreamed-of nothing,
In which the <term>lust for the crown</term><note>original: "kroonzucht". An obsessive ambition for royal or sovereign power.</note> roots, and sheds streams of blood,
For an uncertain possession? And what is the honor of states,
For which the <term>ambitious</term><note>original: "staetzuchtigen". Those driven by a hunger for political status or power.</note> hate each other unto death,
And kick, without end, yet more than a sound
That only for a moment, from afar, rings in the ears?
What snares, craft, and cunning are gathered here together!
What a mountain of abominations and wickedness do they pile up!

Up to the clouds, in hope of earthly pleasure,
And shout with conviction: my sword is my God.
Each smiths his own fortune: everyone strives to strike his mark.
No Deity casts an eye on human affairs.
<term>Pleasure</term><note>original: "welluſt". In this context, it refers to Epicureanism, the belief that the absence of pain and the pursuit of pleasure are the highest goods.</note> alone is the highest good.

One uses the mask of religion only so far as it serves us,

Whoever is defeated, even if his plea and cause prove righteous,

<margin>Proof of God from His providence, and the regularity of nature;</margin>
Nature has never strayed from its designated path,
Unless through the highest guidance. It neither wavered nor succumbed.
The cycle of breeding, and of bearing, and of dying,
Of rotting, and perishing, rising, and decaying,
The animate and inanimate, the reasonless creatures,
And the rational, each keeps its timing: no one dozes at their post.
Everyone knows how to reach their end and goal,
To which they were destined. This order is a sign
That One governs, preserves, and maintains the whole.
He remains blind who does not behold the Deity here,
Who wields the world’s scepter, under which God’s witnesses—
The simplest creatures—willingly bow of their own accord.

That God exists. It torments him day and night,
Appears, terrifies, and threatens, and forces out by power
The report of what lies hidden in the hollow of the heart,
Where God sits in judgment, and leaves no evil unavenged.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument that true foreknowledge is a divine attribute. While the previous page established that predicting the rise and fall of nations requires an infinite mind, this page explores how human beings occasionally receive a "spark" of this foresight through divine grace, despite the limitations of the physical body.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK.

Concerning these mysteries, to see clearly ahead
What shall be, and what shall not, with any firm certainty,
Unless the vision be so strong, so clear in its gaze,
That it sees things as if they were already
In existence, exactly and precisely as they shall be,
And stand reflected from eternity in that appearance,
Just as God foresaw them! The power that knew this
Is certainly unlimited, and knows neither bound nor end;
Since one finds no other stronger than this above,
And no futurity blinds His clear eye.
Here is a consistency, a method, an excellence,
And He who knows the future in one thing, can possess it all.
Foreknowledge of that which is expected from afar,
Is then a sign of the <term>Deity</term><note>original: "Godtheit"</note>, in His power;
A property in no way shareable with lesser beings,
As it depends on God. What can distance do to hinder
Foresight? What can stand in the light of the all-seeing eye!
Whatever foresight man possesses, it is taught from on high
By God, who foresaw all. Now listen to how souls
Retained this <term>fief</term><note>original: "leengoet" - a legal term for property held in trust or granted by a lord, implying that foresight is not an inherent human right but a divine loan.</note> of foresight through heaven’s favor.

<margin>Occasionally revealed to some through God’s omniscience,</margin>
The soul itself is a light, capable of seeing ahead
To what will happen in its own time centuries from now:
Yet it is like a candle and a glimmer in a lantern,
Whose darkness prevents the glass from clearing,
And muffles the life and strength of its beam.
The more the body’s mist hinders and dampens it,
The less it shares in knowledge and in clarity:
But God, the knower of all uncreated truth,
Is pleased meanwhile to pull the human spirit
Much higher than the frame and mold of the body,
So that, unchained from matter and earthliness, it may ascend
Into the heavenly cabinet of spirits; there
The souls obtain a vision, and an eye that sees further
Than the eye-beam from the head, clear, and nothing else.

# REFLECTIONS.

If the whole body were an eye, then no soul would be darkened
By the body’s vapors and clouds. There, the soul speaks to God, and listens
To mysteries of nature never before understood.
This is where prophecy originates; therefore, let no one take it
To be a mere science of the future, or a form of knowledge
That settles in the mind through practice and habit,
But rather as a driving quality and a deep-seated longing,
Its power forcefully impressed upon the soul, like light within the air,
And borrowed from God alone, whom it pleases to illuminate
The horizons of souls with that radiance and clarity,
And to mingle them among the <term>spiritual realm</term><note>original: "geeſtendom" - referring to the company of angels or divine spirits.</note>, and their court,
Where nothing is dealt with but divine matters.

So too did the mother of the <term>Greek world-prince</term><note>Alexander the Great. His mother, Olympias, reportedly dreamed of a lion seal being placed upon her womb.</note> see
A proud lion pressed upon her pregnant breast.
The <term>augurs</term><note>original: "wichlaers" - ancient Roman priests who interpreted omens and signs to predict the future.</note> saw, when Augustus was born,
How the great dominion of the globe was destined for him.
In the same way, <term>Hecuba</term><note>The Queen of Troy.</note> dreamed, during her pregnancy,
How she gave birth to a torch, to set <term>Pergamum</term><note>A poetic name for the city of Troy.</note> ablaze,
As was proven in the end by Paris,
Who ignited Troy through the fire of the "fair one" <note>Referring to Helen of Troy, whose abduction by Paris led to the city's destruction.</note>.
A series of histories confirms everywhere
How firmly prophecy, long before the outcome of the event,
Anticipated the matter by many years,
Through which the Deity wished to reveal Himself to everyone.

<margin>The agreement of all nations proves that there is a God.</margin>
Four winds from four corners agree,
And speak with one voice, and point generally
To the same God, who with His sun and clouds
Rains upon and shines over the harvest of all nations,
From where the sun rises in the east, to where it sinks in the west:
And though religion may wander, become corrupted, or maimed,
Misunderstood, or adorned with false pretenses and varnishes;
No <term>idolater</term><note>original: "afgodift" - one who worships idols or false gods.</note>, no matter how wild, wishes to be without his divinity.

### THE FIRST BOOK.

And sooner would they worship stream, and tree, and mountain, and forest, and stone,
Animals, ape, and owl, wilderness, deformities,
And monsters, and ghosts with incense and honor,
Than go entirely without religion and lack their ancestral customs.
Prayers, offerings, altar, and sanctuary,
And temples, rich or poor, scattered everywhere throughout the world,
Century in, century out, and yet to be sown hereafter,
Prove how everyone seeks to appease the Deity
Through some <term>religious ritual</term><note>original: "kerckgebaer" — literally "church-gesture," referring to any outward act of worship or liturgy.</note>; and this confession
Testifies that it is a law among all nations,
To approach the Deity with awe and reverence,
Who punishes transgressors and can bless the devout.
This impulse seems planted by nature in the human heart,
And continually passed on, strengthened from hand to hand,
So firmly that anyone who wishes to teach the contrary
Seems to overturn Nature and her state and right.
Travel across the globe: ask of times long past
Worn away, century upon century: consult with the Hebrews,
Chaldeans, Egyptians, two kinds of <term>Indians</term><note>Typically referring to the inhabitants of both the East and West Indies as understood in the 17th century.</note>,
Philosophical Arabs, and Greeks: follow the banners
And eagle-standards of Rome: speak to the Gaul,
The Tartar, Russian, and German: everywhere they point
To one and the same God. Their schools and priesthoods,
Natural philosophers, and the <term>Sibyl</term><note>Ancient prophetesses who were often cited by early modern Christian writers as pagans who nonetheless recognized the one true God.</note>, both the speaking and the mute,
Trained in letters, astronomy, and signs,
A wisdom that weaves proverbs into flowery foliage;
They report one God, an uncreated being.
They teach to worship, serve, and fear one God;
Except for those who find entirely degenerate savages,
Or some <term>pseudo-philosopher</term><note>original: "waenwijs" — literally "falsely-wise"; a person who possesses conceit rather than true knowledge.</note> who spins a world out of cobwebs,
And more resembles a beast, wishing to be without God,
So as to die, like a stinking carcass, in both body and soul.
<margin>
The
consensus
of all
</margin>
This great consensus is a proof so firm
And clear that no one can rightfully deny God.

# REFLECTIONS.

<margin>The agreement of nations in acknowledging the Deity has its cause,</margin>
The wise and the foolish both speak in the same manner.
The foolish are at times misled by wicked tricks;
The wise never, so long as they preserve the light,
And do not change that title, nor deviate from it.
Whatever happens to sprout from human whim
Is not common to everyone, and must encounter many obstacles,
Or is followed and understood in various ways,
And is not universally determined, but changes itself, and bursts out
Now here, now there: the <term>wild</term><note>The author refers to "wilden" (savages), a common 17th-century term for non-European peoples or those perceived as living outside "civilized" law, to argue that even those without formal education possess an innate sense of God.</note> are inclined
To follow their nature, and crooked and devious paths,
Avoiding whatever restrains their lust and wickedness.
They should rightly then—since wickedness dreads
God's justice—outright reject the Deity;
Yet they know how to instill the faith in their children,
Even though the knowledge of God is slowly stifled in the soul;
However wild and savage it is altered and raised;
However much each differs from others in the knowledge
Of the Deity, whom they fear to offend and violate.
<margin>And it springs from God's oracles, or a tradition, or a conviction of reason, drawn from the created world and the order of things.</margin>
To consider the cause through which this knowledge springs;
It flows from the mouth of heaven <note>Referring to divine revelation or prophecy.</note>, or is born out of
A tradition, or the manifest evidence of things
And a foundation, and fundamental proof, which received their power
From convictions of reason and discernment,
In no way to be denied except out of stubbornness.
The mouth of heaven could not deceive itself.
No tradition of the ancestors could deceive the child
In such a fundamental matter: for they noticed from what stem
And uncreated state <note>original: "ongeschapenheit" — the quality of being uncreated, referring here to the divine origin of the human soul.</note> their being took its origin.
The manifest fundamental proof, determined by the light of reason,
Could be resisted by no one, unless they fought against themselves,
And was firmly established by the consensus of all,
Where neither tradition nor God's oracle applies;
A fundamental proof that, even if man wishes to suppress it,
Remains rooted in the heart and cannot be uprooted.

## THE FIRST BOOK.

F 2

Whether it be oracle then, or tradition,
Or the light of reason's use, through which one received this;
The Deity shone so clearly, that the clever doubt how one
Should call this knowledge: learning, or nature itself.
Here someone stops me, and says: why so much deliberation?
<margin>
A concise
proof
that there
is a God.
</margin>
If it please you, then pave the shortest way
To find God, and not to trot so far afield.
One needs only to dig through three miles from Memphis <note>The author refers to the ancient city of Memphis in Egypt. This is a historical allusion to the "Canal of the Pharaohs," an early attempt to connect the Nile/Mediterranean to the Red Sea, long before the modern Suez Canal. He uses it as a metaphor for a "shortcut" to a great destination.</note>
Toward the Red Sea, and so the sailor sails with delight
From our North Sea shortly to the Indian coast.
Well then, now listen. God is not far to seek.
The truth rests upon itself, and needs no mountain of books.
No dream, nor drunkenness clouds me, who know
And set beyond doubt that I am in being,
Yet was not eternal. So my being came to sprout
From nothing, or from a <term>something</term><note>The Dutch "iet" (something/some thing). The author is using the classic philosophical argument *Ex nihilo nihil fit* (Nothing comes from nothing).</note>. From nothing—that cannot hold:
For nothing is powerless. Thus I sprouted from a something.
This "something" was eternally in being before nothingness,
Was the all, and the only all, and knew no bounds.
It was the beginning of all that came to descend from it:
For if there were anything besides this "something" in reality, or in appearance,
It must necessarily have sprouted from nothing.
If this is the simple "all," what exquisite quality
Of wisdom, goodness, power, is there that in this pure being
Does not infinitely excel? Climb and scramble not now so steeply
Through thorny hedge and rock: here is your hope and salvation.
If you can consider yourself and your origin,
The Deity himself runs toward you with open arms.
<margin>
Conclusion
that there
is naturally
a capacity
in man to
know God
here in
some
measure.
</margin>
This then appears clearly, whether one argues it briefly or at length,
That God sowed into man a seed and a capacity
For <term>reasoning</term><note>The original term "redenkavelen" suggests a methodical sorting or partitioning of thoughts to reach a logical conclusion.</note> upon that which the senses grasp,
And sharing it with the understanding when it comes to ripen,
Through which one can conclude that one alone certainly
Depends on no one, and this is the origin of all beings,

Those who exist by this One, and willingly, as if bound,
Obey His law and omnipotence. Upon those grounds
Rests the unanimity of the endless number
Of tongues, so divided in customs everywhere,
And speech, and nature, and inclination, yet united in this possession
Of the Deity, as a target <note>The original Dutch uses the word "wit," which refers to the white center or "bullseye" of an archery target, implying that the Divine is the ultimate goal of all human effort.</note> that everyone aims to hit,

The punishments that await, both now and hereafter,
Should they recklessly neglect to know God's nature

## God’s Attributes.
### The Second Book.

<margin>The progression from the proof that there is a God, to God's attributes.</margin>
The light that beams and shines forth from nature and reason,
Before which irrationality flees and misunderstanding vanishes,
Is like a morning star, risen in the east,
And has assured and proven to the atheist <term>ungodist</term> <note>Original: "ongodist." A 17th-century Dutch term for one who denies the existence of God.</note>
That God exists in essence, and no one may doubt
This necessity and truth,
Unless he, more wicked than the beasts in this life,
Wished to wickedly misuse the reason, and the use of reason,
Given to him by that all-good God.
A watchdog remains faithful to his master, even unto death,
And knows the master and benefactor who fed him,
While he watched and guarded the house night and day:
And should the rational intellect, with heart or mouth,
Deny its Lord? Surely the faithful dog
Puts them to shame in their reason, who bear the name of men,
And only muzzle their blasphemous tongues out of fear when necessary.

<margin>Depicting to some extent of what kind, how great, and what God is.</margin>
Now it remains for us to observe in this mystery
Of what kind, and how great, and who this Deity is,
So far as our reason and understanding can follow His steps,
And know Him in nature, essence, attributes,
And work; while the husk <note>Original: "schors," literally bark or shell; used here to describe the physical body as a casing for the soul.</note> of the body prevents the soul
From beholding this clarity in its power, without blemish;
And we, covered with shadows and mortality,
[Look upon] the Deity with awe and our respectful eyes

<summary>The author argues that reason and nature provide an undeniable "morning light" proving God's existence, shaming those who deny Him by comparing their lack of gratitude to the loyalty of a dog. He then begins to explore the specific nature and attributes of the Divine, acknowledging the limitations of the human body in perceiving such glory.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of God, moving from the realization that God exists to the realization that His essence is beyond human comprehension. # REFLECTIONS.

...contemplate in the distance, against the heavenly blue vault.
Deity alone knows Himself most intimately.
<margin>The impossibility of expressing what God is;</margin>
When Hiero, tyrant <note>Original: "dwingelant." Hiero I was the ruler of Syracuse in the 5th century BCE, known as a patron of poets but also as a stern autocrat.</note> of Syracuse, asked
Simonides <note>A celebrated Greek lyric poet (c. 556–468 BCE). This famous anecdote about his inability to define God was often cited by Renaissance and Enlightenment thinkers to demonstrate the limits of human reason.</note>, whose style of poetry pleased him,
What God was, the poet requested of the cruel king
A delay of one day, which he needed to deliberate
On such a weighty question. The day having passed,
The poet requested, before delivering the answer,
A two-day delay, and after that a double amount,
And each time a longer postponement. The tyrant finally
Ordered him to give an account for this delay:
Then the poet burst out, driven to it by compulsion:
"The longer I have deliberated and agonized,
The darker and less speakable this appears to me."
Even Aristotle confesses to us how the immeasurable—
By which one understands God and Deity—is unknowable.
It is also said more easily what God is *not* than what He
Is in essence. He who remains silent, and is too in awe 
To speak freely of God, knows best how to speak of God’s nature.
Even the wisest choke on their own breath.
Tongues stammer in the naming of that which 
Is unnamable, and has never appeared to man stripped of mystery.
<margin>and the reason for this impossibility.</margin>
The impossibility of knowing God clearly in His perfections,
As one knows creatures, springs from reason.
The work of a creature and the cause of that work stand as equals,
And are equally narrowly defined; but the Deity and the manifestation
Of the Deity in His work are entirely unequal. The limits 
Of a creature can be reached, but one can never overtake
The infinite God. No proportion <term>evenredenheit</term> <note>A term used here to describe a mathematical or logical ratio or correspondence between two things.</note>
Exists between these two: for the Most High denies
To His work infinity, and enclosed it within bounds.
To strengthen our knowledge, then, which is weak,
One first denies to everything that radiates from God’s essence
The nature of creatures, which is determined by heaven’s power;

<meta>catchword: Than</meta>

<summary>This page explores the "via negativa" or the idea that God is best defined by what He is not, illustrated through the story of the poet Simonides and the philosophy of Aristotle. The author argues that because God is infinite and His creations are finite, there is no logical proportion that allows the human mind to fully grasp the Divine essence.</summary>

# THE SECOND BOOK.

Then we attribute to God the highest points
Of virtues, as one sees shining in the created world,
Yet differently and infinitely in God,
Who has trimmed and pruned our perfection
From a higher branch and top, so that we do not climb
In pride too steeply, and deny Him His incense <note>Incense here serves as a metaphor for the honor, prayers, and worship due to the Creator.</note>.
Through this denying and attributing of the one
And the other, God is then confessed, according to our understanding,
Even more clearly through the denial of things, in this life
Given to the creature for his perfection:
For if one rightly calls God an "uncreatedness,"
That is truth in its power, and more clearly and truly said
Than attributing something to God that men
Receive according to their wishes—be it goodness, wisdom, or power:
And our understanding thus grasps a certain measure,
Where God's perfection goes infinitely higher,
And no air can carry the spirit of the swiftest pens
To race after God’s path and progress.

<margin>God cannot cease to be.</margin>
If God arises from eternity out of Himself, no shadow
Of reason allows that God could cease to be:
For self-existence <note>original: "zellefstandigheit." In a theological context, this refers to *aseity*, the property by which a being exists in and of itself, independent of anything else.</note>, which is powerful enough to be able
To arise, has been continuous, and never begun,
Or could ever be [begun]: but this last is denied
In the necessity of God’s being, without end.
If God arises from Himself, then cause is excluded,
Sprung through a necessity from God's arising:
But God arises from Himself: so this testimony denies
An impossibility in God's endurance.

<margin>God is not matter.</margin>
The Deity is no matter <note>original: "stof." Refers to physical substance or material.</note>: for matter allows itself to be molded <note>original: "bootseeren." To model or shape, as a sculptor does with clay or wax.</note>,
And listens to the hand that can transform materials.
Also, no matter is the beginning of the worker, who steps forward
In order; the workpiece follows the worker in his track.
If God and matter are the same, and one, and indistinguishable,
Then the Deity suffers from Himself in the preparation <note>Refers to the act of creating or shaping the world.</note>

<meta>catchword: En</meta>

<summary>The author explains that while we attribute virtues like wisdom and power to God, He is better understood through "negation"—recognizing Him as uncreated and infinite. The text further argues for God's necessary existence and distinguishes the Divine essence from physical matter, which is merely a tool of the Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Aseity, Divine Perfection, Via Negativa, Materialism, Creation, Necessity of God, Virtues, Theology</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of God, specifically focusing on His simplicity and incorporeality—the idea that God is not made of parts and does not have a physical body.</meta>

### REFLECTIONS.

And in the changing of matter, which, when formed,
Is improved or worsened [which assaults God’s nature,
And denies His purity], the worker and the matter
Are thus melted together: this stains the purest vein <note>The "purest vein" refers to the unadulterated essence of the Divine source.</note>.

<margin>God is not a composite:</margin>
God admits no coupling <note>In a theological sense, "coupling" or "composition" refers to being made of different parts. God is considered "simple," meaning He is not a combination of elements.</note>: for whatever is fused together
Is later than each part, which differs greatly from God,
Who suffers no melting, and is the first of all others,
Which change more or less through another’s power.
The mixture is separable, and when it allows itself to be divided,
Then each part returns to its first state;
Then the essence of the mixed-clump is lost.
This does not suit the Godhead, who remains as before.

<margin>He contains nothing contradictory or unnatural within Himself.</margin>
Where any being suffers, there a conflict stirs
In essence and nature, which separates friendship:
For where there is equality of nature, no suffering occurs.
No like-natured thing has anything to struggle with.
This equality of nature takes place in God alone:
Thus no conflict is endured in God’s nature.
Where conflict falls, in the end the weakest must give way to the stronger.
But God feels no conflict: so He cannot succumb.
No being suffers violence from itself, which wars
Against its nature, and burdens its <term>substance</term> <note>original: "zelfstandigheid." In 17th-century philosophy, this refers to the underlying essence or "substantia" that exists in its own right.</note>:
Thus no violence nor unnatural things
Were ever endured in God’s substance and being.

<margin>God is no body.</margin>
God is no body that has its measure and limbs,
And is divisible: for this contradicts His wholeness.
If God is a body, and someone favors this view,
A body is either natural or <term>mathematical</term> <note>original: "wiskunstigh." This refers to "abstract" or geometric bodies defined by points and lines, rather than physical matter.</note>.
It is not natural: for God was never moved.
It is not mathematical: His pure being wipes out
The <term>accident</term> <note>original: "toeval." In Aristotelian logic, an accident is a property that a thing may or may not have without losing its identity (like color or size). God is argued to have no accidents because He is pure essence.</note>, which no mathematical thing can leave behind,
Which has its magnitude and distinct measures.
Every body is defined, in the round or in the square,
And everything defined is grasped by the understanding:

<meta>catchword: Honor</meta>

<summary>This page argues for the "divine simplicity" of God, asserting that He is not composed of parts, contains no internal conflict, and is not a physical or geometric body. The author uses logical deductions to show that if God were a body or a composite, He would be subject to change and destruction, which contradicts His eternal nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of God, transitioning from the argument that God is not a composite being to the argument that God is incorporeal (has no physical body) and contains no "accidents" or contingent properties.</meta>

# THE SECOND BOOK.

Before our narrow understanding shall encompass God’s being,
And be greater than it, this firm foundation must burst <note>The author suggests that for a human mind to fully "contain" God, the mind would have to be greater than God, which is a logical impossibility.</note>.
A living body takes the prize over the lifeless
In nobility, and that which is animated always lives
Through life; therefore let the body freely thank life,
That it sees no [lifeless] bodies striving ahead in nobility.
If now nothing is nobler than God, what appearance
Disguises the idea that God’s nature could be a body?
God is no body, no: for the supreme mover <note>This refers to the Aristotelian concept of the "Unmoved Mover" (Primum Mobile)—the primary cause of all motion in the universe who remains unchanged themselves.</note>
Moves the bodies, and all the starry host,
And remains himself unmoved; while he eternally turns
The celestial spheres, thickly sown with stars.
A body that moves, also moves itself
By <term>accident</term> <note>original: "toeval." In 17th-century philosophy, an "accident" is a property that can change or be absent without the subject ceasing to exist (like hair color), whereas God is seen as "Necessary Being" with no changing parts.</note>: but that takes no place in God’s nature:
And God’s nature is never likened to a body,
Except by the blind, who seek him only by touch:
As in former times Heathendom confined the infinite,
Enclosed in the raw matter of wild, mingled clumps,
Or in each element, or even in all four,
Or in the heavenly host, and its circling path;
Or named God the "soul of the world" against all reason;
Or painted him as a man, with outlines and limbs.
Thus they wandered in a dream, and outside of right judgment,
Ignorant of God’s pure and sheer <term>simplicity</term> <note>original: "eenvouwigheit." This is a technical theological term meaning that God is not "folded" or made of parts; He is a single, unified essence.</note>.

<margin>In God there is nothing accidental.</margin>
The "accident" can in no way belong to God:
For what is already perfected can receive nothing more.
The accident is only added to a being
That still lacks something, before it is precisely perfected
In its essence: but God, glorified by the rays
Of his perfection—which knows neither beginning, nor bounds,
Nor cause, through which he climbed to this height—
Needs no accident for his completeness.
The essence is followed by the accident, which is less perfect.
This touches too closely upon God's crown; it shall not be taken from him.

G
<meta>catchword: Through</meta>

<summary>This page argues that God must be incorporeal and immutable, using the Aristotelian concept of the 'Unmoved Mover' to demonstrate that God remains unchanged while moving the universe. It critiques pagan anthropomorphism and explains that God possesses no 'accidents' because He is already eternally perfect and simple.</summary>

<keywords>incorporeality, Unmoved Mover, divine simplicity, accidents, contingency, Heathendom, celestial spheres, perfection</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of God's nature, moving from the rejection of God as a physical body (on the previous page) to the concept of Divine Simplicity. The author argues that God cannot have "accidents"—properties that can change—because God is pure essence.</meta>

### REFLECTIONS.

Due to our imperfection <note>The author argues that human limitations force us to use categories like "substance" and "accident" to describe God, even though these distinctions don't truly exist within God's unified nature.</note>, God’s pure simplicity
Allows in our understanding, by necessity, the distinction
Between <term>substance</term> <note>original: "zelfstandigheid." In 17th-century metaphysics, substance is that which exists in itself, rather than in another thing.</note> and <term>accident</term> <note>original: "toeval." A property that can change or be absent without changing the nature of the thing (like the color of a coat).</note>. In God’s being
The pure essence is most highly praised to us:
For God is mere existence and essence; therefore
God’s pure unity of being alone excludes all accident.
   Where accident takes hold, there are fluctuations;
For accident, by its nature, passes in and out of things.
If then something accidental is applied to the Divinity,
Then he becomes changeable; thus the Divinity suffers harm:
But it sounds ungodly that God’s nature should change.
Who then marries substance and accident to each other here?
   Whatever contains accident in itself is not everything
That its nature requires: for here the "applied-occurrence"
Is found outside the essence of its own support <note>An "accident" relies on a "substance" to exist; the author argues God needs no such external additions.</note>:
But God is purely all that is to be grounded in him;
Thus accident is rightly rejected in God,
Unless one sought in him something that does not concern Divinity.
   God’s essence itself has never received anything from without,
Which naturally remains excluded from his essence;
Since it cannot borrow anything from outside itself:
For nothing simpler, nothing more uniform than
God’s essence in itself is believed and praised:
But God’s substancehood is actually his essence:
Thus everything God’s essence has is substance,
To whose simplicity no accident hangs, nor clings,
Nor shall ever attach, unless one wants to teach us
That God can change, decrease, or increase.

<margin>
God is 
pure 
being.
</margin>
   From this denial flows, like a stream from a spring,
That God is his essence and nature, and nothing else:
For if one wanted to allow anything else in God besides this,
One would mix the purity of God with something else,
And attribute something to God outside his nature and purity,
Which his nature denies.

## THE SECOND BOOK.

<margin>
*It is necessary to contemplate God more deeply in His attributes.*
</margin>
Since God’s essence is the most simple <note>In 17th-century theology, "simple" (original: *eenvouwigh*) means "not composed of parts." It is a technical term for a being that cannot be divided or changed.</note> of all,
It might not suit anyone’s eye or insight
To see God in attributes, divided by distinctions,
Mirrored through the weak brain of man;
For this simplicity cannot allow itself in the least
To be mixed into a variety of things—
A mixture that directly conflicts with God’s nature.
What counsel is there to release our necks from this reproach?
If there were a means here to comprehend God’s essence
In one way and glance, and without straying
Into these varieties; we would quickly slide a bolt
Before this boldness, and cry: God is God!
But then God’s nature with its qualities would remain
Still equally unknown, and outside the bounds of reason:
Therefore reason, necessity, and utility force us to approach
This <term>diversity</term> <note>The "diversity" refers to the various names and characteristics we give to God, such as Justice or Mercy, to help our limited minds understand Him.</note>, in order to grasp this most simple One
In such a concise way—much like composite things,
Which hung together through their form and matter and more:
For the Supreme reveals Himself according to our understanding,
In many an attribute, planted within His handiwork,
And the visible universe, in so many diverse pieces,
To press His simplicity into every heart.
Whoever then observes the attributes of the Divinity,
Uses them as <term>recognizabilities</term> <note>original: *kenbaerheën*. These are markers or conceptual tools that allow the human mind to recognize the Divine, even though the Divine itself is a single, undivided unity.</note>, not a mixture in fact,
Nor a plurality. The only One—simple, pure, uncompounded,
Is never split, nor knows of crease or wrinkle.

<margin>
*The multiplicity of many beings points toward a simple being.*
</margin>
The human race, which in the early beginning
Cast its eyes upon the nature of all things,
Saw an uncountability of many diversities
In the essence of nature, from above to below,
And stood, as if amazed, before such a great edifice.
The wise saw in these diversities a light,
That led them to one being and the very first father,
From whom this multiplicity sprang; as from a single vein

These attributes are presented as tools for the human mind to understand the Creator through the diversity of the natural world.</summary>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page regarding God's "simplicity" (His undivided nature). It explains how humanity's confusion led to the worship of many gods (idolatry) and proposes that we must use reason and the natural world as our first guides to understanding the Divine before turning to the Bible.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS

Many brooks; like a forest of branches from a single trunk.
False wisdom—when deceit, greed, and the lust for power
Entered the world, and the people's simplicity turned to base nature—
Did not approach the trunk, which birthed the power of the branches,
Nor the spring-source that breeds so many brooks,
But took the workmanship for the cause, and the image
Of the Lord for the Lord, and honored the properties
Of matter itself as God. Along these winding stairs
An infinity of gods came into vogue,
Bolstered by pleasure, hypocrisy, and force.
One must then distinguish the properties of things
From God, whose purpose was to lead us higher
Toward Him; His attribute does not differ from His essence,
And His <term>simple unity</term> <note>Original: *simple eenvouwigkeit*. In 17th-century philosophy, "simplicity" refers to something that is not made of parts and cannot be divided.</note> is neither mixed nor divided.

<margin>God’s attributes must first be proven outside of the Bible, through the light of nature and reason.</margin>
To unfold the attributes of the Supreme then,
At least some of them, and to behold Him through this
In His qualities, we borrow no light
From revelations, oracles, or visions,
History, or the proverbs of God’s sacred pages;
But will first consult with nature and reason,
To properly lift the veil from the eye
Of the <term>dim-sighted</term> <note>Original: *schemerenden*. This refers to someone whose spiritual vision is blurred or who is still "in the dark" regarding divine truth.</note>, who at first cannot look so high
Into that radiance, and fears to give faith
To the never-failing pages, written by Heaven’s Spirit,
Who guided the hand of the writers as if with His own hand.
Let him then follow nature and reason with his staff.
When the mist is wiped from the glass of the eyes,
The light shall clear, where he first sat deprived.

<margin>All wise men attribute His properties to God.</margin>
What was attributed to God with reason and discernment
By wise men, age after age, inclined and ready
To follow the right trail of the supreme pleasure,
That may well bear the title of God’s attribute.
These attributes are known throughout the world,
And for long, from hand to hand, imprinted in the rational brain.

## THE SECOND BOOK

Even so, they lose the name of rational,
Those who choose the dead-end path over common consensus,
And follow into the desert and useless wilderness
A Heathen breed, which has long since cast off humanity.
The general consensus of the wise reports the fools,
Who stubbornly, here and there, huddle together.
Proof is lost upon them; for them, one and two do not make three.
They slander the watchful eye with their drowsy murmurs.
Thus can self-delusion darken both understanding and sense.
A stubborn mule <note>Original: *muilpaert*. Used here as a metaphor for an irrational or headstrong person who refuses to be guided by reason.</note> will listen to neither bit nor spurs.

<margin>God’s essence is simple, and unmixed.</margin>
The simplicity of God <note>Original: *eenvouwigheit*. In 17th-century theology, "Simplicity" refers to the doctrine that God is not composed of different parts (like body and soul, or matter and form) but is a single, unified essence.</note> meets me and floats ahead,
An attribute that strictly and purely excludes
The melting together of anything mutually bound;
A fusion never found in God’s nature.
In all creatures, many things are distinct,
Even in their very actions; but in the Creator of things, all is one
And the same. Do you ask how God’s attributes differ,
If they are uniform, and unity consists of many?
They are only perceived as different within the brain,
According to human understanding and reason; beyond that,
Their distinction signifies no actual division within Him.
The "fusion" exists only in our understanding and senses:
In God is pure oneness. He who recklessly concludes otherwise,
Does not foresee what monster sprouts from this mist.
Perfection immediately degenerates into the imperfect,
If purity is taken away from God’s essence:

For, strictly speaking, the Godhead remains always
A purity, a spirit, bodiless and matter-less,
In no way joined together, incapable of suffering anything,
Perfect, undivided, whole on every side,
In no way participating in, nor consisting of, distinctions
Of accident <note>Original: *toeval*. In philosophy, an "accident" is a property that a thing has (like color or size) which is not essential to its nature. The author argues God has no such "accidents" because He is entirely His own essence.</note>, form, matter, or substance,
Nor any cause, whether external or internal.
Thus simplicity stands constant in its purity.

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation of God's nature. Having established that God is simple (unified) rather than composite, the author now argues that God must be immaterial and unmixed. If God were made of matter or a mixture of parts, He would require a "binder" outside of Himself to hold Him together, which contradicts the concept of a First Cause.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS

But I desire to go deeper into God's sanctuary,
To serve everyone who wishes to understand this divine oracle.
Who would not wish to commune with the Deity,
To learn in humility and stillness,
Until, released from this mortal burden, <note>Original: *sterflijck pack*. A common 17th-century metaphor for the physical body which weighs down the soul.</note> this curtain
Is pulled back, and God appears to us in His living presence?
&emsp;He who is the first worker of matter could not be
Anything but immaterial, since even the first elements arose
From His all-working power: <note>Original: *alwerckzaemheit*. Refers to God's omnipotence and His role as the primary "actor" or "worker" in creation.</note> therefore God is not matter.
Matter, when it works and produces the fine and the coarse,
Changes and transforms: but God remains unmoved,
And will allow neither transformation nor change
In His nature; thus the Deity cannot, in a material way,
Be the support and cause or principle of anything.
If God's simplicity <term>Simplicity: the theological doctrine that God is not "composed" of different parts or substances, but is a single, unified essence.</term> will tolerate no passive power
Within itself, then no power can mix His purity.
Where there is a mixture, there several things are one,
Bound together by a single tie in common,
And this binding requires a cause for the connection;
So one would have to find a "binder" outside of God,
Who existed before this mixture and glued the mixture together:
But this counterfeit coin <note>Original: *haegemunt*. Literally "hedge-mint," referring to illegal or debased currency; used here as a metaphor for a false or worthless argument.</note> sounds false and absurd.
The separator of matter <note>Original: *stoffescheider*. Likely a reference to an alchemist or a chemist who breaks compounds down into their elements.</note> can divide mixed substances;
Therefore the mass of mixture requires a separator between them,
And once the general bond that held the mixture together
Is broken, like a string, the mass lies destroyed.
Sound reason can in no way think this of God:
For His purity allows itself to be violated by nothing.
In all mixtures, the virtue of the part
Is not as great or noble as it is together in the whole;
I speak of virtue and value contained in this whole:
For a divided value is inferior, more cramped
In the part than in the whole. If God’s value then consists
In the mixture of the whole, then the Deity must...

<summary>The author argues that God must be immaterial and unmixed, because any composite substance requires an external creator to bind its parts and is subject to decay or separation. He concludes that God's "simplicity" (purity) is necessary for His perfection and immutability.</summary>
<keywords>divine simplicity, immateriality of God, First Cause, immutability, theological philosophy, nature of God, reason</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of God's nature, moving from His immateriality to His "simplicity" (His status as a non-composite being) and His "perfection." The author argues that because God is the "First Cause," He cannot be made of parts or influenced by external forces.</meta>

### THE SECOND BOOK.
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

Every individual part and limb borrows its virtue and worth:
That is a sign of imperfection, which has never seemed to dull
God's pure clarity and radiance—a brilliance that permits no mixing,
And finds its richness in unity alone. No opposition, however hard one may press <note>original: *wrijt*. Literally "rubs" or "grates," suggesting physical friction or conflict used here to mean opposition or trial.</note>,
Can rob God's simplicity of its luster.
Only a diamond can break a diamond <note>A traditional proverb used here to suggest that only something as pure and hard as God could affect God; since nothing else is like Him, He remains indestructible and immutable.</note>.

The number one precedes the many. Within the many,
Each individual unit possesses only its own single part:
Who then would dare to place God—who is the first of all—on a lower level,
And stain the unmixed "One" with the corruption of multiple numbers!

God borrows no necessity from without, but instead bestows
Necessity upon all things that rest upon His being.
Therefore, any "mixture" <term>Mixture: in 17th-century philosophy, a "mixture" (mengsel) refers to any substance composed of different elements. Such things are considered "lower" because they depend on their parts to exist.</term> is entirely excluded here,
For a mixture consists of many things cast into a single lump.
Simplicity rejects multiplicity and stands firm
Even when multiplicity collapses because its binding ties have snapped.

<margin>God is perfect.</margin>
Perfection <term>Perfection: from the Latin *perfectio*, meaning "completeness." In this context, it means God lacks nothing and possesses every attribute to the highest possible degree.</term> reaches its highest degree in God,
And shines like a sun from all of God's attributes,
With such a full radiance that you cannot name
A single quality in which her light does not excel.
If this perfection were to fade in God, the Godhead itself would faint.
Oh, if only she would ignite my spirit with her glow while I rhyme,
How one would see God glowing in my poetry!
Yet now my verse cannot soar into the unapproachable light <note>A reference to the biblical description of God in 1 Timothy 6:16: "Who alone is immortal and who lives in unapproachable light."</note>
Of God's perfection, of which only a tiny spark
Has been given to creatures in this earthly life.
Imagine for a moment: what are sight and sharp hearing
In a physical eye and ear? God is nothing *but* eye and ear.
What does He not hear, what does He not see—He who allows Himself to be seen or heard
Only in a shadow and a sound, from that perfectly built tower <note>original: *volbouden toren*. A metaphor for the height of divine majesty; "volbouden" implies something built to completion or perfection.</note>,
Whose spire pierces through all things and rises ever higher?
There, neither air nor spirit can carry a pen that does not shudder with cold <note>original: *yst*. Literally "ices" or "freezes." The author suggests that human intellect is chilled and paralyzed by the overwhelming height of divine truth.</note>,
And hardly has it reached the lowest sphere in its ascent,
Before it falls back down from above, utterly overwhelmed.

<summary>The author argues that God’s nature is defined by absolute "simplicity" (unity) and "perfection," meaning He is not composed of parts and is the source of all necessity. The poem concludes with a humble admission that human intellect and language are too weak to scale the heights of God’s infinite majesty.</summary>
