And cries: shut your eyes, and cease your undertaking:
In God's perfection all the stars are blind.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Understand briefly all that you must praise outside of God,
The <term>Godhead</term> <note>original: *Godtheit*. Refers to the divine nature or essence of God.</note> surpasses them in her perfections.
She does not lean on anything outside herself, but remains unshakably standing
In her simplicity. Created beings cast
Their eyes only upon her perfections together,
And their limited nature <note>original: *bepaeltheit*. The state of being bounded or finite, as opposed to God’s infinity.</note> yields, and must rightly feel shame
Before the infinite good, which defines all good,
And shares of its virtue, which beams down endlessly,
And flows down into the <term>universe</term> <note>original: *heelal*. Literally "the whole-all."</note>. The Godhead, from the peaks
Of her perfection, pours down, as if by drops,
The gifts of this good, as it pleases her,
Where each bears the title of perfection
According to the distribution, poured down from heaven's pinnacles and roofs,
Not to be approached by any perfections of a lower kind:
For whoever compares the inequality of perfections
Together, finds how here the lesser yields to the greater,
And when one encounters the greatest good, in these shadows,
[ For this universe is night compared to God ] then no climbing
Higher avails, and one remains standing before the most high,
Which is sufficient, and here nothing imperfect attaches to it.
Whatever virtue was instilled in the <term>creatures</term> <note>original: *schepfelen*. Referring to all created things—humans, animals, and plants—as the "work" of the Creator.</note>, God's work,
That was in the <term>cause</term> <note>original: *oirzaeck*. A philosophical term for God as the "First Cause" or origin of all things.</note> first, like the royal coat of arms
In the golden seal, before it was imprinted in the wax.
Thus it appears, in the finite and the perfect, without end,
An equality in virtues: for they resemble
God's perfections, from which they take their strength;
Although they excel infinitely in the cause,
Yet are finite in the work that springs from its cause.
In the necessary existence of God's perfect being,
Which cannot *not* exist, we are thus commended
The perfect being of this inexhaustible source,
That if this being could not exist in essence,

# THE SECOND BOOK.

He would not be the spring-vein nor the origin of the streams
That deliver such an Ocean. This perfection <note>original: *volkomenheit*. In 17th-century theology, this refers to the state of being complete and lacking nothing.</note>
Strikes no shore anywhere, and is never measured
By sounding-lead, nor by the intellect that sails upon that abyss.
In this perfection is fullness and contentment.
Here nothing is lacking: here there is nothing else to be added:
Just as a chalice, poured full of wine to the brim,
Swallows not a single drop more; just as the sun, by fire
And living light and heat radiated and glowed through to the full,
Can swallow no more heat into its sphere while we see it roll
High around the globe of the world;
Just as the air, stuffed full, leaves nothing empty or deficient.
But let humility restrain the bold tongue:
Its imperfection tarnishes God's perfection,
And stammers word upon word. The painter’s spirit goes astray
Who with his dull paint depicts the fire of Troy, <note>A common classical reference to the legendary burning of the city of Troy; the author uses it to show that art cannot capture the true intensity of reality, just as language cannot capture God.</note>
Or the glowing of the sun as it shines in life.
Why? No dull matter is given such power.

<margin>God is one, single, and alone.</margin>
God’s singular unity in essence flows and derives
From His simplicity, <note>original: *eenvouwigheit*. A technical theological term meaning "not composed of parts." Unlike humans, who have bodies and souls or various traits, God's nature is one undivided whole.</note> which remains without mixture,
And in no way consists of diverse things
Poured together; unlike everything sprouted from matter,
Which, bound by a tie, consists of form and matter, <note>A reference to the Aristotelian idea that all physical things are a combination of "form" (the essence or shape) and "matter" (the physical stuff).</note>
And scatters into parts again when the thread is broken.
In unity God's perfection is found,
Which needs no bond, and surpasses in nobility
Everything that hangs connected, and cries: I alone am He
Who originates from myself, the only pure One,
From whom the countless number of such diverse things,
Like brooks from their source, received a single essence,
And in which they end. God's singleness sets the measure
To each thing's quality, quantity, nature, and state.
It also appears in every work, which in all its parts
Must thank its cause: and since the many...

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological argument regarding the nature of the Divine. While the previous page discussed God's "simplicity"—the idea that He is not made of parts—this page extends that logic to prove God's absolute unity. The catchword "Een" (One) at the bottom of the previous page signals the transition to this discussion on oneness.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

Whether multiplicity in number, or a fixed unity,
Are applied to beings as their attributes,
Both this unity and multiplicity, like the streams
Of water from their source, descend from the single Being. <note>original: *het enckle wezen*. This refers to the concept of "Simple Being," which in 17th-century philosophy means an existence that is not composed of different elements but is a pure, undivided whole.</note>
Thus this oneness remains an attribute, planted
Within the essence itself, in a steadfast state.
If no perfection can be lacking in the Most High,
Then no multitude of Gods can deprive Him
Of that single unity; unless one wished, against all reason,
To count out more than one perfection:
But this idea shames itself, since such throne-peers, <note>original: *troongenooten*. A poetic way of describing multiple gods of equal status sharing power.</note>
Cast down from their thrones by their own imperfections,
Would tumble to the earth in a conflict over their differences.
The heavenly court tolerates in its supreme majesty
No defect, nor any blemish; therefore the smallest speck
Of imperfection distinguishes God’s unique and single self
From the countless gods and deities, long and widely
Sown throughout the world by blind idolatry:
Though even they generally place one Godhead at the peak,
Obeyed in its laws by so many lesser ones.

Where the highest power is divided among many,
There is no monarchy; for no ruler differs
From the others, since they run with equal spirit,
And maintain a common burden with equal hands:
Thus in such a realm there appears a shared participation
Of power, and of authority, and luster, and majesty;
For each carries his portion. If the bond of state is to hold,
Binding this division, then majority voting must prevail,
And each individual yields to the general rule:
That certainly does not suit the Arch-Monarch of heaven;
Therefore a single power sits at the peak of the kingdom of kingdoms,
And the lower must strike the flag <note>original: *de vlagh... strijcken*. A nautical metaphor meaning to lower one's colors or surrender, common in Dutch Golden Age writing.</note> to the single, unique One.

This is evident to us in nature, in civil authority,
And in art. One sun illuminates all the earth with the day.

<summary>The author argues for the absolute unity of God, asserting that true perfection cannot be shared among multiple deities. He uses the political metaphor of a "monarchy" to illustrate that divine authority must reside in a single power rather than a divided council.</summary>
<keywords>monotheism, divine unity, monarchy, perfection, idolatry, simplicity of God, Arch-Monarch</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the absolute unity of God. While the previous page addressed the impossibility of multiple deities sharing power, this page uses analogies from the natural and mechanical world to demonstrate that every complex system requires a single governing principle or "head."</meta>

# BOOK THE SECOND.

<sig>H 2</sig>

A single soul alone governs all the members in the body,
One general the whole army, one prince so many cities,
A single rudder the ship, in the bottomless water. <note>original: *hulck*. A "hulk" was a large, heavy type of merchant sailing vessel common in the 17th century.</note>
One clock-spring drives so many iron wheels:
Lest I pass over the king of the bees, <note>Historically, the leader of a beehive was referred to as a "king" before it was scientifically understood to be a queen.</note>
Who take to the field and battle under a single head.
Thus it stands: nature and art lead us to this truth.
Who then can rightly deny God’s simple unity,
Who sets laws for states, who can govern princes,
Maintains nature in its essence, and makes the arts increase?
   Whatever harmony springs from a multiplicity,
Must necessarily exist by one alone:
Now we see in every genus a single thing, which to the many
Provides a measure; thus they receive and share
The multiple from the one: but the harmony of
The multiple rests only upon the essence: for this reason
No multiplicity, but the One, chosen above others,
Can alone be the principle of all other things.
The very essence of all that is found
Is one alone; but God, poured out into many,
Is His essence alone, and nothing else. Where remain
The dreamers now, who advocate for more than one Deity?
   Where there are many orders, unequal in height,
It shall be necessary that one order yield to the other
In worth and in authority; or if they stand together as equals,
Then they share in their state; thus none may exalt itself;
For each borrows and draws its power from the other:
Thus each then has a lack of glory and of strength,
And none of them all is finished and perfect.
This one cannot feel regarding God without trembling,
Who through perfection holds the crown of crowns,
In which the simple "One" shines, the most beautiful diamond.
   If the Deity came to behold His equal,
He would have to strike His feather and crown for a Phoenix, <note>The Phoenix was a popular symbol for uniqueness and the "One," as only one was believed to exist in the world at any given time.</note>

<meta>catchword: Who</meta>

<summary>The author argues for the absolute oneness of God by drawing parallels to earthly leadership—such as generals, princes, and even the "king" of bees—concluding that perfection cannot be shared among equals.</summary>
<keywords>Monotheism, Divine Unity, hierarchy, natural law, perfection, sovereignty, Phoenix symbol</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the absolute unity of God, moving from logical and natural proofs to historical and mythological examples. It specifically addresses how ancient wisdom recognized the One God, even when the common people fell into idolatry.</meta>

->REFLECTIONS.<-

He who raises his hallowed head in our day,
And bellows, since he never saw his equal;
While sprinkled with sun-gold and purple upon his wings,
And followed by a procession, no birds
Look him in the eye except with reverence,
As such a rainbow <note>The "rainbow" refers to the colorful plumage of the Phoenix.</note> travels alone
To the ancient City of the Sun, <term>Heliopolis</term> <note>original: *Zonnestadt*. A major city of ancient Egypt, a center for the worship of the sun god Ra.</note> accustomed to honoring
The phoenix feathers with temple and altar and incense burners,
Instead of the God, who remains simple and one,
And created all beauty on which the sun ever shone.

Let one ask Trismegistus, <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with wisdom, writing, and the "Hermetic" tradition.</note> to be set high in wisdom,
Who for so long has lit God’s light for the Egyptian
Through instruction and laws from of old,
Why the people give him the name Thrice Greatest. <note>original: *Drymael grootste*. This is a literal translation of the Greek "Trismegistus."</note>
Alas, had the City of the Sun taken the hero’s commands to heart,
It would not deny God’s simplicity in endless gods,
Through fire, altar, and sacrificial smoke,
Nor worship animals alone—but even onions and leeks <note>A reference to common satirical critiques (found in authors like Juvenal) that the Egyptians worshipped garden vegetables as gods.</note>
As deities, and gloss over those abominations.
He calls God nameless; for one does not need to name Him
Who requires no name, since the simplicity of His essence,
His unique attribute, clearly distinguishes Him from others.
Thus speaks this great intellect. This is what ten Sibyls <note>Prophetesses of the ancient world who were often cited by early Christian writers as having predicted the coming of monotheism.</note> teach
The teachable, who wish to learn of God and truth.
Even the wisest sages, famed in Heathendom, <note>original: *Heidendom*.</note>
Determine one God, and unity everywhere;
Although this knowledge was clouded by many dreams,
And the light, as if through a cloud, beamed upon them incompletely,
They who were more inclined to pleasures and vanity,
Or slow in inquiry, too reckless and blind,
Following the tracks of the blind, firmly took the wrong path,
And behaved all too ungratefully toward the appearing light.
Thus false speech <note>original: *logentael*.</note> gains currency, with a semblance
Of truth, and it tied a following to its line.

### THE SECOND BOOK. 61

H 3
<meta>catchword: Sha-</meta>

God’s unique simplicity is godlessly slandered.
The number of gods grows while the people degenerate.
The speech of the wise follows the plural number
Of gods, according to the fashion, to please political ambition. <note>The author suggests that philosophers often spoke of many gods just to suit the political climate, even if they knew better.</note>
Recklessness cannot find the middle way:
It either denies simplicity or denies them all together.
This one honors no deity; that one, hundreds in a row.
This suits the idolater, and that the atheist. <note>original: *ongodist*. Literally "un-god-ist."</note>

<margin>God is infinite.</margin>
That God is infinite lies exposed before the light of reason,
For one who esteems Him perfect in essence, in magnitude,
In His quality, in wisdom, goodness, power,
And every attribute, effect, virtue, and strength.
All that you encounter in God testifies that He is infinite;
Whether He is reflected inwardly or outwardly;
Inwardly, unbounded in origin and essence;
Outwardly, in the universe that radiates from His essence;
He sees infinity embrace all perfection,
And essence, and origin, and everything—except
Whatever gloriously hides within Him or shines out in the eyes
Of creatures: however deep, long, wide, or high,
There is no being, nothing exceptionally sprouted, <note>That is, nothing created or brought into existence.</note>
That is not contained by His essence and enclosed
In the primal perfection of His infinity.
This heavenly attribute distinguishes the Creator
From the creature, who is unprovided with such a magnificent weapon; <note>The term "weapon" here refers to a defining characteristic or heraldic mark of status.</note>
Although it denies to God and His deity that which limits the created,
It nonetheless establishes something incomprehensible
That suits the incomprehensibility of God’s nature.

Athenian wisdom <note>Refers to Greek philosophy.</note> attempts to penetrate this secret,
And calls the deity Pan, or the "All-ness" of all things; <note>original: *Pan*. In Greek mythology, Pan was the god of the wild, but philosophers used the name (which means "All" in Greek) to describe the totality of the universe or the "World-Soul."</note>
For what is, and was, and shall be hereafter,
Imagine endlessly: the deity is the all,
The Ocean of things, since one can fish there
For an innumerable abundance of light and forms,

->### REFLECTIONS.<-

Variety, beings, virtue, and their perfections,
Gathered together as if in a bosom of all that is most noble.
Here I would wish for tongue and language to express how one
Might name the beginning and end of all things with a single name—
He who is nameless, and remains so, and desires no name,
Or names without end, through which He is fully honored,
And simultaneously expressed. What tongue has this power?
No tongue: for if this were known, God would not remain uncircumscribed
In His reach; as when in the school of the Greeks
One wishes to draw such a sun with a piece of charcoal. <note>The author uses the metaphor of "drawing a sun with charcoal" to illustrate how human attempts to describe God are crude and inadequate compared to the reality.</note>
But to paint Infinity from life,
She must herself descend into our understanding,
To paint herself in her infinite light:
Though she paints herself partially in finite form before the eyes
Of men, who long slept through the day of the Godhead,
How clearly this day dawned, and was seen in the deepening
And in the heightening of the light and darkness
Of the creatures, in whom God is imperfectly
Beheld, for the time being, until the soul, released, <note>That is, released from the body at death.</note>
Beholds the morning light, followed or pursued by neither
Night nor evening hours, and she may endure
To see Infinity on her most beautiful day.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If infinity were taken away from God's essence,
That would immediately deny the most perfect perfection
In essence, and origin, necessity, and strength,
Effect, singleness, simplicity, and power,
And whatever shall continually guide this essence, which is
Unmixably indivisible in its attributes.
God’s attributes—no matter what intellect storms against this
To inspect them through distinctions of form—
Are nothing but pure form and essence altogether.
The weakness of the understanding seeks to ennoble
God’s nature and essence through various names: but this shines
No more divinely than at the preeminent point

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of God’s nature, specifically focusing on the transition from the previous page's discussion on God's infinity to the unity of Divine attributes. It argues that God's characteristics (like wisdom and power) are not separate parts but are identical with His single, indivisible essence.</meta>

### THE SECOND BOOK. ###

Concerning the unique singleness of Being. Whatever particular thing
Is conceived in the brain, it remains contained under
Being. Name now freely whatever you wish to name;
I hear of no attribute that differs in the least
From the others. If this seems too dark and obscure to you,
Reason demonstrates it to you with clarity and bright luster.
That which, through a single form—pure, unmixed,
And boundless—brings so many attributes
To itself, and under itself, surely that is a sign
Of excelling others in majesty,
Who, through variations of forms and reasons,
And still limited, possess this good in common. <note>The author argues that while humans have various distinct traits, God's attributes are actually one single, infinite reality.</note>
If there were now a distinction of unequal degrees
In the highest God’s soul-saving attributes <term>eigenſchappen: the characteristics of God, such as His goodness or justice</term>
And pure essence, then that infinite beauty
Through a single form and reason would not reach this crown,
But would instead gather a string of pearls,
From many differences of a diminished being.
God would be considered immeasurable through one form,
Through another wise and good, and through another the primal power:
That does not suit His greatness: for a distinction of many things
Bound into one, constitutes a whole made of parts: <note>This refers to the doctrine of "Divine Simplicity," which holds that God is not "composed" of different pieces, but is a singular, unified existence.</note>
And what is simplicity <term>eenvouwigheit: the state of being non-composite or without parts</term> in essence and existence
Other than the rejection of difference and mixtures?
He who is rich in Himself needs to gather nothing more:
That suits the created, but by no means the Uncreated.
If there is a distinction between attribute and essence
In God, then He borrows something: this wounds God’s majesty.
Where distinction exists, there something is acquired
That flows from the root of Being, never begun.
If His attribute does not consist within the essence,
And is distinguished from the essence in fact;
Then one must fetch it from elsewhere than from Being’s bosom.
Whoever seeks such a tangled ball of yarn will wander far from God.

To suggest God is composed of parts would be to treat the Creator like a limited, created being.</summary>

# REFLECTIONS. #

And though God’s attribute and essence, as if divided
And shaped by form, stand distinctly depicted in the brain,
Nonetheless, this does not deny or dispute
The simplest singularity of God's perfections;
Since this distinction plants no foothold in Him,
But exists outside God's nature, in the human understanding,
Which, however sharply we whet our wits and senses,
Remains too dull to fully grasp God’s high majesty,
As it is in essence; or according to that simplicity,
Uniform in the highest degree, and shrinking from distinction:
But we understand God only piecemeal, incompletely,
According to the proportion of the creature <note>The "proportion of the creature" refers to the idea that humans can only understand God through the finite things He created, rather than seeing His infinite nature directly.</note>, which gives our eyes
Only a glimpse and a shadow and a ray of the Godhead;
A spirit that secretly drifts through all things:
For if God’s attribute were to truly vary itself,
As it is mirrored in our understanding—prone to learning—
Glimpse upon glimpse; certainly in every appearance
The rainbow would be less diverse than God. <note>The author compares God to light: just as white light contains all the colors of the rainbow but remains one single thing, God’s various attributes (mercy, justice, etc.) are actually one single, unified essence.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The infinity of God, impossible to seize or understand,
Allows itself to be enclosed by neither time nor place,
Nor can human understanding—too weak to actually
Behold this Being—reach the summit of His state.
Infinity floats on uncatchable wings,
And expresses itself in the brain, through the knowing and not knowing
Of God, and all that is infused into things
Is found gathered concisely in Him alone,
At least according to the power through which He, eternally pregnant, <term>eeuwigh zwanger: a poetic term describing God as the eternal source and "womb" of all possible creation</term>
Expanded Himself in time, whether shorter or longer.
Imagine for a moment, that you saw in a single glance
All the beauty and all the excellences
Of creatures piled together—what pleasure this sight would bring!
How your soul, touched by fire and zeal, would depart
From the body, for such beauty and delight!
This is finite: now consider what the infinity of God must be.

<meta>This page transitions from the general concept of God's infinity to the specific theological attribute of His immeasurability and omnipresence, contrasting these with the materialistic theories of ancient Greek philosophy.</meta>

### THE SECOND BOOK. ###

Although God alone is infinity itself, yet the head
Of Epicurus <note>Epicurus (341–270 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who taught that the universe is infinite and composed of atoms and void.</note> reels, as he dares to call the scope
Of the world infinite, and asserts that this universe
Consists of worlds linked by neither measure nor number;
Of worlds built from fibers <note>The author uses "vezelen" (fibers) to describe the "atoms" or "corpuscles" of Epicurean physics.</note> that either float
Or cling together: meanwhile, he leaves a space
And a void, so that every body might move.
So he dreams, and yet Lucretius <note>Titus Lucretius Carus, the Roman poet who popularized Epicurean philosophy in his work "De rerum natura."</note> deems that dreamer shrewd.

<margin>God is immeasurable.</margin>

Now immeasurability <term>Immeasurability: In 17th-century theology, this refers to God's "immensitas"—the quality of being beyond any spatial limit or measurement.</term> accompanies infinity:
For where one finds the one, there one finds them both,
As well as the "everywhere-ness" of God’s presence,
An attribute that never departs from the eternal Being.
Whoever defines God’s infinity in their reflections
Will also see the other attribute permeating everywhere.
Immeasurability encompasses all that is poured out
From God, and is also understood within God’s nature,
In essence and in power: for if the intellect wishes to sail
Upon this Ocean of Beings, its waves
Go wandering without a shore. Wherever the intellect turns,
It sees a naked sea, a single element,
Beholding a single face of the Godhead with fear,
And humility and awe. Upon God’s bottomless being
This universe floats, like a sponge in the salty liquid,
Saturated by the sea, full of foam and water. <note>This "sponge in the sea" was a common 17th-century metaphor (used by thinkers like St. Augustine and later Pierre Gassendi) to explain how God can be "in" everything without being identical to the material world.</note>
The power and the essence of the Godhead live completely
In the very smallest dot. Where God is not perceived
Is nowhere: for He is all at once, according to His power
And essence, everywhere. No darkness, no night
Hides from His all-seeing eye. Neither hypocrisy nor secret thoughts
Hide from the eternal light, which shines through pretense,
Sits in the heart, and sweeps the mists from the soul’s eye;
Therefore God rightly bears the name of Heart-Knower. <note>original: "hartekenner"</note>
But although the Godhead can clearly know our thoughts
Through His omnipresence, nonetheless God’s unmeasured...

# R E F L E C T I O N S. #

Magnitude is here in more perfect splendor,
Since it essentially, and actually, and entirely
Fills and packs the All, and leaves nothing empty,
As much beneath the stars as where no stars drift.

<margin>God is essentially present everywhere.</margin>
This omnipresence of Being, though it flows
Substantially everywhere in all things, and glows through,
And sustains, and feeds so many creatures,
Yet it is not touched, nor does it need to wipe any stain
From its purity: unlike the human soul
Which, from its animating <note>The original Dutch "zielen" refers here to the soul's union with or animation of the physical body.</note> of the body, held a stain from the flesh:
For God is placeless <term>Placeless: From the Dutch "plaetseloos," meaning God is not limited by spatial dimensions or confined to any specific location.</term> in all things,
So present that He can permeate everything,
And remain stainless in essence, endlessly far
Separated from all that exists in space and time.
He remains tightly enclosed in the purity of His being.
Thus He remains far and near, together and poured out:
And if He descends, in an infinite way,
Into the creature, He retains honor and praise in His height.
And although Plato <note>Plato (c. 427–347 BCE). The author refers to the Platonic concept of the *Anima Mundi* or "World Soul," but warns against a materialistic interpretation of this idea.</note> dares to call Him the soul of the world,
He by no means wishes to gloss over the errors
That mix uncreatedness, bluntly and coarsely,
With heavenly bodies and tangible matter.

The diamond-knot of omnipresence, findable everywhere,
And infinity is so powerful and indissoluble,
Woven together, and intertwined, and guided,
That form and matter would sooner part from every being—
While that being remains a being—than these could be
Separated from one another, or God change into non-god.
He who clips the "everywhere-ness" of the infinite Being,
Denies the infinity and perfection in God.
The great Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure to whom mystical and philosophical texts were attributed. The author cites the famous Hermetic definition: "God is an infinite sphere whose center is everywhere and whose circumference is nowhere."</note> establishes the firm foundations,
Where no circumference is found, and everywhere
The same center is found in His perfect sphere.
How then does unreason speak so shamefully, so wickedly

# THE SECOND BOOK. #

Of God, as if this held a blasphemous view.
If the sunbeam in no mud pools
Is desecrated, nor stained, however low the light descends;
How is God's essence itself, which is spirit, then dishonored
And defiled by the mire? It is but coarse minds,
Who spin blasphemy from the omnipresence of God's essence,
And suck such gall, while the honeybee
Sucks out a scent of comfort here, and nothing but a delicacy?

<margin>God is unchangeable.</margin>
The divine essence knows no changes,
And maintains one countenance, while all other things
Change in their turn, arise, and perish;
The firmer creatures exist only through His power.
The Deity, purely one, simple <term>Simple: In classical theology, "simplicity" means that God is not composed of different parts or characteristics but is a single, indivisible essence.</term>, and infinite,
Complete, unmixed, immeasurable, and necessary,
And beyond passive power <note>Original: "lijbre maght." This refers to the philosophical concept of *potentia passiva*, the capacity to be acted upon or changed by an external force; God, being "pure act," cannot be changed.</note>, separated by no change,
Neither in quality, nor in substance,
Nor in magnitude also—of state, and properties;
A firm possession, in which she <note>The author personifies the "Godheit" (Deity) using feminine pronouns, common in 17th-century Dutch grammar.</note> sits climbed to the peak.
One who is fully grown needs no more increase.
That which eternally blooms and blushes is saddened by no storm.
In detachment there is no bond or cord to break.
The Deity is compared to gold, to pure gold,
A phoenix-ore <note>A poetic term for a substance as indestructible and self-renewing as the mythical phoenix.</note>, upon which neither fire nor corrosive liquid,
However long one tests it, wins or gains the slightest hold.

<margin>Objection; whether this does not conflict with God's free will.</margin>
This immovability, just as beautiful in its blooming,
Seems to prune heaven's free will and liberty,
And to snatch away God's discretion by force:
For if He cannot do otherwise than the immovable power
Permits Him, that restrains the wanting of this or that.
If will is one with essence, and beyond difference,
Then a single will, like the essence, stands eternally firm,
Which suits no change of "this way" and "otherwise."

<margin>This is refuted.</margin>
But the immovability, to be perceived in the heavenly,
In no way restrains God's will, in His free choosing;

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the divine nature, moving from God’s immutability to His perfect foreknowledge and how His unchanging essence serves as the foundation for the existence and immortality of all created things.</meta>

-># REFLECTIONS #<-

Since He, in a single glance with His provident eye,
Has seen through all things from eternity, from so high,
He, seated in the light, met all times as they came,
Watching each in its order and turn glide by the same,
In their necessity, in their contingency <note>Original: "gebeurzaemheit." In 17th-century philosophy, this refers to things that happen by chance or could have been otherwise, as opposed to things that must happen by necessity.</note>,
And freedom, according to the lot assigned to each destiny;
Therefore the Deity struggles, neither within nor without,
With His free will, nor with eternal decrees in doubt,
No more changeable than God is in His own nature.
The All-Knower, once set at the helm of every creature,
Was escaped by no cause, no aim <note>Original: "wit," meaning a target or goal in archery, here used for God's divine purpose.</note>, nor any circumstance,
In the bottomless deep, from the heights to the low expanse.
He who doubts the weight needs a weight and a scale:
God needs neither weight nor scale: He knows the whole tale.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That which never changes permits no equals in fate,
And is not poured from above into the bushel's <note>A "schepel" (bushel) is a dry measure for grain; the author uses it here as a metaphor for the limited, measurable capacity of created beings compared to God's infinite nature.</note> state;
Because the bushel remains measurable, by place and time defined,
And shifts and wanders, by form and limit confined.
Since no creature can also contain within its own heart
The complete treasures of every good in every part,
God is necessarily the only perfection alone,
By a nature suited to the attributes He has shown.
The change of creatures from their own nature does spring,
And their very substance flows as a voluntary thing
From God, which is why they quickly lose their shape
And essence, should God's influence allow an escape.
The creature then has no possession but from above,
To praise the Unchangeable One in eternity and love.
From this immutability, which could never fail or sink,
There spring forth livelily, as if from a single brink,
Two streams, twins, known by their names of old,
Necessity, together with immortality, we are told:
Necessary in existence, internal work, and might,
Immortality, shielded from death’s violence and blight.

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into God’s nature, specifically moving from the concept of God’s immutability—established on the previous page—to the concept of Divine Necessity. The author argues that because God is perfect and unchanging, His existence and His internal actions (such as self-love) are not accidental, but necessary results of His own essence.</meta>

## THE SECOND BOOK.

<margin>God is necessarily that which He is.</margin>
Through this necessity of the Godhead, never praised enough,
God can neither be without essence, nor in any way be
Other than He shows and declares Himself in His will and being.
The first Being, who establishes all other beings,
Exists by Himself, through Himself, and borrows no support from without;
Therefore, upon that shield, one can parry any claim of God’s purposelessness.
If perfection adorns the highest majesty,
Who would strip it of the crown of its necessity?
If He consists in simplicity, constant and enduring,
Who then deems the essence of the Godhead unnecessary? <note>Original: "onnootwendigh." In this philosophical context, the author is arguing against the idea that God's existence is "contingent" (something that could or could not be). To the author, God exists by a logical and natural necessity.</note>
Does God stand in free power as a target for neither bow nor string?
What need is there for Him to fear change?
Whoever dares to rob Him of what is necessary,
Denies simplicity, the enduring, and the infinite,
And the unchangeable in God’s nature and character,
As well as His perfections, preserved in a treasury.

<margin>All natural operation is uniform and of one kind:</margin>
Now as far as God’s work is concerned, the Godhead, at every hour,
And also from eternity, brings forth from Her nature
By necessity that which She works, and works nothing against this,
As God from eternity loves and beholds Himself:
For these works express God in God alone.
One cannot, then, tear the necessary from the internal:
For God arises from Himself, and His eternal realm of action
In His internal work <note>This refers to the theological concept of "opera ad intra"—actions that take place within the Trinity, such as the Father begetting the Son, which are considered necessary and eternal.</note> is equal to Himself,
And also necessary: for so that this may be sealed,
Whatever works naturally preserves its course and rule.

<margin>but it does not restrain God’s free will in external operation.</margin>
Yet this necessity in working does not oppose
Any earthly perfection to which God gives its luster;
Since this does not spring from forced compulsion <note>Original: "nootdwang," meaning an external force or coercion.</note>,
But from the constraint of nature <note>Original: "natuurbedwang." The author distinguishes between being forced by someone else and acting according to one's own perfect, necessary nature.</note>. One also cannot conclude
From this necessity of operation, that God’s will,
Which is free, stands shackled, restrained, or still
In operation, excluded from God and God’s nature:
But only what is contingent <note>Original: "gebeurzaem." This refers to things that happen by chance or could have been otherwise. The author argues that while God’s internal nature is "necessary," His external creations are the product of His free will.</note> is here cast out.

I 3
<meta>catchword: Out</meta>

<summary>This page explores the concept of Divine Necessity, arguing that God’s existence and internal actions are necessary consequences of His perfect nature rather than accidents. It distinguishes between internal necessity (God loving Himself) and external free will (God creating the world).</summary>
<keywords>necessity, divine nature, free will, simplicity, eternity, internal works, contingency, perfection</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on God’s nature. While the previous page focused on God's "necessity" (the idea that He must exist and act according to His perfect nature), this page transitions to the "second ray" of His character: His immortality and how His internal stillness contrasts with His external governance of the universe.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

From God’s nature, so that outwardly free will,
Inwardly God’s nature thus works through distinction.
We see the compulsion of nature working internally:
Free choice makes itself known from the outside.

<margin>God is immortal, and the origin of life.</margin>
Now the second ray shines out from immutability,
Immortality, which distinguishes God from created things:
For the origin of life gave the charter <note>Original: "hantveft," meaning a legal deed or grant of rights. The author suggests God "chartered" souls with the gift of life.</note> of living
To the souls of men and the realm of spirits,
Thus it also flows from God that none of them perish:
But it is impossible that the Creator of spirits should die,
Since the power of mortality remains excluded from God,
And the divine essence is not mixed, nor poured out
From substance and accident, form and matter. <note>These are technical terms from Scholastic philosophy. "Substance" is the core thing, "accident" is a non-essential quality, "form" is the design, and "matter" is the stuff. The author argues God is "simple"—not made of parts—and therefore cannot be dismantled by death.</note>
His primary simplicity of being surpassed
Whatever being ever received from God, or shall receive.
So no mortality can cling to the Creator of life;
And this denial of dying establishes entirely,
In God and His nature, the exact opposite
Of death, that is the pulse <note>Original: "vaegh." An archaic term referring to a wave, a source, or a vital pulse of life.</note> of life, through whose blessing
The Godhead, outside of itself, does not only see moving
And stirring what moves, in that created circle,
But also constantly hangs in a reflection
Of Her own purity, enraptured and uplifted,
While, as if in competition, God’s wisdom, and power,
And goodness continue mirroring each other,
And in the contemplation of God’s essence stand motionless,
Stretched in their strength, at the peak of all points,
Which infinitely excel in God’s perfections.
So immortality lives rested and still within itself;
While it governs and steers all things outside itself
Through its free will, voluntarily unconstrained,
And in no way forced by the power of its nature.

<margin>God is eternal.</margin>
Just as immortality denies decay,
And speaks of life in God, the origin of existence

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page explores the immortality of God, arguing that because God is a simple, uncomposed being, He is immune to death, unlike created spirits which only live by His decree. It describes God's internal life as a perfect, motionless contemplation of His own Wisdom, Power, and Goodness.</summary>
<keywords>immortality, divine nature, simplicity of God, free will, scholasticism, origin of life, eternity</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the divine nature, moving from the concept of immortality (the inability to die) to the concept of eternity (the absence of beginning or end). It addresses theological challenges to God's unique eternity, specifically refuting dualism and the idea of eternal matter.</meta>

# THE SECOND BOOK.

Of all things, besides God, who never knew corruption,
So eternity in God denies both beginning and end:
For whatever has a beginning and an end, comes and goes,
And suffers through the jolting and changing of its state:
Just as a creature suffers and endures, by whose suffering
Time exists, and rolls upon the shifting of the ages,
And the turn from being nothing to something, or from something
Seeing the essence of that something melt away as nothing.
From the unchangeability of God, remaining in His state,
Eternity is then with reason ascribed to Him.
He knows neither "before" nor "after": for eternity knows no measure,
Which leaves a space between boundary and boundary.
In eternity there appears an equally joyful blooming,
A being, a countenance, a pulse <note>Original: "vaegh." As noted on the previous page, this refers to a vital source or rhythmic wave of life.</note>, followed by no weariness,
One same strength, a power, a living light,
A presence—the glory of my verse.
If God did not exist before, and became something later,
Then there appears a distinction of origin, progress, and order;
Then He certainly borrows His being from an external power,
For it was brought from nothing into something.
This power would then remain the cause from which He began,
Being older than He: but this is too crudely spun <note>A Dutch idiom meaning a clumsy or poorly constructed argument.</note>.
Thus He knows no beginning, and consequently no end:
For one who was eternal, remains known as eternal forever.
One also cannot—and here no flowery words can help—
Call the created thing eternal alongside God the creator,
Defending without shame, like heathens without reason,
And mad Manichaeans <note>Followers of Manichaeism, a dualistic religion that taught the universe is a struggle between two equal powers: Good (Light/God) and Evil (Darkness/Matter).</note>, two equal eternities,
Or two principles, one for good and one for evil,
Unless through that passion one betrays the one God;
Just as those "wise-in-their-own-eyes" still, to the desecration of God’s praise,
Pay the honor of God’s eternity to primordial matter <note>The author is refuting the Aristotelian or Atomist idea that matter has always existed and was not created by God.</note>;
For without eternity the Godhead is disparaged,
As if estranged from the attribute inseparable from God’s essence.

<summary>The author argues that God’s eternity means He has no beginning or end, unlike created things that change and decay. He rejects the "mad" idea of two equal eternal powers (good and evil) or that physical matter is eternal.</summary>
<keywords>eternity, dualism, Manichaeans, primordial matter, divine essence, immutability, creation vs creator</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological argument from page 71, where the author refutes the idea that anything other than God can be eternal. Having established God's unique unchangeability, the text now explores the logical absurdities that arise if one grants eternity to created things, concluding with a poetic comparison between temporal time and divine eternity.</meta>

### REFLECTIONS.

If one attempts to place anything eternal beside God,
It drags a long tail of monstrosities <note>In this context, "monstrosities" refers to logical absurdities or theological errors that follow a false premise.</note> behind it,
Rebellious against God’s nature and mature reason.
Creatures would then carry themselves loftily, as if they were uncreated,
Immeasurable, endless, and unchangeable.
The <term>Deity</term> <note>Original: "Godtheit."</note> would then no longer be the source of all things, from which
Everything took its origin and stepped forth into the light of day.
The Deity would then, of its own accord, cast off all attributes
And titles that it carries, by which it is now clearly shown
That everything that has being yields to the Eternal Being.
If this primary crown is stripped from the Deity,
Then the creature proudly wears the title of "the perfect,"
And the uncreated and the creature would share
An attribute rightly recognized in God alone;
A distinction that must not be confused or mixed.
The firmament cannot allow any "before" or "after"
In eternity, which encompasses and spans all,
And is measured by nothing but the immeasurable hand.

<margin>
The distinction between time and eternity.
</margin>
Time and eternity differ in their duration,
Like branches from a single trunk, yet they are unequal parts
And set against one another: for whoever observes their span
Sees that one knows its end and origin, while the other does not.
One glides straight ahead, through progress and movement:
The other rests in silence and resists all agitation.
One is eventually halted and takes a turn at the end:
The other stands fast and never wavers.
One receives its measure from clockwork, bells, and stars,
And the sun and moon, so as not to wander in its progress:
The other measures the constellations, the sun, the moon, and all
That would mark even a thumb’s breadth of duration
From its ring alone, noted by centuries, years, and days,
By hours and moments, seasons and summer showers,
By winter, spring, and autumn—whether used or wretchedly misused—
Until God’s eternity clips the wings of Time.

<meta>catchword: Will</meta>

<summary>This page argues that only God possesses true eternity, as granting eternity to created things would strip God of His unique status as the source of all being. It concludes by defining the difference between the moving, measured nature of Time and the silent, unchanging nature of Eternity.</summary>
<keywords>eternity, time, deity, creation, attributes of God, duration, seasons, Joost van den Vondel</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page transitions to "The Second Book" of the treatise. It continues the argument from the previous page regarding the nature of eternity, moving from the definition of time versus eternity to a polemic against those who deny God’s unique, uncaused status. It reinforces the idea that for God to be God, He must be the ultimate source of all existence without being subject to any prior cause.</meta>

### THE SECOND BOOK.

<margin>
The absurdity in denying God’s eternity.
</margin>
If the godless man <note>Original: "ongodist," a 17th-century term for an atheist or one who denies the true nature of God.</note> now seeks to rob God of His eternity,
As if it conflicts with God’s nature if His cause were to soar above Him,
And float in a higher air than God the Creator;
Then he misses his mark: for God knows no cause, nor has one.
And should anything divine have arisen above God,
That would, by right, be God, and the most supreme being.
God’s essence is God Himself, who depends on no other,
But exists by Himself. Whoever accepts a God
Who yet leans on another, wanders away from God—the one
And eternal one, who appears to each in the light of creation.
A subordinate God, and a mere follower
Of something else, excludes Himself from the ring <note>The "ring" is a common historical metaphor for eternity, representing a circle with no beginning or end.</note>
Of endless eternity, which reason must first recognize.
One cannot, without denying or violating God’s nature,
Place anything above God in eternity and power:
Reason knows but one God, one eternity, one power.

<margin>
How glorious God’s eternity is.
</margin>
If duration <note>Original: "duurzaemheit," referring to the quality of lasting through time.</note>, in heaven and on earth,
Grants things their worth, and greater or lesser value,
How gloriously does that which shall endure eternally stand out,
And was and remains through itself in essence, above all
That has being; that is God, who was in being before our hours,
And calculates no beginning nor end of duration,
And outlasts whatever received within His ring
A short duration or a mere moment,
Or shall yet receive, from God’s enduring ages,
Never overtaken by the brain or the swiftest reason!
So many <term>perfections</term> <note>Original: "volkomenheên." In theological terms, these are the divine attributes like omnipotence and goodness.</note> of power, and goodness, and beauty,
And honor, and majesty, and wealth, which enrich
And adorn God’s crown like pearls and diamonds;
That abundance, that rejoicing, that eternal triumphing,
All captured at once in a single point with a single glance, <note>This refers to the concept of God's "eternal present," where He sees all of history in one single moment.</note>
What a mountain this creates, an invaluable treasure
Of every kind of good, to be found in the highest Good!
Now the world toils away at that molehill <note>The "molehill" (molshoop) is a metaphorical contrast to the "mountain" of God's goodness, representing the insignificance of earthly pursuits.</note>, you blinded ones,

K
<meta>Catchword: Who</meta>

<summary>This page argues that God is the uncaused and supreme being, asserting that any being dependent on another cannot be truly divine. It contrasts the fleeting, "molehill" nature of earthly life with the infinite, simultaneous perfections and eternal majesty of God.</summary>
<keywords>eternity, uncaused cause, divine essence, perfections of God, human reason, sovereignty, divine attributes</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the meditation on God's attributes, specifically contrasting the fleeting, fearful nature of worldly power with the perfect, unchanging permanence of divine eternity. It then transitions into a discussion of God's omnipotence as the foundation of all creation.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

He who scorns God’s eternity for earthly pleasure,
For temporary gain, or for beauty that rots
And stinks from the grave; who drinks murderous poisons
From a shallow golden cup, filled with shining rubies,
To appear as a god, yet remains fearful
In the midst of his splendor, trembling before his bodyguard's halberd <note>A halberd is a combined spear and battle-axe, the standard weapon of palace guards in the 17th century; the poet suggests even kings live in fear of their own protection.</note>.
What is your glory, compared to the eternal kingdom?
It is decaying and short-lived. The flatterers who beseech you
Are only at the service of your good fortune, and ready to depart
As soon as fickle fate turns its back on you.
But heaven’s eternity, remaining always in one state,
Is a perfect possession of the never-weary life <note>Original: "nimmerzatte," describing a life so fulfilling that it never becomes dull or over-satiated.</note>,
Upon a firm throne that neither yields nor wavers;
Which sees all below it—everything that slips, spatters, and consumes.
No corpse is embalmed here, nor tolled with the sound of bells.
No funeral procession follows the bier <note>A stand upon which a corpse or coffin is placed.</note> with tears and sobs
In stately mourning attire. No mourning cloth wipes the dust.
No shinbone, skull, rib, nor tombstone, nor churchyard
Reminds one here of the end, or of the approaching omen of death.
Life nests here under God’s turquoise roofs <note>A poetic reference to the blue canopy of the heavens.</note>,
And soars through eternity, which, the deeper one looks,
Is reached the less, and vanishes from sight;
So that the eye, weary and exhausted from searching those spheres,
Has found an equidistance, but never the circumference.

<margin>God is almighty.</margin>
Now follows omnipotence, upon which religion is built:
For God created, governs, and maintains the world
Through His almighty power, without which nothing would have ever come
From nothing—this wonderful universe, established in such an order
That one surely concludes that God, so rich in His works,
Could create many worlds out of nothing all at once;
Just as the art of painting shows, in the glorious display
Of a single masterpiece, how it can follow nature in every detail
Through its depiction; and just as our magnificent town hall speaks
To how many a proud building resides in Van Campen's brain <note>This refers to Jacob van Campen (1596–1657), the renowned Dutch architect who designed the Amsterdam Town Hall (now the Royal Palace), symbolizing how a creator's mind holds more than what is physically built.</note>;

<meta>The catchword "Hoe-" (How) at the bottom of the page indicates the start of the next page's first sentence.</meta>

<summary>This page contrasts the anxiety and decay of earthly power with the serene, unending nature of God's eternity, eventually transitioning to discuss God's omnipotence as the source of all creation.</summary>
<keywords>eternity, vanity, earthly power, funeral rites, omnipotence, creation, Jacob van Campen, architecture, heavens</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of God’s attributes. Having finished the meditation on Divine Eternity, the poet now shifts to Divine Omnipotence (Almogentheit). The text argues that God's power is unique because He creates from nothing (ex nihilo), a concept that baffled the ancient Greek philosophers.</meta>

### THE SECOND BOOK.

Although a craftsman requires material, God can work out of nothing; herein the omnipotence of the Godhead reveals itself most clearly. For there is no proportion between nothing and something, and this capacity alone distinguishes God’s almighty power from all borrowed or secondary abilities. Thus omnipotence triumphs, exalted at the highest peak, at which the Greek school in Athens <note>A reference to the ancient Greek philosophers, such as Aristotle and Plato, who generally held the view that "nothing comes from nothing" (ex nihilo nihil fit) and therefore struggled with the concept of a Creator who does not require pre-existing matter.</note> stood bewildered, because it never grasped this power within its own intellect. 

But from the infinity of His being, one can read that God’s omnipotence follows the very nature of His essence. For His almighty power, in its strength, resembles His being itself—or rather, it is that being, or something that does not deviate from it, being His own inherent characteristic. If the essence is infinite, then the power must also necessarily follow the essence in its infinity; for each of the two consists of a perfect proportion: a divine measure that is without measure. 

Through this omnipotence, God can then, at every hour, bring forth through the strength and impulse <note>Original: "vaegh," referring to the divine "sweep" or creative stroke of God's nature.</note> of His nature far more than the entire host of creatures is able to accomplish. This is a truth as clear as day: since no power is contained within a creature that does not spring from the Godhead above, which excels as the root of the tree. If, then, omnipotence encompasses the power of the creature with greater majesty, surely the divine power strives far beyond His creation and out of the sight of men. 

God’s almighty power drives straight through; no rock can break its progress—except for that which directly contradicts itself, such as being and not being at the same time <note>The poet refers to the "Law of Non-Contradiction." Theologians often argued that God's omnipotence does not extend to doing what is logically impossible, as a contradiction is "nothing" rather than a "thing" to be done.</note>. This is a firm truth, a logical deduction, a test, and the first clarity. For God is capable of all that can be grasped by the understanding, both that which is denied and that which is established as fact. Yet, to bind the established to the deniable at the very same time is impossible, since they would immediately consume one another.

<sig>K 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: That</meta>

<summary>This page explores the nature of God's omnipotence, emphasizing the doctrine of creation from nothing and the philosophical distinction between divine power and human or creaturely ability. It concludes by defining the logical boundaries of omnipotence, noting that God can do all things except that which is inherently self-contradictory.</summary>
<keywords>omnipotence, creation from nothing, ex nihilo, Greek philosophy, Athens, divine essence, logic, non-contradiction, theology</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological discourse on Divine Omnipotence. Following the previous page's argument that God’s power is an inherent characteristic of His infinite essence and allows Him to create from nothing, the poet now explores the scope of this power—suggesting the possibility of multiple worlds—and distinguishes between God’s "internal" (necessary) and "external" (voluntary) actions.</meta>

->### MEDITATIONS.<-

That God can create more worlds infinitely,
Through His omnipotence, and more glorious than
This wondrous globe, in whose immeasurable rounds
Untold thousands of miles are found;
The wisest does not deny, who considers this completed universe
A point and a ball compared to God’s immeasurability.
You painters, here is spirit and light to learn from further.
The richest in invention, and the wisest in ordering,
**The most powerful to lead His work to the very furthest reach**
**So that nothing of perfection is lacking,**
**Is God alone, in whom [oh, that one might fully know Him!]**
**Wisdom and power race as if on a racetrack,**
**And claim a prize upon such a long course,**
**Where neither of the two in art outruns its match.**

<margin>The distinction between God’s internal and external workings.</margin>
To conclude nothing absurd from omnipotence,
One contemplates His work, from within and from without.
**God works internally by necessity and quietly,**
**By virtue of His nature; externally, through His will,**
**Which is free, and in no way bound by compulsion.**
**God’s necessary work is found to be always the same and of one kind:**
**The free work diversifies itself through variety.**
**God’s omnipotence works generally by nature steadily on,**
**Voluntarily, not always, but according to His pleasure.**
**One should also maintain this distinction in omnipotence:**
**That God does not always work to the full extent of His power,**
**Nor has He ever brought forth work against reason,**
**But above reason, as was shown in the creation,**
**When, for lack of material, God’s masterpiece did not falter.** <note>A reference to "creatio ex nihilo" (creation out of nothing). While reason suggests "nothing comes from nothing," the poet argues God's power acts "above" such logic without being "against" it.</note>
**Working from nature or heaven’s free will,**
**Does not destroy omnipotence through sharp school-disputes;** <note>original: "ſpitſe ſchoolgeſchil," referring to the complex, often pedantic debates of Scholastic theologians regarding the mechanics of divine will.</note>
**For each retains its right in balance together:**
**This [one] God’s free will desires, the other God’s nature requires.**

<margin>God is all-wise.</margin>
Wisdom in God is a knowledge that encompasses all
That was from eternity, and is, and shall be,

<meta>catchword: Through</meta>

<summary>This page explores God's power to create infinite worlds and distinguishes between His internal actions (governed by His nature) and His external actions (governed by His free will). It argues that God's power and wisdom work in perfect harmony, often operating above human reason but never against it.</summary>

<keywords>omnipotence, creation, free will, divine nature, scholasticism, wisdom, infinity, internal vs external works</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of God’s attributes. While the previous page distinguished between God’s internal necessity and His external free will in the act of creation, this page shifts focus to Divine Omniscience—how God's perfect knowledge encompasses all possibilities, past, present, and future, without being the deterministic cause of a thing's nature.</meta>

## THE SECOND BOOK.

Through the highest and primary causes, in their clarity
Mirrored in God Himself, the mirror-sphere of truth:
For He who knows His own essence, understands and knows at once,
From their very origin, so many created beings,
And all that is possible to be, and to become,
To grow, to perish, each in its own order,
Even down to the smallest detail; withal what each thing
Can or cannot do, according to every circumstance.
It <note>Wisdom or Divine Knowledge</note> knows the conditions, situations, and the reasons
Of outcome and result, manner, and progress—tomorrow, today,
And yesterday—and what path the racecourse of time,
Which flies forward, carves and cuts from the ring of eternity.
Let one then call this wisdom "God’s light," which, risen
From eternity, out of the power and knowledge of God’s essence,
Discovers all in a single glance; provided that
A purpose is sought, perceived so far in advance
By the all-seeing eye; since one can find nothing in nothingness,
And without an object to see, the eye is no better than the blind.

<margin>God is all-knowing.</margin>
This knowledge receives at once, if one expands the scope,
The title and the name of God’s omniscience <note>original: "alweetenheit"</note>,
Which, being purely ear and eye and light, is by neither darkness
Nor distance prevented from fathoming, from fishing out,
From beholding, and understanding all that may be understood,
And all that is knowable to the intellect, upon the endless row
Of things; they stand naked before her, the sun of suns,
Which shines through and sees all, so they cannot hide themselves;
A knowledge, which is by no means the fundamental cause <note>original: "grontoirzaeck"</note> of all
That is and was, and shall yet come into being;
Since no thing exists in its essence and power
Through the mere knowledge of another, or the sight of another's eyes,
But through its own form: for that which is known to be something,
Exists by its own nature, and not because it is watched.
Thus omniscience, which knows all accurately, brews
No wild medley <note>original: "mengelklomp"; a confused mass</note> of the necessary and the accidental:

<summary>This page describes God's omniscience as a "mirror-sphere" that reflects all of time and possibility. The poet argues that while God sees and understands everything perfectly, His knowledge of a thing is not what gives that thing its specific nature or form.</summary>
<keywords>omniscience, divine wisdom, eternity, causality, nature of being, God's essence, time, mirror of truth</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological discourse on Divine Knowledge, specifically addressing theodicy—the problem of evil—and the relationship between Wisdom and Providence. It argues that God's foreknowledge does not negate human free will.</meta>

### REFLECTIONS.

And she <note>Wisdom/Divine Knowledge</note> distinguishes between the good and evil intended here,
Between what God necessarily wills, and what He merely tolerates,
And permits, so as in no way to serve as a cause
Of wicked abominations, or the slanderous stains of fatalism:
For He who sees what is to come, knows clearly and nakedly beforehand
The root and the ground from which the blasphemous act springs.
He who foresaw good and evil, foresaw the characteristics of each,
And their nature, and upon what footing each one came walking.
Thus the free will is not distressed by necessity,
Nor is it wrenched from its carpenter's square <note>meaning its natural alignment or proper measure</note> by blunt force.
Thus one easily places a barrier against unrestrained desecration,
Which dares to alienate God from His nature through ignorance,
And calls Him the supreme cause of both good and evil at once—
Far more wicked than the impulse of the mad Manichaeans,
And blind Heathendom, who with their convoluted schemes
Breed good and evil out of two different principles <note>The Manichaeans were an ancient religious sect that believed the universe was a struggle between two equal powers: one Good and one Evil.</note>.

Since God’s Wisdom is regarded by the wisest
As a knowledge that contemplates all things in its power,
Beforehand, creating, governing, and maintaining them in being,
In God’s wisdom, God’s providence is praised,
And the one is blended into the nature of the other by some.
Although the Divinity Himself easily allows this blending
Within our understanding [which cannot divide God’s wholeness,
Whose attributes do not differ from His essence,
Nor from each other, but are one by their nature]
Nevertheless, so that this one does not disappear into the other,
Just as one see one color dissolved into another;
So shall we at last give the radiance of wisdom
To God’s providence—two sisters, once and one,
And as identical as if carved from God’s own face.

<margin>God is all-good.</margin>
Now let God’s goodness appear here upon the page,
An attribute from which God has taken His name:
For Divinity is called goodness, and "good" as the Germanic tongue <note>original: "Duitſchlant"; refers here to the Germanic linguistic roots where "God" and "Good" (Goet) are etymologically linked</note> speaks;
An expression of every thing, in which no thing is lacking,

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>This page explores how God's foreknowledge coexists with human free will without God being the cause of evil. It further defines the relationship between Divine Wisdom and Providence, concluding with the etymological link between "God" and "Goodness."</summary>
<keywords>free will, theodicy, Manichaeans, Providence, Divine Wisdom, goodness, etymology of God</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of God’s attributes, transitioning from Divine Wisdom and Providence to the nature of Divine Goodness and its relationship to Perfection. It addresses how God's attributes are unified in His essence while appearing distinct in their manifestation to creation.</meta>

# THE SECOND BOOK.

And that is perfect, wherein various tongues
Agree in meaning, as if compelled by God.
This goodness, however, is defined too broadly
Without distinction, if it encloses God's perfection
Within its own ring, and gives cause for confusion:
For just as the sun can reflect itself
In fixed and wandering stars <note>In 17th-century Dutch, 'losse starren' (loose stars) referred to planets, which appeared to move against the backdrop of the 'fixed' stars.</note>, so perfection shares its luster
With all attributes, from its higher sphere,
Wherefore they are called "perfections" by this reasoning.
To rightly measure God's goodness here,
Though it is immeasurable and wide, in length and breadth,
Like an endless and bottomless sea;
So we best call her the nurturer of all things,
In God and outside of God: for all good things spring
Only from this untiring fountain of goodness.
Whatever shines in the Godhead, and lives, and thrives, and blooms,
Infinitely in honor, accomplished in majesty,
Infinitely in beauty, in wisdom, and power,
Infinite in the possession of joy, wealth, and treasure,
In this goodness it is all concisely contained,
And what is poured out beyond that, as if by sprinkling,
Into the creature, to a greater or lesser degree, flows from a single
All-good Blesser, who never closed His rich lap
And full bosom to any creature.

Although the creatures now vary and differ
In goodness, according to how much or how richly they share in it,
As it pleases God; this unequal lot
In no way denies the goodness in God,
But speaks of His free will in the pouring out of His gifts;
His wisdom at the highest, and His equity <note>original: "billijckheit," meaning fairness or distributive justice</note> in the sorting
And distinguishing of so many kinds of good,
For which even the very least of creatures must rightly thank Him:
And the inequality gives a luster to the created things,
Which stand out through their varied dress and unequal armor <note>The author uses "wapen" (arms/heraldry) to suggest that the diversity of species is like the different coats of arms or distinct characteristics of various noble houses.</note>.

<summary>This page defines Divine Goodness as the "nurturer" of all existence, arguing that while God is essentially Perfect, His Goodness is the source of all specific attributes and the variety found in creation.</summary>
<keywords>Goodness, Perfection, Godhead, creation, inequality of gifts, Divine Will, Divine Equity</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the diversity of creation as evidence of divine wisdom to the problem of evil. It argues that if all things were equal, creation would be a product of blind necessity rather than God's choice. It then begins to address the source of human wickedness and whether God can be the cause of evil.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

<margin>Objection: what the cause of the evil of morals is:</margin>Of the creator, just as a commander and soldier
Differ in their attire and unequal ornament,
In service of one prince: for if in their raising up there did not shine
An unequal worth, and every creature shared equally
In the good, one would rightly conclude that the supreme majesty,
By the power of her nature, and a necessity,
Had brought forth the world; this masterpiece would have become
The result of reasonless power, where wisdom, direction, nor order,
Nor choice, nor equity was generally noticed,
But everything stood painted equally, and limited;
Just like the painter's spirit, who, clumsy in ordering,
Places the images all the same, and can shade no colors:
If God is the source of the good, and all that is created is good,
What is the origin of the evil that, like a world-flood,
Overflows the globe, and, against right and reason,
Overwhelms and violates discipline, and those golden morals
Introduced by the wise? To now pass by
Natural hereditary defect <note>original: "erfgebreck", referring to the theological concept of original sin inherited from Adam</note>; can any evil arise
That does not have its cause, from which it comes to sprout?
And if one searches for the first cause, against whom, and where shall it stop?
The Godhead is implicated in guilt. One posits through this evidence
That wicked morals themselves are like God's will,
And denies that the good, enclosed in heaven’s breast,
Gladly shares itself, and looks out for companions.
<margin>sees God,</margin>This rule maintains its position, it is true, even in God,
Where supreme goodness works, and every thing its fate
Grants from her bosom; therefore all things resemble
The highest good, from which they take their origin:
For in an essential way God's goodness is in no way
Actually infused into the creature from above;
Since by nature no proper qualities depart
To the fixed resting place, from where they were rooted.
He who shares does not immediately leave his essence,
Nor any limb, but awakens something in another,
->That<-

<summary>This page argues that the diversity and inequality of the world prove God acts by free choice and wisdom rather than blind necessity. It then introduces the "problem of evil," asking how a perfectly good God can be the source of a world filled with moral corruption.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry into the origin of evil. While the previous page established that God is the source of all good, this section argues that evil does not originate in God, but in the free will of rational creatures who turn away from their creator.</meta>

### THE SECOND BOOK.

That resembles the giver’s nature. The pupil learns attentively,
And thus becomes a partaker in his master’s art and wisdom.
In this way the sun, from the heavenly blue vault,
Variegates the flower garden, yet keeps its own essence for itself.

<margin>but the rational creature, being changeable and temptable, voluntarily neglects the good and turns toward evil.</margin>
But to find the pool of moral evil now,
Or the evil of transgression; let the blind on the track of the blind
Bewilder themselves, and accuse man’s refuge,
The source of all good, as the origin of evil,
Against all right; reason shall lead us
Toward the creature, degenerate and separated from virtue,
Because it is changeable and fickle by nature,
It does not preserve the innate good of the Divinity,
And fails in its duty voluntarily, through the enticement
Of apparent good <note>original: "schijngoet," meaning something that looks good but is morally hollow or deceptive</note>, in which God's image and favor are lost;
By no means compelled by a fate too coarse:
For where compulsion reigns, virtue loses its substance.
Were the human race unchangeable, how then
Would the virtues keep their luster, crown, and reward!
Free will, aiming with equal beauty
At one goal or another set before us,
And the fickle mind of man are then the vein
Of evil, in the turning toward wickedness: but the Father
Of goodness bears no guilt, even though this Turkish doctrine <note>a contemporary polemic term for absolute fatalism or strict predestination, which the author argues makes God the author of sin</note>
Drives free will, like a slave, to the violation of its master.

<margin>God is love.</margin>
From heaven's goodness flows divine love,
Which loves itself and the creature, far from grieving its creation,
But leads it up toward God, the ultimate end of things
And the source, so that this—in which God has imprinted His nature—
Might be united in harmony with Him and His nature.
Love always seeks the best for the beloved,
And desires to unite with that beloved pledge:
For this virtue takes delight in laying a mutual bond
Between two or more diversities.
The more this bond pinches, the stronger reason fastens it

<summary>This page argues that moral evil originates from human free will and mutability rather than divine decree. It rejects fatalism (the "Turkish doctrine") and emphasizes that God is love, seeking only the restoration of the creature to its creator.</summary>

<header>82 REFLECTIONS.</header>

->On the joining of the beloved and the lover as one.<-
How this bond appeared to touch the lover more closely,
How love in the act of loving glowed more hotly.
God’s goodness, from whose nature this love flowed into the creature,
Touches God most closely in His fatherly heart:
Thus love burns in God with a perfect glow;
Since love, inseparable from God’s essence,
Could not refrain from extending itself into the creature,
Any more than an eternal being, in the slightest degree,
Could endure or exist if separated from its form.

<margin>The effects of God’s love.</margin>
No passion is more natural to behold in God
Than both love and joy, though they unfold
By no means as passions <note>original: "lybaerheên," referring to the theological concept of "passibility"—the capacity to suffer or be changed by external forces. The author clarifies that God feels love and joy without the "suffering" usually inherent in human emotion.</note> in God, who never suffers.
Even ancient sages exalt God’s love—which never fades,
But can penetrate through all things with endless power—
As the beginning and cause of all things,
And one that does not allow the stem of God to remain forever
Without fruit, and harvest, and branchless and without shoots.
When the sun begins to glow in the heart of the tree,
The fruitful tree begins to bud and to bloom:
Then the root imparts sap and strength to its branches,
Which shade the ground all around, far and wide:
For sincere love is recognizable by its signs.
It seeks fellowship and nurtures its own kind.
The reciprocal love of the beloved, being of the same mind,
Then justly embraces in return that which first loved her.

The Divinity felt a glow of love kindle in the heart,
When goodness and the radiance of beauty shone within her,
And during this reflection <note>the author uses the metaphor of God "mirroring" or contemplating His own perfections</note>, they awakened the will
With an impulse toward this good, this beauty, without blemish.
God’s love, inclined through the will to pair itself,
Becomes pregnant through that glow, and wishes at last to give birth to
This wondrous universe—whatever lives and does not live—
In which God’s likeness floats, to a greater or lesser degree.

<meta>This page continues the poetic exploration of the relationship between the Creator and the created.
