<page-num>83</page-num>
# THE SECOND BOOK.

After He shines upon those creatures, overshadowing them from on high,
He intends to see His own glory reflected in each of them,
And to manifest—as if in an unlocked throne—
All the infinite goodness and beauty that the Most High possesses.
The Creator, out of love, comes down to meet His creation.
What a vast disproportion is found here when considering
The dignity of the one compared to the other! The heavenly court
Here glorifies nothingness, or that which is made of ashes and dust.
When the soul turns back toward that from which it sprang,
Then love triumphs in the salvation of those it has joined.

Just as a torch sees its flame reflected in a mirror,
And from the mirrored sphere the flame shoots back
Toward the light from which it shines; so do all things finally end
In God, from whom they received their very being,
And they find rest in that single point that draws everything toward itself.
Reciprocal love <note>original: "wederliefde," referring to the responsive love of the creature back to the Creator.</note> rests in the Good that first awakened it.

<margin>God is the ruler and owner of all things.</margin>
The dominion upon which God sits most highly enthroned
Consists of the right to command, and also in the ownership
Of all things beneath Him. To command and to forbid,
To permit, to reward, and to punish: these belong to this Lord,
To whom the greatest scepter of heaven and earth is due
By the power of His nature, from which everything sitting at His feet
Derives its worth and its might;
The creature rightly recognizes Him as the Head, the most glorious member.
By the power of ownership, He is permitted to increase,
To diminish, to protect, to strip of luster, or to honor,
Just as it pleases Him, for the glory of His kingdom,
And without God’s power ever wronging God’s justice.
This right He reserves for Himself, beyond the meddling <note>original: "bedillen," meaning to find fault with, criticize, or interfere with another's management or will.</note> of anyone:
For the first law of all is the very first Will.
A shepherd thus handles his flock, whether gently or strictly;
It lives and dies in the service of the master and his staff.
This dominion was not inherited by the Divinity,
Through birthright, nor acquired through purchase, gift, or favor,

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and philosophical argument regarding God's sovereignty. Following the previous page’s discussion on the "return" of the creature to God through love, this text establishes that God’s right to rule is not based on force or long-standing possession, but on the fundamental act of creation and ongoing maintenance of the universe.</meta>

->### CONTEMPLATIONS.<-

Neither antiquity of possession, nor intrusion, nor force:
The right of creation has placed Him in possession,
As well as the sustenance of His creatures.
Who then can erase that title from heaven's brow!
If the creature depends on God's being alone,
Then it stands under God's command, and none other.

<margin>Concluding argument, confirming God's providence, to be unfolded more broadly in His external works.</margin>
Since no <term>creative genius</term> <note>original: "werckvernuft"; refers to the inventive or artistic intellect of a craftsman or architect.</note> employs his wisdom and power,
Without first envisioning in the piece of work
A certain end, and goal, for which he shall labor;
So the world cannot have <term>erupted</term> <note>original: "opdonderen"; literally to 'thunder up,' implying a chaotic, sudden, or accidental emergence like a storm.</note> by any wild chance,
But demands a Master-builder, who, <term>refined</term> <note>original: "besleepen"; literally 'ground' or 'polished,' used here to mean intellectually skilled or sophisticated.</note> in thought,
Had grasped the sketch of this universe in his mind,
And the quality, magnitude, and weight of each thing,
Its number, measure, and form; and how he would best
Maintain, direct, and govern this edifice,
Before his power began to create, to <term>mold</term> <note>original: "bootseeren"; to model or shape, typically used in reference to a sculptor working with clay or wax.</note>
This glorious masterpiece, crowned with honor and praise.
Thus God's providence is shown to us beforehand.
A general arranges his banners in battle order for this reason,
Before they even come to hand-to-hand combat with the enemy's troops.
The art of navigation thus prescribes laws for the <term>seafarer</term> <note>original: "zeebou"; an archaic term for maritime travel or the 'cultivation' of the sea.</note>.
Thus everything that exists trots along the pre-ordained path:
For God's providence is the sketch of all things,
And the order of creatures, inclined to reach their goal,
For which they are suited, ordained by God,
Each one step by step, and with a certain conclusion;
Just as the Divinity imagined these in Her mind
From eternity, before She came to begin this masterpiece.
This truth, though it be denied stiffly and strongly
By the <term>godless rabble</term> <note>original: "ongodist gespoock"; 'ongodist' refers to atheists or those who deny God's involvement; 'gespoock' implies ghosts or contemptible spirits.</note>, shall be evident from God's work,
As clear in the creation as in the governing of the created,
And God's providence, like <term>arrow-proof armor</term> <note>original: "scheutvry wapen"; literally 'shot-free weapon,' meaning a defensive shield or armor that cannot be pierced.</note>,
Shall defend the heart that builds upon the Divinity,
And contemplates and beholds Her in the created things.

# CONTEMPLATIONS
### of
# GOD'S WORKS.
## The third book.

<margin>Progression from God's attributes to the external works of His providence in particular.</margin>
Just as in the evening, while the shadows grow,
We see the glow of the evening sun, descended below the <term>horizon</term> <note>original: "kimmen"; refers to the edge of the sky where it meets the sea or land.</note>,
Shining through the sea and the foaming brine,
With variegated rays, and purple glow, and gold,
Witnesses of the light that, having barely vanished,
Has refreshed, nurtured, and shone upon the world;
So shall we now contemplate God, and the supreme majesty,
In the work of His providence;
Since our eyes cannot endure that sun of all suns
In its full radiance and day.
A boundary and measure is set for mortals,
At which their knowledge falters, though they melt with longing
To once see God in His perfect being,
Known by no understanding, praised by no tongue.
We then only watch God's footsteps from afar,
And follow Him fearfully, as if in His shadow.

<margin>The atheists denied God, or rather divine providence, like Lucretius, Epicurus’s squire.</margin>
One heard and saw of old how raging <term>atheists</term> <note>original: "ongodisten"; literally 'ungodly-ists,' a term for those who deny the existence or role of God.</note>
Denied the Godhead, and varnished lies
With a painted mask of truth, which nonetheless
Did not cease to shine through the deception with God's luster:
Another school, meanwhile embarrassed and abandoned,
Renounced—because this sounded too godless and arrogant—
Not the Godhead so stiffly as God's providence,
And their mouths were stopped with reason and judgment.

<page-num>86</page-num>
# CONTEMPLATIONS.

Their following, however, disarming piety,
Dares still to blaspheme and astound God’s providence,
Just as Lucretius did of old, following Epicurus,
Who would recognize no providence other than nature,
Or reckless chance, and the dust of fibers <note>original: "vezelingen"; refers to the "atoms" or material particles that Epicurean philosophy claimed were the fundamental building blocks of the universe.</note>,
As the cause of the universe and the great structure of things;
Though previously restrained and refuted by proof.
It is therefore time that we now defend and maintain
God’s providence, [as it is revealed in creation,
And in the keeping and governing of heavenly creatures,]
So that every <term>atheist</term> <note>original: "ongodist"; literally "un-god-ist."</note>, silenced by broader evidence,
Might strike his godless flag in honor of God’s crown.

<margin>God’s providence is revealed in His creations.</margin>
That creation gallops magnificently ahead in the works
Of God’s providence, has been shown to us in the strengthening
Of reason, which honors Him as the cause of the entire
Universe, and in no way attributes this to nature or wild chance
Outside of God—which would be against all reason;
As the Epicureans do, and those who still honor
Their darknesses above the light of reason and discernment.
No wandering, meddlesome wit can rob us of this certainty,
So long as it appears and remains uncontested
That by a firm decree and counsel, all the members
Of the world’s structure are fitted upon the mold <note>original: "leeſt"; a "last" or wooden model used by cobblers to shape a shoe, here functioning as a metaphor for a divine blueprint.</note>
Of wisdom, as it lay in God’s all-wise spirit,
Understood with all its circumstances, to aim
At the target and end for which the creator intended it.
This also appears since every creature is accustomed
To work for itself, toward a particular end
To which it was destined: while they also all strive
Harmoniously to intend God’s honor and glory;
Thus one clearly notices in every being something
That, through its order and fitness, eyes and looks
Toward the proposed target. From this regulated order,
God’s providence can be very clearly proven.

# THE THIRD BOOK.

<margin>In the regularity and order of the creatures:</margin>
As from a mark that she <note>Providence is personified here as a female figure, following the gender of the Dutch word "voorzienigheid."</note> imprints upon the creature,
So that one may know the mother by the offspring.
The creatures each received, in their creation, their station and places,
Their powers, properties, and their qualities,
Moderation, form, and order, weight, and measure,
And number, as fits each, according to its duty and state,
To better reach the end for which it aims;
As this clearly appears through many a visible sign.
This ordering, this well-arranging of every thing
Toward its rightful end, within the world's round ring,
Declares that providence, before time began to stir,
Resolved in her counsel to create this wondrous work.
The active power, which has set before itself
A certain beneficial end, at which it aims and attends,
Wisely looks ahead, and is praised in the work
That draws its origin and essence from foresight <note>original: "voorzichtightheit"; implies both prudence and the ability to see ahead.</note>.

<margin>and their end, to which they are destined:</margin>
Such is the work of creation, where one sees
That it aims in every way at its end and useful goal:
For creating is a work of God, the first worker,
Who brought everything forth from nothing for the ultimate end—the Deity Himself—who, more strongly
Than anything else, seeks the well-being of each,
And completed and perfected it for His own glory;
Therefore God's providence, seated in her counsel,
[Where everything is weighed, counted, and precisely measured,
And destined for its end,] has built the world
Into such a massive structure <note>original: "gevaert"; implies a large, complex, and impressive construction or vessel.</note>, as one beholds it,
And its innumerabilities, qualities, powers,
Form, and state, and place, and time, vault, and arches,
Diversities, and order, and measure, endowed with attraction
Or opposition, whether it rests or rushes along,
And floats, and revolves in unequal circles,
To the honor of God's eye, the sentry of all things.

<margin>in man, the little world:</margin>
I leave the mirrored sphere of the great world <note>The "Macrocosm," referring to the entire universe.</note> be,
And first look upon man, the little world <note>The "Microcosm"; a common Renaissance and Early Modern concept suggesting that the human being is a miniature reflection of the entire universe.</note>,

# REFLECTIONS.

Honored and exalted by that name in Greece, <note>Referring to the term "microcosm" or "little world," a common philosophical concept that the human being reflects the structure of the entire universe.</note>
Since one can measure here in miniature, according to life,
What is reflected in the great, in this vast universe;
And God has poured Himself, condensed, into man,
As the steward of the One never seen,
Under whose intellect all other creatures serve.

<margin>
in the
anatomy of
the body.
</margin>
Whoever views man externally, from head to toe,
What limb lacks its function, or fails to keep its pace <note>original: "hoefslagh"; literally "hoofbeat," used here to mean the regular, orderly rhythm or duty of a body part.</note>,
Where it is fit? And who would wish to reshape
Even the smallest part of the body, or restore it?
Would they not assault its propriety,
And dishonor the Deity in His image, where no man—
Not even Prometheus <note>In Greek mythology, the Titan who fashioned humanity out of clay. The author suggests even this legendary craftsman could find no flaw in God's design.</note> himself—could find a fault?
The form and posture of the limbs teaches even the simplest to take note;
The feet, ready for the path; the hands, to work;
The head, built like a court for the sovereign intellect;
Which, from that high vault, keeps watch through the eyes as sentries,
And through the ears hears all that reveals itself from without.
The mouth receives the food. The tongue, through sound, can utter
What is hidden in the breast, according to the needs of every matter;
While no other animal is gifted with speech.
How the face shines, full of divine grace,
Lifted up toward heaven, while all other animals
Go bowed toward the earth! What a variety
Of countenances exist in such a narrow and small
Space as the face, to distinguish one person from another!
How luxuriously the nose wanders through all scents!
How playfully the foliage of the blonde hair curls about the head,
Natural as a crown, which outshines the gold of crowns!
And what shape can please the eyes more
Than the head? For roundness is the most excellent of all,
Like a heavenly vault, which, skillfully built in a circle,
Keeps the powers of the soul enclosed within itself,
Under which the other limbs move among themselves,
To carry out their duties in service to the head's authority.

# THE THIRD BOOK

-> If modesty permitted, one could not express richly enough <-
How much care the all-wise Father took
To plant and conceive humans through humans,
To cherish and to feed them; but shame colors the cheeks,
Therefore we now cover the fitness, form, material, and position
Of each specific reproductive organ <note>original: "teellidt"</note>, and instead
See the milk spring from the breast and source-vein of the mother.
Where did nature show itself more cautious and wise,
In the nurturing of the offspring, which, after the pain of childbirth,
Sucks the blood of the mother and draws it from the heart <note>This reflects the early modern medical belief that breast milk was actually "whitened" or refined blood redirected from the uterus to the breasts after birth.</note>:
While love seems to entrance the mother,
And she cannot stop herself from pressing the child to her heart,
To kiss it, time after time, as if she would kill it
Out of friendship, without measure, carried away by favor and loyalty!
What inventions did God’s wisdom not know how to find
To bind the reproduction of man with such bonds!
He has secured and preserved them, knot upon knot,
Even against the edge and steel of Alexander's sword <note>A reference to the Gordian Knot; the author argues that the biological "knots" of human generation are even more unbreakable than the legendary knot Alexander the Great cut with his sword.</note>.
Let poison, let murderous weapons exhaust and weary themselves,
The human race immortalizes itself through its growth,
Until the last human shall close the turnstile <note>original: "draeiboom"; a metaphor for the final barrier or the end of time.</note>
Of such an endlessly increasing number.
   If we behold man and woman, as beautiful as ever glowed
With blameless love, these naked ones
Put to shame the most beautiful of cattle and birds, who upon the wide
Earth and air boast themselves, dressed in skin and plumage.
Here the art of Apelles <note>A renowned painter of ancient Greece, known for his realism.</note> yields, though he has spent his whole life
Drawing, and blending his paints to the very likeness of life,
Seeking light and shadows, and height and depth;
Yet his images stand mute, and deathly, and unfinished.
Praxiteles <note>One of the most famous Greek sculptors, noted for his statues of the human form.</note> falters and loses heart,
Though he lives successfully in the carving of ivories:
And Phidias, who conquers time and marble stone,
Is grieved that no ray of light shines from the eyes of Venus.

# REFLECTIONS.

While the artist strives to satisfy his zeal for art;
It grieves him that his hand can feel no pulse,
No limb moves or stirs. Whatever eye admired his work,
It lived only in appearance, and without spirit or soul:
How then could God’s masterpiece lack providence,
Which outshines all art, no matter how skillfully the masters teach!
If we search deeper with the hand and knife
Of the anatomist <term>ledefnijder; literally 'limb-cutter' or dissector</term>
Through the secrets of the entrails,
And the lawful duty of each, what wisdom is found
In those hollows, what ingenuity and deliberation are hidden there!
How the veins flow here, like brooks, through the body!
What workshops serve here for man’s convenience!
The stomach provides drink and food, and cooks <note>In early modern medicine, digestion was often described as a 'cooking' process driven by internal heat.</note> for all the limbs.
The liver delivers blood. The gallbladder below
Receives the yellow bile, which cleanses the body of slime,
And other impurities, sometimes as thick as glue.
The lungs draw air, and act as bellows during exertion,
To cool the heart, the source of heat, in its glowing fire.
The spleen, a cesspool <term>stanckriool; literally 'stink-sewer'</term>, swallows the black bile,
And purifies it throughout, for the benefit of the body.
But not to wander too deep into these cellars,
Let this investigation be left to the intellect of Vesalius <note>Andreas Vesalius (1514–1564), the author of the influential anatomy book *De humani corporis fabrica*.</note>,
Who fervently spent his spirit and his life’s time,
While he cut corpse after corpse into shreds,
And complained that the brain of even the greatest men
Still hesitated to investigate every limb with the sharpest care;
Whether it be the skeleton, muscle, nerves, or vein,
The brain within the skull <term>beckeneel; an archaic term for the cranium</term>, a mixture, wondrously
Set, like cement, to grasp clearly
Reason, the intellect, the will, the treasury
Of our memory and sleep: I say nothing of sight,
In which ophthalmology <term>ooghkunft; literally 'eye-art' or the study of vision</term> loses its way, seeking this light;
The smelling of the nose; the echo and sound in the ears;
The taste, the speech, reborn on the leaf of the smooth tongue;

The sense that spreads and grazes from head to toe;
And how the soul goes wandering through the limbs,
Wholly present in every part, excelling through its discoveries,
Until life’s thread is cut and it is finally released.

<margin>On the essence of the soul:</margin>
If it were possible with colors and brush
To paint the rational soul, man’s most precious part,
And its form, just as it lives, depicted from life,
Oh, what a divinity would shine from this outline!
But its invisibility, in which it resembles God,
And its origin, do not allow anyone to look deeper
Into the inner secret of such a heavenly being,
Reflected and praised through its works:
For just as our eye, on the earth and toward the vault
Of heaven, sees everything, but never itself,
So it is with the soul; it cannot know itself,
Nor follow in flight the swiftness of its own wings. <note>The original Dutch uses "pennen," which can mean both quill pens and the primary feathers of a wing, suggesting the soul's intellectual and spiritual flight.</note>
The eye sees itself somewhat in crystal <note>A reference to mirrors or glass.</note>:
So the soul is seen only in its work, as if in a reflection:
Just as the nature of the lodestone <term>zeilsteens; a naturally magnetized mineral used in compasses</term> makes itself known
Through its pull, recognized in many different works.

What a tough bond binds the soul and body together,
A pair so unlike in nature, of different minds,
Which, married for years, so reluctantly come to part;
That is a mystery for which the wisest have waited,
As long as the world has hung, and it rests with God alone:
But none of them found the key to this lock,
Though one might write whole stacks of pages about this bond,
The riddle remains, as does the freedom to guess,
Until the essence of the soul is known, clear and pure,
Known beyond the work and marks of its nature,
Just as the Divinity brought it forth from nothing,
Completely pure, without blemish, and first placed it in the body.

The soul <insert>gifted by God with reason, which distinguishes man from animals</insert> rising to the border

<meta>catchword: Now</meta>

Of heaven, above the light of the stars and their spheres,
Forgive me if I forget her <note>The soul, discussed on the previous page.</note> for a moment in my singing,
Until my lyrical urge soon draws the strings higher,
Where she, released from the body, takes no human form.
<margin>In the great world:</margin> The small world <note>The "microcosm," a common Renaissance term for man as a miniature version of the universe.</note> yields to the arrival of the worth
Of the great world <note>The "macrocosm," or the universe at large.</note>, the compass of heaven, air, and earth,
Which encloses pure wonders within its sphere:
High grandeur, innumerability, diversity, and powers,
Created out of Omnipotence; also beauty, and such elements
That pull a raptured spirit upward from the earth
Toward God, with whom the mind unites, seeing from above
How everything, outside of God, vanishes into mere nothingness.
<margin>In the greatness of the earth:</margin> The earth is so large that no one, however skilled at sea,
In fifty centuries and another three pairs of hundred years <note>A poetic way of saying 5,600 years, reflecting the estimated age of the world at the time.</note>
Has wandered through and visited the entire globe,
Although the art of sailing seems now brought to its peak,
And Tycho’s <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the famous Danish astronomer.</note> disciple, Blaeu <note>Willem Blaeu (1571–1638), a renowned Dutch cartographer and maker of globes and instruments.</note>, to know this compass,
Has measured that globe <term>aerdkloot; literally "earth-clod," a common term for the planet</term> so precisely on the Dutch shore,
That he surpasses many ancients in celestial measurement,
To the honor of the city that raises itself on the IJ <term>the IJ; the waterfront and historic harbor of Amsterdam</term>:
And yet the earthly structure, compared to the heavenly,
Is a mere point in the eye of one enamored with stargazing:
For when the globe stands directly in the light of the stars,
That globe cuts no point out of heaven’s face <note>Meaning the earth is so small compared to the heavens that it does not even cast a significant shadow or block a "point" of the celestial expanse.</note>.
If every star is as large as the globe in its rising,
Or larger, according to the conclusion of all astronomers,
And yet they seem, however far they stand apart,
So small to our sight; who can from here below
With his memory determine the heavenly circle,
Where so many thousands of diamonds shine
Upon that illustrious ring, fitted as if to the hand
Of God’s immensity, which spans east and west?
If the globe is now so large, as the surveyors conclude,
How large then is the vault of the heavens from the outside!

### THE THIRD BOOK.

Now gauge the height of heaven, if you can,
Down to the navel of the earth, or the center of the abyss:
If this greatness yet has its end and certain borders,
Who then can overtake the infinity of God?
And what is this universe, if one contemplates God,
In greatness any more than a drop of morning dew!
<margin>In the innumerability of things.</margin>
Let Euclid be summoned here, the prince of accounts <note>Euclid (fl. 300 BCE), the Greek mathematician often called the "Father of Geometry."</note>,
To count for us the innumerability of all creatures,
Drop by drop, poured from heaven’s crown,
And all the drifting sand, blown along the sea in the dunes.
But rushing past this, the precious value
Of all kinds of metal, gems, and treasure, buried
In the earth, and discovered, while the greedy man
Never spares his mother’s <note>A common poetic personification of "Mother Earth."</note> entrails and bosom,
Is never fully valued. What do God’s fields produce
In herbs, plants, orchard-fruit and crops, under the cultivation
Of the slave-like <note>The poet describes the labor of the "ackerman" (farmer) as "slavish" to emphasize the grueling, repetitive toil of tilling the soil.</note> husbandman, who breaks the clay and clods,
Where it is colder, or where the sun stings with heat!
What do sea and land produce in fish, birds, animals,
Who thrive by their element, either earth or water,
Or soar in the air; while each builds nests,
In rock, hollow, and forest, or dwells in meadows,
Gives birth to its young, and broods, and feeds them, without end!
And though man, by fire, and water, and the misery
Of plague, and famine, and war, is destroyed;
One sees how the circuit of the wide world swarms
With people, without number, so that we do not now press
Into the kingdom of the spirit-world, named "intellectuals" <term>verstandelingen; literally "beings of understanding," a term often applied to angels or celestial spirits in Scholastic and Neoplatonic philosophy</term>
By the old Athenian school <note>A reference to the philosophers of ancient Greece, such as Plato and Aristotle, who categorized the hierarchy of beings based on their capacity for reason.</note>; a terrifying number,
That no one shall ever reckon, nor fully count.
How can the innumerability of things strike us dumb,
<margin>In diversity:</margin>
When God’s perfections resound in all beings,
And the endless number, diversely spread out,
Leads us into the knowledge of the single simplicity <note>This refers to the theological doctrine of "Divine Simplicity," which posits that God, despite creating a complex and diverse universe, is Himself a singular, indivisible essence.</note>!

Still more wondrous appears the Godhead in the variegation
Of so many creatures who honor their creator,
As if from the horn <note>A reference to the *cornucopia* or horn of plenty, a symbol of abundance and nourishment.</note>, stuffed full of abundance,
With every kind of fruit, and crammed up to the throat,
Filled and heaped up, whereby the wild wilderness
Can know the Godhead in its treasure and generosity,
So rich and poured out: for if a signet ring
Only presses a single image and coat of arms into a thing,
And a printing mold can only print one and the same letter;
Who then doubts that God, in endless pieces,
Has shown his power and generosity through creation,
And crowned his majesty in this wonder-work?

I pass over the diversities in so many things.
How richly he variegates plant, and herb, and flower, and foliage,
And scent, and color, and taste, and juices, and form!
Though the flower-brush of <term>Seghers</term> <note>Daniel Seghers (1590–1661), a Flemish Jesuit priest and a master of flower still lifes whose work was highly sought after by European royalty.</note> pleases the eye of princes,
His paint is without soul, or scent, or power, or life:
And <term>China’s</term> flower-brush must give praise to the Painter <note>The author uses a capital "P" to refer to God as the ultimate Artist.</note>,
Who paints such a rainbow and wreath of flowers,
Boasts in the peacock, parrot, and phoenix-feathers,
And living festoons, growing upon the trees,
Where the water flows toward <term>Beijing</term> <note>Original: "Peking".</note>, through the veins of the streams,
To water the Eastern court, in which the <term>Tartar</term> <note>A term for the Manchu people who conquered the Ming Dynasty in 1644, an event of great contemporary interest in the 17th century.</note> prides himself,
Crowned with sun-gold, ignited by the morning light,
Which, rising from the dew, blooming like roses,
Sees that <term>imperial wall</term> <note>Original: "rijcksmuur". A reference to the Great Wall of China, which was breached during the Manchu conquest.</note> demolished, its north star broken in.

If we observe the wild and tame beasts,
That swim in the waves, or soar in the clouds,
Or graze in the meadows, deserts, and preserves,
Must <term>Snyders</term> <note>Frans Snyders (1579–1657), a Flemish painter specializing in animals, hunting scenes, and market still lifes.</note> not throw the brush from his hands,
And cry: all our art is not worth contemplating.
Here is diversity of an inimitable nature.
Has <term>Rubens</term> <note>Peter Paul Rubens (1577–1640), the preeminent Flemish Baroque artist, famous for his dynamic compositions and mastery of the human and animal form.</note> not complained, in painting and stroking
Upon his panels, that he could scarcely find two alike

### THE THIRD BOOK. 95

In every face found in the King’s Netherlands?
I say nothing of each person's nature. Also, the evening hour tells us
How all the hosts, who play in golden armor, <note>A poetic reference to the stars appearing in the night sky as a celestial army.</note>
And show themselves in the sky, differ in their brilliance:
From which one surely concludes and confirms, in every appearance,
That these diversities in God are infinite;
Since they flow only from him, who never fades while blooming,
And from his rich lap and full bosom.
<margin>in the power and the ability of creatures:</margin>
The power of the creatures raises us into the light
Of the highest power and might, which can do all.
Where nature and art stand still, to bring forth
The wonders in which God’s power makes itself felt in living form,
The source and origin of nature and science?
One takes no step on earth, in sea, air, or fire,
Where God’s omnipotence and power do not meet us,
When the elements stir and move,
And tear themselves from their shroud <note>Original: "lijck." While it usually means "corpse," in this context it refers to the physical shell or the calm state that "buries" the raw power of the elements before they break out in a storm.</note>. Though sea and earth may sleep,
And the air and heat caress you; as soon as the earth gapes open,
And the abyss, and the sea; the wind begins to blow,
The sky to thunder, to flash with lightning, to rage,
To rain; what remains of humans or of cattle,
Or forest, or land, or city, resting in its place!
What is not struck by hailstones and thunder!
What rocky cliff does not collapse! Where do heaven’s plagues
Not strike cities and kingdoms! What stone <term>jetty</term> <note>Original: "beer." In Dutch hydraulic engineering, a *beer* is a masonry dam or wall built to protect a moat or divert water.</note>, what shore
Protects the mountains, dunes, and the flat land from the breach!
One sees the fleet at sea then rise, then fall,
Like balls struck back upon a racket,
Bouncing back in their path, following their fall.
Gather together the race of giants from everywhere,
Also the gruesome beasts: lions, tigers, bears,
The bull and elephant, the whale in the lakes
And Northern Ocean, the dragon, the crocodile,
The snakes, ducked under the grass, cunning and still;

## REFLECTIONS.

What a mountain of powers they pile up! A single plant,
A worm kills a hero. A stinging tarantula
Robs man of his rest and sleep; he weeps and cries,
Or dances and laughs himself to death, unless the evil departs
Through the power of an antidote; <note>This refers to "tarantism," a historical phenomenon where people bitten by spiders were said to dance uncontrollably until they collapsed from exhaustion.</note> and to speak of art, <note>In this context, "art" refers to "techne"—human skill, engineering, or science.</note>
Place Archimedes once outside the world’s sphere and regions;
He lifts the globe from its axis with a lever.
I say nothing of gunpowder—a mere moment, which quickly
Crushed the wall of Mechelen to grit and mortar,
And seemed to rip Delft, the country’s capital, from its roots. <note>This likely refers to the "Delft Thunderclap" of 1654, a massive gunpowder magazine explosion that devastated the city.</note>
I speak not of stars, nor of the sun and moon,
So strong in radiance, though they stand infinitely far from us.
Now calculate God’s powers from each in particular,
And sum them all up: who would not yield before this wonder!

<margin>in the beauty of the creatures:</margin> Now the beauty of the universe reveals itself here,
Which strikes me upon the forehead. At which limbs shall
I best begin to dissect the beautiful body
Of the world? Who ravishes my spirit, understanding, and senses,
In the sliding open of this divine stage,
And God’s performances? O worldly pleasure-bower!
O landscape, in which nature has outdone itself,
And each part most richly wrought, completed, nothing forgotten!
What Orderer has sought out these inventions,
And arranged each so well, and brought it to its place?
The foreground, the background, the fading, and the receding; <note>The author uses Dutch painting terms like *verschieten* (to recede or fade) to describe the landscape as if it were a canvas using atmospheric perspective.</note>
Here forest, there cornfield, yonder meadows, elsewhere streams,
And open sky, and a spring that leaps from the hill?
Yonder the mountains and rocks turn blue: here the nightingale
Warbles and sings in the wild. What many birds fly there
In flocks through the trees, or drift on their wings,
Satisfied in the air, or over the smooth lake!
The cheerful countryside, refreshed by the fair weather,
Is growing so that one hears the herbs and green grass peeping.
Apelles <note>A celebrated painter of ancient Greece, often invoked as the symbol of the perfect artist who can perfectly mimic nature.</note> follows nature with art, in creating depth

### THE THIRD BOOK.

And high in its measure, he stares, and scatters
His lovely colors. Whoever rests or stays behind,
He boldly follows his guide <note>The "guide" likely refers to Nature or the personification of Art.</note> and wishes to walk with her,
And rests his spirit upon the nature and qualities
Of life, just as one finds each thing in life.
He gazes until blinded by the sun and its rising,
In all the play of water, light, and clouds,
And purple radiance and gold. Here the cow is milked;
There the fisherman’s boat <term>pink: a small, flat-bottomed fishing vessel</term> puts out to sea for fresh fish.
Here an entire fleet rides at anchor in the roadstead,
And yonder the hunt speeds on the trail of the greyhounds.
The hounds sniff to find the fleeting hart.
The nest, full of storks atop the castle and manor,
Waits with open beaks for the bait fished from the green fields
By the father’s care. Who can name the beauty
That reveals itself in the lush cattle, and fruit, and seed, and flowers,
And stars, sun, and moon, and clouds!
How beautifully nature variegates the tulips, treasures worth
Their weight in gold, exalted as "Augusten" <note>This refers to the *Semper Augustus*, the most famous and expensive tulip variety during the Dutch "Tulip Mania" of the 1630s.</note>,
The fairest and costliest flower upon which bees ever rested!

<margin>On the beauty of woman.</margin>
The beauty of woman, which surpasses all else
On earth, and never satiates the heart or eye,
Inflames even lions; it reveals itself here in life,
As beautiful as God created her, to give to man
A taste of luxury and pleasure which, poured out eternally,
Is enjoyed in the contemplation of God alone.
The sight and enjoyment of woman's beauty,
Esteemed as a blessedness, has plunged thousands over their heads
Into sorrow and the shipwreck of their status, health, body, and soul.
It held and fettered the wisest and strongest
Like slaves to the chain of blonde and hanging hair.
No animal, possessed and bewitched by a ghost, rages so
As the overly-errant man, who blindly reaches for this bait
Of beauty, everyone’s desire,

### REFLECTIONS.

And lust, at the first taste, sets God and name and fame and lineages
Aside, to melt itself in the flatterer’s <note>The Dutch "vleiſter" refers to a female flatterer or seductress.</note> flames of love,
Like ice, or a waxen image in the fire.
Such can a wanton misuse of beauty do; and so dearly,
So dearly a fool buys this sham-treasure, which is no sooner
Enjoyed than repentance comes darting in,
And treads on the heels of the misuse of God’s creature.
Wretched then was he who fell into her snares,
Captured and smothered for a single glance of the eyes—
A joy that, in a dream, vanished like a vapor.
What then does this beauty demand? What was God’s aim and end
In creating this image <note>The "image" refers to the female form.</note> that violates the power of souls?
He wishes to bind the heart of man to her faithfulness,
And teach him to find in this beauty a greater beauty:
A pleasure which the soul shall enjoy in God,
Who is the supreme beauty, the true abundance, and the only All.
You would hear the woman described as even fairer in this song,
But I fear to charm even a saint with this siren, <note>original: "meermin" (mermaid/siren)</note>
Should grace and virtue, and favor and spirit,
Fall from above and dwell within a body of beautiful form.
Youth, which prunes the heart and eye and weans them from loving,
Numbs even the brave who conquer an entire world—
And who meanwhile stand in service of the female form,
That has often played with their crown and scepter,
And performed many a tragedy upon bloody scaffolds, <note>The Dutch "stellaedjen" refers to a theater stage or a raised platform for executions.</note>
Still smoking from the murder of the greatest personages.
Whoever doubts this, seek no further for clear proof and evidence:
The theater speaks of this, as does the most certain chronicle.

<margin>On the primary elements:</margin>
The visible world generally strengthened the reason
That seeks God in His creatures; now reason reflects upon
Its principal members <note>Refers to the fundamental components of the physical world.</note> in this earth, in water, air, and fire:
Four brothers, born of one birth, to serve us here
For our convenience, to mix among themselves,
And through their exchange to bring forth many mixtures,

# THE THIRD BOOK.

N 2
<meta>catchword: According</meta>

According to each's varying nature, which last or first,
Rules more or less, and dominates in the mixture.
Thus our blood consists of yellow and black bile,
And blood and phlegm. <note>This refers to the four humors of ancient and medieval medicine: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile. Their balance was thought to determine a person's health and temperament.</note> The black is the lowest of all,
Being earthly by nature, surrounded by wet phlegm,
Like water, cold and moist. The yellow bile-flame glows
At the peak, like fire, and gives all others life.
In the middle floats the blood, driven like the air.
As long as each mixture maintains a proportion,
The body is at its strength: but if immoderation rules,
By leaping out of bounds and overturning the rule,
Then war arises, as in a battle of lords.
This transition of moist and dry and hot and cold
Brews either a temperance or a wild chaos, <note>original: "baiert" (a term for primordial chaos or a confused mass).</note>
And is Proteus himself, who, bubbling from the streams,
Can take on so many faces and forms. <note>Proteus was a Greek sea-god capable of changing his shape at will; here he represents the shifting states of matter and health.</note>
This is the only cause of the growth and decay
Of beings, who consist only of elements,
And change their state, whether died or reborn.
The matter keeps its duration; the forms are lost.
So the soft wax suffers, and remains the same thing,
Changed according to the mark of the king’s signet ring.
So one hears a sound with unequal tones
Crowning the father of the universe in the elements,
While he moves and drives everything here,
And, eternally unmoved, <note>A reference to the "Unmoved Mover," a philosophical concept of a primary cause that moves all things without being moved itself.</note> remains in state and being.

<margin>In the earth:</margin>
Now the earth reveals herself, who though sunken low,
Seems the least <term>primary substance</term> <note>original: "hoofftstof," referring to one of the four fundamental elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note> and drowned to the neck;
She is nonetheless no less than water, and surpasses
Every element in value, and looks over their heads.
If God holds court above with his intelligences, <note>original: "verstandelingen," referring to rational celestial beings or angels who govern the spheres.</note>
And pure spirits; the father of all things
Granted mankind the palace of the earth as a fief,
Where the beautiful vault of heaven shone over him;

<summary>This page explains the theory of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) as the basis of human health and the four elements as the foundation of the physical world. It describes how balance brings harmony while imbalance causes "war" within the body, ultimately praising God as the stable force behind all material change.</summary>

Sown with the sun, the moon, and lesser twinkling fires; <note>The "twinkling fires" refer to the stars.</note>
He <note>God</note> sees the earth alive with herbs, trees, and animals,
And the glittering of metal and precious stones;
While the water is merely granted to the fish,
The elements of air and fire remain almost empty,
Being poorer by their nature, for the service of human life.

The earth rests upon God and upon His counterweight,
Encircled by the air, while so many lights
Revolve around the globe, completing their cycles,
And the sentinel, the Ocean, bound in peace with the earth,
Takes her in its arms and kisses the lips of the shore.
Through this stability of the earth in its position,

Man treads the ground and works during his hours,
Or sleeps and rests, just like all other living beings, <note>original: "natuuren," referring to creatures or natural entities.</note>
Who, like humans, are gifted with five senses,
And the life that trots upon the earth in life's racecourse;
She <note>The earth</note> is their nourisher, growing herbs, and ears of grain,
And fruits, to preserve everything that breathes.
She yields materials for every kind of use,
So that man may shelter and hide in huts and houses,
Making use of metals and tools to build upon
The field of the sea, <note>A poetic reference to maritime trade and navigation.</note> and cities, and countrysides.

Now run through the globe, and hear and see until you are weary,
And touch, and smell, and taste: the Divinity shines toward you,
Meeting you everywhere in His creations;
All is God that you perceive, from top to bottom.
Where does one find a man now, who outwits every intellect,
And yet cannot find God if he seeks Him?
That monster may tread the ground disguised as a man,
But the claw betrays the animal, alienated from God and reason.

<margin>In the healthfulness and medicine of things:</margin>
Whoever wishes to make use of medicine, let him go
And open the wise book of nature, full of secrets, <note>The "Book of Nature" was a popular theological concept suggesting that God's revelation could be read in the natural world as clearly as in the Bible.</note>
Searching through mines, matter, and stones, plants, and animals;
Then it will be clear to him how nature knows how to direct everything

### THE THIRD BOOK.

Towards some wholesome end, and for the preservation of each,
Of which ancient custom is a witness, <note>original: "ondervintster," literally an "experiencer" or "female investigator," referring to the lessons of history and practice.</note>
Also such that deadly and murderous poisons
Of aconite <term>Aconite: A highly toxic plant, also known as wolfsbane, used historically in both poisons and minute medicinal doses.</term> and snake, through refining and sifting,
Abandon their nature and stinging venom,
Transformed by art and science into medicine.

<margin>in the gemstones:</margin>
The diamond, the prince of precious stones,
Expels melancholy from the heart and stills weeping.
The cheerful emerald wrests from poison its power,
Calms the surge of wrath, and possesses by its nature the power
To restrain dizziness in the head and illness,
And to strengthen our memory and make it sound.
The blue sapphire heals the skin and membrane,
Refreshes the countenance, and reshapes the sorrowful spirit.
The glowing ruby consumes poisonous tricks,
And quenches the lustful blaze kindled by Venus's fire. <note>A poetic reference to carnal desire or passion.</note>
A hyacinth <note>In historical lapidaries, this refers to a zircon or similar reddish-orange gem, not the flower.</note> dispels the vapor that dulls the brain,
Stills drunkenness, and whets the bluntness of the wit.
The green jasper staunches the blood-fountain of the woman,
Strengthens her in the peril of childbirth, and releases the heart from sorrow.
The heavenly turquoise cheers the sight and the mood.
The virtue of the bloodstone shines in the staunching of blood:
The power of the loadstone <term>Loadstone: A naturally magnetized mineral (magnetite), essential for early navigation.</term> draws five rings together,
And follows the Northern Bear <note>The constellation Ursa Major, used to find the North Star.</note> and the southern axis, steady and clever,
Guiding the hulk <note>A heavy, broad-beamed sailing ship.</note> at sea, now before, then in the wind:
For without a sea-compass, eyes and stars are blind.

In order not to penetrate through all the bowels of the earth,
<margin>in the ore:</margin>
Quicksilver <term>Quicksilver: The element mercury.</term>, sulfur, and salt, mixed in the heart of things,
To separate, and the clay of every kind of pigment,
To dig from the mine and Pluto's <note>The Roman god of the underworld, here representing the subterranean depths where minerals are found.</note> dark domain,
For the healing and service of man; nor to descend
To the center; my song forgets the baser metals
Of iron, copper, tin, and lead, and their use,
For the sake of noble gold and silver, in the belly

Of the earth, by nature hidden so hellishly deep,
Through God’s providence, and fatherly care,
To prevent disasters. O miser! O pain!
It is said, when the naked weapon was set upon the heart
Of silver, gifted in its youth with rosy cheeks,
That youth lost its color, turning white as a sheet from deathly fright. <note>A poetic explanation for the white color of silver; the poet suggests it "turned pale" with fear when humans first mined it with iron tools.</note>
Although the lust for gold has long and fiercely watched over gold,
The gold retained its color, which is eternal and firm,
Also made drinkable through art, which still, in every corner,
Does not rest, seeking from baser matter the soul of gold— <term>Drinkable gold: Known as *aurum potabile*, this was a legendary alchemical medicine thought to grant eternal youth.</term>
That power which murders souls, and plunges them into a flood
And sea of tears and spilled human blood.
The durability of gold nonetheless keeps its value,
And points to the eternity of God, who never changes,
But remains the creator of gold. The alchemist <note>original: "stoffefcheider," literally "separator of matter."</note> rages,
Who blows away life, time, and matter into smoke for gold,
And imagines he can seize the Golden Fleece, amidst the snarling of dragons,
Through the help of magic and her love. <note>A reference to the Greek myth of Jason and Medea, where Medea's "magic and love" helped Jason steal the Golden Fleece from a guardian dragon.</note>

<margin>In flowers, foliage, herbs, and animals.</margin>
The rose, the queen of flowers, strengthens the heart
In fainting, and cools the burn in a sick man’s fever and pain.
Narcissus, which loves the springs, dries and cleanses wounds.
The brain, the liver, the heart, released from heavy burdens,
Thank the comforting lemon balm <note>original: "melis," referring to *Melissa officinalis*.</note> for its virtue.
The head, as soon as the vapor in the brain is driven away—
That which was first clouded by cold humors—
Acknowledges the lavender’s <note>original: "levenders," likely referring to lavender, prized for its scent and "loyalty" to the body's health.</note> faithfulness, shown to the honor of the limbs.
Saffron cheers the spirit. Rosemary strengthens
The memory and the brain, so that it works as before.
The holy laurel doubles our strength,
Warms the gut, and creates joyful thoughts.
The hyssop clears the chest of chilly dampness,
And when the wide air feels too small for the breath,
It quickly opens the lungs and all that lay closed.
With the poppy, <term>Poppy: specifically "mankop" or the opium poppy, used as a sedative.</term> sleep is poured into the skull. <note>original: "beckeneel," an archaic term for the cranium or brain-pan.</note>

### ->THE THIRD BOOK.<-

Peony helps the kidney and curbs madness. <note>original: "razerny," likely referring to "mania" or "frenzy," as peonies were traditionally used to treat nervous disorders and seizures.</note>
Rhubarb clears the bile. <term>Rue: *Ruta graveolens*, an herb once believed to protect against the plague and improve eyesight.</term> Rue, always bright
Of color, extends the span of life, chases away plagues,
And extinguishes the fire of the air in burning regions. <note>A reference to "feverish" climates or perhaps the "miasma" believed to cause disease.</note>
St. John's Wort heals and soothes the nerve, which, warmed
And dried, feels the strength that protects it.
Stiffened nerves, frozen dead by cold,
Are healed by the fox, reviving as if reborn. <note>In early modern medicine, fox grease or oil was a common remedy for tremors, paralysis, and joint stiffness.</note>
Beaver oil strengthens the nerve; it drives away
The storm-wind that continues to rustle in the ear, full of storms. <note>original: "beveroli" (Castoreum), a substance secreted by beavers used to treat earaches and tinnitus.</note>
The earthworm soothes the limbs that tremble from paralysis.
Hartshorn awakens the corpse that lay dead and poisoned. <term>Hartshorn: The antlers of a stag, which were distilled to produce ammonia (spirits of hartshorn) to revive those who had fainted.</term>
The scorpion warms and drives the water down. <note>Refers to the use of "oil of scorpions" as a diuretic to treat dropsy (edema).</note>
The sturdy elk-claw—a creature of such delicate spirit—
Offers its head to Hercules’ club, and heals and helps the evil
In the head, when it, mad with pain, rages and raves; <note>Epilepsy was known as the "Herculean disease." The left hind hoof (claw) of an elk was a famous folk remedy for "falling sickness," often worn as a ring or amulet.</note>
Thus providence has established a counter-force,
Which repays human miseries with healing remedies.

<margin>
On the
lessons of
the creatures:
</margin>
Besides the fact that plant, herb, and animal heal us,
Each can read from their nature and properties
A lesson for the soul. The brave date-branch,
Called "palm" by the Germans, does not succumb under the weight,
But lifts its head on high, and teaches one in adversity
To persevere, and comfortingly repay loss with gain. <note>It was a common belief in the Renaissance that palm trees grew stronger and taller when heavy weights were hung from their branches.</note>
Chastity resists the flame, just like the laurel,
And makes a sound in the unholy fire of love. <note>Laurel leaves crackle loudly when thrown into fire; here, it symbolizes a virgin's "noisy" resistance to the heat of passion.</note>
The vine, entwined with its arms around the elm,
Teaches husband and wife to faithfully protect each other out of love. <note>The "Elm and the Vine" was a classic emblem of marriage and mutual support.</note>
The heavy ear of grain, which, most blessed with seed
And fullest of all, lets its head hang toward the earth,
Teaches humility to the rich, the noble, and the learned.
The sunflower, which never turned away from the sun,
Admonishes us to cast our eye toward the source of all light,
And to recognize the face of God in the sun.

De trees teach us to seek God in the root.
The chaste widow follows the turtledove in solitude,
Upon her withered branch. Arachne <note>A reference to the mythological weaver turned into a spider; here she represents the spider's instinctual skill.</note> quietly teaches
The housewife to set her hand to wool and spindle,
The silkworm likewise. The mute and silent cranes,
With stones in their beaks, <note>A classical reference to the belief that cranes carried stones to remain silent while flying over dangerous mountains to avoid predators.</note> admonish us to silence,
And to keep watch. The swallow, the stork,
Each calls out: let everyone observe the seasons and the times.
The busy field-ant teaches us to save for necessity in time:
And the industrious bee teaches winning and gathering,
And also subjection to the king's majesty;
The proud lion teaches mercy, the dog faithfulness,
The snake prudence. The tortoise teaches women,
As housewives, to stay under the roof, wedded to their house and threshold:
And the eagle, who teaches its nestlings to fly in the air,
Shows parents the path of strict child-rearing.

In the 
providence 
and 
preservation 
of the 
creatures:
</margin>
The highest wisdom and providence from above
Cannot be praised enough by any creature within the creation,
Much less can it be denied, since it appears to us in every way,
And shines back powerfully, and never falters.
We saw this in part before in the human limbs,
Adapted for their use, from top to bottom:
And if one condemns or removes,
Changes or displaces anything in an animal or plant, that is a deformity.
Every thing, provided for and standing as if on guard,
Testifies that the origin flowed and came from a wise,
Intelligent Nature, who strokes and wraps in swaddling clothes
That which she breeds and bears, like a mother.
The bark clothes the tree, just as the peel does the fruit.
The flower opens and closes according to the different skies.
The trunk preserves its moisture <note>original: "vaegh," referring to the vital sap or life-fluid of the plant.</note> in the root and in the core.
The wild beast defends itself with claws and teeth.
The bird is provided with tough feathers for flight;
The wild beast is strengthened for the hunt with legs for running;

# THE THIRD BOOK.

The earth, through and through, is refreshed with veins;
The chilly north is gifted with woods and timber;
The shore is provided with dunes, or terrifying rocks,
And mountains, to defy the violence of the Ocean.
The burrow keeps the fox. The bird builds its nest.
The wind drives the air, and purifies it of plague. <note>In the 17th century, "pest" or plague was often thought to be carried by stagnant "miasma" in the air; moving wind was seen as a cleansing agent.</note>
The cattle manure the field, as if grateful for their fodder.
The Deity gives birth and feeds, more faithful than a mother.

<margin>in the water :</margin>
Now water follows the earth inseparably, just like
A suitor, who keeps hanging around his lover's neck,
On one side and the other. The water with its pools
Can purify the earth, and wash away all stains.
It extinguishes the fire's blaze, when the sulfur seethes,
And all heat is tempered, and cooled
By the water, which mixes with many created things,
Unites them with one another, and helps to knead them together.
The water quenches thirst, provides man with drink.
It springs out from the <term>fountainhead [bronaêr]</term>, and goes swiftly on its way,
Takes a breath, and runs through the veins of the brooks,
Until it gives birth to streams, and comes flowing into the sea,
And roars, from sea to sea, into the Ocean,
From which it was drawn, and ascended toward the moon, <note>A reference to the water cycle and perhaps the influence of tides, then often associated with the moon’s power to "draw" water.</note>
From where, purified by the sun, it descended
Into the open lap of the earth, refreshed with dew and rain,
Abounding in fruit and progeny, and giving birth, year after year.
The water delivers fish from stream and sea, from where
The fleets drag the world's harvest into every harbor,
So long as the Ocean carries on its back the ships
Like entire forests, for the convenience of men.
Thus all members of the world serve the whole body
For the maintenance of life, and show us in song,
Toward God's providence, the <term>wet-nurse [voestervrou]</term> of all things.

<margin>in the air :</margin>
Although the element of air, in the first book,
Pointed us toward the Deity, this schoolmistress is too clever,

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the four elements. Having discussed Earth and Water on the previous page, the author now turns his attention to Air, describing its vital role in sustaining life, enabling the senses, and its mysterious, quasi-spiritual physical properties.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

In the teaching of eager pupils,
Lest they fruitlessly and quietly pass their school by. <note>The poet suggests that without air—the medium of speech—education would be impossible, and students would wander aimlessly without purpose.</note>
   While the air gives breath to the lungs of animals,
It preserves and maintains in life all that lives:
While it serves the tongue, and eyes, and all ears,
Each can speak, see, and hear sound and voices,
And without its service, which supports these members,
Each would be mute, and blind, and deaf, and entirely useless.
Unless the living here draw air and breath,
None of the living can stir nor move:
They all stand dead at once, like statues in a row,
In marble, and metal, and wax, and painting.
They can for a time lack food and drink in distress,
But must without air and <term>breath [aêmtoght]</term>, die immediately:
For <term>innate heat [ingeboren gloet]</term> <note>According to the medical theories of the time, "innate heat" was a vital fire in the heart that had to be tempered by the cooling breath of air to keep a person alive.</note> demands coolness, also wind, and air,
Which ignites the blood, before the fire flees the spirit with a sigh.
The air refreshes and feeds the living fires
Of the spirits, which through blood and vein and nerve do swirl.
The air draws heat and cold in and out by turns,
By which all procreation and upbringing consist.
   The thinness of the air rightly teaches us to ask how one
Shall best and properly understand and name it,
A body, or a spirit, or something that, hung
Between the two, is called an element, or none.
It requires a great understanding to fully unfold itself:
Yet its thinness is neither to be felt nor beheld;
As if it had that in common with the spirit and <term>realm of souls [zielendom]</term>,
Or with the Deity itself, who never appeared to the eye.
   Even more wonderful it is, that this thinned being,
A body in deed, is immediately, as if healed,
Congealed together, no matter how fiercely one cleaves and divides it.
The torn body is healed on the spot.
No spider's web or <term>Cambric [Kamerijckſe]</term> <note>original: "Kamerijckſe" - referring to fine linen cloth from Cambrai. The poet contrasts the air's seamless nature with the finest man-made fabrics.</note> threads
After tearing are sewn back together; one sees the tear and seams.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the element of Air, transitioning from its physical definition to its role as the medium for all sensory experience and its superiority over water and land for movement.</meta>

# THE THIRD BOOK.

Here this primary element now sails past the spinning and weaving
Of human ingenuity, as if it lay still.
   Also, the glory of God’s wisdom can never die,
Where that same point of air <note>The author uses "luchtpunt" (point of air) to describe the focal point or medium through which light and color are transmitted to the eye.</note> seizes and paints countless colors,
In a single moment, as at night, in clear weather,
And bright moonlight, there appear, to God’s honor,
The stars in the sky, and below the lands,
Full of fruit, full of trees, and cattle on all sides,
In one and the same air, which hangs before our eyes,
And where the mirror of sight receives that radiance,
Like a <term>spherical mirror [spiegelkloot]</term> <note>A "spiegelkloot" or mirror-globe was a popular 17th-century scientific curiosity—a silvered glass ball that reflected a panoramic view of its surroundings, often used as a metaphor for the eye or the mind.</note> in which shapes play,
And whatever nature varies, to share with the eyes
The visibility of things. Now I wish, by the moon
And moonlight, to go and sit upon a hill,
Or on a mountain, in the greenery, where a thousand shrill throats
Of nightingales early stroke my ear with wild song;
The <term>life-awakener [levenwecker]</term> <note>The rustling wind or breeze that stirs the trees.</note> rustles firmly in oak and beech,
The Southern Sea <note>The Zuiderzee, the large inland sea in the Netherlands (now the IJsselmeer).</note> roars on the shore with gentle waves,
The singing of maidens, and joyful musicians,
And the echo of flutes rises up, from branches and tender plants:
Here I catch at once both sight and sweet sound,
Of color and tone and radiance, in one and the same air;
While the dawn <note>original: "morgenroot"</note> glows toward me from the east,
Promising a morning sun that shall comfort the slowest heart,
And shine in the dew, upon the earth’s round ring,
As lively and beautiful as when she went into the water.
   The thinness of the air can carry and transport
From place to place what moves and what cannot move,
More peacefully than the keel carried through the foaming green;
While the mountain of water rises before the bow and the <term>galleon [galioen]</term>,
The curved prow. How burdensome it is to row
Against the stream, which can tire even horses and winds!
And if one rides over mountain and rock, or a path,
Prepared long ago, at how much labor does it stand?

Even the shortest paths! How the mule and wagoner
Sweat and gasp to climb to the summit!
But the airy element grants bird, cattle, and man
A light and open path and passage, as they wish,
Inclined to help and serve man,
And, in man's service, the Never-Seen <note>The "Never-Seen" refers to God, whose presence is manifested through His creation.</note>.

<margin>In the airy realm:</margin>
Now striving through the air, high in the airy realm,
Which, in overcast weather, masks the heavens everywhere
From the face of the earth and the peoples,
Now less, now more, as the drifting clouds and the vault of heaven
Spread themselves before our eyes. Here thunder makes its home,
The lightning, hail, ice, and the fearsome din
Of winds, rain, dew, and comets <note>original: "staertstar," literally "tail-star."</note>, like heralds,
To plant God's battle-standard against godless blood;
Or to bless the land, so kindly and faithfully,
As if heaven descended upon the earth, his wife,
Caressed her bosom, so that she, pregnant every year,
At each set season, might bring forth fruits for him,
To refresh at her breast, to nurture in her lap,
From which crops, and fruit, and flower, and grain sprouted.
The clouds in the air are watery vapors,
Carried aloft by the power of heaven's golden lamps <note>A poetic reference to the sun and stars.</note>,
From where they spread before our sight, here and there,
Painted and variegated by water, air, and fire,
And hot or cold, as the mixture happens to rub together.
Thus the rainbow wishes to cast its colors high
In the sunlight. Thus the red rod <note>A "roede" (rod) was an atmospheric phenomenon or comet interpreted as a divine omen of punishment or warning for rulers.</note> threatens
The proud kings. Thus a crown shines toward us.
Thus a shooting star appears to dart forth in the dark.
Thus the waterspout <note>original: "hoos."</note> sucks up the cattle, or bursts upon lake and stream,
And proclaims that one power governs and steers all,
Bridles human wantonness, or lets loose the reins of evil.
Then one court descends, another climbs and clambers:
The sea drowns her shore: the earth shakes and trembles:

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the element of Air to the element of Fire, and then ascends further into the celestial spheres of the stars. It maintains the theme of God’s sovereignty over the natural world.</meta>

# THE THIRD BOOK.

Burning Mount Etna stands in a glow and sulfurous flame:
The cities collapse before anyone notices how it happened.
When the Divine is not at the helm, neither city nor walls,
Nor towers, mountain, nor rock can endure God’s wind and weather.

<margin>
In the element of fire:
</margin>
If I fly past the realm of air, which is coarse,
Toward the very finest air <note>The author refers here to the "aether," which in classical physics was thought to be a much finer substance than the air we breathe, filling the space of the universe.</note> on high, my breath seeks to
Fail me: for it can bear neither pen nor breath,
Being pure, undisturbed by storms, vapor, and gusts,
A neighbor to the fire that ignites my spirit
With a fresh glow, through which the Divinity speaks,
And threatens Nimrod’s temple <note>Nimrod was the biblical figure associated with the building of the Tower of Babel; here, his "temple" represents human pride.</note> and his Chaldean cities,
Where fire was incensed, celebrated, and worshiped <note>A reference to the historical belief that ancient Persians and Chaldeans were "fire-worshipers."</note>.

Although earthly fire is but a shadow of the element
Of Fire, which is not visibly known at the height of heaven,
Yet our fire below can both ignite and protect;
It is the goldsmith and blacksmith; it can cook and warm,
And heal through medicine. It tests the integrity of matter,
Separates purity from the false, and sifts the fine from the coarse;
It is the origin and the source of motion and movement,
And without the blessing of fire, mankind would sit in distress.
On this foundation, altar-fires were lit
In Asia and Europe for Vesta and Vulcan <note>Vesta was the Roman goddess of the hearth and home; Vulcan was the god of fire and the forge.</note>.

<margin>
In the constellations and the course of the stars:
</margin>
But it pleases me to go higher, into heaven’s blue tents,
Through all the spheres and above the elements,
To draw from the divine day, out of God’s created light,
Where no gloom nor clouds forbid my sight
To watch the clockwork that distinguishes the ages
Of the world, and, as so many wheels glide along,
Shows the hand that surely
Proves God alone is the Prime Mover <note>original: "albeweger." A philosophical term for God as the first cause of all motion in the universe.</note>.

How faintly the earth now recedes! I have left it far below.
All that appears to my eye is wonder upon wonder.
What greatness, beauty, power, and rich innumerability
Unfolds itself in the stars; a more beautiful majesty—

<meta>This page continues the transition from the physical elements to the celestial realm. Having discussed the element of Fire on the previous page, the author now ascends to the stars and the moon, using the metaphor of a cosmic marriage between Heaven and Earth.</meta>

->### REFLECTIONS.<-

More than ever the peacock can paint and variegate,
When he, before his mate, with his unfolded feathers,
Boasts a tail full of eyes, and blind with pride
With his wings rustling, he strives past her.
The green and blue, sprinkled with gold, makes known
What riches Mother Nature spent upon those quills.
I look upon the heaven, like a bridegroom
Of the earth, his bride. He hangs a mantle around,
Full of embroidered stones, hemmed with golden borders,
And pearls, each more beautiful than the next. She glows, and sees him coming
Thus arrayed, and receives, with a stronger glow
Of love, in her arms the prince, who meets her.
It delights me here to behold the dance of fixed and wandering stars, <note>In traditional astronomy, "fixed stars" are those that maintain their relative positions, while "wandering stars" (planets) move across the sky.</note>
Which never stray nor confuse one another,
In the joy of the heavenly wedding feast.
How that beautiful belt of the bridegroom shines, where my spirit
Marks the twelve signs, <note>A reference to the twelve signs of the Zodiac.</note> through which the sun completes
Its rounds for every year. It seems he is entwined
By three pairs of lights, <note>This likely refers to the six other "planets" known in the 17th century (Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, and the Moon) that appear to dance around the Sun's path.</note> which, with a beautiful swing,
Go round him in a dance; while the wedding procession
Of so many thousands, with hand in hand together,
Concludes the round dance, and cheers toward the father of all radiance <note>The Sun, or metaphorically, the Creator.</note>
From afar, and thanks him for the rich light,
That shines in everyone's eyes, and beams from his face,
And without which the air and earth and heaven would be dark,
Perishing without luster or living splendor.
What sentries the heaven sets out here by night!
The darkness lends the stars their glow and power.
How the bride is then fully, then half, then not at all illuminated!
Here Hevelius <note>Johannes Hevelius (1611–1687) was a famous astronomer known as the "father of lunar topography." His book *Selenographia* featured the first detailed maps of the moon.</note> needs to lend no telescope,
To search for freckle and spot and light, in the face of the moon,
With the lens, and to paint the earth and Ocean
Upon his lunar map, and the parts
Of the sublunary world, <note>original: "onderweerelt" — Literally the "under-world," referring to the Earthly realm situated beneath the sphere of the moon, traditionally viewed as a place of decay and conflict.</note> rich in bloody quarrels,

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous discussion of the cosmic marriage between Heaven and Earth. Having established the beauty of the celestial canopy, the author now contrasts this divine order with the chaotic violence of human warfare, before delving deeper into the mechanics of the heavens and the revolutionary theories of Copernicus.</meta>

### THE THIRD BOOK. III

An arm of safety, unity, and peace,
Which here go in procession, in the heavenly palace.
O creator of the sun, by which we draw breath!
Everything that lives longs, in the morning, for the rays
Of the most beautiful light that You have created;
How is it that man thus misshapes himself,
More irrational even than the wild and blind;
And cruelly tears and devours his kin
With teeth of metal; <note>A metaphor for weapons of war, such as swords or the "iron teeth" of cannons.</note> hatching no evil
Too gruesome, out of a lust for change of state,
And desire for mastery, without casting an eye on the Uncreated;
Seeing nor knowing any light or Divinity but their weapons,
And the glint of a crown, which, fragile through violence,
In a moment, like snow, just like their lives, melts away?
What a wonder it is, that heaven knows how
To drive exactly around on its axis every twenty-four hours,
To muster the heavenly host, in the eyes of all men,
From east to west! What field-cannon, <note>original: "veltkortou", a type of heavy artillery used in the 17th century.</note> what bow
Can shoot ball or arrow; what lightning flashes
So fast through air and clouds! What waterfalls flow
So fast down the stone rocks! And yet the wandering star <note>The author uses "loffe ftar" (loose star) to refer to planets, which appear to move independently against the background of fixed stars.</note> preserves
Its region and course, from west to east,
Either fastest or slower, according to its stride, where the seven <note>The seven classical "planets" of antiquity: the Sun, Moon, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>
Were given a higher or lower quarter and watch-tower,
Many hundreds of thousands of miles apart;
To say nothing of a fixed star, which can be reached by no
Wit nor star-bow <note>original: "ftarreboogh", a cross-staff or early sextant used by navigators and astronomers to measure the angle of stars.</note> to shoot or measure,
However laboriously the stargazers sweat by night,
While Copernicus holds the starry vault still,
And teaches the globe to turn on its own axis. <note>A reference to the Copernican revolution, which proposed that the Earth rotates on its axis and revolves around the Sun, rather than the heavens revolving around a stationary Earth.</note>
O what bodies of light, what spheres
Here stun the intellect and the findings of star-wise men,
In observing what God, resting on high and silent,
Only shows us from afar through the glass of eye and lens! <note>original: "bril", which could refer to spectacles or the lenses of an early telescope.</note>

<meta>catchword: Here</meta>

<summary>The author contrasts the violent, power-hungry nature of humanity with the perfect, orderly precision of the heavens. The text highlights the vast distances of the cosmos and acknowledges the Copernican shift from a geocentric to a heliocentric understanding of the universe.</summary>

<keywords>Copernicus, astronomy, planetary motion, pacifism, celestial mechanics, star-bow, seventeenth-century science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the celestial bodies. Having described the seven "wandering stars" (planets) and their specific paths, the author now turns to the philosophical and theological implications of their movement, arguing that such perpetual motion must be driven by God rather than the inherent nature of the objects themselves.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

Here reason calls God the origin of movement,
&nbsp;&nbsp;Who, beyond Lady Nature and her custom and blessing,
Drives the stars alone by His hand,
&nbsp;&nbsp;And not by a form <note>Refers to the Aristotelian concept of "substantial form," an internal principle that was thought to give a thing its essence and natural movement.</note> implanted in their essence:
For all that is moved by nature must rest
&nbsp;&nbsp;Upon its seat, set by God, the All-Knowing:
And if one pulls it from there, it seeks its old state,
&nbsp;&nbsp;Its own element, outside of which it perishes. <note>A reference to the classical belief that the four elements (earth, water, air, fire) naturally strive to return to their proper place in the universe.</note>
Constant turning wanders infinitely from perfection.
&nbsp;&nbsp;As long then as the stars are deprived of rest,
Is it certain that nature does not speed the heaven’s course,
&nbsp;&nbsp;But Almighty power rows the vessel of the spheres. <note>original: "kreitſen", referring to the concentric celestial circles or spheres of the Ptolemaic geocentric model.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Naturally, each thing strives to reach its own end,
&nbsp;&nbsp;And its own benefit, by no means another’s: this is a sign
How not nature, but an intelligent Supreme Power
&nbsp;&nbsp;Drives the stars, who wished to strengthen the human race
&nbsp;&nbsp;And the sublunary world <note>The physical world below the moon, subject to change and decay, as opposed to the "perfect" heavens.</note> through their influence,
Verleenen moisture, <note>original: "vaegh", likely referring to dew or vapor necessary for life.</note> and growth, and life, and by no means working
&nbsp;&nbsp;For their own sake, out of knowledge and ingenuity.
&nbsp;&nbsp;Here now the atheist <note>original: "ongodiſt".</note> stands bewildered and dazed;
While the Divinity shines upon him with heaven’s blessing,
&nbsp;&nbsp;Along unnatural and unusual paths,
&nbsp;&nbsp;And he so blessedly receives that abundance,
Through the heaven’s course, which depends on the All-Mover, <note>The "Prime Mover" (Primum Mobile), a philosophical concept for the first cause of all motion in the universe.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;Is moderated, and follows the eternal pleasure,
&nbsp;&nbsp;As long as heaven shall carry the light of the wanderers, <note>The planets.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;And the forty-eight <note>The forty-eight classical constellations described by the astronomer Ptolemy.</note> stand firm on their watch,
&nbsp;&nbsp;Who move in harmony with the eighth sphere, <note>The "achtſten kreits", the firmament of fixed stars which was believed to rotate as a single unit.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;And, in their innumerability, to mark each one’s district,
&nbsp;&nbsp;Divide the whole globe into so many star-fields.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Whoever learns to know the Divinity from the star-book, <note>A common early modern metaphor where nature is seen as a "book" through which one can read the will of God.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;And never dishonors Her in the misuse of the stars,
&nbsp;&nbsp;Through blind idolatry, or star-divination, <note>original: "ſtarrewichleryen", specifically referring to judicial astrology (the practice of predicting human fate), which many theologians argued wrongly denied human free will.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;Which oppose man’s free will and God in this;

Has <note>Catchword: "Heeft"</note>

<summary>The author argues that the perpetual motion of the stars proves the existence of God as the "Prime Mover," since natural elements seek rest rather than constant rotation. He posits that the heavens move specifically for the benefit of humanity and warns against the "misuse" of stars through astrology or idolatry.</summary>
