--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical mechanics of the heavens discussed on page 112 to a spiritual and philosophical exploration of the human soul. The previous page concluded that the movement of the stars is driven by God’s power rather than natural necessity; here, the author begins "The Third Book" by looking past the sun to the nature of the soul itself.</meta>

### THE THIRD BOOK.

He has seen in countless light the unique Light,
Which first ignited the sun, and before which nights flee.
Now I would wish to strain my throat to sing
How God shows Himself in the sun—the fairest of all things—
Just as the living radiance of the heavens
Allows itself to be seen in crystalline mirrors.
But my inadequacy shall excuse my wish and will;
Since even the celestial spheres, rejoicing, unite
Their tones in diverse harmony on high,
And the realm of souls <note>original: "zielendom", referring to the collective existence or domain of spiritual beings.</note> above has me in its sight.

<margin>In the soul, outside the body:</margin>
I depart from the realm of the body, and soar on finer wings, <note>original: "pennen", literally "quills" or feathers, a common poetic metaphor for the flight of the mind or soul.</note>
To know God in the presence of the world of spirits,
Which resembles the Deity, to which in our day,
No other creature can compare in likeness.
Though this truth seems strange, it shall be proven by reason,
If we see God and the soul resembling one another.

The soul is spirit, no body fashioned from earthly dust,
And God is bodiless, and spirit, yet the greatest of all,
An uncreated spirit: thus the soul rightly soars
Even higher than the sun, where the essences <term>essences (original: "zelffstandigheden"): the fundamental, independent realities or "substances" of spiritual beings, as opposed to material objects.</term>
Of spirits take their station, and approach God's throne.
The soul is unconstrained, and therefore wears the crown
Of God's immortality—a title, in this life,
Never given to the unreasoning and mute creatures,
Who perish with their bodies; while the soul, in a state
Of endurance, upheld by God’s hand, does not pass away,
And thanks the Eternal One from whom it was born.
What light of knowledge has been bestowed upon the soul,
Which through the senses grasps all that reveals itself
From without, and gathers and preserves within itself
An invaluable treasure of all sciences,
Until it approaches God, along His created steps,
By reasoning <note>original: "redenkavelen", a term for logical deduction or philosophical disputation.</note> from the effect to the cause,
From the work back to the cause—so heavenly, above the firmament, <note>original: "zwerck", referring to the moving clouds or the visible sky.</note>

<sig>P</sig>
<meta>catchword And</meta>

<summary>In this opening of the third book, the author moves from the physical sun to the spiritual world, arguing that the human soul is a mirror of God. He posits that because the soul is an immortal spirit rather than material matter, it can use reason to ascend from earthly observations to the knowledge of God.</summary>
<keywords>soul, spirit, immortality, God, reason, celestial spheres, knowledge, divinity, theology</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the soul’s relationship to the divine, building on the previous page's assertion that the soul is an uncreated-like spirit that surpasses even the sun in its capacity to approach God.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

And air, and element, and sparkling fires,
Also fainter light, painted like imagery, and animals.
Thus God meets the eye, that sees everything.
The Deity knows it all, and does not reason. <note>original: "redenkavelt", meaning to reach a conclusion through step-by-step logic or dispute. God possesses "intellection"—immediate, total knowledge—rather than "reason," which is a human tool for finding truth over time.</note>
What is present, what was, what is yet to be,
The Deity sees it all, each in its proper order:
The soul requires spectacles <note>original: "den bril", a metaphor for the human intellect or the physical senses which are imperfect and need assistance.</note> while it languishes in the body,
And to her a new star appears, now here, then there.

<margin>*on free will:*</margin>
The free will, which has remained with the soul of man,
Exalts her in dignity, to soar next to God
With all the spirit-realm that flies through heaven.
Here she is nurtured and cradled in the shell <note>original: "schors", literally bark or rind.</note> of the body,
To increase the use of her rational powers.
She is not struck off, like flowers, from lineages
In descent, but springs from God, her stem, and goal;
She governs the entire body, and is whole in every limb, <note>This reflects the traditional theological view that the soul is not located in just one organ (like the heart or brain) but is "tota in toto," or entirely present in every part of the living being.</note>
Yet more glorious in one part, and darker in another.
Thus God and the soul border as neighbors to one another.
Did she not receive immortality from God as a loan,
She would have shared this majesty with her God,
Who now remains the owner and liege-lord. All branches
Of souls must thank him for the heavenly fiefdom. <note>The author uses the legal language of "leengoet" (fief) to describe immortality as something humans possess but do not "own" outright—it is held at the pleasure of the Creator.</note>

<margin>*on the immortality of the soul,*</margin>
But to show the immortality of the soul solely through the light
Of reason and nature, as if in a distant view,
To the understanding; since the eyes of man
Can in no way behold the essence of the soul,
So a gracious person will excuse me, that I am here
Brief in the proof, so as not to stain this soul-paper
With sounds that vanish without power,
And seem more like cleverness than grounded proofs.
The reason is too coarse, which creeps low along the earth,
The grounded proof too thin, that drips through the fingers:
The middle way holds firm. In strengthening a truth
So necessary, heed must be paid to conciseness, and clarity;

<summary>The author describes the soul as a divine "fief" granted by God, emphasizing that free will elevates humanity to a near-divine status. He concludes by stating his intention to prove the soul's immortality through clear, concise reason rather than overly complex or grounded arguments.</summary>

<keywords>free will, immortality, omniscience, nature of the soul, divine light, reason, leengoet, Scholasticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the soul’s immortality and its relationship with the physical body, specifically addressing the limits of human reason and the "Athenian school" of philosophy.</meta>

# THE THIRD BOOK.

Save according to the nature of the matter: for if
one demands mathematics here, to demonstrate
through measure and number the proper nature of souls,
which never, like the body, fell into the fate of mortality;
that would be an indiscretion. One should not demand a clearer day
of reason than the matter can naturally give,
and this satisfies a heart that does not, like the "seeing blind,"
demand a tangible reason which cannot be found here.

The same fundamental proof and proportion
testifies to the immortality of the Supreme Majesty
and man’s soul, [is it permitted to compare her with God?]
from both their operations, which are evident to the senses,
and point our understanding toward that which is invisible.
The wise *Athenian school*, <note>Refers to the ancient Greek philosophers, such as Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle, who argued for the soul's immortality.</note> though it sometimes misses the mark
in methods and inventions to strengthen the case for immortality,
nevertheless clearly understood it from all the beautiful works
of the soul, which here surpasses the mute <note>original: "stommen", referring to animals that lack the power of speech and reason.</note> creatures,
and, being neither elemental nor earthly, raises itself so heavenward;
while the body here crumbles into dust and earth,
while she, released from the burden of earthliness, according to her worth,
seeks heaven, from whence the spirit took its origin,
which did not come sprouting from coarse matter and earth.
And before she strives upward again with her wings, <note>original: "pennen", literally quills or feathers, used here metaphorically for the wings of the soul.</note>
she makes herself known through her works, like a deity.

<margin>recognizable from her operations:</margin>
If the body's <term>form</term> <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "form" is the animating principle or soul that gives life and structure to matter.</note> does not exist in its operations
by the body, then its essence actually exists least of all
by the body; for work and essence are held in kinship
and bound together; therefore the form does not follow the paths
of the mortal body when it is destroyed,
which was formed and given life by the soul, its form.
But that the soul can perform its work outside the body,
our reason shall clearly strengthen through the power of the essence,
which provides the body's form, from which the operation
of the body flows unwearied, as if from its own vein.

And air, and element, and sparkling fires,
Also a fainter light, painted like imagery and animals. <note>Refers to the constellations of the zodiac and the celestial spheres perceived as divine artistry.</note>
Thus God encounters them, the eye that sees all.
The Deity knows everything, and does not reason. <note>In classical theology, God possesses immediate, intuitive knowledge, unlike humans who must "reason" through steps to reach a conclusion.</note>
What is present, what was, what yet shall be,
The Deity sees it all, each in its proper order:
The soul requires spectacles while it languishes in the body,
And a new star appears to her, now here, now there.

<margin>on free will:</margin>
Free will, remaining with the human soul,
Exalts her in dignity, to soar next to God
With all the host of spirits that fly through heaven.
Here she is nurtured and cradled within the shell of the body,
To increase the use of her rational powers.
She is not set down, like flowers, from generations
In descent, but springs from God, her stem and goal; <note>The author supports "Creationism"—the belief that God creates each soul individually—rather than "Traducianism," which suggests the soul is inherited from one's parents.</note>
She rules the entire body, and is whole in every limb,
Though more glorious in one, and darker in another. <note>The author references the scholastic principle *tota in toto*, meaning the soul is fully present in every part of the body, though its functions (like thought vs. sensation) vary in clarity.</note>
So God and the soul border as neighbors to one another.
If she did not receive immortality as a loan from God,
She would have this majesty in common with her God,
Who remains the owner and liege lord. All branches
Of souls must thank Him for this heavenly fiefdom. <note>original: "leengoet." The author uses a legal/feudal metaphor, suggesting the soul's immortality is held as a "fief" granted by God, rather than being an inherent power equal to God's own.</note>

And seem more like cleverness than grounded proofs.
Reason is too coarse, crawling low along the earth,
The fundamental proof too thin, dripping through the fingers:
The middle way holds fast. In strengthening a truth
So necessary, one must look to brevity and clarity;

# THE THIRD BOOK.

Except according to the nature of the matter: for if
One demands mathematics <note>original: "wiskunst"</note> here, to demonstrate
Through measure and number, the proper nature of souls,
Which never, like the body, fell into the lot of mortality;
That would be a folly. One should not demand a clearer light
Of reason than the subject can naturally provide,
And this satisfies a heart that does not, like the seeing blind,
Demand a tangible reason, which is not to be found here.

The same fundamental proof and proportionality
Testifies to the immortality of the Supreme Majesty <note>A reference to God.</note>
And the human soul, [is it permitted to compare her to God?]
From the workings of both, which are evident to the senses,
And point our understanding toward that which is invisible.
The wise Athenian school, <note>Refers to the ancient Greek philosophers, particularly Plato and Aristotle, who argued for the soul's immortality based on its rational functions.</note> though it sometimes misses the mark
In its methods and inventions to strengthen immortality,
Nevertheless understood it clearly from all the beautiful works
Of the soul, which here surpasses the mute beasts,
And, being neither elemental nor earthly, raises itself so heavenly;
While the body here crumbles into dust and earth,
The soul, released from the burden of earthliness, according to its worth,
Seeks heaven, from whence the spirit took its origin,
Which did not spring from coarse matter and earth.
And before it strives upward again with its wings,
Like a deity, it makes itself known through its works.

<margin>knowable through its actions :</margin>
If the body’s form <note>In the Aristotelian tradition used here, the "form" is the organizing principle or essence that makes a thing what it is; the soul is the "form" of the body.</note> does not consist in its working
By the body, then its essence consists in fact
Least of all by the body; for work and essence hang
Kinship-bound and knotted; therefore the form does not follow the path
Of the mortal body, when it is destroyed,
Which was formed and ensouled by the soul, its form.
But that the soul can perform its work outside the body,
Our reason shall clearly strengthen through the power of the essence,
Which provides the body's form, from which thus untiringly
The activity of the body flows, as if from its own vein.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument for the immortality of the human soul. The previous page established that the soul should be studied according to its spiritual nature rather than through physical mathematics, and that its works prove its heavenly origin.</meta>

### REFLECTIONS.

The essence of the soul then, existing by itself,
Can endure outside the body, when this shell <note>original: "schors" (bark/rind); a metaphor for the physical body.</note> perishing,
Sees the guest move out, whom she had housed,
And seeks its origin, along the free and open path.
The soul changes its state, but not its nature:
For, as the form of the body, or beyond the striking of the hour, <note>A poetic reference to the hour of death.</note>
Separated from her hut, <note>The "hut" is a common early modern metaphor for the physical body as a temporary dwelling.</note> she with a distinction
Makes her state known through action and guidance.
As the form of the body, she works in the brain and all the limbs;
As bodiless and free, she begins to step higher,
Flies through the spirit-realm, and witnesses and declares everywhere
Her separation, and durable eternity.

Even if the soul ceases her work in the body’s limbs,
And the corpse, when she moves out, and the body lies abandoned,
She lives nevertheless in state and essence. The instrument fails
Its work through her departure: the smith, if the fire is
Taken from him, forges no more. No winnowing-fan can winnow corn
Or sift, without a hand. When the horse is unharnessed,
Then the wagon stands still. The soul can nevertheless
Still work, outside the body, her work, that requires neither limb, nor sense,
Nor skull. <note>original: "beckeneel" (cranium/skull).</note> Although the soul-destroyers <note>Referring to materialist philosophers who deny the soul's existence apart from the body.</note>
And the mockers and deniers of her immortality
Consider this possible, yet they deny quite stiffly
That a departed soul continues to work outside the body.
They imagine that what one grasps is first understood in the brain
And senses: no understanding, however sharp, however finely honed,
Can work except on that which plays in the human brain,
Appears, and through the sense the concept is imagined.
We also agree with them that our soul the treasures
Of all the understandable here can indeed grasp in a material way
Through her imaginings; but conclude nonetheless
That she does not judge these in a material way,
Since she often judges, as they themselves confess,
Things that directly conflict with the human senses:

<summary>This page argues that the soul is a distinct essence that survives the death of the body, using metaphors like a smith leaving his forge or a horse unharnessed from a wagon to explain how the "instrument" (the body) may stop while the "agent" (the soul) continues to exist and act.</summary>

### THE THIRD BOOK.

Just as when understanding or reason magnifies a star,
Making it far larger than the ring of the earth's round sphere; <note>original: "aertboôms ronden kloot." In early modern Dutch, "aard-boom" refers to the axis of the earth or the globe itself. The author argues that reason perceives the true, immense size of stars, while the physical senses only see tiny points of light.</note>
Or as it knows how to distinguish immaterial things from the material,
And separates the colors and shapes, in which the senses delight,
From their physical substances. The will also expresses this,
Which, through the understanding, restrains luxury and lust
For the sake of reason, and embraces—according to the timing
Of events—those matters or disasters that conflict with sense and inclination.

If the understanding only looked upon earthly pleasure,
And what the senses demand in the world's bright day,
Upon the world’s vanity, transience, and feasts;
How would the human soul, within the body, mingle with the
Spirits, and gather with God in conversation?
From whence would spring her desire and divine longing?
For those who are blind or deaf, whether by accident or by birth,
Do not bind themselves to colors or sounds, nor gaze at them in awe:
Now the soul lives nowhere so blessedly
As where she contemplates and perceives the highest good,
The Deity, who through the light of beauty and power,
Can keep the soul's faculties uplifted
In heaven’s highest sphere. <note>original: "trans" (orb or celestial sphere).</note> Now she yearns for the testimony
Of the wise, to behold the radiance of her immortality
In the fullest clarity, before she is unleashed; <note>The soul being "unleashed" or "unbound" refers to its release from the body at death.</note>
She pities the deniers of this infallible truth.
Before so many workings, which the eternal soul-realm
Unfolds, the mouth of the opponent stands silent.

<margin>
and in the
power of 
the con-
science.
</margin>
The tribunal of the mind <note>original: "vierſchaer van 't gemoedt." A "vierschaar" was a traditional Dutch court of law. The author is describing the conscience as an internal courtroom.</note> summons godlessness
And convicts it, and punishes it for its proven wickedness.
Although the tyrant sits in judgment of his own case,
He passes his own sentence, lashed by vengeance. <note>The "vengeance" here refers to the internal torment of a guilty conscience.</note>
In death he denies, with trembling and quaking,
That the pious, who are oppressed here, and the scoundrels, who are
Blessed in this life, rot with the soul in the grave,
And that God crowns no virtues, and punishes no wickedness.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument for the immortality of the soul. # REFLECTIONS.
<meta>catchword: Then</meta>

<margin>In the purity of the soul,</margin>
If the soul, being a spirit, feels no conflict such as the limbs do;
A conflict of the primary elements and all their qualities, <note>original: "hoofdtftoffe." In the science of the time, this refers to the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) or the humors. Physical decay was thought to be caused by these elements fighting for dominance.</note>
A conflict that is the cause of rotting and passing away,
Then she can exist outside the body and upon herself:
And if animal nature here is only fragile and perishable,
Then the soul reaches her destination, and lasts, and lives immortally. <note>original: "treft de ziel haer ende." Here, "end" implies the soul's ultimate goal or fulfillment rather than its cessation.</note>
And if the primary matter remains, only changed in state,
Who dreams that the noble soul, which treads higher, perishes?

<margin>and its constancy.</margin>
One sees through old age man's strength succumb,
The senses, slow and blunt, least resemble youth;
In actions and power: hearing, the quick sight,
Smell, touch, and taste fail in their duty,
When the old man rises to his peak, sown with gray hairs,
And the world-weary one groans under the weight of years,
While he already walks with one foot in the grave:
That signals the downfall of the mortal, supported by the staff
And crutches, and how those powers depend
On elements from which they receive life:
But if the judgment and understanding, poured into us,
Are by no means diminished by the passing of time,
But rather grow, that is a mark and an infallible sign
Of how man’s soul, when the body has succumbed,
Does not decrease, as she is not attached to the body,
And exists outside the flesh, by an immortal right.

<margin>Trismegistus testifies to the immortality of the soul.</margin>
The thrice-great intellect, <note>Refers to Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure from late antiquity often cited by Renaissance thinkers as a primary source of ancient wisdom (Hermeticism).</note> in all Egyptian cities,
Worshipped for his high-flown spirit and wisdom,
Testifies how God mixes man from two natures,
And brings him both mortal and never-dying
Into the world's round ring, as if between two natures:
The immortality of God, and that which changes in duration,
Alters, and dies away; so that man faithfully
Might here reflect upon all wonders in the light.

<margin>Apollo’s oracle, and the Sibyls</margin>
Apollo, when counsel was sought in the Heathen choir at Malta, <note>The text mentions "Malte." While Apollo’s most famous oracle was at Delphi, early modern authors often cited various Mediterranean locations for these ancient prophecies.</note>
Whether, after the corpse, the soul still remained in its form,

The author cites Hermes Trismegistus and Apollo’s oracle to support the idea that humans possess a dual nature of mortality and divinity.</summary>

### THE THIRD BOOK.
<meta>catchword: The eternity:</meta>

<margin>witness the same.</margin>
Then, whether it melted <note>Referring to the soul's supposed "melting" or dissolving into nothingness at death, an idea the author rejects.</note>, Polytes <note>A recurring character in this philosophical dialogue who asks questions of the narrator.</note> is instructed upon his question:
The soul, bound to the body, feels and suffers the plague
Of decay, and succumbs through mortal suffering;
But if it quickly escapes the body as it perishes,
Then it rises into the heavenly realm; there it never grows old,
Nor is its state impaired. This is what God’s first wisdom,
The Providential Being, wished to provide and ordain.
The Sibyls <note>Ancient prophetesses of the Greco-Roman world who were often co-opted by Christian writers as pagan witnesses to divine truth.</note>, famous for prophesying before Augustus,
And never caught in a lie under Nero,
Have depicted for us the punishment and reward of souls.

<margin>And the oldest and most illustrious sages, and all nations witness the immortality of the soul.</margin>
Pherecydes <note>An early Greek thinker (6th century BCE) often credited as the first to teach that the human soul is immortal.</note> first promoted this eternity
Among the Greeks; Pythagoras confirmed his master’s lesson:
Then Socrates and Plato followed Pythagoras,
And Aristotle trod the same path:
Though he seems clouded in his clarity to some,
The God-enlightened Aquinas <note>St. Thomas Aquinas, the 13th-century theologian who harmonized Aristotelian philosophy with Christian doctrine.</note> sees the Stagirite <note>A common nickname for Aristotle, referring to his birthplace, Stagira.</note> contemplate
This truth in its power, where he pleads for the immortality
Of the soul before the court of the most just.
The consensus, the law of nations, has this reason,
Throughout the whole earth, even now in the Indian cities,
Sealed with the ring of the sages, who lit their light
From the Hebrews, Chaldeans, and the keen vision
Of Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure associated with ancient wisdom and the "Hermetica" texts.</note>, through whom the ancient Egyptians,
At the peak of science, had climbed so high,
Until they, through the misuse of his flowery doctrine,
Sank into the night of idols, which tarnished
God’s honor. Nevertheless, they defended in those times,
The survival of souls after the body’s passing,

Against which the wit of Democritus and Epicurus,
Strengthened by Lucretius <note>A Roman poet whose work "On the Nature of Things" argued that the soul is made of atoms and dies with the body.</note>, has raved and doted,
As if mankind were then saved and helped,
When tombstone and funeral urn overwhelm both soul and body.

<margin>God’s providence.</margin>
Now the realm of spirits closes the barrier <note>original: "draeiboom." Literally a turnpike or gate, here marking the boundary between the material and spiritual worlds.</note>, where it sees
Nothing created rising higher in majesty above itself.

<summary>This page catalogs a lineage of ancient authorities—from the Sibyls and Greek philosophers to Thomas Aquinas and the Egyptians—who affirm the soul's immortality. It contrasts this "universal consensus" with the materialist views of Epicurus and Lucretius, who argued the soul dies with the body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of the soul and the divine order. While the previous page established the immortality of the soul through the testimony of ancient sages like Socrates and Plato, this page transitions into a discussion of the angelic realm and uses earthly analogies—the complexity of music and the navigation of ships—to argue that the orderly universe must be governed by a divine Providence rather than mere chance.</meta>

<header>### REFLECTIONS.</header>

<margin>This is evident in the realm of spirits, called by us the angel-kind.</margin>
The divine spirit of Plato has attributed the origin of their life
And being to God, the Highest.
Although Heathenry <note>The author refers to the classical "pagan" philosophers of Greece and Rome.</note> differs regarding their duration
Of before and after, as well as in essence and nature—
Whether they were created bodiless, or appeared
In the light together with their bodies,
Born of elements or of something heavenly: in general,
The ancient school of the sages consistently determined
The service, office, and end for which the Great Father
Brought them all forth, for the service of God and man;
Just as it shall now appear how God's providential nature
Governs, maintains, and preserves all that He created.

<margin>Parables, proving that God's Providence rules and maintains the world.</margin>
If a wild man <note>A common 17th-century trope for a person "unspoiled" by civilization or education, used here to represent a basic human perspective.</note> heard, through light leaps
And finger-dancing, what spirit came pressing through the organ;
What divine resonance spread into everyone’s ear,
And rolled in the high vault, through church, chapel, and choir;
So many mixtures of registers and sounds,
For which our centuries still thank Orlando’s <note>original: "Orlandoos". Refers to Orlande de Lassus (c. 1532–1594), one of the most famous and influential composers of the late Renaissance.</note> passion for song;
Would he believe that chance mixes those tones,
Like colors blended together, spread with art and spirit?
Would he believe that those rhythmic sounds and measures,
That echo, rich in spirit, in the following and repeating
Of high and middle and low and coarse sound,
Now more stately, then wild, then long, then briefly checked,
Unfolded themselves freely without an intellect,
And were not through Sweelinck's <note>original: "Swelings". Refers to Jan Pieterszoon Sweelinck (1562–1621), the legendary Dutch organist and composer whose "illustrious inventions" made Amsterdam a center for keyboard music.</note> hand and his illustrious inventions?
Certainly he would have to confess that intellect
And hand set those pipes, and strain their throats:
If the wildest Moor saw the light of the Admirals
Triumphing along the Amsterdam pilings,
Sailing from the rich sea onto our blessed IJ <note>The river and waterfront that forms the heart of Amsterdam’s harbor.</note>;
Through a forest of masts, which to lee
And windward honor him with trumpets,
And flag, and pennant, where he, while tacking <term>laveeren: a nautical term for sailing zigzag against the wind to make progress</term>,

<meta>This page continues the argument for Divine Providence. Having previously used the example of complex music (the pipe organ) to illustrate how an invisible hand must guide complex systems, the author now turns to the imagery of a great ship and a well-ordered household to further demonstrate that the universe cannot be the product of mere chance.</meta>

<header>### THE THIRD BOOK.</header>

As many keels approached and discharged, layer upon layer,
The thunders of the cannons <note>original: "kortouwe". A heavy type of artillery used in the 16th and 17th centuries.</note>, to wake the free The Hague
With its arrival; would this Moor <note>original: "Moorjaen". In 17th-century literature, this term often refers to a person from North Africa or a "foreigner" used as a character of natural, unlettered common sense.</note> believe—
Even if one saw neither sailor, nor crew, nor steersman above—
That such a sea-castle grew by mere chance,
And without a steersman’s hand came rushing to the shore?
Surely the dullest brain, though it learned no letters,
Must conclude that ingenuity built this, and ruled it.
Who then can deny that supreme Nature <note>The author uses "supreme Nature" as a synonym for God’s creative power.</note>
Lets the world, as it sails, drift without a rudder?
God’s work and the very nature of that work, revealed
In contemplation, speak against this from every side,
Through the greatness, movements, fitness, beauty, decline,
Temperance, existence, and the richness of the universe.
Whoever wishes to remove God from the eternal rudder of things
Robs Him of His most glorious office, which consists in watching
Over His created beings, and mankind most of all,
To whom everything bows. In God is neither power nor spirit,
Nor Divinity, if He sits deprived of Providence;
By Epicurus <note>Greek philosopher who taught that the gods exist but do not involve themselves in human affairs.</note> too meanly and mockingly compared
To a wrinkled fortuneteller. Thus does
His blindness plead wrongly against the majesty of the gods.
  The world is compared to a great and glorious house,
In which one sees God excel in power and wisdom,
As father, steward <note>original: "huisvooght". A head of a household or an estate manager.</note>, lord, and head of the family.
The office of the steward is to look after the gain
And benefit of the house, and to keep all housemates
To their duty: the small and the great,
The children, foreman, and boys, the head-maid,
Female slaves, and the household, as it pleases him,
To lead them by his hand, so that everything runs
Toward some wholesome end; that the means and property
Increase in blessing, and the growth, cattle, and fruit
Prosper, and all that serves remains obedient to discipline.

<meta>Catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page argues against the Epicurean view of a hands-off deity by using the analogies of a masterfully steered ship and a well-managed household to prove the necessity of Divine Providence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on Divine Providence. While the previous page argued against the Epicurean idea of an indifferent God by using the metaphor of a ship and its steersman, this page expands on the nature of God's governance, comparing it to the rule of a monarch and the relationship between the soul and the body.</meta>

<header>
# REFLECTIONS.
</header>

<margin>
It is evident in the governing and sustaining of creatures:
</margin>

The first and highest duty of a ruler, according to the wise,
Is to protect the kingdoms entrusted to him
From their downfall: for if the aim here is poor,
Then his office wanders from the mark <note>original: "wit". Literally the "white" center of a target in archery.</note> for which it was intended.
The Divinity, keeping an eye on the well-being of those entrusted to Him,
Directs the work, to guard and preserve it,
For the benefit of man, and to the glory of the power
And wisdom that brought everything out of nothing.
The governing of all that moves, and cannot move,
Is to perfectly carry out God's Providence.
The best form of rule, if Plato is believed,
And Aristotle <note>The author refers to the classical philosophical preference for monarchy (rule by one) as the most stable and natural form of government, mirroring the singular rule of God over the universe.</note>, consists in a single head.
Thus a single God sits at the helm of all things,
Who through His wise guidance brings them to their ultimate end.
The rational soul, which supports life,
Governs the limbs in this way, for the body's best and use,
Each in its own office, and can, for a row of years,
Keep and preserve man in his state.

But though the Divinity reigns in the blue vault of heaven,
And everywhere, He reigns either directly, Himself,
Or also through means: and, without the cooperation
Of the Divinity, one would notice neither power, nor movement, nor life
In God's creatures; no thing would hit its end and mark,
If it sat like a block or an idol <note>original: "molock". While referring to the ancient deity Moloch, here it signifies a lifeless, heavy, and useless object or false god.</note>,
Abandoned by intellect, and strength, and ability.
If God's influence were withdrawn from the creatures,
The stars would stand still in their rounds,
The attraction and opposition of the elements would perish,
Also the rising and setting, the withering and the blooming,
The seasons, each season, the ebbing and the flowing,
The turns and changes of things would be halted;
Whatever lives and does not live, it would all be over at once.
A manifest proof and irrefutable reason,
That Heaven’s foresight <term>Providence <note>original: "voorzicht". A literal translation of the Latin *providentia*, meaning to see ahead or look out for.</note></term> works through its created things.

<meta>catchword: So</meta>

<summary>This page argues that God’s Providence is the active governance of the universe, comparing God to a monarch and the soul of the world. It asserts that without God’s constant sustaining influence, all natural processes—from planetary motion to the seasons—would immediately cease.</summary>
<keywords>Providence, Divine Governance, Plato, Aristotle, Natural Law, Soul and Body, Sustenance of Creation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of God's governance of the world. Having established that God rules the universe, the author now addresses the concept of "concurrence"—how God works through created beings (secondary causes) without diminishing His own primary role. This is illustrated through the metaphor of a master guiding a student's hand.</meta>

# THE THIRD BOOK.

Thus the master bravely guides the pupil's pen and hand,
And writes through them for a prize. No reasonable mind
Can say, when he sees the letters flowing there:
"The pupil's hand and pen alone have written this."
They both wrote, it is true, yet not without the master,
As is seen from the art of script and stroke.
They wrote together, with zeal for the most artistic result;
But the honor belongs properly to the very first writer. <note>This metaphor explains the theological concept of "concurrence," where God (the master) and the creature (the student) both act, but God is the primary cause of the action.</note>

<margin>
And from the office of the spirit-realm, known to the Greeks, Romans, and oracles:
</margin>
The school at Athens itself admits that God everywhere
Has the heavenly spirit-realm <term>spirit-realm: original "geeſtendom," referring to the hierarchy of spirits, including angels in a Christian context or "daemons" in a classical one</term> as co-workers,
And expresses their titles. It calls them beings, watches,
Intellects, younger gods, and divine powers,
Guardians, mediators, observers <note>original: "gaſlagers," meaning those who watch over a path or keep guard.</note>, who advise
Against evil, and determine the good with their counsel;
Also domestic witnesses of each person's works and deeds,
Defenders before the court, punishers of the conscience
That lies without blushing before their piercing eye;
Elevators of the mind and virtues toward the heights,
And, between God and man, deliverers of prayers
And gifts—the former into the light, and the latter down below;
Protectors in need, the help and support of the poor,
Invisibly at hand, and those who, for man’s benefit,
Work salvation through dreams or signs, and turn away calamity.
The righteous Socrates held his household guard <note>A reference to the *daimonion* of Socrates, a private divine voice that he claimed warned him against making errors.</note> in honor.
Besides observing the one who prays, they interpret
What is to come through certain prophecy,
Proclaim God’s will as supreme ambassadors,
Perform wonders, famous on all sides,
Among the Greek and Roman people, in writings and chronicles.
The voice of the mute statue; the church door, without any sign
Of hands, opening from its lock; the doves at Dodona;
The oracles of Apollo at Delphi; and Trophonius;
And Ammon, where Jupiter <note>original: "Jupijn."</note> sat neither idle nor silent,
All sealed the nature and the work of the spirit-realm.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "spirit-realm" (guardian spirits or angels) as instruments of God’s providence. The previous page established that God uses these spirits as co-workers to guide and protect humanity. Now, the author cites the Stoic philosopher Epictetus to reinforce this idea before shifting to how Providence governs the rise and fall of great empires and addressing the opposing view of the Epicureans.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

<margin>Especially also in Epictetus.</margin>
Here I cannot honorably pass over Epictetus,
Whose earthen night-lamp came, after his death, to rise
To such a high price when Lucian bought it,
Because he had perfected his writings by this light. <note>Epictetus (c. 50–135 AD) was a famous Stoic philosopher who lived in poverty. The satirist Lucian of Samosata reportedly bought his clay lamp for a fortune, mocking the idea that owning the lamp would make one as wise as the philosopher.</note>
The Divinity, he says, has given everyone in this life
A spirit that cares for and watches over them,
A guard that never sleeps, nor is ever deceived.
When the shadow of night falls into the room,
And the dark chamber door is locked and bolted,
Do not say then that you are free from companions
And alone; for your spirit, a divinity, is within.
Thus the spirit-realm <term>spirit-realm: "geeſtendom," here referring to the "daemons" or guardian spirits that Stoics believed were assigned to every person</term> watches over the house and the family.

<margin>It is evident in the governance of Monarchies.</margin>
How God’s providence can stretch the reach of empires,
And change the state of the greatest monarchies
Through the power of princes and their swords,
Which, under His guidance, spare no army nor military force;
This is told by Ninus, the staff of Cyrus, the sword of Alexander,
And Caesar, who crushed the power of Rome, one after another,
And taught the free territory, feared throughout the earth,
To yield to a single head with knees bowed low. <note>The author lists the founders of the great world empires: Ninus (Assyrian), Cyrus (Persian), Alexander the Great (Macedonian), and Julius Caesar (Roman).</note>

<margin>Objection of the Epicureans, that everything drifts at random.</margin>
Now let us also hear from the student of Epicurus. <note>Epicureanism was often viewed by 17th-century thinkers as a philosophy of "chance" that denied divine intervention in human affairs.</note>
While I sat busy, diligently tracing
The cause of things, my judgment hung between two
In doubt: whether a God alone steers the world,
And watches and cares for it, or whether here below
One sees things drifting by chance, without reason,
Or rudder, or a steersman's hand. For when I considered
How orderly everything behaved according to its prescription,
And how the elements firmly understood one another,
Bound together by a single bond in their conflict;
How the earth produced fruits and was embraced
By the Ocean; how the course of stars, sun, and moon
Governed day and night, and divided the seasons
Of the year, covered with snow or lively greenery;

<meta>catchword: Then</meta>

<summary>The author uses the Stoic Epictetus to illustrate the existence of guardian spirits and argues that God's providence directs the history of great empires. He then introduces the Epicurean dilemma: whether the world is governed by a divine hand or by random chance.</summary>
<keywords>Epictetus, Lucian, Providence, Monarchies, Cyrus the Great, Alexander the Great, Julius Caesar, Epicureanism, Natural Order, Guardian Spirits</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's internal conflict regarding Divine Providence. After previously discussing how God guides empires, the author now reflects on the personal doubt that arises when observing worldly injustice. This lead into a critique of "Fortune"—the personification of random chance—as a false deity, specifically referencing the famous allegorical "Tablet of Cebes."</meta>

### THE THIRD BOOK.

Q 3
<meta>catchword: That</meta>

Then it seemed to me that the power, counsel, and will of God
Commands the whole, and steers, and keeps it standing in His lock,
And at the nod of the Highest the heavenly axes turn;
The shore halts the waves; reaping follows sowing,
The snow follows the summer, each in its own time:
But when I was met again by the strife
Of men, and how wildly the states, rich in calamities,
Teem together, in mists and vapors
Of eternal misunderstanding; how justice wanders from the path,
Virtue sits rejected, while murder wins the crown,
Then I denied the power and religion of the Gods,
And what one calls Divinity, and I mocked their commands,
To follow a surer compass, which teaches us
How neither God nor any like God governs the state of men
With wisdom and guidance, upon which the blind lean;
Or if Gods are found, and they do not concern themselves
With human welfare and misfortune, one need not keep them as friends,
Whose majesty is served by neither smoke nor temple.
Fortune governs it all: who wants to heed the Gods!
The bold have long outgrown the rod of heaven.

<margin>The denial of divine providence eventually elevated Fortune to a goddess, cleverly depicted in Cebes’ Tablet.</margin> <note>Cebes’ Tablet (*Tabula Cebetis*) is a famous ancient Greek philosophical allegory, attributed to Cebes of Thebes, which describes the human journey through life and the dangers of falling prey to false virtues and random Fortune.</note>

This blind frenzy and impulse of the ancient time
Has recklessly consecrated Fortune as a Goddess,
Built an altar and temples to a blind, insane woman,
Sacrificed to the flow of the Tiber, upon her thresholds.
The mature Theban wit <note>Referring again to Cebes of Thebes.</note> stripped her entirely
Of shame and the use of reason, in the *world-tablet*,
Upon her world-globe, where all the crowd babbles,
And rages, and presses around her, for gold and gifts, grabbing
For crown, and sword, and treasure, and pearls, which she scatters,
Hairy in the front, entirely bare from behind, <note>This imagery describes the figure of *Occasio* or Fortune: she has a long forelock to be seized as she approaches, but because the back of her head is bald, she cannot be caught once she has passed by.</note>
Where the lame and blind, lyre and crutch, gather their necessities:
An unequal state of birthright in creation.
One flows with pleasures in the court, full of excess,
And the other suffers want, and gives up the ghost in the street.

<vocab>**God:** God; **providence:** divine guidance; **Fortune:** the personification of luck or chance; **Cebes’ Tablet:** a philosophical allegory of life's journey; **Theban wit:** the intellectual skill of Cebes; **divine:** relating to God; **world-tablet:** the visual representation of human life and fate.</vocab>

<summary>The author describes a crisis of faith where worldly injustice leads him to doubt God and consider "Fortune" as the ruler of life, before critiquing the ancient Roman and Greek personifications of chance. He specifically references Cebes’ Tablet to illustrate the chaotic and unequal distribution of worldly wealth.</summary>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's philosophical reconciliation between the apparent chaos of the world and the belief in a divine order. Following the previous discussion of "Fortune" as a blind goddess from the Tablet of Cebes, the author now refutes this "varnished deceit" by asserting the absolute sovereignty of Divine Providence.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

<margin>Fortune is still worshipped by the worldly-wise and the vast majority of people.</margin>
The fact that the causes of prosperity and adversity are hidden from us
Within God's secret counsel, dug these dark pits
Of error and "fortune" in ancient times; and even today,
Noble men step forward to worship this varnished deceit,
Whenever they strive more for the status of the state, the clarity
Of a name, and earthly gain than for the truth
And for religion—the enemy of that state-idolatry
Which holds the entire world in its slavery.

<margin>Concluding argument, confirming that God’s providence governs and directs all things for the benefit of mankind and the glory of God.</margin>
Though all the heavens and the bright stellar spheres <note>original: *starretranssen*. This refers to the celestial orbits or "wreaths of stars" in the Ptolemaic universe, which were believed to move in perfect, musical harmony.</note>
Continually dance in a circle to a single rhythm
For the sake of God's providence; while, beneath the moon,
The state of the world seems, in its rising and setting,
To listen neither to the bridle nor the rod, the spur nor the rule
Of Justice—the crown of the pious, the flail <note>A flail (*vlegel*) was a wooden tool used for threshing grain; here it serves as a metaphor for the harsh punishment or "beating" the wicked receive.</note> of the godless;
Nevertheless, God on high is the power who governs it all,
And, according to His wisdom, He Himself releases the bridle of affairs,
Or reins them in at the proper time, from the first to the last,
As a punishment for the wicked, and for the benefit of the good soul,
And both to the praise of the Highest, who guides,
Employs, and turns everything to the outcome of His glory.
In the end, wise governance is generally praised.
Even if this book <note>The "Book of Providence" or the "Book of Life," a common metaphor for the unfolding of history and destiny which remains illegible to humans while they are in the midst of it.</note> appears too dark for us to read;
God’s wisdom steps back only to take a greater leap.
It aims differently, and hits its mark where no one would expect.
God’s care watches everywhere; it floats through all the members
Of heaven, earth, and sea, and all His created things.
The highest Love takes heed of all its works,
But first and most of all of the human race,
Which it seeks with all its might to rescue and to win.
No mortal man could ever love himself as much
As God loves man, but above all the soul,
Upon which His merciful eye fell with such delight
During creation, that it shares in the uncreated life, <note>The "uncreated life" refers to the eternal, divine essence of God; the author suggests the human soul is uniquely elevated to participate in this immortality.</note>
Exalted and glorified as high as the spiritual realm.

The text concludes by celebrating the unique dignity of humanity, which shares in God's eternal life.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on God's governance of the world to the beginning of Book 4, which focuses on the necessity and nature of religious worship. It establishes religion as an innate "natural law" while introducing the philosophical opposition of Epicureanism.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS
### on
## RELIGION.
### The fourth book.

<margin>The transition from God’s being, attributes, and works, to the practice of religion.</margin>
The Deity, thus revealed through our contemplation in her nature, character, and works—wherever she moves and shines forth beyond herself in the creation, preservation, and governance of the universe, and in whatever shines upon us within her structures, where man holds the highest domain—thus is the doubter shamed. Thus the foundation stands firm upon which reason teaches us to establish the worship of the Deity. This is a service that shall yield neither to violence nor to slander, but throughout the world and eternally, without end, seems imprinted in the human heart as a law of nature. For although nations, to a greater or lesser degree, even horribly, wander in their understanding of God and his worship—having been taught and introduced to it by differing schools—the general conclusion remains firm upon this ground, as far as the name of God has ever passed the tongues and lips of men. This is true unless it be among those of a heathen sort, wild and corrupt, or the self-willed who long for a new path and turn aside from the main highway to follow indefensible and weak arguments, seeking to surpass the authority of the wisest men.

<margin>Lucretius, the squire of Epicurus, denies divine providence and mocks religion.</margin>
Lucretius <note>original: *Lukrees*. Titus Lucretius Carus (c. 99–55 BCE) was a Roman poet and philosopher whose work "On the Nature of Things" promoted Epicureanism.</note>, the squire and the interpreter of Epicurus <note>Epicurus (c. 341–270 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who taught that the gods exist but do not intervene in human affairs, a view the author here seeks to refute.</note> (who was as a god to him), probes the bosom of nature, unfolding from his master's mouth all her secrets, in order to erase from the pious hearts of men...

<meta>catchword: The service:</meta>

<summary>This page opens Book 4 by arguing that the contemplation of God's works naturally leads to the necessity of religion, which the author views as an innate law of nature. He begins to contrast this with the philosophy of Lucretius and Epicurus, who denied divine involvement in the world.</summary>
<keywords>religion, natural law, Lucretius, Epicurus, divine providence, contemplation, reason, worship</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Epicurean philosophy begun on the previous page. It specifically examines Lucretius's arguments regarding the detachment of the gods and the material nature of the soul.</meta>

-># REFLECTIONS.<-
<meta>Catchword: Ge-</meta>

The imprinted mark of God’s providence,
Religion, and faith—he pleads as if
For the honor and majesty of the Gods, whose greatness
Does not descend from the throne to the world's lowliness and baseness:
For, says he, it is absolutely necessary that the
Nature and essence of the Gods, immortal in itself, follows a tone
Of ancient peace, and, in the high heavens,
Separated far from our state and human affairs,
Dwells alone, as one who is always
Freed from sorrows and need, and painless,
Appearing all-powerful through its own means,
By no means needs to borrow anything from our necessity,
Is moved to grace by no merits,
Nor spurred to a desire for revenge by anger.

<margin>He also denies creation out of nothing, the immortality of the soul, and mocks all hope for reward and fear of punishment.</margin>
The same poet <note>Lucretius</note>, who governs everything by the order
Of generative nature <note>original: *teelende natuure*, referring to the procreative power of the physical world</note>, denies that anything can be made
Out of nothing, and creates and births a world by chance,
From atoms <note>original: *vezelinge*, literally "fibers" or "particles," used here to describe the Epicurean concept of atoms</note> and matter, gathered from everywhere;
He gives the human soul a physical form, and lets that soul perish,
Dying without any remnant, just like the body;
He furnishes this great universe with bodies, and leaves
An empty void where no body occupies space.
He respects neither immortality nor salvation, but only to live
Quiet of heart, peaceful, carefree, and free from terror and pain,
Without hope for reward or fear of punishment,
Until the headstone covers the soul and the urn <note>original: *dootbus*, a funeral urn</note> in the grave;
Just as he fashioned it from the findings of *Epicurus*,
Who boldly untied the bonds of religion:
For although his pen refutes the worship of idols,
Because it also fights against the service of the true God himself,
It gives free rein to all godlessness,
Where neither dike nor piling stops the spring tide of its wickedness,
If no Deity concerns itself with the works of man;
A dream of the atheist, supported by plausible arguments.

<summary>This page critiques the Epicurean philosophy of Lucretius, arguing that his portrayal of indifferent gods and a material, mortal soul serves as a foundation for atheism and moral lawlessness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of the Roman poet Lucretius, specifically addressing his famous argument that religion is the source of human cruelty and superstition. The previous page discussed his views on the material soul and the indifference of the gods; here, the author focuses on the sacrifice of Iphigenia as a rhetorical weapon used by Lucretius against faith.</meta>

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

Just as our fundamental proof has revealed, in the strengthening
Of God’s providence, all too recognizable in His works.

<margin>He unjustly accuses religion of human sacrifice, confirmed in the case of Iphigenia.</margin>
Lucretius <note>The author refers to Titus Lucretius Carus (c. 99–55 BC), whose poem *De Rerum Natura* serves as the primary target of this critique.</note>, to unburden the necks of the people from the yoke
Of religion—as he so boasts—unfolds the horrific deed
Performed in Aulis <note>A Greek port where the fleet was stranded on the way to Troy.</note>, where even the Greek princes
Defiled their weapons with the murder of the pure maiden.
The daughter <note>Iphigenia, daughter of King Agamemnon.</note>, with the headband <note>original: *huif*, a ceremonial head covering</note> on her combed hair
That flowed down, noticed on her left side
Her father, who stood oppressed and troubled by the altar;
For which reason the servants quickly hid the bared sacrificial sword,
Where all the people, bursting out in tears and lamentation,
Sadly witnessed this bloody tragedy.
She knelt down, mute with terror and anxiety, in humble submission.
It does not help the sorrowful girl that she, a princess so tender,
Once delighted the king with the name of "father":
For, seized from him by the priests as a punishment, and consecrated
For sacrifice, she is led, all trembling, alas,
By no means—according to the custom—to celebrate a wedding sacrifice <note>original: *offertaetsi*, referring to the ritual aspects of a wedding ceremony</note>
With the torch at a distinguished feast,
But chaste and of marriageable age, to be mistreated and fearful,
Like a sacrificial lamb, to die in her father's service,
So that the fleet might strive with a fair wind toward Troy.
Thus Lucretius concludes, and cries out so that we understand him:
> Religion leads man to such an abomination! <note>original: "De godtsdienst voert den mensch tot zulck een' gruwel aen!" This is a translation of Lucretius's famous line: *Tantum religio potuit suadere malorum*.</note>

<margin>Counter-proof that wisdom is an inseparable companion of religion.</margin>
If we do not distinguish between use and abuse,
Nor between services that lead to God and those that lead us away from Him,
Then Lucretius wins the field when he calls an abomination
That horrific murder of Iphigenia, disguised
Under the appearance of sacrifice and religion, to lead the ships <note>original: *kielen*, literally "keels"</note>
Across the sea through the slaughter and destruction
Of the royal maiden before the godless altar:
And how many abominations—it is all too public—
Were once carried out in the name of religion,
Strengthened by the shameful awe for false gods,

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of true religion against the critiques of Lucretius. While the previous page addressed the "horrific deeds" done in the name of superstition (like the sacrifice of Iphigenia), this page argues that such abuses do not invalidate genuine faith, which is inextricably linked to wisdom.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

Gilded with empty oracles and sorcery:
But surely this does not burden religion with any guilt,
Since wisdom and religion, free from blemish,
And linked to one another, pull together on one line,
In such a way that neither can exist in essence,
Unless they go together, inseparable and wedded.
Thus one shuts out both irreligion and superstition,
And all idolatry, and erroneous conclusions,
By way of worldly fiction, like a "useful dream,"
Used to lead the people by the bridle for the service of the state:
For religion <term>godsdienst</term> <note>Literally "God-service."</note> looks toward God, and teaches us to serve
The unseen and eternal One, in the heart and also outwardly.
Thus the knowledge of God comes first, and from this knowledge sprouts
The service, just as the name implies in the Dutch language.
Where this knowledge still hides from the eyes of the blind,
What wonder is it that no religion is to be found there,
Or that it wanders from its path, by reason of its abuse,
Which, like the mute cattle, regards no salvation but the belly!
And if one must abandon the use of things because of their abuse,
Then one would find no use for any matter, nor for any state.

<margin>
The atheist
asserts that
God is
not helped
by any
service from
humans.
</margin>
Yet the atheist <term>ongodist</term> <note>A term used here for one who denies the necessity of religious worship or the existence of a personal God.</note> still boards us with insolence:
"If there is no proportionality <term>evenredenheit</term> between God and man,
What does the Godhead care for mankind?
The Godhead, eternally rich, needs neither service nor blessing
From a base worm, who crawls here along the earth.
What benefit is a drop of moisture, dripping from a sea-cliff,
To the great Ocean? What profit can God receive
From the creature, who must depend entirely on another,
And who, if not upheld by a higher power, perishes?
What passion of mercy or of wrath can move
The countenance of the Godhead, which is never changeable?
Or is it the same with man and God,
In subjection to passion, stirred up
Either by reverence, or by that which leads to insult?"

<summary>The author argues that true religion and wisdom are inseparable, contrasting genuine "God-service" with political superstition. He then introduces the atheist's counter-argument: that an infinite, unchangeable God would have no need for or interest in the worship of lowly humans.</summary>

<keywords>religion vs. superstition, God-service, atheism, divine nature, proportionality, Dutch linguistics, Lucretius critique</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's rebuttal against the "ongodist" (atheist) arguments introduced at the end of the previous page. Having established that religion and wisdom are inseparable, the author now addresses the specific claim that God has no need for human worship due to the lack of proportionality between the infinite Creator and finite man.</meta>

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

From Him who sits invulnerable upon the highest thrones?
Surely no wise man can hold so low an opinion of God.
He employs what nature gave him from her bosom,
In no way enticed by reward, nor deterred by punishment,
By the black Moloch <note>An ancient Levantine deity associated with child sacrifice; used here as a symbol of terrifying superstition.</note>, of whom children are afraid.
If anything good follows the corpse, he shall enjoy it too.
As long as one waits for the messenger from the other world,
One gropes wildly astray in this blind night.

<margin>and is refuted.</margin>
Even though between God and man no proportionality <term>evenredenheden</term> <note>A mathematical and philosophical term referring to a lack of common measure or ratio between the finite and the infinite.</note>
Is to be found, yet religion conflicts with no reason
Which allows that a prince be served by an inferior,
Whether it be man or beast, as we see daily.
Even if the prince’s honor cannot rise through the service,
And even if this gives him no benefit; he will not refuse
To draw the service, even at a loss, from a cast-off slave,
Or even from criminals; nor will their meager gift,
Though often rejected, stop him from honoring and rewarding them,
And glossing over and excusing what is forfeited
Through his grace <term>gena</term>, so long as the submissive nature
Of the servant respects and preserves the lord’s favor.
Thus the prince’s praise for goodness, generosity, greatness,
Mercy, and favor grows out of dust and disregarded wretchedness,
And poverty, and want. Even though God desires no honor
For Himself, whose glory neither diminishes nor increases;

<margin>A parable, indicating in some way how the gratitude of an inferior toward an incomparably greater being is not unseemly.</margin>
    His goodness seeks to place man, through the honoring of God’s laws,
In His immortal light and full radiance;
To pour Himself out into the creature that He created
For eternity, which never runs its course to an end.

    If the greatest prince, through one of his attendants,
Had a poor little dog gathered from the mud of the street,
And nurtured it in the court with wines and banquets,
Solely out of pure favor, satisfied that it politely and neatly
Served him, and thanked him on both its hind legs,
For undeserved virtue, for the lord’s love enjoyed,

# R E F L E C T I O N S .

Would anyone cry out that a kindness bestowed upon a dog
Dishonors the master, or stands against reason?
<margin>That many things, without personal benefit, serve man, who is bound to religion.</margin>Man, however small he is, is led by the Unseen One
To such a high peak that the sun and moon serve him,
And all the heavenly host <note>original: "hemelsch heir"; a traditional reference to the stars and angels.</note>, and whatever is composed 
Of elements, whether living or not, recognizes this rational creature,
And honors him in silence, as if they spoke with tongues:
"We serve, under God and compelled by His law,
One nobler than ourselves"; provided that nobility does not consist 
In a mathematical body <term>wiskunstigh lijf</term> <note>Meaning physical dimensions or mere bulk, as opposed to spiritual or intellectual substance.</note>, and the oversized scale 
Of the physique, but in spirit, wit, the use of reason,
And virtue, and power, and all excellences:
For a single gnat, or ant, or busy honeybee,
Which lives, outshines the sun and stars in value.
The sun, moon, clouds, air, and rain all "sweat" <note>An evocative metaphor for the labor of the natural elements.</note>
To measure out their powers for the growing mustard seed.
All a mother's strengths are spent in labor for her fruit <note>Meaning her unborn child.</note>.
How many a kingdom, land, and military rank,
Even whole worlds where other stars shine,
And courts, and halberdiers <note>Guards armed with halberds; symbols of royal authority.</note> obey at the mere nod
Of a crowned dwarf, a child supported by a guard!
Or does nature serve no man? What advantage or use
Does the sun enjoy while it, with its bright splendors,
Richly furnishes the heavens and all the starry spheres <term>starretranssen</term>;
Gives life to the earthly realm with its heat and rays,
Clothes it with greenery and foliage, and sprinkles it with flowers and ears of corn?
What benefit does the moon draw from the ebbing and flowing
Of the tides of the sea, and the decay and growth of every thing;
Since the stars understand nothing from on high,
And stand at the service of others, just as a light serves the eye?
Or do these things serve only the tame and wild animals?
They neither know nor understand, nor allow themselves to be guided
By a free will, which is not imprinted on the mute <note>The "mute" refers to animals, who lack the human gift of speech and reason.</note>.
Thus man alone is the target <note>original: "wit"; literally the white center of an archery target.</note> and end of all things.

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

So they all serve man, and under him the capacity,
That has raised man up to this dominion.
Now it appears clearly how this reason prevails,
That all of humanity remains bound to the service of God;
Since that nature alone, exalted by so much goodness,
To recognize the charter <note>original: "hantveft"; a legal document or privilege granted by a sovereign, here implying the natural law or covenant God has placed in the human heart.</note> of his God in this life,
And the sensible use of reason is granted.
Upon this, religion turns, as if on its axis and point.
So the least rises to the top, in service of the greater,
Until it approaches God; then the service can go no further.
The heaven serves the fire, the fire the thin air,
And the water. Water and air with a single sigh
Serve the earth. Water serves the plants.
The plants serve cattle, and beasts on every side;
The animals in turn serve man, and men serve God,
The Dispenser of everyone's service, and allotted fate.
But, you say, man's service shall not constitute religion
<margin>Objection that God can do without man's service.</margin>
If the Supreme One can derive no benefit from man's service,
Just as here creature serves creature for benefit,
Since the life of man exists by such a support.
A general is served, maintained by soldiers:
A king's prestige floats upon the power of subjects:
But God exists by Himself, almighty, rich, and wise.
He borrows nothing from outside, nor increases in value.
What sound rings more false than to strike this false string:
That God lets Himself be carried by man, as if by a mule,
And harnesses man for His service as if before His wagon,
Just as man here bridles the cattle with his hand!
Man needs that service, the cattle their fodder and pasture.

The cattle please the master; the master rewards the cattle.
Take away the utility: what cord, what bond connects these two?
Thus the mighty stand on their thrones, in the midst
Of the retinue, who seemingly serve and worship them,

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s response to the skeptical objection raised on the previous page. If God is self-sufficient and does not "need" human service, why does religion exist? The author argues that throughout nature, higher or more powerful entities serve those "below" them without receiving any personal benefit, simply because it is their ordained purpose. This leads to the conclusion that the entire physical universe is designed to support humanity, the only creature capable of rational worship.</meta>

### CONTEMPLATIONS

<margin>That many things serve man—who is bound to the service of God—without seeking any benefit for themselves.</margin>

Would anyone cry out that a kindness shown to a dog dishonors its master or contradicts reason? Man, however small he may be, is carried so high to the summit by the Unseen One <note>A poetic reference to God.</note> that the sun and moon serve him, as does all the heavenly host and whatever consists of the elements, whether living or inanimate. This rational creature <note>Humanity</note> recognizes this and honors it in silence, as if the elements spoke with tongues: "We serve, under God and compelled by His law, one nobler than ourselves." 

This nobility does not consist in a mathematical body <note>original: "wiskunstigh lijf"; referring to the physical proportions, symmetry, or sheer geometric size of a body.</note> or the excessive magnitude of the frame, but in spirit, wit, the use of reason, virtue, power, and all such excellences. For a living gnat, an ant, or a busy honeybee surpasses the sun and the stars in value. The sun, moon, clouds, air, and rain "sweat" <note>labor or exert themselves</note> to measure out their powers for a single growing mustard seed. All a mother's strength is spent in labor for the sake of the fruit <note>The unborn child.</note>. 

How many kingdoms, lands, and disciplined armies—even whole worlds where other stars shine—and courts and <term>halberdiers</term> <note>Guards armed with halberds, traditional symbols of royal authority and protection.</note> obey at the beck and call of a crowned dwarf: a child-king supported by a guard! Does nature not serve man in the same way? What benefit or use does the sun enjoy while it richly furnishes the heavens and all the starry circles with its bright radiance? It grants life to the earth with its heat and rays, clothes it in greenery and foliage, and sprinkles it with flowers and ears of corn. What benefit does the moon derive from the ebb and flow of the tides, or from the waning and waxing of all things? 

Since the stars understand nothing from on high, they must stand at the service of others, just as a lamp serves the eye. Or do these things serve only the tame and wild animals? No, for animals neither know nor understand, nor do they allow themselves to be guided by a free will, which is never imprinted on mute creatures. Thus, man alone is the aim <note>original: "wit"; the white center of a target in archery, signifying the ultimate goal or purpose.</note> and end of all things.

<meta>catchword: Thus</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the physical universe serves humanity not for its own benefit, but as part of a divine order where spirit and reason are valued over size and power. He concludes that man is the ultimate purpose of creation because only humans possess the free will and reason to recognize God's work.</summary>

### THE FOURTH BOOK

Thus they all serve man, and under him the capacity that has raised man to this dominion. Now it appears clearly how this reason prevails: that all humanity remains bound to God’s service; since that nature alone—exalted by so much goodness—is granted the rational use of reason and the ability to know the covenant <note>original: "hantvest"; literally a charter or legal document, here referring to the divine law or relationship between God and man</note> of its God in this life. This is the axis and point upon which religion turns. Thus the lowest rises to the peak, in service of the greater, until it reaches God, at which point the service can go no further. The heavens serve the fire; the fire serves the thin air and the water. The water and the air, in a single breath, stand at the service of the earth. The water serves the plants; the plants serve the cattle and beasts on every side; the animals, in turn, serve man, and man serves God—the Distributor of everyone’s service and assigned lot.

<margin>An objection that God can dispense with man's service.</margin>
But, you say, man’s service would not constitute true religion if the Supreme Being could derive no benefit from it, in the same way that one creature here serves another for utility, where human life is supported by such a prop. A general is served and maintained by his soldiers; a king's prestige depends on the power of his subjects. But God exists in and of Himself—almighty, rich, and wise. He borrows nothing from the outside, nor does His value increase. What sound could ring more false than to strike this jarring string: the idea that God lets Himself be carried by man as if by a mule, or harnesses man to His service like a horse to a carriage, just as man bridles cattle with his hand! Man needs the animal’s service, and the animal needs its fodder and pasture; thus, necessity remains the bond between these two. The cattle please the master, and the master rewards the cattle. Take away the utility, and what cord or bond would connect the two? So it is that the powerful stand on their thrones, in the midst of their followers, who in appearance serve and adore them,

# R E F L E C T I O N S .

In their hearts, their fortune is but temporary pleasure.
The worldly-wise <note>original: "weereltwijzen"; referring to those who prioritize secular logic and material success over spiritual truth</note> approach the altar for that god,
And they shape religion upon the cobbler’s last of honor,
Status, and profit; while the common people zealously follow,
Without judgment, driven under the yoke of services
In which the Godhead is more often insulted than given
The lawful honor and glory due to Him. This they themselves
Admit all too clearly, as they fight one another everywhere in armor,
Yet remain reconcilable when it comes to worldly authority,
But never in religion; if it may even be called religion—
All that is carried out under this name and title
By the servants of fortune, who give this a false color <note>original: "verwe"; a paint or veneer used to disguise the true nature of something</note>
And hawk their poison with official letters and gilding,
To which the people listen with open-mouthed patience.

<margin>
A defense
that God
desires the
service of
man not for
Himself, but
for man’s
own benefit.
</margin>
Love—an attribute of God described previously—
Through which it pleased Him to give man, out of nothing,
The essence of the soul and the body, now quietly seeks
Man out; yet not for His own sake, but for the sake of man,
Whose reciprocal love is drawn toward the lodestone <term>lodestone: a naturally magnetized mineral, used here as a metaphor for the irresistible pull of God's power</term> and the power
Of the Godhead, who bore him and brought him forth.
Therefore, man’s service turns to the service of his God,
The highest state of worthiness; not so much for profit
Or pleasure, but because reason and truth
Lead him, beyond himself, toward this revealed clarity,
And the Good with which he finds himself united—
The most precious Good, never yet sufficiently loved,
Praised, or celebrated. If a most beautiful,
Perfect, and noble maiden—a flower honored
With the crown of beauty in both soul and body,
Triumphing over the whole race of maidens—
Can ravish the noble-hearted youth,
And carry him out of his senses, so that he presses to his heart
The pure love he bears for her worth;
What then can the Godhead Himself do, if He pleases our eye?

"Het Vierde Boeck" (The Fourth Book). Page 135.

    *   *Text:* "Wat kan de bron van al de ſchoonheit? wat, de luiſter..."
    *   *Meaning:* What can the source of all beauty, the luster of the uncreated sun (God), do if its brilliance doesn't work in our dark hearts?
    *   *Analysis:* God is the source of beauty and light. God, being perfect, poured Himself out in creation to bless man.

    *   *Meaning:* God's love rests when it reaches man, its final target. Man is added to heaven through this service.

    *   *Meaning:* No passive passion is attributed to God. He isn't driven by human-like disturbance, lack, or weakness. Human hearts feel strife, desire, fear. But God uses mercy and wrath for His praise and man's eternal salvation.

I will translate as "ultimate target."<meta>This page continues the discourse from page 134, further exploring why God—who is self-sufficient—desires the service and devotion of humanity. It argues that this "service" is actually the means by which God pours His infinite nature into the human soul, which was created for immortality.</meta>

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

What can the source of all beauty, what can the luster
Of the uncreated sun <note>original: "ongeſchape zonne"; a common theological metaphor for God as the source of light that was never itself created</note> do, if its brilliance
Does not work within the spirit, enlightening our dull and dark heart?
The Godhead, by its very nature perfectly beautiful and good,
Who poured Himself out in the act of creation in order to find
And to bless man—that dear, God-beloved,
And first-sought son—desires and demands only
That man remain in His service; so that, having become
United and one with him, He may no longer pour out
The sea of the Godhead into the creature by mere drops,
But by flowing streams, or all at once—into a being endowed
With reason and free will, and an immortality that strives for the stars.
Thus God's love finds its rest when it is able to reach
Man, its beloved, as its ultimate target.
So the fire of God's love is satisfied in the service of man,
And man, through this service, is joined to heaven.
the abuse of God's service, by the lowly or the great,
Can in no way overrule the rightful use of religion,
Just as the abuse of God's name by a wild generation
Does not strip the Godhead of its essence or its power.
Let the ungodly <note>original: "ongodiften"; a term for those who live as if there is no God or who corrupt religion for political gain</note> freely debase His service for the sake of the state,
Or deny Him in their hearts, mock Him, and slander Him;
When the hourglass has run out <note>original: "als 't glas verloopen is"; a reference to the end of a person's life or the end of time</note> and the world's glory melts away,
The tongue shall testify to what the heart knows: which coin holds the most value.

<margin>
Grace and
wrath are
attributed
to God,
yet not in
a human
manner.
</margin>
No passive passion is attributed to the Godhead,
As if He were urged or driven in a human way
By disturbance, or lack, or weakness: for the state
Of God's perfection in nature and essence allows
No infirmity. Man feels conflict here within the heart—
Desire, pleasure, fear, and the pain of injustice;
But in their own way, grace and wrath find their substance
In God, who uses them only for His own praise
And for man's eternal salvation, which flows down from the vein
Of that righteous and merciful Father.

<vocab>Godhead, man, religion, grace, wrath, immortality, passion</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that God desires human service not for His own benefit, but as a vessel to share His infinite goodness with humanity. It also clarifies that while we use human terms like "wrath" or "grace" to describe God's actions, He remains perfect and unmoved by human-like passions.</summary>
