<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the Divine nature, moving from the general concept of God’s love (discussed on page 135) to the specific ways God interacts with human suffering, virtue, and wickedness. It addresses the theological problem of how a perfect being can experience "wrath" without it being a human flaw.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

<margin>God is the refuge of the wretched:</margin>
Whenever a person falls into struggle and adversity,
And wrestles with need and misery, they approach
The throne of supreme grace in prayer,
Confident that it will not despise the cries of the weeping.
Compassion finds here the material upon which it works,
Reaching out a hand to the wretched and strengthening them.

<margin>Just in rewarding, and benevolent,</margin>
One sees the pious <note>The original "vroomen" refers to the "God-fearing" or "virtuously religious" individuals who were the ideal subjects of 17th-century Dutch society.</note> generally behaving justly,
Because justice and virtues please God,
The Father and the Lord of all His created beings.
They offer Him their hearts, with sacrifices and prayers,
Exalting His name with gifts and songs;
This is the reason they receive so generously from heaven’s bosom
The blessings and the treasure of which He overflows:
For it suits God’s nature—never weary of doing good—
To crown godliness and virtue; it would be a sign
Of ungratefulness <note>original: "onherkentenis" — here meaning a failure to recognize or respond to the merit of the virtuous.</note> if He chose not to listen
To the pleas of the pious; so, to avoid such a blemish,
He must stand ready to serve the pious and upright heart in its need,
To recognize virtue, and never stray from rewarding it—
A failure that would be a shameful blot even in a rational man.

<margin>and the punisher of the wicked.</margin>
Yet one finds shameful people who respect neither God nor man,
Who turn their backs on the laws, authority, and justice,
And destroy state and land through robbery, murder, and arson.
The Deity is rightly and justly incensed by this.
It would be a stain upon Him if He remained still,
And watched, as if mute and deaf, with favorable eyes
The villainy <note>original: "ſchellemſtucken" — literally "scoundrel-pieces" or acts of great malice.</note> that makes the innocent mourn,
While no comfort or help in their need may reach them.
Wrath <note>original: "gramſchap" — in this context, it refers to a "holy anger" or divine justice, rather than a loss of temper.</note> then suits God as honorably as mercy,
So that His dominion may endure in its strength,
By showing grace to the good, and avenging the evil.
Grief, fear, envy, and greed are failings
That wander outside of God: but love, and equity,
And wrath, and mercy are the crown of His majesty.

<vocab>God, virtue, justice, mercy, wrath, the pious, the wretched, majesty</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that God’s character is defined by both benevolent rewards for the virtuous and necessary wrath against the wicked, framing these not as human emotions but as essential pillars of divine justice. It portrays God as a refuge for the suffering and a ruler whose majesty depends on maintaining moral order.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the Divine nature, moving from the general concept of God’s love (discussed on page 135) to the specific ways God interacts with human suffering, virtue, and wickedness. It addresses the theological problem of how a perfect being can experience "wrath" without it being a human flaw.</meta>

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

[The stars] of heaven, like rubies and diamonds, adorn [the sky];
While the world allows itself to be governed from above,
And, under the high authority of the heavenly palace,
Enjoys desired rest and undisturbed peace;
Unless Divinity lets loose the reins, as when through the resentment
Of kings, kingdoms everywhere are shaken,
Or fly out of balance <note>original: "uit hun loot" — literally "out of their lead," referring to a plumb line used by builders to ensure a structure is perfectly vertical.</note>, for a specific purpose,
Known only to the Supreme and His counsel,
Who raises virtues to the throne by first suppressing them,
And crowns wickedness, only to violently pull it down,
After it has played its role on the earthly stage
And lays aside this mask <note>original: "grijns" — a theatrical mask or a distorted facial expression, suggesting that earthly power is merely a temporary role.</note>, which differs from the true face.
Then the outcome tells us which of the two is esteemed as best:
Temporary happiness, or the eternal at the last.

<margin>That God's justice rewards good and punishes evil either here or hereafter.</margin>
For if there were neither punishment nor reward when soul and body separate,
It would directly contradict God's justice
That those who were unequal here, in luxury or sorrow,
Should be equal after death and enjoy the same state.
What use is it that virtue resembles its Father <note>Referring to the "Father of Lights" or God as the source of all virtue.</note>,
And dies away suppressed, hearing neither comfort nor salvation?
What harm would there be for immorality to plague the innocent here,
If it feared neither punishment, nor scourges, nor blows?
Now the pious man suffers in hope, patient in his mourning,
Just like a farmer <term>ackerman: a traditional term for a plowman or farmer, often used in parables to represent hard work and patience</term>, who after sweat and tillage,
Expects the fruits of the harvest with open barns.
The hope of holy bliss can endure disaster upon disaster.

<margin>Objection that wrath does not suit God:</margin>
That God shows mercy and love to His creatures
Is excused <note>In this context, "excused" or "verschoont" means "accepted as reasonable" or "granted" by the critics.</note> by the fairest of this lot;
But no sudden anger, ignited by fury,
In a single moment, inclined to avenge its injury.
This, they cry, does not befit the highest Goodness,
Whose bile <note>In the 17th century, "gal" (bile/choler) was believed to be the bodily fluid responsible for anger and bitterness.</note> does not overflow, nor surge, foam, and seethe.

<margin>which is answered.</margin>
To then consider this passion with distinction:
In its abuse and its proper use lie both evil and good:

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological argument from page 137 regarding divine justice. Having established that God rewards the virtuous and punishes the wicked, the text now addresses the specific nature of "divine wrath," arguing that a God without the capacity for anger would be a God without the power to maintain moral order.</meta>

### REFLECTIONS.

Anger is a middle ground; blameworthy is the man
Who revenges himself with injustice and cannot restrain himself:
The Divinity, never deranged, becomes wroth with reason.
Thus <term>wrath</term> <note>original: "gramſchap" — a righteous indignation or divine anger, distinguished here from human loss of temper.</note> is even tolerated in the wise,
Yet restricted to its proper time: therefore our misdeeds find hope
That God, being merciful, allows Himself to be reconciled after the offense.
Whoever sought to strip himself entirely of anger and resentment
Has cut off one of the sinews of religion,
Transformed man into a beast, implanted wickedness,
Grown wild in reason, and bewildered in understanding.
The <term>Sibyls</term> <note>Ancient prophetesses of the Greco-Roman world whose "books" were often cited by early modern Christians as pagan testimonies to divine truth.</note> tell us from ancient and wise pages
Of God’s wrath for transgressions and blasphemous deeds,
Appeasing heaven's throne by turning away from evil.
The fear of punishment preserves and maintains city and state.
Where discipline and wrath are absent, there is neither rest nor order in life,
And whoever honors the laws is surrendered as prey to violence.
Whoever feels this way about God, who ordains everyone's fate,
Certainly does not think lowly, but most highly of God.

<margin>Piety keeps wickedness in check and prevents it from bursting out.</margin>
But if <term>Epicurus</term> <note>The Greek philosopher whose teachings were often interpreted (or misinterpreted) in this era as advocating for a world without divine oversight or fear of the afterlife.</note> still pleases to let his animals
And herds roam the wild without fear, giving them free rein;
How can they graze peacefully and quietly,
Fattening themselves like the swine with acorns and leaves,
If God does not restrain their wantonness? How can he, quiet of heart,
Restful, and carefree, and free from terror and pain,
Use what nature gave him from her bosom—
In no way enticed by reward, nor deterred by punishment—
If the weeds grow, and untamed wickedness
Takes root through the seed of his godlessness,
Which seeks no crowns from the highest mercy,
Provided it only escapes the pain of punishment?
For if his followers <note>original: "ſpreeuwen" — literally "starlings," used here metaphorically for chatterers or those who mindlessly follow a teacher.</note> now regard this as a <term>Moloch</term> <note>A biblical reference to a cruel deity; here used to suggest that followers of Epicurus view the idea of a judging God as a terrifying monster.</note>,
What earthly peace is to be expected here in the world?
Though the orchard blooms beautifully, what use is the cherry tree?
No idle threat keeps the gluttony in check.

It critiques the Epicurean view of a world without divine punishment, suggesting that without the fear of God, human society would descend into animalistic lawlessness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Epicurean philosophy. Where the previous page argued that a just God must possess "righteous wrath" to maintain moral order, this page refutes the Epicurean idea that the soul is mortal and that the gods are indifferent to human behavior, ultimately defining religion as an inherent bond between the Creator and the rational human soul.</meta>

# THE FOURTH BOOK.

S 2

Thus one learns backward, in <term>Epicurus's</term> <note>A Greek philosopher who taught that the soul was mortal and the gods were indifferent to human affairs, a view the author considers a dangerous error.</note> schools.
The great master wanders, how should the school not wander!
He still waits for the post from God's immortality:
And who assures him, when life departs,
That all wickedness remains buried and forgotten,
Even if the most wicked denies this in his conscience,
Where God convenes the <term>tribunal</term> <note>original: "vierſchaer" — a traditional Dutch term for a court of law, referring to the square area where justice was administered.</note>, and torments the tyrant,
Who upon this soft bed can close no eyes,
While at night, deathly pale with terror, he sees pale ghosts
Of the murdered crowd grimly staring at him.

<margin>Religion is a bond with God, by virtue of the Latin word.</margin>
Although religion is strengthened by crown and rod <note>Representing the authority of the state and the power of punishment.</note>,
Yet reason does not only grant religion,
But keeps man bound to it, in body and soul;
Just as the <note>original: "Roomſche tong" — the Roman tongue, referring to the Latin language.</note> found the duty to God,
By virtue of man's bond, and an obligation <note>A reference to the etymology of "religion" from the Latin *religare*, meaning "to bind fast."</note>,
Which to the observant heart is not far to seek:
For God, established in power with all His attributes,
And, beneath Him, man, on so much higher steps
Than stone, and plant, and animal; while, gifted with reason,
He, with his head held high, runs toward life's origin,
So the reverence of the child flows to God the Father
Necessarily, like a stream from its source and vein:
And this reverence demands religiousness
Toward God's worthiness and supreme majesty.

<margin>God's perfection and excellence are worthy of all honor.</margin>
The right of religion is clear: for God's perfections,
Infinitely spread out, appear in the members
Of the world everywhere, like rays from its sun.
The rational creature knows the Deity as the source,
From which its being sprouts. It hangs with heart and senses
Entirely upon God, and esteems Him worthy to love,
To serve, He who raises man so high from nothing.
Whoever perceives God and man thus from both sides,
And observes, finds the ground of religion upon which to build,
As the surest thing upon which the creature can rely:

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument from page 139, moving from the necessity of a judging God to a formal definition of religion. It transitions from the etymological "bond" mentioned on the previous page to a discussion of natural law and a refutation of the ancient Greek and Roman philosophers who either misinterpreted religious fear or sought to abolish it entirely.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

For the noblest nature, due to its excellence,
Is rightly not only prepared the service of honor,
But has the power and the right of all rational beings
To demand the duty of religion, and its tokens:
Humanity in turn, since it receives everything
From the supreme nature, and hangs upon its being,
With reason remains bound and obligated to the unseen one,
With all it possesses, to celebrate and to serve,
According to the unwritten law, also, without the command,
Voluntarily and of its own accord to serve its God.

<margin>Definition of religion</margin>
To then strip religion bare from the book of nature:
Religion is a virtue, through which man with reason
To the creator, owner, and highest lord
Of things, according to his power, the service and due honor
Wholly offers up, both internally and externally.
Whether things waver or not, this truth remains steadfast.

<margin>Religion has declined in Heathendom, and piety is mocked by Epicurus.</margin>
If justice settles all inequality,
And withholds nothing from the creditor of his rightful right,
Then religion may well bear the name of head virtue,
Which honors God through services, and, looking to His high pleasure,
Dreads to withhold that which belongs to Him;
Dreads, through injustice and whatever disturbs the Deity,
To wound: for this is rightly called, fearing God,
From which the <note>original: "Godenvrees" — literally "fear of the gods," here referring to polytheistic superstition.</note> fear of gods has arisen by misuse
In Heathendom, which denies God’s <term>simplicity</term> <note>original: "enckelheit" — a theological term meaning God is one, undivided, and not made of parts.</note>,
Bewildered in the number of deities, without end.
From such an idle fear of powerless Gods,
Introduced by <term>Cecrops</term> <note>A legendary king of Athens said to have introduced the first religious rites and marriage laws.</note>, commanded by <term>Numa’s</term> <note>Numa Pompilius, the second king of Rome, who established the city's religious laws and priesthoods.</note> law,
Has <term>Epicurus’s</term> student, <term>Lucretius</term> <note>original: "Lukrees" — a Roman poet who argued that religion was a source of human misery and that the world functions without divine intervention.</note>, fed the mockery,
Who at once wants to take all fear from the human mind,
And strip it of piety,
Under the appearance of showing and dissecting every nature;
Although he aims and shoots outside the target of truth,
He who rejects God’s <term>providence</term> <note>original: "voorzienigheit" — the belief that God actively cares for and directs the universe.</note> from its domain.

<summary>This page defines religion as a "head virtue" based on natural law, arguing that humanity is inherently bound to serve the Creator. It critiques the "idle fear" of polytheistic Heathendom and refutes Lucretius's attempt to abolish religion by denying divine providence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument concerning the nature of religion, specifically defining it as a duty owed to the Creator. It moves from the definition of religion as a "head virtue" (from the previous page) to explaining the dual necessity of internal and external worship.</meta>

# THE FOURTH BOOK.

Our senses teach us how to reckon outward honor,
From words, and gestures, and distinct signs,
Witnesses of the heart, which submits itself
To the majesty of the Deity with all reverence.
<margin>Perfect religion consists of both the internal and the external:</margin>
The heart internally agrees with outward deeds,
Acknowledges and praises the source and <term>fountainhead</term> <note>original: "ſpringaêr" — a spring or source of water, used here to describe God as the origin of all grace.</note> of grace,
Which binds all who use reason to gratitude.
It wishes, being of a similar nature and one in mind with God,
With all its might and power, through rational evidence,
To resemble this noblest nature of the Creator.
Thus mankind honors God, just as the obedient child
Loves the good father—not in appearance alone—
And honors, and follows him, but also in fitting conduct,
Suited to the father’s nature, and justice, and reason.
True religion looks entirely toward the highest prize,
It exists, just like man, in two distinct ways:
The outward mark is the body with its members;
The internal offering—God worshipped from within,
And loved above all, piously feared,
Attentively believed—is the life, and the spirit,
And the soul of this service. They then go together,
As is fitting for those joined in religious union:
And without this bond of the heart and the exterior,
Where there is power to ensure neither fails the other,
Religion seems to lack its requirement and <term>circumference</term> <note>original: "ommetreck" — the scope or boundary of a concept.</note>:
For God desires the deed, and the inner desire,
The heart, and also the hand. Thus the divine law stands sharply defined.
Thus religion stands complete, in its full <term>circle</term> <note>original: "krits" — an archaic term for a circle or orbit, implying completeness or a finished sphere.</note>.
<margin>Just as man consists of soul and body,</margin>
Reason itself seems to teach and command this:
For since man consists of unequal parts,
Of body and of soul, and the Highest’s wish
Desires not the half, but the whole man;
How then could religion suffice with only one of these,
Or suffer that soul and body be divided and separated?

<summary>This page explores the dual nature of religion, arguing that true worship must be both internal (spiritual devotion) and external (physical actions). The author posits that because God created the whole man—both body and soul—He requires the service of both to consider religion complete.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse from page 141 regarding the nature of religious worship. Having established that religion consists of both an internal spirit and an external form, the author now explores how the internal contemplation of God’s attributes—such as His eternal existence and simplicity—necessarily directs our outward actions.</meta>

### ->REFLECTIONS.<-

Religion demands these two, rightly belonging to the Lord,
Who is never placed high enough at the peak of praise and honor.

<margin>and its effects.</margin>
Just as the movements of the body and limbs spring
And flow from the soul, recognized by outward works;
So the focus of the attentive mind directs
The outward service, ignited by its inner glow.
Just as the <term>compass needle</term> <note>original: "leli" — literally "lily," referring to the fleur-de-lis shape often used to mark North on a compass rose.</note>, touched by the <term>loadstone</term> <note>original: "zeilſteen" — a magnetic rock used to magnetize compass needles.</note>,
Follows the nature of the stone, and expresses it through its movement;
So the hand follows the heart, and whatever the heart commands.
Internal devotion is ignited by the mental
Contemplation of God's nature and His attributes:
And though we encounter Him from below in His creatures
As if through a cloud or mist, or by rising steps;
Divinity convinces all of mankind in general
That all who are rational must bow before her,
Just as the consensus of the nations testifies to this,
In a diverse sound of tongues, spanning wider than
One can shoot the stars across the sea and sand.

<margin>Since God cannot cease to be, no one has reason to doubt the deity.</margin>
If God exists eternally and of Himself, as we have concluded,
And cannot be anything other than "to be": who then dares to cast Him
Out of his memory, as if it were possible
That God might exist or might not? For <term>atheism</term> <note>original: "ongodiſtery" — a 17th-century Dutch term for godlessness or the denial of God's existence.</note>
Derives its strength from this, and wavers in its trust
In the Deity, upon whose existence the whole must firmly build?

<margin>God's independence declares His endurance, and comforts us with His constancy.</margin>
In the <term>independence</term> <note>original: "onverknochtheit" — refers to God's "unbound" or "uncompounded" nature; His freedom from being tied to or dependent upon created things.</note> of God’s being, without bond,
His endurance is planted within our senses;
While everything else found outside of Him fails,
Borrowing its being from God, who keeps all things bound
To this confession of the supreme majesty,
Which strengthens His worshippers with His constancy.

<margin>God's uniformity—</margin>
If no violence or conflict of unnatural things
Is tolerated or suffered in God’s <term>single-naturedness</term> <note>original: "eenaerdigheit" — the theological concept of Divine Simplicity, meaning God is not composed of different parts or conflicting emotions.</note>;
Why then do people attribute to Him that which alienates His nature,
And does not harmonize with the equality of God's character?

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation of God's nature from page 141. While the previous section argued that the inner spirit must direct outward worship, this page explores how specific divine attributes—incorporeality, independence, simplicity, and perfection—mandate a corresponding moral and spiritual posture from the believer.</meta>

### THE FOURTH BOOK. 143

<margin>
God’s incorporeality <note>The quality of not having a physical body.</note> teaches us to exclude all that contradicts God's nature.
</margin>

<margin>
God’s bodilessness teaches us to despise all earthly desires.
</margin>

If God has no body, why would one wish to seize Him
And touch Him with the hand, or imprison and confine Him,
Restricting Him to the earthly? Why does one seek salvation and rest
In something corporeal, and in sensual desire,
Which, like the body, melts away? The wise see beyond this,
And leave these thoughts to the earthly-minded and the crude.

<margin>
If God is no accident <note>original: "toeval" — in 17th-century philosophy, an "accident" is a property that is not essential to a thing's nature. God, being "substance," needs no "accidents" to exist.</note>, then we ought to fear no accidents of disaster.
</margin>

Is God no accident, who needs a prop and support?
Does He exist by Himself? Who then is rightly grieved
By the occurrence of disaster, and the turning of fortunes,
Seeing that one may rely on God, who stands firm,
And speak, when all is in need and distress:
Let waver what will: whatever wavers, God stands firm.

<margin>
If God is a pure and unique being, then countless things point us toward the one God.
</margin>

Is God a purity, a single unique being,
And nothing other than this? What can one gather
From a myriad of beings, but one "I",
A Divinity, who shows Himself to be seen in a single glance,
Tasted in every morsel and drop, and to be heard
In all kinds of sounds and voices, never to be stifled,
Smelled everywhere in an endless fragrance,
Touched in every way, through all the world’s members,
And by five senses generously bestowed upon our understanding,
Which, full of God, satisfied, provided for, and intoxicated,
Melts God's pure essence <note>original: "eenwezenheit"</note> into attributes,
Like letters, from which one spells God in a single name?

<margin>
God's simplicity <note>Theological simplicity means God is not made of parts, but is entirely one.</note> admonishes us to shun all duplicity.
</margin>

That God is simple, unmixable, without a wrinkle,
Admonishes us to approach His throne simply, piously, and plainly,
And purely, without good and evil mixed together,
Since His light permits no crease.
No bond can join earthly matter and the heavenly together,
And endure in God’s glow, which suffers only the upright,
In whom neither ambiguity nor craftiness sticks,
But a mind that clearly refutes false virtue.

<margin>
God's perfection excludes the
</margin>

If God is perfect, who must not rightly fear
To wander in a service that is halting and imperfect...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical meditation on the attributes of God from page 143. The previous page discussed God’s incorporeality and independence; this page addresses how God’s unity, infinity, and omnipresence demand sincere, inward devotion rather than the empty performance of "worldly-wise" hypocrites.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

<margin>
...results in imperfect worship.
</margin>
...is offered to Him? <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the nature of worship.</note> Who does not shun error with reason, when he sees the light of clearer truth? Who does not strive to increase piously in virtues that resemble the example of the Perfect Being?

<margin>
God’s singular unity suffers no idols.
</margin>
God is the only One, according to the voice of every sage. Let no one then arrogantly set an idol beside Him—neither outwardly, nor in the heart—nor deny in that One a single simplicity, a Being revealed to all?

<margin>
God’s infinity admonishes us to humility and reverence.
</margin>
If God’s infinity is determined by neither place nor time, encompassed and overtaken by no understanding or brain, and embraced by no love according to His requirement and worth; and if this great universe of heaven, sea, and earth is not even a point to be reckoned against this infinite greatness, which alone contains itself and enclosed within its being whatever beautiful or glorious things are to be wished for or desired; who then can bow too low before His throne, and, crawling in the dust, offer this high Majesty the full measure of praise and gratitude?

<margin>
God’s immeasurability and omnipresence shames the hypocrite everywhere.
</margin>
If God is immeasurable, and floats in all things, fills the great universe, gives it soul, and can penetrate all, no darkness hides from Him, and no lock shuts Him out: Who then would not lay aside the mask? Who would not strip off the pretense with which the hypocrite plays his part and goes about in disguise <note>original: "mommen" — refers to wearing a mask or acting in a masquerade</note>? The heart betrays the rogue, even among the silent: for though no torture rack can report or betray him, one cannot escape oneself, and least of all the Deity. Let the worldly-wise <note>Philosophers or politicians who prioritize earthly success over spiritual truth.</note> feel free, on worldly stages, to play their roles in the church out of a desire for profit or status, deceiving the eyes of the people who waver or lie; he who wishes to deceive God deceives himself first. God’s presence keeps all His courtiers in discipline and silence, and bridles whatever wishes to leap out of bounds.

<margin>
God’s unchangeability.
</margin>
Is God always the same, and unchangeable in His being, His countenance, and His counsel <term>original: "raetſlot" — divine decree or the eternal, fixed purpose of God</term>: what danger...

<meta>catchword: Un-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that God's infinite and all-encompassing nature makes outward hypocrisy impossible, as God sees into the heart, and His absolute unity and immutability demand total, humble devotion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of God’s attributes, moving from His immeasurability and omnipresence discussed on page 144 to His steadfastness, immortality, eternity, and omnipotence. The poet continues to use these divine qualities as a mirror for human conduct and a source of spiritual comfort.</meta>

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

<margin>
[God] requires steadfastness.
</margin>
Does he revere the Lord, who holds fast to this Unswerving One, and who—through the changing of times and human failings—never falters or turns back from doing good, but serves and honors God with constant devotion? Thus the soul comes to resemble Him who will never fail anyone, and stands as firm as a rock when all other things give way.

<margin>
God’s immortality assures us of our own immortality, and the life that flows from Him.
</margin>
If God is immortal, and if He not only lives but also animates <note>The original Dutch uses the verb "zielt," literally meaning "to give a soul to" or "to breathe life into."</note> all that lives throughout so many ages; then who should fear cold bones and that which cannot move, or the death’s-head <note>A memento mori symbol, such as a skull.</note> and death itself? He can create life even when life has been extinguished. From God’s own immortality, a charter <note>original: "hantveſt"—a legal document or deed of rights.</note> has been granted to the noble soul of man: that having burst from the husk of the body, it shall never die. Embalming balsam cannot keep a corpse from rot and decay, but God’s immortality preserves the mortal man who flees the graveyard of this world for a higher desire. Does God sit peacefully in eternity while the passing times begrudge us the presence of the world, flying by like an arrow or the blink of an eye? What vain eye still clings to the painted image <note>A metaphor for the superficial and temporary nature of worldly life.</note> of the world, which loses its color in the sun and smoke? Who would build a throne in the kingdom of this world, which floats upon smoke, vapors, and mists, and consists only of sounds and rumors? Who would nest here? Who builds castles in the air that perish like a vapor, scatter, and disappear? The Godhead can cause the varnish of the world to fade away entirely through the power of His radiance, honor, and majesty. One seeks these things in time, but finds them only in eternity.

<margin>
God’s omnipotence strengthens our trust in times of need, and inspires reverence and awe.
</margin>
God’s omnipotence—beheld in His work wrought out of nothing—is the stronghold <note>original: "vryburgh"—a place of sanctuary or a city of refuge.</note>, the refuge, and the trust of the pious, for it protects them in their deepest sorrow. It awakens reverence in all that God commands. It gives birth to awe in the heart that trembles at trespassing against what natural law teaches and what is strengthened by the light of reason;

T
<meta>catchword: For</meta>

<summary>This page reflects on God's attributes of steadfastness, immortality, and omnipotence, arguing that these divine qualities provide a "charter" for the human soul's eternal life and a refuge during earthly trials.</summary>
<keywords>steadfastness, immortality, eternity, omnipotence, memento mori, natural law, sanctuary, divine radiance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the poetic meditation on divine attributes. Having previously discussed God's immortality and eternity, the author now turns to God’s infinite wisdom, goodness, and love as motivations for human piety and gratitude.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.
<meta>catchword: To</meta>

For she <note>Refers to Divine Justice or Wisdom mentioned on the preceding page.</note> carries out the sentence, and strictly measures back to the godless for eternity, according to the measure of the evil committed.

<margin>God’s wisdom exhorts us to humble obedience and sincere piety.</margin>
God’s wisdom, which can convince everything that lives,
Admonishes the wisest to bow themselves and their intellect
Reverently beneath her; she who rises far above that which
Our senses and understanding pile up here,
Ignorant of the treasure that eternally hides within
By God, in wisdom and omniscience infinite.
She shows us here only—by the light of the moon,
The starlight, and the sun—in this Ocean
Of wisdom, a single drop of that which is locked in God
And poured out in His infinity alone.
Who does not kneel, hearing this, and fall upon their face?
Who refuses service to this all-wise Lord?
Who imagines—in excess, mischief, and desecration—
To hide from the all-seeing eye, the omniscience, and the knowledge
Of this sun? Or thinks themselves to be free anywhere,
While the heart violates the Deity and worships only with appearances?

<margin>God’s goodness exhorts us to gratitude and reciprocal love, and strengthens our hope.</margin>
God’s goodness, appearing to each as His power and wisdom,
Seems to borrow its very name from the Deity Himself in our tongue, <note>The author notes that in Dutch ("Duitsch"), the words for "God" and "Good" (goed) share the same root, suggesting that goodness is the very essence of His name.</note>
And leads us back again to His perfection.
She draws man to herself by showing the distinction
Of those blessings in which humanity, among many,
Might share: immortality, power, and the gift of reason,
And free will, through which man resembles a God,
Tending toward the countenance of heaven, before which sun and moon give way.
If this goodness soars too high for any pen to describe,
Who would refuse to acknowledge her with the fragrance of gratitude?
God’s love—which unites the beloved in will and nature
With itself, and through a desire to do good humbles itself,
Bending down, and of its own accord strikes the flag <note>A nautical metaphor: to "strike the flag" means to lower it as a sign of respect or surrender; here, God humbles His own majesty.</note> of its greatness
And fullness, in order to take a worm, a ghost, an earthly wretch
Upon her lap, to cherish them as her own fruit;
What reciprocal love and longing does she demand from man...

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic meditation on divine attributes. Having previously discussed God's wisdom and goodness, the author now transitions to how these attributes compel humanity toward gratitude, obedience, and the formal practice of religion.</meta>

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

To this philanthropic nature, the good father!
Who does not come closer to Him through the service of heart and mind,
Drawn toward His heart, focused on our own welfare,
And never exhausted of grace and treasures!
He who gladly gave us the lesser <note>Referring to the earthly gifts mentioned previously.</note>, is able to give us more:
In His immortality, the infinite life.

<margin>God’s sovereignty demands grateful obedience and piety.</margin>
God confirms His right of sovereignty everywhere,
As owner and lord in the eternal property
Of all that man received here as a fief <note>The author uses a feudal metaphor here: just as a vassal holds land from a lord (the "leenheer"), humans hold their lives and talents on loan from God.</note> from above,
Bound for the fief to praise the liege lord Himself,
To acknowledge Him for this virtue in body and in soul,
To fear the punishment, which swiftly at the heel
Overtakes and pursues the wicked, if he so degenerates
That he breaks the Lord’s main law, and dishonors and blasphemes Him.

<margin>God’s providence strengthens one against mistrust and demands resignation.</margin>
Does God’s providence not keep a sharper eye and watch
Over His creatures, for the sake of the human race?
Does He not govern and maintain all that is created in its being?
Who dreams that God remains sleeping peacefully and unconcerned,
And does not trouble Himself with humans or with religion—
Which separates mankind from the cattle, and rests upon this truth?
Who does not draw comfort from this in his troubles?
Who resists God’s will, if he is afflicted?
Who is not awakened to piety and virtue?
Who would create transitory joy out of vanity?
Who could doubt the soul, when the body lies wasted away,
Rotting in the grave, abandoned and forgotten by God,
Since even the villain himself, at the parting of his spirit,
Sees his punishment approaching, and fears the Judge?

<margin>Further instruction on religion.</margin>
Here religion <note>original: "godtsdienst", literally "God-service".</note> comes forward: but to unfold that service
Even more closely: and the way how God, the father of things,
Is naturally served in common, according to the light
That first rose from afar before the mortal sight;
So let us lead the stream of religion from its source,
Which kept the pious and the wicked separate,

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the human relationship with the Divine. While the previous page focused on God’s sovereignty and providence as the foundation for obedience, the author now moves toward the "Summum Bonum" (the Highest Good). He argues that true religion is a universal necessity that transcends individual upbringing and that lasting happiness cannot be found in physical pleasures, which he compares to the fleeting sweetness of honey.</meta>

<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>REFLECTIONS.</header>

From the very origin of the world, when no written law
Nor revelation had yet established formal religion:
For since worship consists of both the outer and the inner,
By the deeds of the soul, the body, and the senses,
There is rightfully required here a universal way
Of religion, introduced to praise the Supreme Being,
Achieved through a shared unity, even where the many
Differ in their physical perceptions and their upbringings.

<margin>The end <note>The "end" here refers to the ultimate goal or purpose (from the Latin *finis*).</note> of religion is the highest good:</margin>
Serving is an act of the body or the mind,
Or of both these together, through which the servant attains
A certain good, as if hitting a target: therefore he must consider,
Before entering into service, where that good is located,
Which deserves the name of the highest destiny.
This single best good exists in God alone,
And outside of Him exist the false or lesser goods,
As reason teaches, determined by all the wise:
For from the nature of things it appears certain and sure
What good, out of all goods, is to be judged as the best.
The "seeming-good" <note>original: *schijngoet*. A philosophical term for things that appear beneficial but are ultimately hollow.</note> carries the appearance of goodness to the blind,
But its falsehood is unmasked by experience.

<margin>this does not consist in the body,</margin>
Human happiness does not consist in the body,
Which is less than the soul and man’s spirit; thus
Sensual pleasure falls far too short; it merely strokes
The external limbs, but in no way satisfies; whether by delicacies
Enjoyed upon the tongue; or beauty, which, mirrored
Through the eye, vanishes like a dream in smoke and vapor.
So many immoderate people, conquered in body and soul,
And bewitched and enchanted by this siren <note>original: *meeremin*. Literally "sea-maiden" or mermaid, used here as a metaphor for a tempting but dangerous distraction.</note>,
Confessed at last that this sates no desire,
But inflames the thirst for more, and longs for something better;
Just as the bees witness to us with their buzzing,
Filling themselves from flower to flower, never weary of sucking honey.
Even if the buckwheat blooms beautiful and bountiful in the fragrant field,
What use is the taste of sweetness that melts even while it is tasted!

<meta>catchword: Koft <note>This catchword likely points to the word "Kost" (Cost or Food) on the following page.</note></meta>

<summary>The author argues that religion is a universal human requirement and that true happiness (the Highest Good) is found only in God, whereas physical pleasures are fleeting and addictive like the nectar gathered by bees.</summary>
<keywords>Highest Good, Summum Bonum, universal religion, sensual pleasure, soul vs body, vanity, buckwheat metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument regarding the nature of the "Highest Good." Having previously dismissed bodily pleasures as the source of true happiness, the author now examines worldly status—specifically honor and fame—demonstrating why these external validations are insufficient for the soul's ultimate fulfillment.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
# THE FOURTH BOOK.

<margin>
nor in worldly honor.
</margin>
If worldly honor could satisfy a person according to their wishes,
Then human salvation would depend upon the honor offered
By other men, for the goodness that shines within one's being.
But if they deny this goodness and its worth, then the honor
He sought becomes hollow. Furthermore, virtue is praised
As the work of free will: but the honor thus shown
To any virtuous hero by common consensus
Cannot be a person’s ultimate salvation: for honor does not belong to him,
But to those who honor and exalt that virtue.
One cannot, therefore, find the primary <term>target of salvation</term> <note>original: *heilwit*. A metaphorical "white mark" or bullseye in archery, used here to represent the ultimate goal or "Summum Bonum" of human life.</note> in honor.
To the virtuous alone, because of their inherent worthiness,
Is the well-deserved lot of honor assigned:
Even the wicked can show honor to the virtuous;
True worthiness is therefore more to be praised
Than receiving honor from the wicked and their rabble:
Thus, no external honor is rightly a person's most precious destiny.

<margin>
nor in fame,
</margin>
An extensive fame, announced by trumpets
Throughout the wide world, cannot place any person
In the highest state of happiness. Renown is desired
So that, being known everywhere and praised with honor,
One might stand out illustriously among many thousands;
Yet renown has the crown brushed from its head
By the very honor that eclipses it. If honor is not the highest good,
Who can boast that a person's fame hits the ultimate target!
Knowing <note>The author uses a Scholastic philosophical argument: the active capacity to perceive or "know" is a higher power than the passive state of being "known" by others.</note> trots far ahead of being widely known,
If one observes the proper order of things.
The most noble entity *knows*; the least significant is *known*.
How then could renown place a person, in the end,
At the peak of salvation? Since this <term>seeming-good</term> <note>original: *schijngoet*. A recurring term in this work for worldly distractions that mimic true happiness but are ultimately empty.</note> is located
In the awareness of a reputation, obtained through honor and praise—
A fame that floats loosely on the delusions and vapors of the senses,
And fails in its flight, unless this fickle air supports it!
What is believed today was disparaged yesterday.
A salvation so unstable cannot be the highest.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation of the "Highest Good" (Summum Bonum). Having dismissed external honor and fame on the previous page, the author now turns his critique toward material wealth, political power, and physical attributes, arguing that none of these constitute true human fulfillment.</meta>

<page-num>150</page-num>
-># REFLECTIONS.<-

And if the entire globe is but, in view of the universe,
A speck, a compass-point, much less than a round ball,
Fenced in by waters, full of wild wildernesses;
How much name and recognition must <term>ambition</term><note>original: *faemzucht*, literally "fame-sickness" or the obsessive craving for renown.</note> lack,
Where among thousands Fortune does not exalt even one,
Or where no one dwells, much less even perceives one's titles.

<margin>
nor in
riches,
</margin>
One does not win this blessed crown through rich possession of treasures,
Though <term>Midas</term><note>The mythical King Midas, who was granted the wish that everything he touched would turn to gold, only to realize he could no longer eat or drink.</note> turns whatever he can touch and grasp
Into pure gold, which supports no one’s life.
One seeks no gold for gold’s sake, but for service and utility,
Enjoyed from the ore, which was hidden so <term>hell-deep</term><note>original: *hellediep*. The author suggests that "Mother Nature" buried precious metals deep in the earth specifically to keep humans from being burdened by the greed and care they provoke.</note>
By mother nature, to relieve us of those cares.
One seeks the precious metal to spend it once more
On some truer good, and richer excellences.
The generous giver is rightly praised for his gold.
The miser misses the prize, and wears away his time in fear,
For the love of that treasure, and wealth’s abundance.
Who then prizes wealth as the most precious good?

<margin>
nor in
power,
</margin>
Power—embittered, envied, and guarded so bitterly,
Besieged by terror, never places the possessor
In a state of perfection. What a burden the crown is,
Common to both the good and the wicked! How weak is the king,
Who needs support from subordinates, and sees head and crown roll,
If the people "play the king," and once begin to run wild!
Might is subject to both right use and abuse;
Thus earthly happiness cannot consist in ruling.

<margin>
nor in
bodily
gifts,
</margin>
This destiny consists not at all in the body's well-being and gifts,
Health, beauty, strength—however proudly the senses gallop,
Encouraged by the luster of these excellences:
For the body’s gifts are common to both the good and the wicked;
The soul does not enjoy them, though it employs the limbs.
The soul governs the body through <term>reason</term><note>original: *reêngebruick en reden*. This refers to both the capacity for logic and the practical application of rational thought.</note> and logic,
Understanding and comprehension; so it appears in every way
That the good of the body cannot be the pinnacle of all goods;

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the "Highest Good." Having previously dismissed wealth and power, the author now examines physical attributes, the five senses, and moral philosophy (ethics), arguing that true human fulfillment must transcend what we share with animals and must instead reside in the divine qualities of the intellect and will.</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>
->### THE FOURTH BOOK.<-

Since physical excellence is also granted to the animals,
Who themselves excel in speed, and strength, and sight, and scent,
Far beyond humans, a trackless course,
As is evident in the horse, the lion, the eagle, and the <term>hound</term><note>original: *brack*, specifically a scenthound or beagle.</note>.
<margin>*nor in the five senses,*</margin>
The <term>salvation</term><note>original: *heil*, referring to human flourishing, bliss, or the ultimate state of well-being.</note> of man, which is perceived through the five senses,
Can consequently win no prize in this race,
As it is common to both man and beast, and lower than the intellect,
Which grasps what is sensible and plants it in the brain.
One praises the senses for the sake of enjoying <term>utility</term><note>original: *oirbaer*, meaning benefit, profit, or practical use.</note>,
And pleasure, and delicacies—their luxury and aim; thus
The senses here fall short, if in this dispute
One intends to find man's most useful good within them.
<margin>*nor in ethics,*</margin>
<term>Ethics</term><note>original: *zedekunft*, the "art of morals." The author argues that while morality is good, it is a tool for self-improvement rather than the final destination of the soul.</note> bridles passion and flaws,
So its hand reaches no further than this mark.
To become like the <term>Godhead</term><note>original: *Godtheit*. A central theme here is *theosis* or *divinization*—the idea that the ultimate human goal is to reflect the divine nature.</note> itself is the foundation
Upon which our happiness rests. If then our salvation consisted
In the means that lead us toward heaven's attributes,
One would not need God, nor need to step any higher.
Thus man does not become like the Divinity through this [morality] alone,
But through intellect and will. Let one demand no clearer proof
Of whether humans do not resemble God and the Divinity;
One can perceive it in the intellect and in the will,
The powers of the soul from which the Divinity radiates,
Like a reflection of the light that has descended upon it.
Did our earthly happiness consist in the practice of morals,
The mute beasts would walk along this highway,
Through a participation and a glimpse of virtue and discipline,
Yet without the intellect, which flees from irrationality.
Let one then not place man’s salvation in moral works.
Man soars higher, with wings taking flight.
<margin>*nor in the goods of fortune, nor in morals,*</margin>
If man’s salvation consists now in no outward good,
Enjoyed by <term>fortune</term><note>original: *fortuine*, referring to external luck or material circumstances beyond one's control.</note>, through the body or the mind,
Delighted by the senses; nor in intelligible joys
Sprouted from the act of morality and virtues;

<meta>catchword: Nor</meta>

<summary>The author argues that human bliss cannot be found in physical strength or the senses, as animals often surpass us in these. He further claims that while ethics are necessary to bridle passions, true fulfillment lies in the intellect and will, which reflect the divine nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument from page 151. Having established that the highest good is not found in the body, the senses, or basic ethics, the author now identifies it as the intellectual and spiritual contemplation of the Divine, referencing classical philosophy and the concept of natural law.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>
<header>CONTEMPLATIONS.</header>

<margin>
neither in
science, and
art,
but in
the contem-
plation of the
Godhead.
</margin>
Neither knowledge nor understanding of art and science;
So must our <term>salvation</term><note>original: *heil*, meaning ultimate well-being, bliss, or spiritual health.</note> exist upon a higher step,
In the quiet <term>contemplation</term><note>original: *bespiegelen*, a term for deep meditative reflection or speculative thought, often linked to the Latin *contemplatio*.</note> of the Godhead, and the clarity
Of the divine light of immaculate truth;
The very target at which our being aims,
And toward which all lesser good from God is directed.
Thus man resembles God, the father of all things,
Who eternally within himself and his contemplations,
Persists peaceful and still, and remains rapt;
While he moves all that drifts around him <note>This is a reference to the Aristotelian concept of the "Unmoved Mover" (God), who remains still while setting the entire universe in motion.</note>.
For this contemplation of God, the unseen,
Our health, peace, and undisturbedness must serve,
Which flows from morals: and every outward thing,
To be grasped by us, serves toward this contemplation.
The Sages concluded then: one cannot run higher
Toward the enjoyment of salvation than to know God in his nature,
Yet according to each person's power and allotted measure.
They concluded that in virtue and science consists
The wisdom that teaches how to distinguish good and evil:
That <term>piety</term><note>original: *godtsdienstigheidt*, literally "God-service-ness" or religious devotion.</note>, the chief virtue, must lead them
Toward one God alone, who gives the origin to all,
And is never placed high enough by all that have reason,
Nor sufficiently known, feared, and adored.
Thus <term>Religion</term><note>original: *Godsdienst*, meaning the service or worship of God.</note> first comes into practice through the light of reason,
Drawn from God's nature, and goodness, wisdom, power,
Known throughout the world by the human race.
From this knowledge, religion first sprouted,
Cultivated and nourished by all seekers of the divine,
And Aristotle demands punishment for every mind
That doubts whether one must reverently honor God.

<margin>
Nature and
reason teach
us toward
religion,
a law of
nations.
</margin>
The reason for God's service was by no means far to seek,
Nor required a letter, mark, and law, printed in books:
Nature had, as it were, carved this law into the heart,
And in this, all the wisest men agreed.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "highest good" established on page 152. Having identified contemplation of the Divine as the ultimate goal, the author now describes the social and spiritual hierarchy—moving from the relationship between the simple and the wise to the foundational relationship between humanity and God.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>
### THE FOURTH BOOK.

The simple followed steadily with harmony and pleasure
The wise, to whom they paid respectful awe,
And looked to their lips <note>original: *zagen naer den mont*, an idiom meaning to listen with rapt attention or to depend on someone's words.</note>, seeking comfort and advice
From them—the support of the pious, their hope and refuge
In need and adversity. The child loves the father,
The generous blesser, and follows him ever closer,
As heir to the good expected from the father’s bosom.
The wicked man fears the rod of the judge, to whom the guard
Of the people remains entrusted. The most excellent in rewarding
Virtues is greeted, and adored; and crowns
And gifts are dedicated, and richly served.
The giver of fortune is everyone’s God and friend.
Whoever wrongs the law must pay for the injustice,
Reconciles with justice, and dares not struggle against the sentence
That is passed by the righteous over evil.
Whoever seeks favor with the great knows how to adapt
His conduct and countenance to their law and will.
Thus was <term>religion</term><note>original: *godtsdienft*, literally "God-service."</note> first established, and grew
From the proportionality of a shared desire,
The justice and fairness that exist between God and man,
And man and God, to give God His due—
The Lord of body and soul and the origin of our life:
For all excellence, and power, and majesty,
Justice, mercy, and benevolence,
Generosity, and love, and inexhaustible treasures,
And wisdom, without end, beyond any intellect’s grasp,
Are <term>essential</term><note>original: *wezentlijck*, meaning these qualities are not just attributes but the very "essence" or "being" of God.</note> in God; and essence is everything
That God shall <term>contemplate</term><note>original: *befpieglen*, to mirror or reflect upon internally.</note> within Himself from eternity.
Who then could refuse to please the Deity,
And at once offer body and soul to that Being,
Out of love for the service which pleases and honors God,
And returns to the servant like a <term>reflection</term><note>original: *weêrglans*, meaning the glory of God reflects back onto the worshiper.</note>.

<margin>Flows from the interior.</margin>
A heart, turned toward God, and pregnant through contemplation
Of this perfect beauty, can no longer [hold] its thoughts...

<page-num>152</page-num>
<header>REFLECTIONS.</header>

<margin>
Neither in science nor art, but in the contemplation of the Godhead.
</margin>
Neither knowledge nor understanding of art and science,
But our salvation must rest upon a higher step:
In the quiet contemplation of the Godhead, and the clarity
Of the divine light of immaculate truth;
The very target at which our essence aims,
And for which all lesser goods from God are intended.
Thus man resembles God, the father of all things,
Who eternally in Himself and His contemplations,
Remains peaceful and still, and remains enraptured,
While He moves all that drifts around Him.
For this contemplation of God, the unseen,
Our health, peace, and tranquility must serve,
Which flows from ethics <note>original: *zeden*, referring to moral conduct or character</note>: and every outward thing
That we might grasp, serves this contemplation.
The Sages then concluded: one cannot run higher
Toward the enjoyment of bliss than to know God in His nature,
Though according to each person’s power and assigned measure.
They concluded that in virtue and science consists
Wisdom, which teaches the distinction between good and evil:
That <term>piety</term><note>original: *godtsdienstigheid*, the quality of being devoted to God's service.</note>, the chief virtue, must lead them
Toward one God alone, who gives the origin to all,
And who is never placed high enough by all who have reason,
Nor known, feared, and worshipped.
Thus Religion first came into practice through the light of reason,
Drawn from God's nature, and goodness, wisdom, power,
Known throughout the world by the human race.
From this knowledge, religion first sprouted,
Nurtured and fed by all those who share in God <note>original: *godtgenooten*, those who are "companions" of God or partake in the divine nature.</note>,
And <term>Aristotle</term> demands punishment for every mind
That doubts whether one must reverently honor God.

<margin>
Nature and reason teach and guide us toward religion, a law of nations <note>original: *recht der volcken*, referring to *jus gentium*, the concept of laws common to all mankind.</note>.
</margin>
The reason for God's service was by no means far to seek,
Nor did it require a written character, mark, or law printed in books:
Nature had carved this law as if into the heart,
And in this, all the wisest men agreed.

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

The simple folk followed steadily with harmony and pleasure
The wise, to whom they paid respectful awe,
And watched their lips, and sought out comfort and counsel
From them, the pillar of the pious, and hope and refuge,
In need and adversity. The child loves the father,
The generous blesser, and follows him ever closer,
As heir to the good, expected from the father's lap.
The wicked fear the rod of the judge, to whom the guard
Of the people remains entrusted. The most excellent in rewarding
Virtues is greeted, and worshipped, and crowns
And gifts dedicated, and richly served.
The giver of fortune is everyone's God, and friend.
Whoever wrongs the law must pay for the wrong,
Reconciles justice, and dares not resist any judgment
That, passed by the righteous, lies over the evil.
Whoever seeks favor with the great, knows how to adapt
Their walk and countenance obediently to their law and will.
Thus <term>religion</term><note>original: *godtsdienst*, literally "God-service." The author argues that religion is a natural development of human reason and social order.</note> was first founded, and grew
Out of the <term>proportionality</term><note>original: *evenredenheit*, suggesting a balanced correspondence between human social relations and the human-divine relationship.</note> of a similar desire,
The justice and equity that exist between God and man,
And man and God, to give God His due,
The lord of body and soul and the origin of our life:
For all excellence, and power, and majesty,
Justice, mercy, and benevolence,
Charity, and love, and inexhaustible treasures,
And wisdom, without end, by no wit to be grasped,
Are essential in God, and essence is everything
That God shall contemplate in Himself from eternity.
Who then could refuse to please the Godhead,
And at once offer body and soul to that Being,
Out of love for the service, which pleases and honors God,
And returns to the servant, like a reflection.

<margin>
Outer religious worship, which is most clearly seen in sacrifice, without which no religion is complete.
</margin>
It does not hide, but bursts forth in praise and sounds,
And reverence for God, declared and indicated
By a respectful mark and signs, which, according to reason

In wisdom, and in power, and benevolence, attributed [to Him],

Yet in a nobler sense than any created being

For otherwise God and man would stand equal in honor.
That every creature should <note>The Dutch phrase "de vlagh strijcken" (to strike the flag) is a nautical metaphor for surrendering or showing ultimate respect to a superior vessel.</note> strike the flag of honor

Is just. On that basis, the sign of honor was established,
Generally denied to creatures,
And dedicated to none but the supreme majesty
Of heaven: and this mark consisted in <term>sacrifice</term><note>original: *offerhande*. The author argues that sacrifice is a "natural" instinct of the rational mind to acknowledge a higher power.</note>

Thus sacrifice came into use among all the pious.
Where this was lacking, religion was incomplete,
Severed of its most precious limb, like a body without an eye,
And a world without sun and light in heaven's arch.
All who were grounded in reason (we speak not of savages)
Never wavered nor differed in offering to God,
Before greed and ambition encouraged shameful abuse—
Offering to a <term>false god</term><note>original: *ongodt*, literally "un-god."</note>, while remaining in debt of sacrifice to the true God,
Or offering human blood and people upon the altars,
At which the wise man recoils, his hair standing on end.
Thus sacrifice itself remained, even for the idolater,
The mark of religion, never erased by men,
As writers testify to us, century after century,
And convict those who bow before no Deity.

# THE FOURTH BOOK.

<margin>
The purpose of sacrifice and the rule of life, drawn from nature and reason, teaching one to choose the good and reject the evil.
</margin>
As if from a lord and owner of the estate, received in fief,
Whether once or many times; as one who must depend entirely
With love and loyalty upon the Head, who can, for
Rebelliousness, remove his subject from the possession
Of this held property: so did the sacrificers recognize
The greatest owner of all things upon the altars,
With the dedication of their cattle and fruits, by the fire
Consumed into ash and smoke, yet each in his own manner.
The wisest concluded this through divine impulses,
And without the instruction of books or writings.
They knew God, as if by a mark of gratitude,
Worshipped out of awe the highest Majesty,
And hallowed with praise—the Father of all things—
Everything in its entirety that they received from His hand,
Offered by their own hand to the burning of the sacrificial flame,
From which the <term>burnt offering</term><note>original: *brantofferhant*. The Dutch term literally combines "burnt offering" with the word for "hand," emphasizing the physical act of the giver acknowledging God's providence.</note> took its name and origin.
The sacred churchly gestures occurring around that service,
[ Whereby the priest, the most venerable of all,
Respectfully meets the one great God,
Maintained as a divine duty according to each land’s custom,
Devoutly introduced, ] is rational and to be praised,
Since it points toward God, the refuge of all the wise.
Now we see how the service to heaven’s honor began,
As holy as nature could regulate the world.
<term>Natural law</term><note>The author refers to *natuurwet*, the idea that certain moral truths are "written on the heart" and discoverable through human reason without divine revelation.</note> taught how to distinguish right from wrong,
The honorable from the useless and base; decencies
From indecency, through reason, by the will
Chosen or despised, once judgment had settled this dispute
Within the understanding: for if the pen draws two lines,
The one crooked and the other straight, the defect shall appear more clearly
Through the contrast of the straight against the crooked.
What is good by its nature, reason then establishes everywhere
As the measure, just as reason sets the measure and the rule
To the will of man, before he seals the matter;

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of how humanity recognizes the Divine. While the previous page discussed the external act of sacrifice as a "fief-payment" to God, this page moves inward to the "Natural Law"—the innate moral compass that guides human reason and conscience regardless of formal religious training.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.
<meta>catchword: From</meta>

And this righteousness, understood by the intellect,
Embraced by the human will, remained forever implanted
In the rational soul, inclined to strive for what is right;
Therefore, it was given the title of <term>natural law</term><note>original: *natuurwet*. In 17th-century philosophy, this refers to a universal moral code accessible to all humans through reason alone, independent of divine revelation.</note>;
Since it commands and also forbids the world of reason,
Which sees itself clearly herein, as if in a mirror,
Either pure or stained—unless a film over the eyes
Of judgment and understanding had deceived mankind.
Whenever a person transgresses this primary law,
He laments and feels the wound in his heart with sorrow.
Wherever the conscience turns, it finds neither rest nor profit.
The judge sets up the <term>tribunal</term><note>original: *vierſchaer*. A traditional Dutch term for a court of justice, literally referring to the four benches or ropes that marked the boundary of the court.</note> in the heart, and delivers the verdict
Inwardly; exactly as if nature had implanted that drive,
That inclination toward the good and aversion to vice,
In the human soul, and knew how to imprint it so deeply
That no force could ever pull out the root.
Equity, justice, the fittingness of a man,
Discipline, and honor maintain their inner radiance,
Their praise, and their value: their direct opposites
Are shameful to everyone, like stains on panels
Full of art and painterly spirit. Thus one hears how unbecoming it is
When the unbridled mouth blasphemes, hurts, and hates God;
Curses and dishonors its parents; mercilessly mistreats
The innocent; envies and wishes plagues upon a soul
That is generous and charitable; betrays its fatherland,
Willfully lies, deceives, and respects neither God nor man.

<margin>The natural law is generally accepted, and even remained present in false religions to some extent;</margin>
Although good and evil were reversed among various nations
In name and distinction—being more or less clouded
By the mists of misunderstanding—the foundation remained for everyone:
That one was bound to embrace the good and shun the evil with all one's might;
That one man should meet another in no other way
Than he wishes for himself; that crimes must be punished;
That everyone owed service and honor to the Godhead,
As well as to parents and masters. Upon this axis

It argues that conscience acts as an internal judge, and that even in cultures with "false religions," the core principles of the Golden Rule and duty to parents and God remain universal.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the innate moral compass. Having established that a "natural law" exists within the human heart, the author now examines how this internal light can be obscured. He argues that while the impulse to worship is universal and rooted in truth, it often wavers between two extremes—idolatry and atheism—leaving the "middle path" of true religion difficult to maintain.</meta>

# THE FOURTH BOOK.

<meta>catchword: For</meta>

Religion, which neither time nor the ages will destroy, 
Has always depended on truth among all rational beings; 
And no corruption of faith was ever so vast 
That it did not encompass these principles within its circle.

<margin>When natural and rational religion became corrupted.</margin>
The degeneration of God's service [which was established 
By nature and reason, and through which the Deity 
Was worshipped, celebrated, and honored] manifests itself 
In outward deeds and in the inner heart. This can be 
Seen in the denial of God Himself, upon whom the ancients 
And the wise generally founded this service.

The corruption of religious worship leads to wicked morals; 
The corruption of the knowledge of God is the path 
Toward blind <term>idolatry</term><note>original: *afgodery*. The worship of false idols or physical representations of the divine.</note>, which sought to exalt false gods.

Truth alone is to be found at the central point:

<margin>Religion stands between idolatry and godlessness.</margin>
For if a pen draws three lines on a white sheet 
Of paper, spaced equally apart, 
The middle line alone represents pure religion. 
The line to the right is idolatry, where a thousand idols toil; 
The one on the left bears the name of <term>godlessness</term><note>original: *ongodistery*. A 17th-century term for atheism or the rejection of divine authority.</note>. 
Thus, the middle line remains free from stain and error.

Whatever strays from the center, to either side, 
Is a corruption, no matter what guise the wandering spirit assumes. 
The middle line stands firm as a highway and the path 
Of knowledge leading to God, the highest good. 
And should anyone still doubt, the great and wise pagan 
<term>Plutarch</term><note>A Greek philosopher and historian (c. 46 – 119 AD). The author references Plutarch's essay "On Superstition," which argues that both atheism and superstition are extremes to be avoided.</note> wishes to guide him along this middle path; 
Although the moral writings of that illustrious man 
Cannot be cleared of pagan superstition. 
Nature enlightened the eyes of all peoples, 
Yet it did so from a sky not entirely free of the mists 
And dark clouds of misunderstanding. This causes many things 
To appear in colors and forms other than their true nature and essence.

<margin>Causes of the de-</margin>
The decline of religious worship, appearing so early and so erratically, 
So far-reaching, so universal, so stubborn, and so enduring...

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

->V 3<-

Religion has always depended upon truth 
Among all rational beings, a bond that shall not yield 
To time nor the passing of centuries;
And no corruption of religion was ever so great
That it did not include these rules within its circle.

<margin>When natural and rational religion became corrupted.</margin>
The degeneration of the service of God [which by nature
And reason was introduced, whereby the Divinity 
Was worshipped, celebrated, and honored,] manifests itself 
In outward deeds and in the heart within; and this allows
Itself to be seen in the denial of the Divinity itself, 
Upon which the ancients and the wise generally built this service.
The corruption of God’s service leads to villainous morals;
The corruption of the knowledge of God is the path
To blind idolatry, which came to exalt false idols.
Truth stands alone, to be found at the center point:

<margin>Religion stands between idolatry and atheism.</margin>
For as if the pen, upon the white of a paper field, 
Sets three lines, separated equally far from one another, 
The middle line alone shall be called pure religion;
The right, <term>idolatry</term><note>original: *afgodery*. The author views this as an "excess" of worship directed at the wrong objects.</note>, where a thousand idols sweat. 
The left carries the name of <term>atheism</term><note>original: *ongodistery*. Literally "un-god-ness." In the 17th century, this often referred to a lack of proper fear of God rather than just a philosophical denial of His existence.</note>.
Thus the middle line remains free from stain and error.
Whatever wanders from the middle, to one side or the other,
Is a slander, in whatever guise the spirit of error transforms itself.
The middle line stands firm, as a highway and the path
Of knowledge, to go toward God, the highest good:
And if anyone still doubts; the great wise Heathen 
<term>Plutarch</term><note>A Greek philosopher and historian (c. 46 – 119 AD) whose moral essays were highly influential in the Renaissance and Enlightenment for their ethical clarity.</note> wishes to guide him along the middle line:
Although the moral pen of that illustrious man 
Cannot excuse itself from Heathen superstition.
Nature enlightened the eyes of all kinds of nations,
Yet from a sky not free from mist and dark clouds
Of misunderstanding, which gives many things a different appearance
And color than they are in their nature and essence.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the corruption of religion, moving from the theoretical "middle path" of the previous page to the psychological and historical reasons why humans turn away from true, rational worship toward self-interest and idolatry.</meta>

<header>REFLECTIONS.</header>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<margin>The decay of natural and rational religion.</margin>
It [religion] took its origin from everyone’s inclination, desire, and wish,
Which varies between individuals, yet is inherent to man,
And is as diverse as the "good" toward which body and soul lean.
Each of these two aims for its own benefit. Five senses set
Their pleasure on the body’s delight, enjoyed through sight,
Touch, hearing, taste, and smell. This is easy
And attractive to the body, such as wealth, honor, and status,
Comfort, and pleasures: but reason teaches us to hate these
If such apparent goods hinder man’s spirit and soul:
Yet the good of the body, so alluring by its nature
To the flesh—while its opposite is abhorrent to the senses—
Is powerful enough to win the field in that struggle of choice.
Everyone yields to what is readiest, and shuns sorrow and pain:
Thus the world ties men fast to its tether.
The wise Plato saw everyone running toward profit,
And found it advisable to bind virtue to profit,
So that the greatest multitude would give their heart to virtue.
As soon as religion degenerated into this <note>The author suggests that when religion is practiced only for personal gain, it loses its "natural" and "rational" purity.</note>,
Though the name of "chief virtue" continued to cling to it,
It was more easily followed for the sake of advantage, with longing
By all those who looked for comfort, gain, and high offices:
And even today, this "sinew" <note>original: *zenuw*. Used here metaphorically to mean the driving force or strength behind an action.</note> still strengthens
The coarsest errors and most extreme misunderstandings,
Maintained and crowned in unenlightened lands:
And where the truth shines, many honor the enjoyment
Of gain and office more than God’s altar, and God;
As God’s altar and God take a backseat to the conquering
Of kingdoms. So can the lust for power <term>bewitch</term><note>original: *betoveren*. Suggests a loss of rational control due to worldly ambition.</note> mankind!
Political ambition <note>original: *staetzucht*. Literally "state-greed," referring to a hunger for power and social standing.</note> knows neither God, nor blood, nor fatherland,
And sets fire even to God's temples and the sanctuary.

<margin>The origin of idolatry.</margin>
King <term>Ninus</term><note>A legendary king of Assyria, often credited in early modern texts with the founding of Nineveh and the invention of idolatry.</note>, who ruled ancient <term>Babylon</term><note>original: *Babilon*.</note>,
Commanded that everyone honor the image of his father,
Erected with state ceremony in the middle of the city, as a memorial.
This statue of <term>Belus</term><note>A Babylonian deity (Baal); the author here follows the historical tradition that gods were originally just deified kings.</note> is

<summary>This page explores how human sensory desires and political ambition corrupt rational religion, transforming it into a tool for personal gain and leading to the historical birth of idolatry through the deification of rulers like Belus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on how religion became corrupted by human self-interest and the pursuit of status. # THE FOURTH BOOK.

The city of refuge <note>original: *vryburgh*. A sanctuary or asylum for those seeking protection from the law.</note> for the people—anyone who knows they are guilty
Of some atrocious deed, flees there, is mercifully
Received, and unburdened of their godless existence.
Thus the honor of the image grew, like a religion,
Among the crude and ignorant masses. Following such an example,
Many others step forward; and the hero, who has fought bravely
And shed his soul in blood for the fatherland,
Is, like a God, served and hallowed after his death.
This service, by laws, is strictly empowered and defended,
With the death penalty for whoever disturbs, slanders, or offends it.
Thus idolatry gradually won a wider field,
And undermined God's service through cunning and violence.
<margin>Hermes Trismegistus introduces religion, wisdom, and arts to Egypt, the school of the Greek sages:</margin>
Egypt, which takes its name from darkness <note>In 17th-century scholarship, the name Egypt was often associated with "darkness" or "hiddenness," or linked to the biblical Ham.</note>,
Had this fortune, when the people began to lack the day
And the radiance that naturally shone in the eastern lands,
That Hermes, a Hebrew, born in Chaldea <note>The author reflects a Renaissance tradition identifying Hermes Trismegistus with biblical figures like Enoch or contemporaries of Abraham.</note>,
Chose the Nile over the Euphrates to teach everyone God's service,
And to turn back the morals and the service of false gods.
What a man this great one was [clever in experience,
And who rightly received the name of Thrice-Greatest <note>original: *drymaelgrootsten*; the Dutch translation of the Greek 'Trismegistus'.</note>,]
Gifted with knowledge, through traditions
Handed down from hand to hand, and through contemplations
And fiery investigation, in the wilderness and the woods,
Of matters that nature does not entrust to the sluggish,
But to an elevated mind, intent only on living
For the benefit of others, and giving God His due!
Here he lives, as if in his own element.
He invented an art of letters <note>Writing or calligraphy.</note>, known to no one before,
And committed the teachings of morals to books,
For the instruction of the people. To the sharp-witted and the brave
He entrusted the treasure of wisdom, which, flowered
With signs, is rightly called flowered wisdom <note>This refers to hieroglyphics—symbolic writing that "flowers" with meaning beyond the literal.</note>;
Just as it stands forever carved upon needles <note>original: *naelden*; a common historical term for obelisks.</note>,
To be unfolded only by priest, prophet, and the divine-tongued <note>original: *godstolck*; one who interprets the divine will.</note>,
