As orders, consecrated to God’s service and the holy office.
This was the source and the light of wisdom of his time,
From whom the *Egyptians*, and their descendants,
Received mysteries, art, and science,
From which the *School of Athens* so early lit its torch,
And learned to see how much its knowledge still lacked.
*Homer*, *Pythagoras*, *Empedocles*, *Socrates*,
And *Aristotle*, and *Plato* himself left
*Athens*, *Pallas's citadel*, to learn within the *City of the Sun* <note>Heliopolis, a major religious center in ancient Egypt where philosophers were said to study.</note>
What the obelisk <note>original: *naelt* (needle). A 17th-century term for an obelisk, referring to the "Needle of Hermes."</note> holds and contains of all wisdom
In dark signs <note>hieroglyphics</note>, to reveal them to the *Greeks*,
And to claim *Hermes’* labor and fame for themselves.
<margin>but the abuse of his teaching multiplied the idols.</margin>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But the invaluable treasure of wisdom, raised
To heaven, and so dearly entrusted to the stone obelisk,
What use is it, when the light, entrusted to God’s interpreters,
Turns to darkness through abuse, which leads the nations
Away from *Hermes’* aim and intent, and away from God;
The interpreter lacks the key to the wise letter-lock <note>A metaphor for the complex, "locked" system of hieroglyphic symbols.</note>,
And misinterprets sign after sign, driven by the wicked,
Out of greed for profit and status? Then all the gods
And idols grew in number, for shameful gain.
The flower of wisdom, sucked dry by the spider,
Turned to poison, through perverted misinterpretation.
They worshipped the stars, and deities hidden under
Hides, and fur, and plumage, and scales. Cat, and dog,
The snake, and crocodile, and ox, and hawk stood
Incensed upon the altar. They sought the Gods in gardens,
And honored the field-fruit, and garlic, and onions <note>A reference to the Roman satirist Juvenal’s mockery of Egyptian religion, where he claimed they worshipped vegetables grown in their gardens.</note>.
The kingdom where *Europe* first sought the light of wisdom
Is cursed for its madness by *Heathendom*,
Like a wilderness, full of irrational savages,
Who differed greatly in their godhood from a modest people,
Who embraced wiser errors, with a glimpse
Of apparent reason, less subject to everyone’s mockery.

<meta>catchword: I</meta>

<meta>This page continues the critique of religious decay. Following the previous page's description of how the wisdom of Hermes was corrupted into Egyptian animal worship, the author now addresses the violent consequences of religious strife, the hypocrisy of state religion in Greece and Rome, and the failure of Epicurean philosophy to provide comfort at the hour of death.</meta>

# THE FOURTH BOOK.
X
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

I pass over the grudges and bloody quarrels
Of neighbors, differing in religion, which cannot be healed
Except at the murderous feast, where, robbed of understanding,
One scratches the eyes, in drink, out of his neighbor's head;
They set upon each other with stones, clubs, and sticks,
And arrows, and weapons drawn from their sheaths;
In the end eating the dead, quite raw and unroasted,
And quivering, before the soul has departed the corpse. <note>A reference to contemporary accounts of cannibalism and tribal warfare in the Americas or Africa, often used by 17th-century writers to illustrate the "state of nature" without true religion.</note>
Athens and Rome, though they consider these peoples as savages,
Are nonetheless not free from human sacrifices,
And lewd horrors, made known by ancient fame.
Thus religion wanders far from its original name,
As far as idolatry, through unholy offerings,
Spreads itself, like a world-flood, through every land.
This error is nourished to support the empire and state,
At least with the appearance and outward countenance,
By clever men who recognize no more than one Divinity,
Yet do not hesitate, for the utility of the state, to desecrate God
Through a countlessness of divinities, devoid of power,
Hatched by human brains, and brought to the altar. <note>The author argues that "enlightened" leaders of antiquity maintained polytheism only as a tool for social control, despite knowing better.</note>
An *Epicurus*, instead of gauging the ground of truth,
And sailing between the atheist and the idolater,
In the middle between the two, falls to the other side;
He denies the service of God, and strengthens atheism: <note>original: *ongodiſtery*. A 17th-century term for atheism or the denial of divine providence.</note>
Like a perverse physician, who comes to spoil the plague-house,
Serving poison to the sick man while he lies dying.

<margin>
Conclusion
that the 
atheists 
themselves 
confirm 
religion 
on their 
deathbeds.
</margin>
Though the evil conscience is slow to bow before the Divinity,
And remains stubborn for long, even when convicted,
Nonetheless, when death approaches the sickbed,
Gaping like a death’s-head with fleshless jaws,
And shins without skin, and touches the sick man
With his chilly hand, the pulse begins to throb,
The heart to tremble, the spirits to fail:
Then the death-sweat breaks out; the false comforters retreat.

<summary>The author critiques the violence of religious sectarianism and the hypocrisy of ancient states that used polytheism for social control, ultimately arguing that even the staunchest atheist is forced to recognize God when faced with the terrors of death.</summary>
<keywords>religious strife, idolatry, Athens, Rome, Epicurus, atheism, deathbed conversion, state religion</keywords>

# THE FOURTH BOOK.

<meta>catchword: De</meta>

I say nothing of the grudge and bloody quarrels
Of neighbors, differing in religion <note>original: "godtsdienſt"</note>, which cannot be healed
Except at a murderous feast where, robbed of reason,
Men scratch the eyes from their neighbor’s head, amidst drink,
Assaulting one another with stones, clubs, and staffs,
And arrows, and weapons drawn from their sheaths;
In the end eating the dead, even raw and unroasted,
And still quivering, before the soul has departed the corpse.
Athens and Rome, though they regard these others as savages,
Are nonetheless not free from human sacrifices,
And lewd cruelties, made known by ancient fame.
Thus religion wanders far from its original name,
Just as idolatry, through unholy offerings,
Spreads itself like a world-deluge through all lands.
The error is nurtured to support the empire and the state,
At least with the appearance and outward face,
By the shrewd who recognize no more than one Divinity,
Yet do not hesitate, for the benefit of the state, to desecrate God
Through a countless number of deities without power,
Hatched from the brain and brought to the altar.
An Epicurus <note>Epicurus (341–270 BCE) was a Greek philosopher whose emphasis on a material world often led later Christian writers to characterize him as the father of atheism or self-indulgence.</note>, instead of gauging the foundation of truth
And sailing between atheist <note>term: atheist (ongodift). Literally "one without God."</note> and idolater <note>term: idolater (afgodift). A worshiper of false gods.</note>,
In the middle between the two, falls to the other side;
He denies the service of God and strengthens atheism <note>original: "ongodiſtery"</note>:
Like a perverse physician who comes to ruin the plague-house,
And serves poison to the sick while they lie dying.

<margin>Conclusion that even atheists confirm religion on their deathbeds.</margin>
Though the guilty conscience bows slowly before the Divinity,
And remains stubborn for a long time, even when convicted,
Nonetheless, when death approaches the sickbed <note>original: "dootbedde"</note>,
Gaping like a death's head with fleshless jaws,
And shins without skin, and touches the sick man
With its chilly hand, the pulse begins to throb,
The heart to tremble, and the spirits to fail:
Then the cold sweat of death breaks out; the false comforters retreat.

### REFLECTIONS.

162

The stern judge steps to the tribunal <term>tribunal (vierſchaer). In the historical Netherlands, the "vierschaar" was a cordoned-off square where legal judgments were formally pronounced.</term> of the conscience,
Accusing the guilty for the wanton neglect of the good,
Or for cankered evil, and delivers, after weighing the evidence,
The verdict in the soul of the atheist <term>atheist (ongodiſt). Literally "one without God."</term>, who is troubled
In that oppressed state of shipwreck and danger.
He looks out for help, and for wreckage here and there,
To hide himself, if possible, in this extremity:
And he who spent his life slandering and provoking
The Divinity, takes up the ghost of religion out of fear
For his benefit, with faint hope, that he might thus
Escape God’s powerful day; he weeps for his recklessness,
And wishes for a little time to do penance for his wickedness.
If one in thousands could conceal that terror,
That is a perilous gamble. One can hide from the clear midday sun
In a corner, or in dark dens of murder,
And caves, and dungeons, sunk deep into the earth:
But no degenerate person conceals the pain of conscience so cunningly
That the face does not reveal the gnawing worm <note>original: "knaeghworm." A common 17th-century metaphor for the persistent, biting pain of a guilty conscience that "eats" at the heart from within.</note> within the heart.

# The Fifth Book.

163

X 2

<margin>*The transition from general religion to specific religion, grounded on the credibility of the holy church and the Bible.*</margin>
Religion, written in every heart as if by nature,
And first handed down from hand to hand and passed along,
Is firmly established, even proven by its very abuse
And by idolatry—sprouted from the thicket
Of unholy superstition that gave birth to the power of idols,
When the people degenerated from the simplicity of the true God,
And simultaneously from the service dedicated to Him by reason.
This Religion has played its part: now the time has finally come
That we also confirm that religion which, by unassailable scriptures,
Through revelations and divine inspirations <note>original: "goddelijcke driften." In the 17th century, "drift" referred to a powerful inner movement or impulse; here, it signifies the Holy Spirit's movement in the prophets.</note>,
Has been revealed to mankind, sealing it so firmly
That atheism <term>ongodistery (atheism). A 17th-century term for the rejection of God or the state of living without divine law.</term> barks against it in vain.
It behooves us then, standing in the portal, to examine carefully
What credibility confirms the truth of God’s books,
And God’s service, contained and held
In the pricelessness of this literary treasure,
Like a diamond, whose rays never fade,
Is set in a ring, or the artistry of golden claws <note>original: "goude klaeuwen." A jeweler's term for the prongs or "claws" that hold a gemstone securely in place. The poet compares the structure of the Church and Scripture to a gold setting for the diamond of faith.</note>.

<margin>*Moses’s credibility confirmed through miraculous deeds.*</margin>
The great Moses steps forward as God’s prophet,
Lawgiver, divine interpreter <term>godttolck. Literally "God-interpreter." Someone who translates the divine will into human language.</term>, pen of the holy decree
Of the Most High, leader and redeemer of the Hebrews,
And, between God and them, the mediator for many centuries

<meta>This page continues the transition from "Natural Religion" to "Revealed Religion." # REFLECTIONS.

Renowned everywhere for his power, leadership, and subjects,
Because at his prayer the waves stand still,
Heaven dews manna, and, after the plagues of Egypt,
God's enemies—destroyed by punishment and defeats—
Acknowledge that the power before which earth and sea tremble
Upholds him wherever he fights and takes the field.

<margin>*and his books, which point us toward the same aforementioned God.*</margin>
Five books, written by the pen of Moses through God’s Spirit,
Have remained entirely preserved for thirty centuries after his time,
Just like a right hand that points with five fingers <note>A metaphor for the Pentateuch (the first five books of the Bible), suggesting they function as a divine signpost.</note>
Toward God the Creator, whom he praises with songs.
Had Moses' pen pointed man toward a God
Whose name is not read on the forehead of Nature,
Whose attributes do not resemble the nature
Of Divinity, as revealed to us through the light of reason;
Then rational man might rightly reject Moses' pen
As useless, false, and overcome
By lies: but now the leader of the Hebrews agrees,
In the honoring of that God, with reason itself.
One can then, clearly convinced, believe by reason
What he testifies of God, who called this hero from above
Into His service, as one who would faithfully
Fulfill the office laid upon him.

<margin>*Moses' books are confirmed by all foreign nations; his sincerity is also evident in the unfolding of true histories.*</margin>
So many illustrious and ancient foreign pens,
Neighbors of the Hebrews, as well as Greeks and Romans, recognize
The name of Amram’s son <note>Amram was the father of Moses. The author uses this patronymic to emphasize Moses' historical humanity.</note>, the subject matter he relates,
And all the circumstances so clearly set down in his books,
From the world’s origin onwards; the lives of the patriarchs,
Received through tradition, recorded in his pages
From their own mouths, mentioning both names and places,
And also without him concealing anyone’s transgressions,
Or flattering or sparing himself. So many dissenting voices,
Ancestral enemies of the Jews, slanderers, and those who are blind,
Confirm Moses' work; or if their knowledge fails,
And they are divided in attempting to hit upon the circumstances,

Recounted by Moses' pen, is testified to by many mouths,
Linked like a chain, firmly bound by its links,

<margin>The chronology of the Chinese, now well known, confirms Moses' chronicle starting from the world-flood, and rejects the imaginary years of the Chaldeans and Egyptians, with which the godless generally mock the truth of the Bible.</margin>
Handed down, century in and century out, from one hand to the next,
And spread throughout from one land to another.
No hundreds of thousands of uncounted years,
Conjured up by either the Chaldeans or the Egyptians,
Can shame the pages of Moses; because the discord
Among those false calculators silences and refutes them,
And their vast numbers carry very little weight,
Since even the Chinese agree with God's chronicles,
And begin their counting from the world-flood:
Just as one will find nowhere else in the world,
Among all of Heathendom, such indubitable signs,
That the world-flood must yield to in antiquity. <note>The author argues that no other historical record is more ancient or reliable than the account of the Flood.</note>

<margin>Aristotle’s lack of proof regarding the uncertainty of Moses, whose deeds are confirmed everywhere by his ancestral enemies.</margin>
That Aristotle now offers little praise to Moses,
As one who relates and says much, but proves nothing,
Is a bold claim from him, and least of all proven:
For Moses’ deeds can be read throughout the world,
And are verified as certain even by his ancestral enemies;
A legacy of proof as great and clear as can be
Found anywhere, and known to his famous Greeks,
Who sniffed out everything that could be scented to the East,
Appropriating for themselves all they borrowed from the Hebrews,
Phoenicians, Arabs, Egyptians, and Chaldeans,
After which Athens dared to boast with this title:
That the fountain of art and wisdom had been granted to her. <note>The author suggests that Greek philosophy and wisdom were actually "stolen" or borrowed from older Middle Eastern cultures, including the Hebrews.</note>

<margin>The steadfastness</margin>
The six pairs of tribes, <note>The twelve tribes of Israel.</note> so steadfast in their law
And religion, passed down from child to grandchild;
Guardians of the history contained in Moses' pages;
Many hundreds of thousands, witnesses to his deeds
And wonders, perceived in every element;
So many prophecies, fulfilled in the end,

<margin>
The Hebrews, in defending their Law, defend Moses.

Proof that he was sent by heaven’s power,
And led no one astray through human inventions,
Unlike Lycurgus of old, and Numa, and Sertorius, <note>Lycurgus (Sparta), Numa Pompilius (Rome), and Sertorius (a Roman general) were historical figures often accused by critics of pretending to have divine inspiration to better control the state.</note>

That Moses did not strive for status out of a lust for power,
Nor attacked any kingdom or kings for this reason,
Was shown by an omen, when, as if choked with grief,
He threw the crown placed upon him to the earth so young, <note>This refers to a popular Jewish tradition (Midrash) where the infant Moses takes the crown from Pharaoh's head and casts it down, foreshadowing his future role.</note>
And later despised the opulent court in favor of exile,
Until God appeared to him, to plague Pharaoh’s
Disgrace, and to relieve God’s people of the yoke.
What has he not endured! What has he not suffered with distress,
For the salvation of Jacob’s house, in heathen lands and wildernesses!

The royal crown could not shine upon Levi, <note>The tribe of Levi, to which Moses belonged, was dedicated to religious service, while the "royal" line was promised to the tribe of Judah.</note>

Gershom, Moses’ own blood, might not consecrate, <note>Moses did not seek to make his own son, Gershom, the High Priest, showing he was not motivated by nepotism.</note> but this office

What virtues were not seen blazing in Moses' countenance:
Justice, and loyalty, gentleness, patience,

Manliness, mercy, and oversight of the poor,
Prudence, and diligence to protect everyone,
Devotion to God, and zeal for God's law,
Sanctified, night and day, through offerings and prayer.

The man’s credibility, in reporting God’s dealings
With the first patriarchs, is strengthened by his pious conduct
And holiness, which, from childhood onward,
Followed him from the womb of his nurse to the grave,
And shine after his death on Tabor beside Elijah, <note>A reference to the Transfiguration of Jesus on Mount Tabor, where Moses and Elijah appeared beside Him (Matthew 17).</note>
Where the light of the Divinity shines in the face of the Messiah,
Raised from among Moses’ brothers, and like unto him, <note>Referring to the prophecy in Deuteronomy 18:15 that God would raise up a prophet "like unto Moses."</note>
As the reality that perfectly completes the sketch and the model,
And so many shadows, given in underpainting, <note>The original Dutch *gedootverft* refers to the initial layer of a painting. The author suggests that the Old Testament stories are a "sketch" that Christ "colors in" or completes.</note>
To live immortally hereafter, clearly illuminated.

As one, from Judah's tribe, and God’s and David’s son,

<meta>This page continues the defense of the Christian faith's historical reliability. While the previous page established the credibility of the Old Testament prophets and the translation of the Septuagint, this page shifts focus to the New Testament. It argues that the life of Christ and the writings of the Apostles are even more firmly attested than the ancient Hebrew traditions, citing both the testimony of early Church Fathers and the unintentional confirmation provided by the critiques of the Roman Emperor Julian the Apostate.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

While he [Christ] came here below to please the Father,
His life and conduct—expressed in books and in letters,
Along with the rise of the kingdom in which he is king—
Do not yield to the legacy of Moses,
Handed down from person to person, nor in the miracles
Confirmed in God's churches throughout the wide world:
And if an ancient Hebrew <note>Refers to the Jewish tradition of the prophet Elijah.</note> believes in Elijah’s <note>original: "Helias"</note> ascension to heaven,
Revealed only from the mouth of Elisha, <note>original: "Helizeüs"</note>
Then with greater right one believes the eleven witnesses <note>The eleven remaining Apostles who witnessed the Ascension after the death of Judas.</note>
Who saw Christ travel to heaven through the clouds.

<margin>Emperor Julian, an apostate and hereditary enemy of Christ, strengthens the truth of the Old and New Testaments.</margin>
The Emperor Julian, <note>Julian the Apostate (reigned 361–363 AD), the last non-Christian ruler of the Roman Empire, who attempted to revive traditional Roman religion.</note> arch-enemy of the cross,
And of Christ’s honor and praise, however closely he sifted and searched
To bolster his slanders against the scriptures to mock God,
He confirms everything that God’s chroniclers of mysteries write,
And the messengers of Christ, and all that Roman fame attributes
To the names of Peter, Paul, Mark, and Saint Matthew;
What Luke and Saint John seal in their writings,
Though so fearfully stormed by conflicting passions.

<margin>The first Church Fathers and Councils confirm the truth of God’s books.</margin>
The Church Fathers in their time, neighboring the century of Christ:
Justin the Martyr, Irenaeus, and Clement;
After them Tertullian, who knew how to give account
Of Paul’s own hand, <note>Referring to the original manuscripts or "autographs" of the Pauline epistles, which were said to still be preserved in certain churches during Tertullian's time (c. 155–220 AD).</note> recognized and preserved;
Origen next to him, they confirm the pages
Of the other Apostles, as they are recorded,
And were later sealed by full church councils,
Carefully testing the unblemished pages;
And where one found of old the most certain record
Of the scriptures, spread throughout all cities and churches.

<margin>The sincere conduct of the Apostles and Christ’s disciples confirms the same.</margin>
The master was beloved and known by his students
Through personal interaction, just as he lives in their papers.
They were countrymen, and circumcised, of one race,
And followed in his footsteps, seeking neither dominion, nor power,
Nor wealth; poor, innocent, and forsaken, they followed
The leader, in the sight of the schools <note>Referring to the religious academies of the Pharisees and Sadducees.</note> that hated him,

Y And

And they slandered everywhere with a terrible noise,
Until he, like a lamb, hung sacrificed on the cross.
What reason was there to deceive the nations,
And, without hope of reward, to lie willfully?
To suffer that cursed name without necessity,
To die, without fruit, a shameful death.
One can choose death over life out of delusion and passion:
But who invents a deed just to lose their life?

<margin>Luke was Paul’s, and Mark was Peter’s traveling companion, which strengthens their testimony.</margin>
Saint Luke, traveling companion of Paul, and Saint Mark
Of Cephas, <note>original: "Cefas"—the Aramaic name for the Apostle Peter, meaning "Rock."</note> [convinced by many miracles
Of Jesus, drawn from their own mouths,] describe the life
Of which the living give testimony
By the hundreds; and those who saw him clearly,
Hanging on the cross, and gloriously after his death
Risen from the grave, assure that their quills
Brought no fable nor dream into the light;
Just as their pages also, perfected in simplicity,
Do not strive for the flowery style that usually embellishes a story.

<margin>If one believes worldly writers, why not holy ones?</margin>
If one trusts in long-worn books,
Written in the style of the worldly-wise and clever,
In which Alexander’s <note>Alexander the Great.</note> campaign and death are mentioned,
Or Caesar with his army challenges Pompey in the field;
Whether written by the hand of Curtius <note>Quintus Curtius Rufus, a Roman historian who wrote a biography of Alexander the Great.</note> or Caesar,
Or younger pens that support the ancient truth;
If one believes Livy <note>Titus Livius, a Roman historian known for his monumental history of Rome.</note> even today,
Who records the rise of the City <note>Rome.</note> and things he never saw
In his books; shall we still doubt the words of those
Who report what they themselves or those closest to them heard,
Or saw with their own eyes, and who lived in that time,
Which allows for no ignorance regarding this main matter?

<margin>Saint Peter and Paul confirmed the truth with their blood.</margin>
The man from Tarsus, <note>The Apostle Paul, originally known as Saul of Tarsus.</note> a student of the honor of the Rabbis,
Namely Gamaliel, <note>A celebrated doctor of Jewish Law and a member of the Sanhedrin.</note> and who, like a wolf in Palestine,
Snapped at Christ’s sheepfold, and—full of heat—

Regardless of the fact he was born from the tribe of Benjamin,
Embraces the Prince of the Cross, who rose from the grave;
Heeds neither misfortunes, nor sorrow, curse, nor fear;
Trumpets among the Heathendom, and among his own circumcised brethren,
The Savior, who appeared alive to him in the air,

In Rome, on a single day, when Cephas <note>original: "Cefas"—the Apostle Peter.</note>, longing 
For heaven’s crown, was hung on the cross by his legs;
Just as the bodies, consecrated at the Roman altar,
Confirm their death after sixteen hundred years.

<margin>Saint Paul alone convinces all who contradict.</margin>
Would Paul, for a dream and fable, abandon honor and status,
The law ingrained in him from childhood,
Recklessly degenerate from his ancient lineage,
And expose himself to the mockery of the people, sprung
From Abraham, who had circumcised him on the eighth day?
Would he—in exile, persecuted in every city,
Scourged, and fettered, stoned, and hated,
Pushed into poverty and cold, on road and street,
Bound over the sea, in shipwreck on the waves,
In riot, and in distress, hemmed in by dangers,
Like a spectacle, stand on display in the crowd of people,
As one who fruitlessly hopes in Jesus, hung
On the cross, and spat upon, and condemned by the law,
Crucified, and stuffed dead into the grave, but never risen?
Certainly that does not accord with his wisdom,
As one sees it shining in his pages and letters.

<margin>The silencing of the oracles, confirmed by Plutarch, Juvenal, Cicero, Strabo, and Celsus, bears witness to this truth.</margin>

In wisdom, diligently searches for the cause of the silencing
Of the ancient oracles, just as his contemporary,

To journey into hell. Go hence, and bridle your mouth."
At such an answer, Augustus had an altar built
On the Capitoline, wherein this title was carved:
*The altar dedicated to the first-born God.* <note>original: "Ara Primogeniti Dei". A medieval legend claimed Augustus had a vision of the Virgin and Child and built an altar at the site of the current Santa Maria in Aracoeli.</note>
Plutarch bridles the mouth of the abyss with a seal,
Where Epitherses, who unfolded the art of letters to him,
Born in his city, trusted himself to the water,
And with all the ship’s crew heard, while sailing across the lake,
Past the island, a voice, in the time of Tiberius:
"Go, report how great Pan is now dead and has passed away." <note>A famous story from Plutarch's *The Obsolescence of Oracles*. Christians interpreted the "death of Pan" as the moment the pagan world fell before Christ.</note>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the silencing of pagan oracles serves as historical evidence for the divinity of Christ. # R E F L E C T I O N S.

As if the oracle-power of the abyss, now in distress,
And all its sorcery lay buried and dead:
Even Diocletian <note>Roman Emperor (reigned 284–305 AD) known for the last great persecution of Christians; he reportedly received an oracle stating the "righteous on earth" prevented Apollo from speaking.</note> hears Apollo himself declare
That now the righteous would silence his lips,
By which his priesthood understood Christianity:
The slanderer Celsus <note>An 2nd-century Greek philosopher and opponent of Early Christianity; though a critic, his writings inadvertently confirm certain historical details of the faith.</note>, firmly grounded in Epicurus,
Also confesses this muteness, as strange as it is unusual,
At Claros, Delphi, and in the widely famed Dodona <note>Major sites of ancient Greek oracles.</note>;
A mark of Christ's coming, power, and authority
Over the idols; a decline foretold to us long ago.

<margin>Other proofs for the credibility of the Bible.</margin>
To now briefly establish the value of God’s book:
The uniformity of style, which nowhere degenerates,
Nor wavers, nor yields in truth and its treasure,
But proceeds in one tone, from the first to the last page,
Deserves belief, since it is sealed with miracles,
And the blood of the saints, and governs countless nations;
It even keeps the degenerate in discipline and restraint,
And fears neither war, flood, nor hostilities,
But firmly outlasts the ages, and does not cease to permeate,
Handed down from ancestors to their descendants.
The church chronicle, a series of ancient fathers, church councils,
Gathered from East and West, see the light of the heavenly pages,
In Christ’s doctrine of the cross <note>The central Christian teaching regarding the crucifixion and redemption.</note>, as a light for all the blind,
Shining as far as human traces can be found:
And if no miracle confirms this holy book, which honors God,
Where it appears and converts worlds,
Then this miracle <note>The author argues that the conversion of the world to Christianity is itself a miracle so great it would eclipse all others if the biblical miracles were doubted.</note> shall eclipse all wonders,
Revealed to separate the light from the darkness.
This holy book discovers the origin of the universe,
The origin of evil, and man’s sad fall,
The origin of the good, as great as the heart can wish;
It exalts God alone at the peak, humbles all men,
Unfolds the soul’s disease, provides us medicine <note>The author uses a common theological metaphor: scripture acts as a spiritual remedy for the "sickness" of sin.</note>,
Rewards golden virtue, and punishes villainy,

<meta>Catchword: With</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the argument regarding the silencing of pagan oracles as proof of Christ’s power and begins a defense of the Bible based on its internal consistency, historical endurance, and moral impact. The author highlights the Bible’s unique ability to explain the origins of the world and human nature while remaining unchanged through centuries of conflict.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the defense of the Holy Scriptures following the discussion of the silence of pagan oracles. It moves from historical proofs to the internal spiritual authority of the Bible, asserting that divine revelation surpasses human reason and provides a definitive moral and spiritual guide.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

To imprint upon hearts with faithfulness, love, and awe,
Through holy promises, curses, and fierce threats,
Followed by blessing and disaster, seen in good and evil,
Which stand here as a useful mirror for the living.
It does not conflict, but agrees in the highest degree with reason
That everyone should submit themselves to credible truths,
Which are undeniable, like the pages of the prophets,
And the book of Moses. Even if a dead man were to rise <note>A reference to the parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus (Luke 16:31): "If they do not listen to Moses and the Prophets, they will not be convinced even if someone rises from the dead."</note>
To reveal to us in what state condemned souls,
When the body lies buried in the grave, have fallen;
That credibility would be less worthy of belief
Than the pen of Moses, and what God's Spirit reveals to us
In the pages of the prophets. Thus faith precedes the trials
Of the restless mind <note>The original Dutch *losse brein* implies an unstable or overly speculative intellect.</note>, which puts the truth on the rack <note>To "put on the rack" (original: *op schroeven*) means to distort or subject the truth to intense, skeptical scrutiny.</note>.
The mouth of truth, too clever for all the conceited brains,
Sends and points us toward God's never-failing book.

<margin>The Bible, handed down to us by the church and even its enemies, then confirms the Godhead and religion through manifold revelations, incomparably clearer and more vivid than the light of reason without the light of the scriptures.</margin>
The Bible then stands firm, in defiance of all the ruses
Of the abyss, which continually misuses the tongue
Of the ungodly in slandering religion, and the grandeur
Of the holy book of God, which, through the trial—like gold in the fire—
Is glorified in the face of hellish rages,
Which, vainer than cobwebs, do not overcome any truth.
One may freely open this book, that priceless treasure,
As if descended from God’s throne; the first and last page
Begins and ends with God, just as God’s pages throughout
Preserve that worshipped name in its power.
God’s essence, attribute, and work, previously by us
Contemplated in the light that shone from reason
And radiated, and also the service offered to the Godhead,
Begin to dawn even more beautifully from this book,
Where God reveals Himself more and more to the fathers,
And finally declares His will most richly
Through His anointed Son, sent from the Father’s bosom,
Who has bound all nations together through one service.

<summary>This page asserts the supreme authority of the Bible over human reason and intellectual skepticism, arguing that scripture is a more reliable witness to the divine than even a message from the dead. It frames the Bible as a "priceless treasure" that reveals God's nature more perfectly than philosophy, culminating in the mission of Christ.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bible's authority, transitioning from the general credibility of the scriptures to a historical overview of the three distinct eras of religious practice revealed within them: the natural, the legal, and the Christian.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

<margin>
Biblical religion is threefold: natural, legal, and Christian.
</margin>
Three eras point us to three kinds of worship,
Made known through revelation in pure pages:
First Adam’s duty, then the law of God written
In stone tablets and given to Amram’s son <note>Amram was the father of Moses (Exodus 6:20).</note>.
The third is the service of Christ, who Moses’ old law
Did not so much abolish as improve and transform,
And imprints His grace in the heart, circumcised by God
From its own desires and fleshly ways:
A religion without equal, bound to no time,
Nor place, nor tribal house whose genealogy decays.

<margin>
Natural religion begins in the Bible with obedience and sacrifice.
</margin>
Obedience, the act of subjecting oneself to the Creator,
Was the first lesson that God wished to instill in His creature,
When in paradise, through the threat of death,
He strictly forbade the holy pair from the tree of knowledge;
Who, enticed by the fruit and the serpent, possessed by a spirit,
Plucked the apple and ate, too blind and ungodly,
And were driven out of Eden by the Cherubim,
Where humanity still laments its fall eternally.
Obedience—when the cattle later burned upon the altar—
Was also estimated by Samuel to be above sacrifice <note>A reference to 1 Samuel 15:22: "To obey is better than sacrifice."</note>:
For sacrificing alienates God’s heart from us
If it does not take its origin from this chief virtue.
And sacrifice, as something inspired by the Godhead,
Begins in Adam’s house, almost with the life of man,
Where Abel’s sacrificial flame rises high into the air,
And Cain on the altar his sheaf and fruit of the field
Ignites—which displeases God, for which the offerer was troubled,
And fiercely and cruelly murdered his brother in the field;
Whose holy offering sprang from a sincere soul,
Which is why the sacrifice pleased the purest eye,
Just like a fore-shadowing of the sacrifice to be expected
For the atonement of Adam’s stain <note>A reference to original sin.</note> and all generations.

<margin>
Enoch restores the...
</margin>
Religion, in its state and essence of nature,
Cracked by Adam’s fall, seemed weak in its duration,

<meta>This page continues the historical overview of "Natural Religion," moving from the earliest patriarchs to the renewal of faith after the Great Flood and the calling of Abraham.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

<margin>A weakened religion, renewed in *Noah’s* sacrifice after the worldwide flood,</margin>
And it lay neglected by many, as if collapsed,
Until in *Enoch* the light of *God* was ignited,
And the name of the Most High, which shone through the mists,
Was rightly honored, celebrated, and adored by the pious,
Whereunder *Enoch* ascended to the heights, taken up <note>A reference to Genesis 5:24, where Enoch is said to have been taken by God without dying.</note>
For his godliness, the mirror of all the faithful.
After him, the weeds <note>A metaphor for sin and ungodly people.</note> took the upper hand on earth,
Wherefore the worldwide flood—which knows neither dunes nor shore—
Buried the human race in fathomless waves,
Which spared *Noah’s* house in the great vessel <note>The Ark.</note>,
And all kinds of livestock, sheltered by the wooden roof,
Until the sea receded and he kindled the sacrifice,
Whose fragrance rose to heaven; [God], moved by that scent,
Promised, by the sign of His rainbows,
Never again to plunge mankind into a sea and bottomless flood
Because of bold defiance;
A clear and firm proof of how sacrifices please *God*,
As soothers of His rod <note>In this context, the "rod" refers to divine punishment or discipline.</note> and pleaders against His plagues.

<margin>and increased by *God* with the seventh commandment, above the six.</margin>
The six commandments of *God*, confirmed by the antiquity of the *Hebrews*,
Common to *Adam’s* seed and *Noah’s* blood,
Increased by the seventh commandment, with the terrors
Against eating animals that suffocate in their own blood <note>The author is referencing the "Noahide Laws." According to tradition, six laws were given to Adam, and a seventh—the prohibition of eating flesh with its lifeblood—was given to Noah (Genesis 9:4).</note>,
Are signs of *God’s* service, introduced into the world
Through an obedience that touches nothing forbidden.

<margin>*Abraham’s* departure and godliness.</margin>
The *Patriarch Abraham*, upon the heavenly revelation,
Left his fatherland and idolatrous altars,
And wandered in exile to the kingdom of *Canaan*;
How the zeal of that most fruitful man blazed!
He built up altars to the honor of the true *God*,
Honored the priesthood with tithes, strengthened with teaching
And example his household, was peaceful toward *Lot*,
Also hospitable, and received three angels from *God*,
Interceded for the guilty and those who fiercely envied him,
And took care, at *God’s* command, to circumcise every male.

<meta>This page continues the chronological exploration of "Natural Religion" through the lives of the biblical Patriarchs, specifically focusing on the virtues of Melchizedek, Isaac, Jacob, and Joseph as precursors to later religious traditions.</meta>

### REFLECTIONS.

<margin>Melchizedek's grain offering and drink offering, the archetype of Christ's eternal and bloodless sacrifice.</margin>
Melchizedek, ordained in the highest priesthood,
And Prince of Salem, met the hero <note>Abraham, returning from the rescue of Lot.</note>, who again
Was journeying home from the battle, for the comfort of Lot, struck
By kings, [now] scattered in their defeats.
The priest seemed to descend as if from a cloud.
He came with wheat and wine to refresh the weary people
Of father **Abraham**, and blessed them all together,
As befits God’s sacrificer, who allowed the ages to perceive
What priest later on, so hospitable and faithful,
Would follow far in the footsteps of Melchizedek,
And offer wheat and wine for the sustenance of souls,
Who kneel before a nobler drink and food. <note>In Christian tradition, Melchizedek’s offering of bread and wine is seen as a "type" or foreshadowing of the Eucharist.</note>

<margin>Abraham's obedience in the sacrifice of Isaac, resembling his father in this regard.</margin>
The Patriarch **Isaac**, laid like a lamb upon the wood
As a sacrifice, gives a sign of his godliness,
Through obedience to God and to his father,
In the offering of himself. From this godly vein
He betook himself to the field at evening in prayer,
Where his bride, sent to him by God, appeared to him. <note>Isaac's meeting with Rebekah as described in Genesis 24.</note>

<margin>Jacob’s godliness.</margin>
The Patriarch **Jacob** fled to shun his brother’s grudge;
He avoided, on his father's counsel, marrying
An idolater in Canaan. He built, before and after,
Altars upon the mountain, and also at Beersheba,
And Bethel, and buried, as a sign of religious devotion,
Jewelry, and ear-ornaments, and idols under oaks. <note>Referring to Jacob’s purification of his household in Genesis 35:4.</note>
He set up markers of stone, consecrated to God,
Paying the godly promise and tithes in his time.

<margin>Joseph’s chastity.</margin>
How dearly **Joseph**, driven by godliness,
Valued his purity, even above status and life,
When *Potiphar’s* spouse, charming with her tongue
And beauty, vied for the flower of his years,
The outcome tells—worthy of being played on holy stages
As a mirror for youth, to play that chaste role,
So that one, along this path, strives for honor and chastity,
And would rather surrender his cloak than his body and soul. <note>Joseph famously left his garment behind when fleeing the advances of Potiphar's wife to maintain his integrity.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the virtues of the Patriarchs—Melchizedek’s priesthood, Isaac’s obedience, Jacob’s devotion, and Joseph’s chastity—presenting them as historical models of "Natural Religion" and moral conduct.</summary>

<margin>His love toward the brothers, and godliness in the burial of his father.</margin>
But the hero’s <note>Referring to Joseph, son of Jacob.</note> godliness seems to run no lower
In the brotherly reception, and the honoring and burial
Of his father, whose corpse he lamented with due ceremony,
Taken so far into Canaan, to the bones of Abraham,
And the ashes of his sons, sunk into the dark grave
At Hebron, carved out of pure rock with great skill.

<margin>The religion of nature gives way to the law.</margin>
Religion, in the state of simple nature,
By the strength of its law, in essence and in duration,
Among households for more than twenty-four centuries,
Saw a service, sworn to all the tribes of the Hebrews,
Approaching, and hoped for the hour and the time
That Jacob <note>Here referring to the nation of Israel as a whole.</note>, consecrated by a law from above,
Would know the name of the Unseen One <note>God</note> even more closely,
And obey and serve Him with thousands of others.

<margin>Moses chosen by God as the redeemer and mediator of the twelve tribes.</margin>
Heaven had previously through its providence
Laid this heavy yoke upon the neck of **Moses**,
A descendant of Levi’s tribe, learned in the sciences,
Gifted with bravery, having fled into exile,
Stern in avenging the wrongs done to God’s people,
A refuge for the oppressed, a protector, and a spokesman,
Bound to the blood of the priest **Jethro** through lawful marriage,
And a keeper of his flock. When he, upon the parched grounds
In **Midian**, near God’s mountain, which is called **Horeb**,
Watched and observed his flocks, scattered everywhere
Both far and wide, he saw a bush set ablaze,
In the appearance of a bright fire, and heard a Deity speak,
In the name of the Most High, and honored barefoot
The holiness of the place, respectfully and full of humility.
Here God announced His name, and chose him from the bush
To be the savior, that he might deliver the people from slavery,
And sealed his commission through power and miracles.
Thus he attacked the court and kingdom of <term>Memphis|The ancient capital of Egypt, representing the power of the Pharaohs.</term> with plagues,
And finally celebrated the feast of the <term>Passover lamb|The sacrificial lamb whose blood protected the Israelites during the final plague of Egypt.</term>, striking the posts
On the sides, above and below, with the lamb’s blood, for the delivered.

When he lay with all his host around **Sinai's** mountaintop,
And had sanctified the people, God burst forth on the third day
With storm and lightning, and thundered from the clouds,
And smoke, and vapor, and flame, in the ears and eyes of the nations.
The entire mountain of God trembles; the whole camp shakes and starts
At thunderclap upon thunderclap, as fire flashes in their eyes,
The heavenly trumpet mingles its sounds among
The crashing heavy cannon <note>original: "kortou" — a type of short, heavy-caliber cannon; the author compares the supernatural thunder to the loudest artillery of the period.</note> and the lightning of God’s thunder;
As **Moses** stood on the mountain at **Aaron’s** side,
And caught with his ears the oracle from God’s mouth,
Which sought to instill in them the service of one God;
To submit themselves to no godless image of idols;
To learn to avoid the misuse of God’s name out of awe;
To dedicate one day to God every seven days;
In no way to tarnish or diminish the honor of parents;
To shed no human blood out of anger or mad revenge;
Not to violate the lawful marriage bed through lust;
In no way to stealthily pursue anyone’s property or goods;
Not to falsely decorate <note>To "decorate" here means to present or embellish a lie to make it look like the truth.</note> with the appearance of truth;

The laws of **Carthage** and **Rome** have long since perished,

<meta>This page transitions from the physical event of the Revelation at Sinai to a theological reflection on the endurance and complexity of the Mosaic Law. It continues the themes from the previous page, contrasting the "pure" divine law given to Israel with the fleeting, bloody laws of pagan civilizations.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

Z 3

<meta>catchword: Al-</meta>

Although Jerusalem changed masters,
And Aaron saw his seat and temple overturned,
Chained, and transported, and finally released;
The law of Moses stood, as firm as a pillar,
For full thirty centuries, and follows the sun and clouds,
Among Christian, Jew, and Turk, like a law of nations,
And a torch of nature, and gives testimony
That it was given by God through the hand of Moses,
And first to Jacob’s blood, his inheritance and firstborn,
To whom this charter <note>original: "hantvest" — a legal document or deed of rights, used here to describe the Covenant.</note> was destined from eternity,
Above all the uncircumcised, so that one people everywhere
Honored one Godhead, one law, altar, and priesthood;
While idolatry and godless sacrifices,
Like a hellish plague, infected all lands,
Where blind Heathendom, almost from city to city,
Split into factions, each had its own idol.
Although the sun shines on everyone, although the sky rains,
And dews from land to land, no nations are blessed
By God, like Israel, with such a pure law,
Compared to which the best [human] law is tainted
With error, or written with the ink of human blood,
And, after the lawgiver’s death, did not remain long in use:
For what springs from men degenerates and rots;
What God empowers, lasts for eternity, like God.

<margin>The burden of the yoke of the ceremonial law, which points toward Christ, its ultimate purpose.</margin>
This law, thundered down from the peak of Sinai's summit,
And since expanded, contains within itself six hundred
Commandments and prohibitions, with one and three and ten <note>This refers to the "613 Mitzvot" or commandments traditionally identified in the Torah.</note>,
Divided into three kinds; those regarding the Godhead,
And religion, the judiciary, and the style of morals;
An unbearable yoke, as long as the flesh 
Overpowers and weakens reason, and supreme grace,
Promised through this law, does not come to man’s aid,
Nor reaches out a hand to the infirm, who wait with longing,
And look out for the hero <note>The "hero" refers to the promised Messiah who would fulfill the Law.</note> promised to Abraham's generations.

The heavenly trumpet mingled its sounds with the cracking heavy cannons <note>original: "kortou" — a large-caliber gun or cannon; the poet uses this anachronism to help his 17th-century readers imagine the deafening power of God's thunder.</note> and the lightning of God’s thunder; as Moses stood on the mountain at Aaron’s side and caught with his ears the oracle from God’s mouth. Although Jerusalem changed masters,
And Aaron <note>The first High Priest of Israel and brother of Moses; here his "throne" represents the priesthood.</note> saw his seat and temple overturned,
Chained, and carried away, and finally set free;
The law of Moses stood as firm as a pillar,
For full thirty centuries long, and follows the sun and clouds,

Sworn, promised, foretold, and depicted—
This is where the Law's service points; while she heals no one,
Nor can she heal in times of need, but by showing the wounds,
She calls for the Messiah <note>The "Anointed One," whom the poet identifies as Christ, the only one capable of healing the "wounds" of sin revealed by the Law.</note>, who, sent from God’s bosom,
Shall comfort the sickbed with the balsam of grace,
And rescue the wounded from distress and misfortune.

<margin>The Law teaches of one single God and no idols; its services point to Christ and the grace to be obtained through Him.</margin>
She teaches us to honor one single God and Divine Being,
And only by His name to swear the truth in times of need;
To follow Him on His path and hear His thundering voice,
Tolerating no idols nor their followers alongside Him,
But destroying all idolatrous blood within His domain,
To plant Heaven’s will from the parent stem into the tender shoots <note>Refers to the religious education of children, passing the faith from "stem" to "telgen" (offspring).</note>;
The Tabernacle <note>original: "d'Orakeltent" — literally "The Oracle-tent," the portable dwelling place of God's presence.</note>, wherein the Godhead shines and dwells,
Was pitched according to the vision shown on the summit of Mount Horeb <note>Another name for Mount Sinai, where Moses received the instructions for the Tabernacle.</note>,
Divided into the outer courtyard, the Holy Place, and the Holy of Holies,
Safe for the people, the priests, and the feet of Aaron <note>Aaron was the first High Priest; "safe for his sole" implies he was the only one permitted in the innermost sanctuary.</note>,
But forbidden to the uncircumcised. The veil strictly separated
The Most Holy choir from the Holy chamber in the center,
Which was furnished with the altar of incense, the table of showbread,
And the lights of the golden candlestick, which serve the priest’s duties.
Within the Holy of Holies stood the golden censer,
And the Ark of the Covenant <note>original: "bontkiſt" — literally "covenant-chest."</note>, furnished with God-consecrated treasures
And the sanctuary: the Law engraved in stone, and Aaron’s rod <note>The staff of Moses' brother that miraculously budded to confirm his divine appointment.</note>,
And the jar with the manna <note>original: "den emer met den daeu" — literally "the bucket with the dew," referring to the bread from heaven that fell like dew.</note> that fed the hungry.
The canopy of God’s Ark, serving as a Mercy Seat <note>The "genade troon" or "Mercy Seat" was the golden lid of the Ark where God’s presence was said to rest.</note>,
Whereon the Divine Oracle was revealed to view,
Was overshadowed by the wings of the Cherubim;
Meanwhile, the High Priests <note>original: "aertsprieſters" — literally "arch-priests."</note> appear before God once a year,
After first sanctifying themselves and the people with the blood
Of cattle, slaughtered to atone before God, who enlightened their minds
With the radiance of the gemstones on the breastplate <note>Refers to the Urim and Thummim, the sacred lot used by the High Priest to determine God's will.</note> and answered their questions
For the service of the rulers and kings, to turn plagues
Away from their necks in times of oppression and need,
Or to bless the land from Heaven’s rich bosom.

<meta>This page continues the description of the religious life of the Hebrews under the Mosaic Law. ### THE FIFTH BOOK.

The priests encamp, together with the Levites,
Around the sanctuary, and close to God’s tapestries <note>These "tapestries" refer to the woven curtains and coverings of the Tabernacle which housed the presence of God.</note>,
Each to perform their office and offerings,
To the honor of God’s name, for the atonement of the community,
Or for each individual member, through the slaughtering of bulls,
And ox, and cow, and ram, and sheep, in altar-fires
Consumed, or scorched from fire into air and ash.
Here wine and blood and oils are poured as libations.
They sprinkle the tent, altar, goat, and people with ashes
And blood. The impure go forth purified, and washed
With water, back to their tents. One offers goat, and dove,
And incense, myrrh, and salt, and flour, and barley, and grape.
The priests fail not in their duty by night or day,
To feed the eternal fire, and to kindle the flames.
They celebrate the feasts, precisely marked by sun and moon.
The seven-year feast <note>The Sabbatical Year (Shmita), during which debts were canceled and the land was left fallow.</note> means releasing debtors,
And granting slaves and the poor the field and grain for their benefit.
One must not infringe upon the lawful rights of the priesthood,
Neither in firstfruits, nor tithes, nor that which is their own,
But offer the gifts at God’s table according to the requirement.
The anointing oil is poured upon the priest’s head,
Consecrated for kings, and reserved for them;
The judgment seat is set, before which defiance trembles,
And whatever resists the High Priest’s <note>original: "aertspriesters" — the chief or high priest who held supreme religious authority.</note> mouth and decree.

<margin>The service of the law is strengthened and glorified by miracles.</margin>
The religion of the Hebrews lacks neither evidence nor signs,
To kindle and strengthen a devout heart.
One finds, step by step, the trail pressed everywhere
By God, and God’s lineage, and lot, and property,
Guided by angels, upheld, and defended,
Comforted and graced with victory and wonders;
Whether Aaron’s rod blossoms <note>A reference to the miracle where Aaron’s wooden staff sprouted buds and almonds to prove his divine selection.</note>, the rock gives water,
The bronze serpent <note>A miraculous image Moses raised on a pole to heal those bitten by venomous snakes in the wilderness.</note> heals, the army of princes perishes,
The flowing Jordan, with its depths torn open,
The Ark of the Covenant <note>original: "bontkist" — literally the "Covenant-chest."</note> grants passage, God’s sword the seven nations <note>Refers to the seven nations of Canaan that were displaced by the Israelites.</note>

->In Canaan, a surprise—whereupon the sound of trumpets<-
->Cursed Jericho, with towers, gate, and wall,<-
->Crashes to the earth, after the Ark of the Covenant <note>original: "bontkist"; the sacred chest containing the tablets of the Ten Commandments.</note>, for three pairs of days,<-
->Has been carried about in procession and prayer.<-
The heavens punish and help their people in the time of the Judges.
The Ark becomes a neighbor to Dagon <note>The national god of the Philistines. According to the Bible, when the Philistines captured the Ark and placed it in Dagon's temple, the idol of Dagon miraculously fell and broke.</note>, deaf and mute,
And is sent back again of its own accord out of terror.
It wanders restlessly, changing houses and lands,
Like a pilgrim, shaded by a tapestry <note>A reference to the Ark being housed in the portable Tabernacle (a tent of curtains/tapestries) before the Temple was built.</note>,
And waits for Solomon, who dedicates the temple to it,
Leading it up the mountain in state, where the throngs
And tribes of the realm gather, fully three times a year;
There, heavenly fire consumes the sacrificial cattle below,
And God Himself furnishes the temple in a cloud
With radiance and majesty, granting the prayer
Of the son <note>King Solomon, the son of David.</note> who possesses his father’s throne in full peace,
In such glory, that never the all-seeing sun
Saw a king as wise and rich as Solomon,
And even the Queen of the Moors <note>The Queen of Sheba, traditionally associated with Ethiopia or Southern Arabia.</note> left her own threshold
To worship Jacob’s God in that temple,
Completed after seven years to the honor of God’s name,
And richer in her sight than even her far-flung fame.

<margin>
The scepter of Judah falls, after many disasters, to the Romans: upon which Jacob’s prophecy regarding the Messiah’s coming looked <note>Refers to the "Scepter of Judah" prophecy in Genesis 49:10, interpreted as meaning the Messiah would come once Jewish self-rule ended.</note>.
</margin>
One counts, after Solomon, a span of a thousand years
That Abraham's descendants, upon uneven waves
Of apostasy, schism, war, and the downfall of city
And temple, were tossed about; the people sat in Babylon
Imprisoned by the Euphrates, and were released again;
Jerusalem rebuilt, and oppressed by fits and starts;
The dominion changes its face by turns,
Into a kingdom, and prince, and priestly state,
Until the Roman power, gradually climbing to the top,
Takes the staff from Judah, disturbs and harms the choice
Priesthood of Aaron, spares nothing sacred,
And in the end, within Rome, crowns Herod as king;

<meta>This page continues the theological and historical survey of the Hebrew people, shifting focus from the glory of Solomon's first Temple to the prophecies concerning the coming of the Messiah and the significance of the second Temple during the period of Roman rule.</meta>

### THE FIFTH BOOK.

A manifest sign, taken from the patriarch’s mouth,
Proclaimed that the People’s Redeemer must at last appear,
For whom so many centuries—both the circumcised <note>The Jewish people.</note> and the uncircumcised <note>The Gentiles, or non-Jewish nations.</note>—
Yearned everywhere with sighs and prayers,
In order to, under a single law, peacefully
Worship, sacrifice, and holily serve the Unseen One.

<margin>And Nebuchadnezzar’s dream of the four great empires, interpreted by Daniel, served as a sign that the Messiah was imminent, as Rabbi Nehumias and Josephus also attest.</margin>
The wise Daniel—who is held in such holy regard
By Ezekiel—was able to foretell the Messiah’s coming
Through the mouth of Gabriel, who was sent
To where Judah lay bound and shackled by the Euphrates.
He proclaimed with his voice a span of five hundred years minus ten <note>This refers to the "seventy weeks" (490 years) prophecy in the Book of Daniel, used by theologians to calculate the birth of Christ.</note>,
Stating that, after the rebuilding of destroyed Jerusalem,
The Messiah, the Promised One, must appear in the flesh:
Just as Nehumias, the master of the Rabbis,
Roughly half a century before Christ ever appeared,
Concluded this from Daniel for the service of his Hebrews.
With this agrees the "stone" which, sliding from the mountain,
Would crush Nebuchadnezzar’s image—the four great empires—
Into dust; which, if one reads Gerson <note>Likely a reference to the medieval scholar Gersonides (Levi ben Gershon), who wrote influential commentaries on Daniel.</note>,
Signifies the Messiah’s kingdom, which, feared far and wide,
Would forcefully crush the veiled crown <note>original: "sluierkroon"; a reference to the imperial authority of Rome.</note> of Rome,
And bring it under the yoke of Christ and his laws;
A point upon which even the mouths of the legal scholars agree.
The pen of Josephus, by no means distant from the time of Christ,
And who saw Jerusalem being "plowed" by swords,
Unfolded in his time the sayings of the prophet
Which pertained to this defeat, by which it became manifest
That the Messiah’s coming followed the predicted path.

<margin>Malachi here exalts the second temple above the first, and</margin>
The second temple also, in the eyes of Malachi—
Which makes Solomon’s building seem tarnished—draws in the Messiah,
The long-awaited one, through heaven’s messenger <note>John the Baptist, often identified as the "messenger" who prepares the way in the Book of Malachi.</note>,
Who clears the highway for him: but the first temple wears
The crown in glory, in the outward eyes of all;
For Solomon’s edifice was completed at the very peak of perfection:

And God Himself furnishes the temple in a cloud

In such glory that the all-seeing sun never
Beheld a king as wise and wealthy as Solomon;
Even the Queen of the Moors <note>The Queen of Sheba, often associated with Ethiopia or "the land of the Moors" in older Dutch literature.</note> left her own threshold
To worship the God of Jacob in that temple,
Completed after seven years of labor to honor God's name,
And richer in her eyes than even widespread fame had promised.

In which Abraham’s lineage was tossed upon the uneven waves
Of apostasy, schism, war, and the downfall of the city
And temple; the people sat imprisoned at Babylon
By the Euphrates, and were released once more;
Jerusalem was rebuilt, and oppressed in intervals;
The government changed its face by turns,
From a kingdom, to a principality, to a priestly state,

Takes the scepter from Judah, disturbs and harms

So must the majesty of the second temple rest
Upon God’s covenant of grace, which shall stand eternally fast,
And God the Messiah himself shall tread Aaron’s thresholds, <note>A reference to the thresholds of the Temple, specifically the priestly lineage of Aaron.</note>
During the time and standing of the second temple.
This also became evident from the fame that, spread far and wide,
Throughout David’s kingdom and across its neighboring borders,
How all the world awaited the arrival of the great
Redeemer, the refuge of heaven’s allies.
Some took Herod, or a leader from Gaulan, <note>A reference to Judas of Galilee (from Gaulanitis), who led a tax revolt against Rome.</note> to be
The Messiah. Titus and his father <note>The Roman Emperor Vespasian and his son Titus; Roman historians Suetonius and Tacitus famously recorded a belief that a world-ruler would emerge from Judea at this time.</note> were also spoken of
In prophecies in Syria and the East,
That they would finally comfort the Hebrew race
In its oppressions; that Judea would deliver a hero
Whose staff would curb all worldly power,
As Suetonius and Tacitus report to us—
The very pages that the oracles of God predicted.
From all this muttering and guessing amongst the people,
Uprisings rose time and again in David’s kingdom and cities.

Archangel Gabriel’s message to the holy Virgin.
</margin>

Elizabeth, married to the priest Zechariah,
Became pregnant with a fruit, the precursor to the Messiah, <note>Referring to John the Baptist.</note>
In her old age, just as the angel Gabriel,
An envoy from heaven’s throne, descended into the undefiled cell
Of David’s daughter; <note>The Virgin Mary.</note> and, upon his greeting and speech,
The Godhead—a power, a breath, a fire—shining down
From the sky with radiance and majesty,
Embraced, kissed, and honored the chaste Virgin, God’s bride,
In her humility and submissiveness,
And mingled with her blood: and she, who desired to receive
God in her heart, now received—it was never heard of before—
God Emmanuel, and carried the all-creating Word, <note>The "Logos" or "Word" from the Gospel of John.</note>
The Messiah, beneath her heart; and, pregnant with the Godhead,
She swooned from fright and hope, and blushing for shame, could no longer
Hide her happiness, but burst into the praise of the Most High
With all her strength; while the archangel [departed] the court...

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

<sig>Aa 2</sig>

Chosen from the high heaven, from where He had descended.
She goes to visit Elizabeth, her cousin, upon this blessing,
Where the fruit, the pre-glimmer of God’s Son, <note>Referring to John the Baptist as the precursor to Jesus.</note>
Rejoices in the mother’s body; while he already sees
Jesse’s crown <note>A reference to the lineage of King David, son of Jesse, fulfilling the prophecy of the Messiah's royal descent.</note> ruling in this unborn child,
Upon his grandfather’s throne, in the kingdom destined for him from above,
And that, neither enclosed nor defined by age or boundary,
Spreads itself wider than the living breath reaches.
<margin>Christ is born under Augustus.</margin>
Augustus in triumph had closed the gates of war, <note>The "gates of war" refers to the Temple of Janus in Rome; closing its doors signified a time of universal peace (Pax Romana).</note>
And taxed all the Roman people, and Jacob’s fellow-citizens,
When Joseph, son of David, came to David’s cattle-stall,
Poor Bethlehem, to be assessed with his tribe
Along with the pregnant virgin, God’s bride.
The small capacity of the city, unable to contain
The great press of people, sends the betrothed onward,
From door to door, and points them to an inn by the gate,
A dark cave, a cattle-stall by the ramparts,
Where the people swarm and bustle. Here the virgin rests at last,
And gives birth at midnight to her first, all-worthy fruit.
Just as the morning dew, from cool and fresh air,
Falls on rose gardens to refresh the roses,
Which, parched with thirst by day, stifle in sun and heat,
So lovelily flows from the virginal womb
That white lily-flower, without even feeling burden or pain, <note>A traditional theological poetic device suggesting a painless, miraculous birth.</note>
God and man, one seed and two natures
Joined as one, a child, upon which the low walls
Of the disregarded hamlet, humble Bethlehem,
Lifting itself from the dust, eclipse the city of Jerusalem,
God’s altar, and the throne of kings;
However poor it lies pushed aside in a manger and cattle-stall,
Wrapped in the cloth; while the air, full of spirit
And angels, gathers by night for God’s birth-feast,
Where they sing praise to the Most High,
At which the shepherds’ watch and flock and meadow leap.

<meta>This page continues from the account of Christ's birth in Bethlehem, transitioning from the narrative of the Nativity to a theological reflection on the nature of God’s revelation to humanity throughout history.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

From her first sleep, joyful and glad, because
The joy of the world came into David’s poorest city. <note>Referring to Bethlehem, the birthplace of both King David and Jesus.</note>

<margin>God revealed Himself for the first time in the creation of the world:</margin>
Before the creation of the high and heavenly vaults,
Full of angels, God was alone and known silently
To Himself, and sat peacefully, filling the ring
Of His infinity, in a reflection <term>bespiegeling: a term for meditative contemplation or a mirror-like study of divine nature</term>
Of Himself and His nature, hidden in that deep light
That knows no cycles, nor evening, nor morning:
But when He, by eternal decree, brought forth
The world out of Nothing, He who was never seen went out
To show Himself to His creatures as if visible,
In His majesty, before all heavenly thrones,
And power, and dominion, bowed beneath Him,
Reverently cheered with a common voice
By thousands upon thousands, raised so high in peak
Of gratitude, because they were permitted to behold God.
Thus creation was God’s first "going out,"
Just as a worldly prince shows himself
In public outside his palace to all kinds of lineages
And peoples, who wait for his exit before the court,
Throwing themselves to the earth and falling upon their faces
As soon as he appears, greeted with praise and honor.

<margin>The second time to the patriarchs, before and after the law:</margin>
The second time God went forth, as if from heaven’s halls,
Was when He spoke to the Patriarchs <note>The biblical ancestors of the Israelites, such as Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob.</note>, either Himself or shining
Through revelations, and dreams, and visions,
And holy angels, and prescribed their duty
In tables of the law, written with His hand,
And given to them from the summit of Sinai,
Strengthened with wonders, and the sound of speech, and flame.
He revealed Himself in the leadership of that tribe,
Chosen from all tribes as God’s priesthood,
As His only and first- and oldest-born,
With majesty around the holy Ark of the Covenant,
Graciously honored, where God’s infallible mouth

<meta>This page continues the theological exposition of God’s "goings out" or revelations. Having previously discussed God’s revelation through Creation and through the Patriarchs/Law, the author now addresses the third and final revelation: the Incarnation of Christ.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

As long as the oracles spoke, and gave answer to the questions
Of his high priests, who lay prostrate before him.

<margin>The third time, in the taking on of human nature, denied and contested by Ebion, Photinus, Arius, and Faustus.</margin>
But now, for the third time, God came forth from His throne
And appeared in His blood, the Son become man,
When God the Son—whose praise endures through all eternity—
Clothed Himself in a body, and joined Himself to our nature
By a bond, as soon as the Spirit of the Godhead worked
Within the body of the Virgin, that pure vessel, and became united;
An unheard-of union, understood by no man’s brain,
Grasped by no wit, however clever or sharply whetted.
Though Socinus <note>Faustus Socinus (1539–1604), a theologian who denied the full divinity of Christ, leading to the "Socinian" movement.</note>, that scholar of Ebion <note>Referring to the Ebionites, an early Jewish Christian sect that regarded Jesus as a non-divine prophet.</note>, mocks this,
And limits and shrinks the glory of God the Son
To that of a mere man, stripped of God’s infinite being,
And of the self-subsisting essence <term>zelveſtandigheit: a theological term referring to 'hypostasis' or 'substantia', the underlying reality of the divine person</term> of the Godhead, never praised enough:
Yet while he nevertheless honors Him with divine honor,
The Father is insulted through this "honor" of the Son,
And Jesus is consequently raised up as a bold and defiant idol,
Elevated higher than any Heathen altar was ever
Raised before: for the Godhead shall never suffer
That anything alongside Her should receive the height of supreme honor
And praise; thus it is by no means rightly permitted
That Christ, as a God, be everywhere worshipped <note>The author argues that if Christ is not truly God by nature, then worshipping him (as the Socinians did) would ironically be a form of idolatry.</note>.
But the honor belongs to God the Son, as an inseparable portion
Of that infinite, co-essential Being, with God
The Father, and the Spirit: one single essence together,
Yet distinguished in three, signified by three names.

<margin>Philo, Maimonides, various Rabbis, and the Talmud even confirm the holy Trinity, by...</margin>
The Jewish Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who used the concept of the 'Logos'.</note> builds the Trinity upon that ground:
The great Maimon <note>Moses Maimonides, the preeminent medieval Jewish scholar.</note> speaks with him as if from one mouth.
They posit One who knows, One who is known, and the Knowledge itself;
In which Albo <note>Joseph Albo, a 15th-century Jewish philosopher.</note> follows them and treads in their steps, without violation.
They give to God the Son the title of "God's Word,"
Through which God created the All; and that He is of no birth
As the Father knows it, who did not come to beget this Word
After the manner of men. In this, many Rabbis differ...

<sig>Aa 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: In</meta>

<summary>This page defends the orthodox doctrine of the Trinity and the dual nature of Christ against heresies like Socinianism, arguing that Christ must be truly divine to be worthy of worship. The author attempts to find precedents for Trinitarian thought within Jewish philosophy and Rabbinic tradition.</summary>

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological argument regarding the divinity of Christ. While the previous page attacked the Socinian view of Christ as a "mere man," this page argues that the concepts of the Trinity and the Incarnation are actually rooted in ancient Jewish tradition (Kabbalah and the Talmud), suggesting that modern denials of these mysteries are a departure from older wisdom.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

<margin>The Catholic Church is steadfastly defended.</margin>
In Christ’s divinity, nor in the title of Messiah, [is there doubt].
This is the archangel, God’s envoy, who commands far and wide,
Redeemer, the God of Bethel, who appeared to Amram’s son <note>Moses, the son of Amram, to whom God appeared in the burning bush (Exodus 3).</note>
From a thorn bush; an angel who, in times past,
Led them out of Egypt, and whose countenance
Went before them; who approaches the holy structure
Of the temple. Ask the wisdom of the shrewdest Kabbalists <note>Practitioners of the Kabbalah, a tradition of Jewish mysticism. The author argues that ancient Jewish mystical thought supports the idea of God manifesting in multiple forms or "rays."</note>:
They distinguish God in three illustrious rays:
The Father, and the Son (or the Word), and also the Spirit.
They see the possibility that God might fit Himself to the mold
Of humanity, and clothe Himself in our nature.
The Talmud <note>The central text of Rabbinic Judaism, containing legal and theological discussions.</note> even ascribes God’s highest title to Him—
The Name that no Hebrew may speak without atonement <note>Referring to the Tetragrammaton (YHWH), the sacred four-letter name of God, which is traditionally not spoken aloud in Jewish practice.</note>—
And sees the time that God points out with His finger.
How then can the ancestral enemy <note>Likely referring to heretics like the Socinians or Arians who denied the Trinity.</note> of the Trinity reasonably
Contend that this point of the Threefold nature serves as a stumbling block
To the circumcised <note>The Jewish people.</note>, and alienates them from the Messiah’s crown;
Since the Kabbalist and the Talmud determine this to be so,
As did many a grey-haired Rabbi who, rejoicing in David’s songs <note>The Psalms of King David, which Christians interpreted as containing prophecies of the Messiah.</note>,
Go forth to meet the Messiah, to receive him on his father’s throne,
To worship, honor, and offer to him? Meanwhile,
The younger Jews have boldly strayed from that path;
Just as the ancestral enemies of the divinity of God’s Son—
Degenerated from the path of the fathers—still oppress Christ
With this crown of thorns, and dim his light,
Because nature and reason yield before this mystery.
Whoever wishes to grasp this point of faith through their reason—
A point that does not conflict with reason, but stands higher
Than human wit and reason can ever aim—
Arrogantly and recklessly oversteps the ban
And the boundary set for him by God, who revealed
His truth to us from above so that we might believe it,
Just as it is carved upon the pillar of truth, His Church,
Which, century after century, proves too strong for the abyss.

<meta>catchword: Ferneel</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the Trinity and the Incarnation of Christ are supported by ancient Jewish traditions like the Kabbalah and the Talmud, asserting that those who reject these mysteries on the grounds of "reason" are overstepping the boundaries set by God.</summary>
