--- Page 225 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological defense of the Trinity. While the previous page looked at Jewish mystical traditions (Kabbalah and the Talmud) to find precursors for the Triune nature of God, this page turns to Classical philosophy—specifically Jean Fernel's interpretation of Plato—to argue that even the "light of reason" points toward the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

<margin>Fernel confirms the Holy Trinity from Plato.</margin>
Fernel, <note>Jean Fernel (1497–1558), a renowned French physician and philosopher who sought to reconcile Platonic philosophy with Christian theology.</note> drawing from Plato, asserts through the clear light of reason that within the One who rules all, there exist three co-eternities: the Good, the Mind, and also the Soul of the world's sphere. By "the Good," he understands God the Father, who established this great edifice of heaven, earth, sea, and all things; simple, unmoved, and—surpassing even the most enlightened intellect—rich in all things and overflowing with benevolence. The Mind, which blooms eternally, originates from this good Father like a radiant light from the sun, the source of all brilliance; and this Mind is an Intelligence which, being divine by nature, is rightly called the best Son of the Great One’s best Good. The Soul of the world, flowing from the Mind of the Good like a ray of light, shines powerfully with its beam through all things, sustaining and giving life to the whole.

<margin>The poet's humility and reverence in investigating this mystery.</margin>
Grace, O God, grace, if we dare to approach Your seat, the throne of the Divinity and the Trinity, within that unapproachable light; if we dare to contemplate the Word-made-flesh, Emmanuel, <note>Hebrew: "God with us."</note> too closely, or probe too deeply into Your high mysteries, in which the minds of the wisest can lose their way. Whoever wades through such a sea sinks over his head. Blessed is he who simply adores and believes this.

Everything that exists is rightly divided into two categories: the uncreated and the created. The Creator alone is uncreated. Now, strip the Son and the Spirit of this Majesty; then they are by no means God, but mere creatures who received their being and nature "on loan" from God. If that were so, we would be consecrated <note>Refers to the act of baptism "in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit."</note> and washed at the font in the name of mere creatures. But this conclusion is refuted by all the untainted pages <note>The "untainted pages" refer to the Holy Scriptures.</note> of Scripture, which clothe both the Son and the Spirit with the same properties and honor as God the Father.

<meta>The text ends with a catchword "De" (The), suggesting the next page will continue the argument regarding the divinity of the Holy Spirit or the nature of the Godhead.</meta>

<summary>This page uses Platonic philosophy, as interpreted by Jean Fernel, to explain the Trinity through the concepts of the Good, the Mind, and the World Soul. It concludes with a theological argument that if the Son and Spirit were not "uncreated" like the Father, Christian baptism would be a hollow ritual dedicated to mere creatures.</summary>
<keywords>Jean Fernel, Plato, Trinity, Holy Spirit, Emmanuel, uncreated vs. created, baptism, Christian philosophy, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the Trinity. While the previous page focused on finding the Triune God in Platonic philosophy and the "Soul of the World," this page shifts to a linguistic and logical defense, addressing common misconceptions about the term "person" used in church doctrine.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

<margin>
A misunderstanding arising from the word *person*.
</margin>
The error regarding the point of the Godhead is accustomed
To draw its strength from the name or the word *person*,
To slander the Church as one that dares to divide God,
Attacking it for a direct contradiction and inconsistency;
Whenever she places three in the one essence,
And names and counts three persons in one Godhead.
The unrefined brain is bewildered, knowing no better
Than that three persons are like Jacob, John, and Peter,
Also three separate entities, and follows the banner of Photinus, <note>Photinus (died 376 AD) was a bishop who taught that Jesus was a mere man who became divine through his virtue, denying His eternal pre-existence.</note>
Or the Arian flag, <note>Arianism, named for the priest Arius, taught that Jesus Christ was a created being and not of the same eternal substance as God the Father.</note> long since sinking.
Whoever sees this scattering of Christ’s church and members,
Bewildered on the path of blind leaders, without reason,
Feels the desire to show each one, through the light shining from God’s Word,
How the understanding is misled and goes astray.

<margin>
How the word *person* is understood here.
</margin>
The word *essence* <note>original: "wezen"</note> points to God’s nature, which is common
To three persons. The word *person* intends, according to usage, to show
A subsistence <note>original: "zellefstandigheit," a technical Dutch term for the theological concept of *hypostasis*, referring to an individual existence within the divine nature.</note> of a rational nature,
Which is unique to an individual. Now the light
Of reason explains how our judgment and thoughts
Regard the three persons in the Godhead differently
Than in three men, when one explains the nature of each.
The essence of man is narrowly defined, and shuts
Itself within its small ring, not to be expanded further:
And for this reason, man is not only distinguished
From other men by his properties: but
These properties are also clearly divided from one another
And in fact separated. Although in three companions,
John, Jacob, and Peter, one nature is instilled,
Common to them three, yet they are three men in fact,
Even in respect to nature and the state of mankind:
For since no father can share all his essence
With his offspring, but only some seed, fit for procreation;
So it must follow that no son from this lineage
Takes the very same nature of the father unto himself,

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>The poet argues that the "three persons" of the Trinity are not separate beings like three individual humans (Jacob, John, and Peter), but rather distinct subsistences within a single, undivided divine essence.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, personhood, essence, Photinus, Arianism, divine nature, subsistence, Christology, theological logic</keywords>

--- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical distinction between human "personhood" and the divine "Persons" of the Trinity. While the previous page ended by defining a "person" as a subsistence of a rational nature, this page clarifies that while human individuals (like Peter or John) are separate beings, the three Persons of God share one identical, indivisible essence.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

But merely one similar being has sprouted from another.
Human essence, thus defined and limited,
Is not truly one and the same across many individuals;
Therefore, Peter, Jacob, and John are three distinct men.
But it is different with God’s nature, worthy of honor:
For in this essence—which no wise man could ever fathom—
Simple, <note>In historical theology, "simple" refers to "divine simplicity," the concept that God is not composed of different parts but is a single, unified being.</note> boundless, and eternal, all
Three persons exist, though they are three in number,
Firmly rooted in the unique essence, with their own characteristics,
Which are indivisible and truly distinct.
And for this reason, one does not count three Gods, one by one,
As one counts three men in John, Jacob, and Peter; no:
Since God’s Son is not begotten of God the Father,
And God the Holy Spirit does not radiate from the two together—
The Father and the Son—by the cutting of the whole
Into three pieces, of which each person enjoys a part
Or a third share; nor does it happen by "flowing away" <note>The author is refuting the idea of "emanation," where God loses part of Himself to create the others.</note>
As in human procreation, where mankind continues to grow;
Nor by production, like the cuttings taken from a rose;
But through a wondrous, unspeakable, always
Hidden and eternal communication <note>original: "meedeelinge," here meaning the sharing of the divine nature among the three Persons.</note> in the workings
Of the pure essence, where no visionary can perceive
The origin, the middle, nor the end. Thus it remains certain
That here is nothing other than one and the same essence
Of the Begetter, the Begotten, and the one who Proceeds. <note>These are traditional terms for the Father (Begetter), the Son (Begotten), and the Holy Spirit (who "proceeds" from the Father and Son).</note>
Whoever understands this will not shy away from the word "person."

<margin>
God’s internal workings from eternity.
</margin>
Besides the word "person," yet a second stumbling block
Must be removed from the path before we step
Into the holy enclosure to defend God’s Trinity.
No one who uses reason can deny God’s internal activity,
Since a being in whom no end
Of wisdom, goodness, might, and power is known,
Cannot remain idle within Himself for all eternity;
For he who denies this separates the form from its very nature.

Bb
<meta>catchword: That</meta>

<summary>This page explains the theological distinction between human individuals and the Divine Persons, arguing that God is one indivisible essence rather than three separate beings. It also asserts that God's nature is inherently active and "working" from all eternity rather than being idle.</summary>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the Trinity from page 193. Having established that the three Persons share one essence, the author now addresses the "internal activity" of God—how the Father eternally "begets" the Son through self-contemplation without any beginning or end in time.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

That is impossible. Even if fire melts no wax,
Nor anything from outside, upon which it works with force:
It nonetheless glows just as strongly within itself.
Consider now with your own power, with a focused mind,
How the eternal, powerful, almighty, and all-wise,
And unique being of the <term>Godhead<gloss>The essential nature or essence of God, distinguished from the three individual Persons.</gloss></term>, infinitely precious,
Could necessarily never be within itself without activity,
And how in God nothing exists that arose later:
For the <term>attributes<gloss>The qualities of God, such as wisdom and power. In classical theology, these are not "parts" of God but are identical to His essence.</gloss></term> in God’s being, never praised enough,
And which for this reason are rightly called His own attributes,
Are one and the same, and do not differ from His essence,
Neither in self-knowledge, nor in wisdom, power, and will;
All form, all pure God. O inner glow, too strong for us!
How does one express this? God Himself is God’s internal work.
This work and essence are simultaneous. However closely one
Wishes to consider it, here there is no earlier, nor later.
„All that is in God possesses a <term>co-eternity<gloss>The state of existing together eternally, meaning the Son and Spirit are just as eternal as the Father.</gloss></term>.
„No moment separates God’s work from His eternal being:
„Although our weak understanding represents it as a sequence,
„And one thing must be spoken after the other:
„For human deliberation requires time to proceed.
„God’s active essence has never existed without activity.

<margin>The co-eternity of God the Father, and God the Son, and God the Holy Spirit.</margin>

That living intellect stood eternally withdrawn,
And focused, so that with all its power
It might behold itself and its own nature, within the ring
Of the Godhead, unmoved in this <term>reflection<gloss>The Dutch word 'bespiegeling' means both philosophical contemplation and the literal reflection in a mirror.</gloss></term>.
That simple unity, God, praised everywhere within Himself,
Calmly contemplated Himself in His own essence,
From eternity, and in this reflection brought forth the <term>Word<gloss>original: "het Woort." Referring to the 'Logos' or the Son, the second person of the Trinity.</gloss></term>,
An image, like Himself in radiance, internally,
And that necessary God had to resemble His Father.
No one gazing in a mirror can look at themselves,
Without simultaneously recognizing, in the mirror-crystal
And glass, themselves, in a living appearance.

<summary>The author explains that God is eternally active within Himself through self-contemplation, an internal "work" that is identical to His essence and results in the eternal generation of the Son (the Word).</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, divine attributes, co-eternity, the Word, divine simplicity, self-knowledge, mirror analogy</keywords>

--- Page 229 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of God's "internal work" described on page 194. Having established that God's essence and His activity are co-eternal, the author now focuses on the generation of the Son and the procession of the Holy Spirit, emphasizing that these distinctions do not divide the single divine essence.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

Like begets like; so it is said with reason:
He is, as if cut from his own face <note>A Dutch idiom meaning he is the "spitting image" or a perfect likeness.</note>,
Entirely like the Father. Thus one sees a manner,
A nature in image and print; a contour, a certain grace.
God beholds that only, and His own chosen,
Natural Son, and eternally firstborn,
And calls him His Son, a Son who pleases Him,
And from whom the name of fatherhood rightfully derives,
Just as sonship takes its origin from the Father:
What did these two—inseparably bound and fastened
Together—radiate, if not the Spirit of love, a glow
Of the Father and the Son, nourished by both?
Thus there revealed themselves, in a widely spread
And single essence, three all at once undivided,
Yet distinct, each by three particularities.
No singularity, however, divides these three from one another.
One essence shares itself among three particularities.
The three, existing by themselves, enter into no mingling <note>This refers to the theological rejection of "Modalism," the idea that the three Persons of the Trinity are just different "modes" or roles of one person rather than distinct subsistent relations.</note>,
But remain in themselves, and are simple in deed,
Since the <term>consubstantiality<gloss>The doctrine that the Father, Son, and Spirit are "of one substance" or essence (Greek: homoousios).</gloss></term> of the three rests upon the essence,
And by the sharing of the essence: that remains simple:
It never sparks away from itself, like a spark, a sprinkle
Spattering from a flame. Here is a flame, a light,
A pure clarity, closed firm and tight;
A pure simplicity, a <term>Godhead<gloss>The essential nature or "being" of God, as opposed to the individual "Persons."</gloss></term>, pure and simple;
A smooth countenance that shows no creases or wrinkles.
All God, and Godhead; an equality in three-in-one,
And one in three: since the <term>essential unity<gloss>The state of being one in essence or nature.</gloss></term> here denies no
Trinity, but continues to crown it
Through indivisibility, even though one hears of three persons.
But before we wander further in that ring
Of the Godhead; listen first to how God’s contemplation,
And image, and the radiance of the love of these two
Never led anyone outside of God, the unique Being.

<sig>Bb 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: God's</meta>

<summary>This page explains the Trinity using metaphors of light and parental likeness, arguing that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are distinct "Persons" who nonetheless share a single, indivisible, and simple essence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the Trinity. While the previous page established the unity and simplicity of God's essence, this page provides a "broader explanation" of the internal relations between the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit, using the philosophical concept of God's self-knowledge and mutual love.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

<margin>
A broader explanation of how the Son proceeds from the Father,
</margin>

God’s self-knowledge begets from eternity God’s image,
Yet no second God: for God’s self-knowledge differs
Not from God, no, not at all; they stand equal and level.
God’s self-knowledge, and contemplating, and perceiving
Is God, and Godhead, and also nothing other than this;
Since simplicity, crowned in the highest possession,
Suffers no joining of parts, nor unequal steps <note>The author is describing "Divine Simplicity," the doctrine that God is not made of parts or different properties, but is a single, unified essence.</note>,
And the level unity remains in form and properties.
Now you see that God’s Son is the Father’s imprint,
Who resembles the Father through His likeness;
Thus you already know a second: and why then, by reason,
Should the third not be admitted into the essence of the Godhead;
Since the mutual attraction of Father and Son
Radiates nothing but love, upon one and the same throne?

<margin>
and the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son.
</margin>

Who can tell us, who can depict how in the birthing <note>In traditional theology, the Son is "begotten" (born) of the Father’s intellect, while the Spirit "proceeds" from the will or love.</note>
The Father’s eyes gaze upon the Son’s form,
And the Son’s eyes upon the lovely countenance of
The Father! Name, if you can, who can conceive of that attraction;
A pull, carried from both sides toward one another.
Here shines mutual satisfaction and pleasure,
An unquenchable love, a Spirit of loveliness;
A glowing love, which separates from neither of the two,
From whom He proceeds, as a third in the one essence,
Not as a third God. A trio gathered
From pearls, indeed provides three pearls upon a crown,
Yet each remains singular. Whether now the name "person"
Lets itself be counted three times in succession in the Godhead,
That can only establish three particularities <note>The original Dutch *byzonderheden* refers to the unique characteristics or "subsistent relations" that distinguish the Three Persons without dividing the one God.</note>,
But not three Gods: for in the essence one finds nothing
But one God; however precisely our own wisdom aims;
However fiercely ignorance thunders against the conclusion of the <term>Trinity<gloss>The "Dryheit" or "Threeness," the doctrine of one God in three Persons.</gloss></term>,
The thrice-most holy, set apart from all stain.
All is purely one in what stands here at the summit;
All God, and nothing but God; one tone, one same measure;

<summary>This page explains the Trinity through the psychological analogy of God's self-knowledge (the Son) and the mutual love between the Father and Son (the Holy Spirit). It emphasizes that these distinctions exist within a single, simple divine essence, using the metaphor of three pearls in one crown.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Holy Spirit, God the Father, God the Son, Divine Simplicity, Self-knowledge, Divine Love, Personhood</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the Trinity, moving from the internal relations (the "processions") of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit discussed on the previous page to their absolute unity of essence. The author argues that while we name three distinct Persons, they share a single, indivisible substance and nature.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

An agreement in the essence; an equality,
In an ocean of priceless richness.
God is then purely one in three: and whoever grasps this
Believes that God, begotten of God <note>original: "van Godt geteelt"</note>, does not stray from God,
Seated from eternity with the other throne-companions.
Thus the ring of God's perfection remains closed.
The Trinity <term>Dryheit|Trinity; the state of being threefold</term> closes itself fast and tight in one. It suffers
No breach, nor feels any mutual strife,
But stands in eternity. If you please, step closer:
Truth is not afraid. Ask boldly: what is the Father?
What else but God Himself, who has been from eternity?
What is the Son? God Himself, nothing else. What is the Spirit?
God Himself, and nothing else. So you hear how three names,
Not mere sounds, but signifiers, together
Signify one God, that wondrous Three-in-One <term>Dryeen|Triune or Three-in-One</term>.
The uniqueness remains in three distinctions.
We name Father, Son, and Spirit, everywhere to be praised,
But not three Gods, no: one Godhead stands above all.
The substance <term>zellefftandigheit|Historically used to translate the Latin 'substantia', referring to the underlying reality or essence of God</term> is one, even if one counts three
Persons. Does it seem to you that there is contradiction here?
None at all; if only we rightly reflect upon this substance
Apart from the three persons: then we conclude
Rightly that our "three and one" in no way misleads us:
For three persons are but one substance.
The three are one, and one is nothing but three, who endure.
Now say: what do you find in the counting? Three natures?
Here is but one nature: or do you find beings? No,
One essence <term>wezen|being or essence</term> is here. Do you find here substances?
One substance: or do you find divine beings?
Oh no, one Godhead, worthy of service and worship.
You say: I do not count the one here, but the three.
What are these others, if one looks at them in a row,
Than one nature alone, a single pure essence,
A substance, a Godhead, never fully praised?

<summary>This page defines the Trinity as a perfect unity where three distinct Persons (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit) share a single, indivisible divine substance and nature. The author emphasizes that these are not three separate gods, but one Godhead existing in eternal harmony.</summary>

<keywords>Trinity, Father, Son, Holy Spirit, Essence, Substance, Divine Unity, Triune, Theology, Godhead</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Holy Trinity. While the previous page focused on the internal logic of the Three Persons sharing a single substance, this page shifts to the limitations of human language and analogies, eventually grounding the argument in the historical authority of the Athanasian Creed to refute Arianism.</meta>

### REFLECTIONS.

Since a likeness illuminates the darkness sooner
Than midday-clear proof, I have furled the sail
Upon such an abyss, and here sparingly borrowed likenesses
From sun, and spring, and tree; nor did I wish to fish this out
From the powers of the soul, threefold in number <note>A reference to the Augustinian analogy comparing the Trinity to the soul's three powers: Memory, Understanding, and Will.</note>;
Nor from mercury, salt, and sulfur, everywhere
Three substances, to be purified from one matter and separated
By the chemist’s <term>stoffescheider|Literally 'matter-separator'; a term for a chemist or alchemist</term> art. Likenesses lead
Opponents only with difficulty toward the earthly mystery,
Of which God’s book and Spirit are the teacher:
For if created light proves too strong in its clarity,
What eye can endure the radiance of uncreated truth?

<margin>Now the truth of the confession of the invincible Athanasius becomes clear.</margin>
Now the symbol <term>kenteken des geloofs|Refers to a Creed or 'Symbolum'; specifically the Athanasian Creed</term> of Athanasius shall
Point out God’s highway, amidst the Arian <note>Arianism was a 4th-century theological movement that claimed Jesus Christ was a created being, subordinate to God the Father, rather than co-equal.</note> clamor,
To worship and serve the never-before-seen One
In a Trinity without looking back,
And in this unity, God in His Trinity,
Which neither mixes the persons, nor separates them from one another,
But only distinguishes them, cautiously and shrewdly:
The Father, Son, and Spirit. Each is another,
But one Godhead, one equal glory,
A co-eternity, the same majesty,
An uncreatedness, a quality <note>In 17th-century theology, 'hoedaenigheit' refers to the divine attributes or essence.</note>, and also
An immensity <term>ongemeetenheit|A translation of the Latin 'immensus' used in the Athanasian Creed, meaning 'immeasurable' or omnipresent</term>, an eternity in peace,
All equally eternal, not three co-eternals,
One omnipotence, not three, one Godhead also, not
Three Gods: one is Lord: one does not count three lords.
To posit three Gods is to insult God’s majesty.
The Father, and the Son—the Father’s radiance and image—
Are uncreated, but the Son alone is begotten
Of His Father Himself. The Spirit beams from the Father
And the Son: one Father, Son, and Spirit are three together,
One single Father, Son, and Holy Spirit alone,
But not three fathers, sons, and spirits; no, oh no.

<meta>catchword: In</meta>

<summary>The author reflects on the inadequacy of worldly analogies (like the sun or alchemy) to explain the Trinity, concluding that true understanding comes through the Athanasian Creed, which defines the Three Persons as co-equal and co-eternal.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Athanasian Creed, Arianism, Analogies, Alchemy, Theology, Joost van den Vondel</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page continues the exposition of the Athanasian Creed, moving from the internal relationship of the Trinity to the specific nature of Jesus Christ as both fully divine and fully human. It concludes by contrasting this "safe highway" of orthodox faith with the "blasphemies" of contemporary anti-Trinitarian thinkers.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

In this Trinity there is no before, nor after,
No greater, nor lesser. Three persons are together
All co-eternal, and equal in radiance and power.
God’s son is Christ, God and man, so long expected.
He is God himself, begotten from God’s own <term>zelfstandigh wezen|Substantial essence; the 'ousia' or fundamental being of God</term>
From eternity; and man, born from the unsurpassed
Substance of the virgin and mother in time;
Perfect God and man; and, if one confesses rightly,
Consisting of a soul, endowed with the light of reason,
And of a body, with its human limbs;
And according to his Godhead, entirely equal to God the Father,
Lesser than the Father according to his humanity in God’s kingdom.
Although Christ is known and counted as God and man:
Yet by this name only one Christ is signified,
A single one: not that this God is changed into flesh,
But God clothed with flesh, who humbled himself for us.
There is one Christ, not by the mixing together
Of the substances, but by bringing them together
And firmly uniting them in one person alone:
For just as the soul, glorified by reason,
And the flesh deliver one single man <note>The poet uses the union of soul and body in a human being as an analogy for the union of the divine and human natures in Christ, a classic argument from the Athanasian Creed.</note> through God’s mother,
Thus God and man are one Christ, the protector of all.
Now no thick mist, so that it might deceive the eye,
Floats any longer in our light; therefore follow the livery <note>The "livery" or uniform of the faith—the standard of belief one wears to show their allegiance.</note> of the cross-faith
Of Athanasius, that invincible Saint
And Confessor of God: the highway here is safe,
Entirely smooth and paved. Thus everyone goes straight to God,
And flees the blasphemers: while one sees Ostorodt <note>Christoph Ostorodt (d. 1611), a prominent Socinian/Unitarian theologian who denied the Trinity.</note>
And Francis of Transylvania <note>Ferenc Dávid (c. 1510–1579), the founder of the Unitarian Church in Transylvania (Zevenburgen).</note> rob God’s son of his honor,
And mock along with Socinus <note>Fausto Sozzini, whose "Socinian" teachings rejected the pre-existence of Christ and the Trinity, emphasizing his humanity instead.</note>, who teaches to worship Christ
Here above, and adds a new Godhead to him.
Thus deformity comes thundering into these three,
A "tri-disunity," three heads, three grimaces.
One hears blasphemers barking at God’s Trinity,

<summary>The poet articulates the orthodox Christology of the Athanasian Creed, defining Christ's dual nature as God and man, while condemning anti-Trinitarian reformers like Socinus and Ostorodt as creators of a "deformed" faith.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Christology, Athanasian Creed, Incarnation, Socinianism, Christoph Ostorodt, Ferenc Dávid, Fausto Sozzini, Orthodoxy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the polemic against anti-Trinitarian heresies. After establishing the orthodox view of Christ’s two natures—divine and human—the poet now turns to specific figures he considers dangerous, specifically attacking the Socinian theologian Christoph Ostorodt and the ancient Ebionites, while invoking the "Sibylline Oracles" as pagan witnesses to Christian truth.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

Like abominations, and as it appears from their blasphemy,
What a cursed fruit was conceived in their hearts.
The godless Ostorodt <note>Christoph Ostorodt (c. 1560–1611) was a prominent leader of the Polish Brethren (Socinians). His denial of the Trinity and the pre-existence of Christ led the poet to label him a "Turk"—a common 17th-century polemical slur against those who rejected the divinity of Jesus, comparing their unitarianism to Islamic theology.</note>, rightly called a "Turk,"
Dares to damn the worshippers of Christ for all eternity
As idolaters; even though all the angels adore God the Son
Where he sits crowned upon his Father’s throne.

<margin>The oracles of the ancient Sibyls and the Church Fathers seal the doctrine of the Holy Trinity and the Divinity of Christ.</margin>
Just as the Church Fathers do not differ on this main point,
So the heavenly Sibyls speak from the mouth of God,
Who prophesied the Divinity of Christ to us, broadly and clearly,
As did many a prophet of the Law before, and many a year
Before the Savior’s coming; after which envy arose,
Which forced the ancients to sharpen their pens against it,
To defend the honor of God, the God-become-man,
Mocked in his manger and stable by Ebion <note>Ebion is the legendary founder of the Ebionites, an early Jewish-Christian sect that viewed Jesus as a purely human prophet, born of Joseph and Mary, rather than the divine Son of God.</note>,
Who honors no divinity in Jesus, the protector,
But considers him begotten by a father and a mother,
Solely natural, just like any other child,
And not from a virgin, who wraps God in swaddling clothes.

He lies there, cheered by angels and men,
As many kings and prophets of God desired,
Along with all the patriarchs, pained by longing,
To see the joyful day that God appears in the flesh.
The Light of Light, and God of God <note>This is a direct reference to the Nicene Creed (*Lumen de Lumine, Deum de Deo*), emphasizing that Christ is of the same substance as the Father.</note>, hides himself beneath
Humanity. Who can comprehend this wonder of all wonders!
How two natures, so entirely unlike each other,
Are joined in one person—extinguishing the glory of David’s kingdom
And Aaron’s sanctuary, God’s <term>bontkist|Ark of the Covenant</term>!
The wandering brain wants first to understand, then to believe.
One must not approach God’s Ark, where the Godhead hovers
Upon the wings of the cherubim, except with fear, and honor,
And full reverence; where the souls
Of the high priests kneel down dejectedly,
And fall down on their faces in the dust.
The soul of man, submerged in the body, is too coarse

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

And earthly to rise higher into this sanctuary.
It is more pious to burn incense, to keep silence
Than to investigate how the second Person <note>The "second person" refers to God the Son in the Holy Trinity.</note>
Of the Godhead united Himself with this child from God’s throne.

<margin>*Christ is circumcised:*</margin>
On the eighth day, He is circumcised according to Moses’ law,
And with this first mark, by which He subjects Himself
To the customs of Aaron <note>Aaron, the brother of Moses, was the first High Priest; his "customs" represent the complex sacrificial and ritual laws of the Old Testament.</note>, He is marked, and He pours out
His precious blood so early in pain, so as not to fall short
In God’s demand to atone through His suffering,
And to liberate humanity from the curse of the law;
As God’s circumcision heals this wound of the law,
And demands that man circumcise himself according to the spirit,
In his own will, heart, passion, and thoughts;
Circumcision required of all generations,
Blessed in the seed promised to Abraham,
Who is called Jesus, and has now come to fulfill the law.

<margin>*Worshipped by the Magi, and honored with gifts,*</margin>
The Magi confirm this, who, by the star from the east,
Beckoned to Bethlehem, undertook a journey
So many miles distant, and, over mountain and valley,
Saw the Light of souls itself rise in the stable;
While Jerusalem lay in sleep, neglecting God’s prophets,
And having worn out their pages in vain.
They set all the service of Mithras <note>Mithras was a Persian sun deity; the poet suggests the Magi abandoned their ancestral pagan astrology and sun-worship to follow the true Light of Christ.</note> aside,
For the light that now drives away the night of all idolatry,
And shines upon everyone from His manger, in the middle
Of donkey and ox. They honor and worship
The King, who wields the scepter with power everywhere,
The Priest, prefigured by Aaron’s priesthood,
Along with His mortality, signified by three gifts:
For gold honors the king; myrrh is dedicated to graves
And corpses, and already anoints Him, as if
Hanging upon the wood <note>original: "aen het hout"—a common poetic term for the Cross.</note>, crucified with painful suffering:
The scent of incense suits that Priest, as He steps forth,
Clothed in the purple of His blood, with offerings and prayers.

Those who hope firmly in Jesus, and wish to hear from His mouth

As if in a preliminary design <note>The poet uses a metaphor of painting: the Old Testament laws of Moses are a "rough sketch" (typology) that Christ eventually completes and brings to life.</note>, upon which the perfect image is

Trust in this fruit <note>The "fruit" refers to the infant Christ, the "fruit of the womb."</note> takes deeper root daily
Through revelations: for while a pair of turtledoves

Lest he should miss that blessing before his end.
His blameless life deserved a promise of comfort:
That he would not drag his stiff limbs into the grave

To join his ancestors, now that he sees the light of nations
Rising in this child, who shall drive all dark clouds

And sets such a crown of honor upon the head of Judah."
May heaven bless the virgin, the pure mother.

A sword that serves as the prize and glory of your patience,

### BOOK FIVE.

So speaks the dry staff <note>A poetic reference to the elderly Simeon, whose life is nearing its end.</note>, full of joy, and in his anticipation
Heaven seems to open before his face,
As if he already enjoyed the joy and the salvation
Intended for everyone through many centuries in this fruit <note>The "fruit of the womb," meaning the infant Christ.</note>.
The widow Anna, more than fourscore and four years <note>A traditional way of saying 84 years old, following the account in Luke 2:37.</note>,
Aged as a tree, accustomed to guarding the temple by night,
To keep watch in prayer, also arrived at that very moment,
Rejoicing that she saw God's blood, the redeemer of the earth,
Who would now adorn Jerusalem with a service
Even more beautiful than all of Aaron's sacrificial fires <note>Refers to the priestly rituals of the Old Testament established by Aaron, the first High Priest.</note>
And religious rites until now—merely a shadow and example of the duty
That drives away Moses' shadows through the new light.

<margin>Christ flees from Herod, and is found after twelve years in the temple among the legal scholars;</margin>
But just as Moses, once the lawgiver of the Hebrews,
Was persecuted in his cradle, about fifteen centuries ago,
By Pharaoh, so Jesus hides himself for a while
From the child-murderer's <note>Herod the Great, who ordered the Massacre of the Innocents.</note> hatred, on the banks of the Nile,
And conceals himself in darkness, in the kingdom where the ancients wandered,
Until the plague of children, Herod, had died.
His parents led him, up to three times a year,
To Zion for the feast, dedicated to God's altar.
As soon as his age had measured six pairs of years <note>Twelve years old.</note>,
His mother herself found him seated in God's vault,
In the middle of the circle of the legal scholars, in order
To unfold his father's will, and the heavenly sanctuary;
Stunning everyone with his answers to their questions,
And with questions that defeated the boldest and most clever among them;
Astonished by the power, and wisdom, and the understanding
Of the holy child, who early on claims the crown of wisdom,
And eclipses Solomon, and all the prophecies
That consecrate God's anointed son as teacher.

<margin>Baptized around his thirtieth year, and,</margin>
After eighteen years of time had glided by since then,
Emmanuel <note>A Hebrew name meaning "God with us," used for Jesus.</note> first began to enter into his office,
With more public evidence, upon which everyone looked and hoped,
Where Zechariah's son <note>John the Baptist.</note>, who testified of him, baptized,

<margin>The Spirit assailed after forty days of fasting.</margin>

Thus the cold and moist element <note>Refers to the water of baptism.</note> received,
Through the movement of God’s limbs and body—never tarnished

He taught what livery <note>The distinctive uniform or spiritual identity that marks a follower of Christ.</note> distinguishes his heroes

Whoever is yet possessed by spirits, or burdened
With illnesses, is helped and saved in their need.
No city, no village, no house where he sets a footstep
Lacks his grace and poured-out blessing.
Whoever trusts him and prays is never left forsaken.
He teaches his school <note>His group of disciples.</note>, simply, without malice;
At last, seated in their circle, on the mountain,

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

He begins to kindle for the people the new light
That shines from his teaching, reporting the corruption and defects
Of the scribes <note>Jewish legal experts and scholars of the Torah during the time of Jesus.</note>, and showing what each person owes
To God, on the holy path toward perfection,
Through paradoxes <note>Likely referring to the Beatitudes, which present a seemingly contradictory path to happiness.</note> that waken those who slumber,
And pruning and fulfilling Moses' rugged law.

His mouth unfolds wherein true salvation consists;
How far the new law exceeds in power,
And in the perfection of her commandments and customs,
The ancient law, which never circumcised the hearts <note>A biblical metaphor for internal spiritual transformation rather than outward ritual.</note>.
He teaches to perform charity quietly for heaven's honor,
And how one pleases God through fasting and prayers;
Admonishes to gather treasures in God's treasury;
Drives away useless worry about saving on clothing and food,
And supports hope with God's providence,
Who feeds the birds, and clothes the flower with richer majesty
Than Solomon. He warns against rushing 
To the judgment seat <note>Refers to the *vierschaar*, a historical term for a tribunal or court of law.</note>, regarding the affairs and rights of our neighbors;
Rebukes the reckless misinterpretation of God’s law;
Exhorts to constantly entreat God in prayer;
To press into heaven through the narrow gate with strength;
To shun the wolves who, disguised as sheep, leap upon everyone;
To build upon his word, strong and firm,
A work that lasts eternally, and is unsuited to any storms.

<margin>*Isaiah and Ezekiel introduced him as teacher, prophet, and king.*</margin>
Thus he takes up the office that Moses, Isaiah,
And also Ezekiel dedicated to God Messiah <note>The Hebrew title meaning "Anointed One," translated here as the promised savior.</note>,
So many centuries in advance, as teacher and prophet,
And shepherd over the people, vested with legitimacy;
A prophet from the Jewish tribe, anointed to anoint the hearts
Of the sorrowful and sick multitude in their pains;
To break the prisoner's chains in times of danger,
To proclaim everywhere the favorable year <note>Refers to the "Year of the Lord’s Favor" or Jubilee, a time of spiritual liberation.</note>;
To guard God’s scattered flock with David’s staff,
And to feed them with the healthy pasture of his teachings.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Christ's role as the supreme Teacher. Following the summary of his moral instructions on the previous page, the author now compares Christ’s divine wisdom to the greatest intellectual traditions of antiquity, emphasizing that his central command of love surpasses all ancient philosophy.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

Just as became a teacher who understood his office,
He taught heaven's will, received from God's own mouth,
In a perfect manner to all kinds of nations,
Either himself, or through his messengers and sent-out interpreters.

<margin>Christ's wisdom summarized in the word Love.</margin>
Even though the thrice-great <note>A reference to the "Hermetic" tradition of Hermes Trismegistus, whose name means "thrice-greatest."</note> intellect of a Chaldean <note>Refers to the ancient sages of Babylonia, traditionally seen as the fathers of astronomy and wisdom.</note>
First enlightened Egypt; and Moses, and Hebrew,
And Arab, and Greek, and all manner of tongues
Kindled the torches of their understanding at his torch;
Though Solomon bore the name of the wisest of all,
And the obelisks <note>original: "naelden" (needles), referring to Egyptian obelisks often associated with the wisdom of Hermes.</note> of Hermes weighed far more in wisdom;
Though Rome sealed the sanctuary of the Sibylline leaves <note>Prophetic books of the Sibyls consulted by the Roman state in times of crisis.</note>,
And went to seek counsel from their oracles;
Though the ancient Roman law, carved into tables <note>The Law of the Twelve Tables, the foundation of Roman law.</note>,
Shone over the empire of the world with such a luster;
Yet has the wisdom of God's Son—who humbled himself
So that he might teach the seed of Adam the law of heaven—
Contained more wisdom summarized in one single word
Than Jerusalem, Athens, Heliopolis <note>original: "Zonnestadt" (City of the Sun), an ancient Egyptian center of learning.</note>,
And Rome, in so much time, through fervent searching,
Ever encompassed in metal, or obelisk, or image, or books:
For in the single word Love <note>original: "Bemin" (the imperative form "to love").</note>, God here encompasses
All of man's duty toward God and man; a chief commandment,
Which fulfills the entire law and all of God's prophets,
And teaches each to measure out what is rightfully due.

<margin>Christ teaches through words and by example.</margin>
To whomever wishes to safely approach God's throne
At the summit of perfection, he unfolds the distinction
Between counsels and commandments <note>A theological distinction: "commandments" are requirements for all, while "evangelical counsels" (poverty, chastity, obedience) are voluntary paths for those seeking higher perfection.</note>, to enlighten everyone.
Though God's commandments bind and obligate every person,
The following of such counsel still depends on man’s choice.
Here the teacher himself goes before his scholars of the cross <note>original: "kruisscholieren."</note>,
In voluntary poverty, and obedience, and chastity.
Thus the twelve follow the master, who lies upon the cross,
Giving his hands, feet, body, and soul for the best
To his Father, and has sanctified himself.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Christ’s role as the supreme teacher and savior, transitioning from his moral philosophy (summarized on the previous page as Love) to his divine authority as demonstrated through prophecy and miracles. ### THE FIFTH BOOK.

So that in him, as in a mirror free from blemishes,
They might see all of heaven's lessons working out and being fulfilled.

<margin>His certainty in prophesying.</margin>
In his predictions, the unfailing certainties of God’s prophetic office <note>The Dutch "profeetendom" refers to the status or collective authority of prophets.</note> appear to us everywhere, through the announcement of his cross and his suffering yet to come; how Judas Iscariot <note>original: "Iscarioth"</note>, envying his honor, would stealthily deliver him to his enemies, even the exact hour and time; the scattering of his flock, and the onset of the struggle of the houses that confessed God’s name and his son; the fame of Mary Magdalene <note>original: "Magdalene"</note>, whose actions were disputed at Simon’s table <note>Referring to the biblical account of the woman anointing Jesus' feet at the house of Simon the Leper, where Jesus predicted her story would be told wherever the gospel is preached.</note>; his rising from the grave, and the downfall of the Temple, a tragedy predicted forty years in advance; predictions which, if one compares Christ to the ancients, do not yield to any prophet, not even to Moses himself.

<margin>Christ's miracles.</margin>
Countless miracles <note>original: "wonderdaên"</note>, performed to the Father’s honor, seal the authority of the Son and his teaching throughout David’s realms. The dead are quickly reborn—on the bed, on the bier, in the grave—they live again as before. He is the ear of the deaf, the eye of the man born blind, the staff of the cripple who leaps upward like a deer. He forces spirits to depart from the shell of the body. The fishing net tears from its burden. He walks upon the waves and smooths the billows flat, which yield before his voice. The incurable and the hungry heart find counsel and comfort in him, who satisfies thousands with a little grain grown in the furrows of his hand <note>A poetic reference to the Miracle of the Loaves and Fishes, suggesting the bread "grew" by his touch.</note>. No curse, no antagonistic barking can deafen his power which, though gloriously poured out, is attributed by the convinced spite of his enemies to hell <note>Refers to the Pharisees accusing Jesus of casting out demons by the power of Beelzebub.</note>; yet it nevertheless does not cease to shine through God’s deeds, foretold for so many centuries in pages that never fail.

<margin>His royal office and joyful entry to</margin>

With palm, and the donkey on which he sits is covered
With clothes, as is the path. Thus comes the Son of God riding
In through the gate, through the throng of all Jerusalem,
That receives him as the Son of David with a single voice,
Singing hosanna. He steps upon the threshold of Aaron <note>The "threshold of Aaron" refers to the entrance of the Temple, invoking Aaron as the biblical archetype of the priesthood.</note>,
To nourish the devotion of prayer in God’s temple,
Which, like a den of thieves <note>original: "moortspelonck," literally a cave of murderers.</note>, is desecrated everywhere.
He disrupts the dove-market, overturns the money-changers' tables,
And drives out the abuse with whips and blows,
To please the Father through a pure service;
Just as he, to plant God’s honor everywhere,
Sent his twelve, and another six-times-twelve <note>A reference to the seventy-two disciples sent out by Jesus in the Gospel of Luke to spread his teachings.</note>,
As God’s heralds, to prepare hearts
For the service to which he now came to lead mankind;
An office that most perfectly suits that High Priest,
Now that the priesthood of Levi <note>The Levitical priesthood of the Old Testament, which was responsible for temple sacrifices.</note> no longer satisfies the Godhead,
Which demands something far nobler than smoking sacrificial fires,
And the blood of goats and oxen, and calves, and bulls.

<margin>
God had, through the prophets, rejected the sacrifices and ceremonial services of the law, and promised to consecrate another and eternal priesthood and pure sacrifice.
</margin>
That God takes displeasure in the services of the law—
Which were by no means established to endure forever,
But rather to be changed and transformed
Into a more perfect and better style and order—
Is evident from the oracles <note>Refers to the prophetic scriptures of the Old Testament.</note> which for so long have unfolded
The distinction, most of all where God denies his people
The sacrificial service; a service in use among the Hebrews,
That chosen people, and for a succession of centuries
Bound to the tribe and blood of Levi. Yet because
The tribes stared too blindly at that style
Of sacrificing, as if it wiped away the stains of abomination
From the human heart, and by its very nature pleased God’s eye,
The Godhead has long rebuked that sacrificial service,
And through that rebuke, occasionally treated it as if already abolished.

### THE FIFTH BOOK.

He turns his face away from smoke and altar-flames,
From the blood of calves and rams offered up:
He himself is the owner and lord of all that lives.
No wealthy man is enriched by what poverty offers him.
How can one hope to satisfy God with the blood and flesh of cattle!
No chalice of blood can quench God’s thirst, nor can flesh satisfy his hunger.
He desires a nobler food and drink, a different feast;
A humble heart and a broken spirit
Please God. Whoever comes bearing such a sacrifice
Is welcome; God will not despise these gifts.
Thus sounds the harp <note>A reference to the Psalms of David, particularly Psalm 51, which emphasizes spiritual contrition over animal sacrifice.</note>, to which all other strings are tuned,
Proclaiming that God loathes sacrifices and instead demands a subject’s
Righteous heart, rather than the fruits of the field, sheaves,
And the fat of cattle. Even the heirs of Aaron <note>The Levitical priesthood, descended from Aaron, the brother of Moses.</note>
Are released from that burden, though bound to their kingdom.
The harp also promises them a king, who is at once
A High Priest, compared to Melchizedek <note>A biblical figure who was both King of Salem and Priest of the Most High God; in Christian theology, he is a precursor to Christ.</note>,
And who shall never lack a sacrifice throughout eternity.
Even Isaiah sees another form of sacrifice
And an altar raised upon the banks of the Nile,
Where Egyptians and Assyrians hasten to bring their offerings,
Feeding their sacrifices outside the tribe of Levi <note>Referring to Isaiah 19:19-21, which prophesies that non-Israelites will eventually worship God with sacrifices.</note>.
Whoever asks Malachi will learn how long ago
The Divine began to loathe the service and sacrifices
Of the Hebrews, waiting for the sacrifice that would finally
Offer pure honor, from the east unto the west.
Wise Daniel prophesies from the angel’s mouth
How God puts an end to the sacrifice of Moses’ old covenant,
Whose temple and altar now lie so utterly crumbled into dust,
That neither time nor the passing centuries can promise they will rise again.

<margin>*Christ, a priest according to the order of Melchizedek*</margin>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Promise, archetype, and prophecy all look
Forward steadfastly to the sacrifice that shall render all other sacrifices obsolete,
As Jesus, on the night of the Passover, reclined with his twelve disciples
At his sacrificial table, after holy contemplation

# REFLECTIONS.

<margin>Points, before his suffering, to the new offering of the New Testament.</margin>
After eating the Passover lamb, which prefigured his own death,
He washes the disciples’ feet, and by this act commands them
To purify themselves and to humble themselves in spirit,
In service to everyone. Now, to honor the Father,
To reconcile, to give thanks, and to seek mercy
And blessing, he begins—very late at midnight—
To dedicate this unbloody sacrifice <note>A theological term for the Eucharist (Holy Communion), which commemorates Christ's death without the further shedding of blood.</note> to the Divine,
As a remembrance of his approaching suffering,
With a desire for the joy of salvation to flow from the power
Of the bloody sacrifice, to be granted to the human race
Everywhere that companions of the altar <note>Those participating in the sacrament of the Lord's Supper.</note>
Enjoy the body, crucified for them, and the blood, shed on the cross,
Through grace and with holy reflection;
How, under the appearance of grain and grape, Melchizedek <note>A biblical king and priest who offered bread and wine; Christians view him as a "type" or foreshadowing of Christ.</note>,
Now reborn and transformed into Emmanuel <note>Hebrew: "God with us," a title for Jesus.</note>,
Performs the sacrificial service, though richer than before,
Since the shadow retreats far before the holy body,
And the appearance falls back and yields to life itself.
He blesses the wine and the grain, and immediately calls both
His body and his blood, so that one may distinguish
The vineyard’s fruit and the nourishing crop of the field
From that which it had already become through God’s power:
A living sacrificial food, given so generously for them,
A sacrificial drink, poured out willingly
As atonement for all that has been transgressed. In such a clear sense
Jesus inaugurates the new Passover feast before his cross,
Through the holy sacrificial lamb, prefigured by Aaron’s lambs <note>The animal sacrifices of the Old Testament priesthood.</note>,
To be slaughtered on the cursed wood <note>A reference to the cross (Galatians 3:13).</note> with much sorrow
And much blood: the seal of this New Testament,
Through which his heirs inherit life without end.

<margin>Through Christ’s unbloody sacrifice, his bloody [sacrifice is granted to us].</margin>
This is the only sacrifice that shall endure through the ages,
And is not bound to tribe, or place, or walls,
Like Aaron’s bloody service, tied to David’s city <note>Jerusalem.</note>,
The temple, and the mountain, for so long splashed with blood.

## THE FIFTH BOOK. 211

<margin>
...dedicated to the unbloody offering.
</margin>
Whoever boasts of religion, yet dares to push Christ away
From the unbloody altar <note>A theological term for the Eucharist (Holy Communion), which commemorates Christ's death without the further shedding of blood.</note>, consecrated as a sign
That eternally in his church bears witness to his death,
Strays from the law of sacrifice, which calls the Christian companion
To this sacrificial atonement and divine blessing,
So bloodily obtained by God the Son upon the cross,
And offered to each in the earthly mystery,
Hidden under the grain and grapevine at God’s table.
Neither the Old nor New Covenant understands secret marks <note>Signs or symbols of sacred mysteries (sacraments).</note>,
Consecrated to support and strengthen weak faith,
As powerfully holy, great, and glorious as this.
Neither the manna, nor the ark <note>original: "kift" (kist), referring to the Ark of the Covenant</note>, a throne where God sits,
Nor the Passover lamb, which shielded from the sword of the striking angel,
Nor all of God's sanctuary and treasure in Aaron's tabernacle;
No treasure in the golden choir <note>The inner sanctuary or Holy of Holies</note> built by Solomon,
Can match the brilliance of Jesus' sacrificial sun,
In the midst of the six secret signs <note>Referencing the sacraments; the poet envisions the Eucharist as the central sun among the other sacraments.</note>, even
Like a Divinity itself raised upon its throne,
As long as the visible sun shines over the earth,
And gives soul and life to all that lives and breathes.

<margin>
The bloody offering follows the unbloody one, which imprints the bloody one in our memory, just as it begins in the garden of prayer and is completed on the cross.
</margin>
But if one cannot appease the angry,
Offended God without blood, then the bloody sacrifice must follow
The unbloody offering, which begins immediately hereafter,
Where Christ finds himself alone in his garden,
And wrestles with death, and, looking to the west and east
For comfort, faints unless the archangel comes to comfort him,
So that he, in the dust of the earth, humbles himself like a worm;
His sweat, from head to toe, turned to blood from anguish,
And thus battled by night, pressed on all sides,
Three times he prays that the cup of all-too-bitter suffering
Might be refused, if it pleases God,
And the Father’s wise will, to which he submits himself.
But no, he must tread the winepress <note>A biblical metaphor for divine judgment and suffering, specifically referencing Isaiah 63:3.</note>, long foretold, thus
Alone and without help during these night prayers.

Iscariot appears, armed with the power
Of priests, elders, and Caesar's temple guard.
Thus the Savior is betrayed here with a kiss,
And in the high priest's court, for the sake of man's slanders,
Slandered and mocked, mistreated and scorned,
Then accused before Pontius <note>Referring to Pontius Pilate, the Roman governor.</note>, spat upon, crowned
With thorns, so that the blood was seen flowing down his cheeks,
The reed staff in his hand, draped in purple dye,
After the scourging, shown to everyone like a spectacle,
Sentenced and condemned; no innocence excuses him.
He then carries his cross with patience, to which the servants
Of Rome nail and fasten him outside the city,
And hang him on the rock, the Mount of the Skull <note>original: "dootshooftbergh" (Golgotha or Calvary)</note>, so clearly
Portrayed for many centuries by Aaron’s blood-altar <note>A reference to the sacrificial system established by Aaron in the Old Testament, which Christians viewed as a "foreshadowing" of Christ's death.</note>;
Where the offerings long cried out for the one sacrifice
That hangs offered here for all manner of people,
And, exhausted from bleeding and woe, his spirit
Finally commends to God, before the start of God's feast.
The soul further seals the truth of the pages
Consecrated by David’s pen <note>Referring to the Messianic prophecies in the Psalms of David.</note>, and descends to where the ancient patriarchs
Long for the day, in the vestibule of hell <note>original: "voorburgh van de hel" (Limbo), where the righteous of the Old Testament were said to wait for Christ's redemption.</note>,
And follow in triumph, to song and the playing of strings,
The most blessed hero and refuge of the redeemed,
As they rejoice, when the gates of the abyss and its posts
Trembled with the terror of death at Jesus' final sigh,
Which stirred the elements in a single moment,
And tore Moses' veil <note>The curtain in the Temple that separated the Holy of Holies; its tearing symbolized direct access to God.</note> at the light that, shining in,
Drove away the shadows of the Law, which then vanished.

rises.

Just as hell did not shut in the soul with its lock,
So the grave does not allow Christ’s corpse to rot.
This is proven on the third day, when he, now reborn,
Awakens Pilate’s grave-watch and disturbs their guard
With his heroic brilliance; while it dawns in the east,
Where the light nears the horizon and chases away the dismal night,

### THE FIFTH BOOK.

With a Sunday, a day of rest, to be celebrated,
With hymns of praise, and festoons, and greenery and foliage to adorn,
In honor of the hero who conquered hell and death;
In honor of the new and risen sun,
Awakening with himself countless corpses at once,
Into heaven’s majesty, full of rejoicing, and full of life.

<margin>The High Priest, walking through shed blood into the Holy of Holies, prefigured Christ’s high priesthood and office as mediator in heaven.</margin>
Just as the high priest <note>The "Aertsprieſter" or High Priest of Israel, who performed the annual atonement rituals.</note> could never set foot in the Holy of Holies
Once a year, except through the shed blood
Of bull and goat and ram; so too the Savior,
Who followed the shadow of Aaron <note>The first High Priest of Israel; his actions were seen by Christians as "shadows" or symbols of what Christ would eventually do.</note>, and took a place in his stead,
Could not set His foot in the heavenly sanctuary
Except through His own blood, shed upon the cross,
To enter into heaven, and, without spot or blemish,
As a throne of grace, to seat Himself at God's side,
To appease the Father’s wrath, and, as a mediator,
Standing in the midst between God and His faithful flock,
To pray, and from there to pour out His blessing,
Acquired and obtained for them upon the bloody cross.

<margin>Christ reveals Himself ten times after His resurrection, commands Peter, the envoy of the cross, to tend His flock, and ascends to heaven.</margin>
But before His ascension He appeared nine times
And once more <note>The text says "negenmael en eenwerf" (nine times and once), meaning ten appearances in total after the resurrection.</note>, outside the grave, and declared through speech, and tongue,
Scars, and sight, and the breaking of the crust <note>original: "korſtbreuck," referring to the "breaking of bread" by which the disciples recognized Jesus.</note>, and through eating,
That He had left the tomb. Five hundred at once are known
To testify with their own eyes how He, in Galilee,
Appeared to His own envoys of the cross upon a mountain.
I shall not pass over how, when all elements mourned His death,
The temple veil, the rocks, the stone tombs were rent,
How many bodies of the Saints within the grave
Arose, and each of them entered into the city,
After God’s resurrection, where many with wonder
Saw them thunder forth from death into the light of life:
If only this testimony of the risen sun
Could soften the city toward the divinity of God’s Son.

And fishermen, unlearned in literature and languages,

Guardian of God’s Book, and the main pillar of truth,

To separate the religion of the Bible from worldly wisdom,

Both in the old law and under the new covenant:
Since he <note>The "he" refers to a critic or skeptic who uses the Bible to argue against religion.</note> found two human sacrifices in God’s pages,
A daughter and a son, both together obedient,
Offered on the altar, for the love of their father;
Therefore he boasts: now protect your religion with God’s leaves <note>original: "Godts blaên"; referring to the pages of Holy Scripture.</note>.
Your religion leads God himself to such an abomination.

But let him listen to David’s harp and tones,
Which punish the tribes for the sacrificing of their sons
And daughters, and shows how this displeases God,
Who plagues the human sacrificers for these abominations,
Delivers them into the power of the uncircumcised <note>A biblical term for the enemies of Israel who did not follow the covenant.</note>,
And drives them into slavery, with iron for the shins <note>A reference to being bound in leg irons or shackles during captivity.</note>:
So far is it from Him regarding this as a religion.
If Jephthah then burns and slaughters a maiden, his daughter,
To keep his promise and blind oath to God,
It results in his punishment: and those who saw this, shunned
No foul altar-stain <note>The poet suggests that such a sacrifice was a "stain" or "blot" on the altar rather than a holy act.</note>; so gross an indiscretion
Of "vowing to God" was called presumption among them.

The sacrifice of God’s Son, when the spearhead grieved him
In his innocent heart, was a proof of love
Of the Godhead for man: for God the Father allowed
That his beloved Son, for man’s salvation, was thus cruelly
Mistreated on the cross: the Son, so strongly driven
By love, did not at all spare his life, offering it voluntarily
As an atonement for the Father, by which it appeared
That he yielded to no one in the wrestling-ring of love.
The Spirit, the love of the merciful Father
And Son, confirms this: so three agree together.
Thus the Son is in the end, for his obedience
And deep humility, crowned with majesty.
What place is there here for the High Priest <note>This likely refers to Christ as the eternal High Priest, or by extension, the authority of the Church.</note> to be ashamed?
Thus religion and the highest wisdom go together;
Since the Godhead is satisfied with this sacrifice,
A sweet-smelling fragrance <note>A common biblical metaphor (the "odor of sweetness") for a sacrifice that is pleasing to God.</note> that befits a wise religion;
