<meta>Catchword: Dis-</meta>

A sacrifice, consecrated by God, the most beloved son,
Thrice confirmed through the mouth of the Father, from the throne
Of heaven, authorized for this office with a voice,
At the water <note>The baptism of Jesus in the Jordan River.</note>, on God’s mountain <note>The Transfiguration on Mount Tabor.</note>, and in Jerusalem,
Where visible signs assure everyone of this truth:
The dove, descended low; the countenance with a clarity
Shining round, like the sun; the voice, heard from the firmament,
Like a thunderclap in Aaron’s great church <note>Referring to the Jewish Temple and the priestly line of Aaron, now superseded or fulfilled by Christ.</note>:
And as men in the school of the wise plead for the prize,
In God’s Anointed <term>Christ; from the Greek 'Christos' meaning 'The Anointed One'</term> hides the entire treasure of wisdom,
And knowledge: so the religion goes, established
By heaven’s wisdom, never unaccompanied by wisdom.

<margin>*The Christian religion is planted and authorized by God, and Christ, through Apostles and bishops, their successors, ordained and authorized for this purpose, to prevent discord and schisms.*</margin>
This religion is known, strengthened by teaching and deeds,
Through God’s embassy <note>The Apostles</note>, and in their footsteps followed
The bishops, authorized and appointed by them,
An order that generally prevails in all churches,
From Christ’s age and time: for outside such an order
No one could, of old, be authorized in this office.
Whoever disturbed this law, desecrated the altar of the cross,
Desecrated the censer, like a tyrant,
And murderer, and thief, crept in from the side,
Through the wrong door: this priestly office stood open
For the peace-loving, who never thoughtlessly
Lay their unconsecrated hand upon the God-consecrated,
Out of schism, self-interest, and a lust for dominion,
Which oppresses the power of souls, and holds them in slavery,
Through lawless compulsion: this bold Lucifer*
Envies the head-authority, and, into a dark star
Transformed, never ceased to shake the head-authority,
And has, over time, pulled down with him in his fall
A tail of angels <note>A metaphor for the followers of schismatic leaders or rebellious clergy.</note>, tearing the whole of Christendom apart,
And raised Mahomet <note>Muhammad, the prophet of Islam.</note> so high to the summit by the sword,
That he, from Buda <note>Budapest, then a frontier of the Ottoman Empire.</note> to the heart of the Indians,
[Challenges] the standard of the cross, with his half-moons,

<margin>* *at Constantinople.*</margin>

<vocab>Christendom, priestly office, Lucifer, Mahomet, Buda, Indians, Constantinople</vocab>

<summary>This page describes Christ's divine ordination as the ultimate priest and sacrifice, and argues that the authority of bishops is necessary to prevent the prideful schisms that the author believes led to the rise and expansion of Islam.</summary>
<keywords>Apostolic succession, bishops, schism, Muhammad, Ottoman Empire, Christ's baptism, Transfiguration, church authority</keywords>

--- Page 252 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological and historical defense of the Church's endurance. Having established the divine authority of the ecclesiastical hierarchy on the previous page, the author now turns to the global challenges facing the faith—specifically the turmoil in 17th-century China—and argues that internal schisms and external persecutions ultimately serve to refine and strengthen the true Church.</meta>

# REFLECTIONS.

Dimmed, and stunned, and whatever inclines toward Christ,
Unless God provides for it, is threatened by the sword of the Tartar <note>original: "Tarters". A reference to the Manchu conquest of China (1644), which caused massive upheaval during the era this was written.</note>,
Who, like a world-flood, keeps all of China submerged,
Drowned in its own blood, flowing out in streams,
From one sea to the other, so bloody that the sun
Could not rise over the eastern horizon without terror.
May heaven bless the Roman Christian envoys <note>The Roman Catholic missionaries, primarily Jesuits, who were active in the Chinese imperial court and provinces during the Ming-Qing transition.</note>,
Now heading there to plant the tree of the cross everywhere,
In the wild Tartar empire, and China, where the night
Of Heathendom still lasts among the unenlightened race,
And Pythagoras <note>The author attributes the Eastern concept of reincarnation (metempsychosis) to the Greek philosopher Pythagoras, viewing it as a "wandering" of the soul through animals.</note> still sees the human soul wandering,
From one animal to another, through the idolatrous schools,
In a vast expanse of lands, where one sees nothing
Of ancient Christianity except a single sign,
And a shadow of the holy cross, among a few who know
Of the mark, while the name of Christ has worn away <note>This likely refers to the "Nestorian" Christians who had reached China centuries earlier; their influence had largely faded, leaving only ruins and the famous Nestorian Stele.</note>.

<margin>Through schisms and heresies the pious are tested and glorified.</margin>
Even though God’s providence allows blind hatred
To oppose the building and growth of religion,
To scatter, chase, and split it into unequal parts,
And fish the staff and miters <note>The symbols of a bishop’s authority: the crozier (staff) and the liturgical headdress (miter).</note> from bloody quarrels,
The right reason remains hidden in God’s all-wise breast.
The flail feeds the people when it threshes the corn <note>A metaphor: just as the grain must be beaten to be separated from the chaff, the Church is refined by hardship.</note>.
Gold, after the trial, will shine much clearer when tested.
Stagnant waters and lazy brooks stink.
The sunshine beams upon us more beautifully after the storm.
Crushed powder <note>Likely referring to aromatic spices or medicinal herbs that release their scent and potency only when ground down.</note> spreads its scent and strengthens the stomach.
The commander makes use of seasoned soldiers,
Who would rather die than abandon their banner of the cross.
Triumph follows the enduring of the struggle.
Who would refuse to win eternity in exchange for time?

<margin>One still finds among the oldest schismatics the...</margin>
This religion defies hell, and never ceased to shine
Among the severed flock. One finds among Abyssinians <note>Ethiopian Christians.</note>,
Among the Greek, and Armenian, and Russian, and Persian,
The old footprints of Roman customs still standing,

<meta>catchword: How-</meta>

<summary>The author reflects on the bloody Manchu conquest of China and the mission to restore Christianity there, while arguing that religious schisms and persecutions are divine "tests" that purify the Church like gold in a fire.</summary>
<keywords>China, Tartars, missionaries, reincarnation, providence, persecution, Eastern Orthodox, Abyssinians, martyrdom, Church history</keywords>

--- Page 253 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of the global state of faith. While the previous page focused on the physical and spiritual turmoil in China during the Manchu conquest, this page shifts to an internal critique of religious "errors" and a comparative look at world religions. The author continues to argue for the unique authority and historical continuity of the Roman Catholic Church amidst a world of competing beliefs.</meta>

## THE FIFTH BOOK.

<margin>
Footsteps and marks of the Church, mingled among errors.
</margin>
Although mingled with errors; just as in a tearing
The spirit of schism <note>The Dutch *scheurzucht* refers to a "thirst for tearing," describing the tendency toward denominational splits during the Reformation.</note> does not hesitate to drag its hem through the dust;
It does not let the Spirit of the Divinity of the Father and the Son
Shine forth as antiquity was accustomed;
It desecrates the single baptism, and violates it through rebaptism <note>A reference to the Anabaptist movement, which practiced adult baptism, rejecting the validity of infant baptism.</note>;
It circumcises the baptized, and opens the gate of error
For the outdated Law <note>Refers to "Judaizing" tendencies or sects that attempted to reintegrate Old Testament laws into Christian practice.</note>; it limits the monarchy
Of Christ, and prunes the lawful guardianship,
The power of the keys of the church <note>A theological term for the authority of the Church, based on Matthew 16:19.</note>, given to the sole head,
And, as a property and title, attributed
By broad church councils to the successor, who guards God’s city
And for many centuries has occupied the first seat,
Recognized by emperors, and kings, and lords,
Who with reverence honor God’s vicar <note>The Pope.</note>.

<margin>
Among a few sham religions, an observant person can still recognize the light of the true religion.
</margin>
However large the world is, however unevenly divided
In religion, or in the appearance of religion, disputed,
According to each person's coarse wit, or more or less sharpened;
The manner of religion is generally understood in brief
As idolatry, and "god-ism" <note>likely referring to deism or polytheistic traditions.</note>,
Hebrew circumcision <note>Judaism.</note>, and "Turkism" <note>A common 17th-century term for Islam.</note>: add to this
The holy faith of the cross, and compare them all together,
Also each one’s era: observe the lives and the names
Of the founders, along with what credibility
Each brings: what mark, what seal and testimony
Assures the followers of their doctrine that they are being led
Into an immortality of life, to be expected
After this moment: you shall see impartially
[So far as inherited custom, and inherited doctrine, and perhaps
The enjoyment of profit, status, and prestige do not blind you,]
What laws, what evidence binds those yearning for holiness
To exchange the night of errors for the light
Of truth, and the radiance of Jesus' countenance,
That here below, like a sun, shone on Tabor <note>Mount Tabor, the traditional site of the Transfiguration of Jesus.</note>,
Drowned in blood on the cross, and drew breath again,

Ee 2
<meta>catchword: Then</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the fragmentation of Christianity caused by "the spirit of schism" and rebaptism, while contrasting the historic authority of the Papacy with other world religions such as Judaism, Islam, and idolatry.</summary>
<keywords>schism, Anabaptism, Papacy, comparative religion, Mount Tabor, immortality, ecclesiastical authority, 17th-century theology</keywords>

--- Page 254 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the historical and ecclesiastical authority of the Church discussed on the previous page to a philosophical defense against atheism. The author argues that the evidence for divine intervention is overwhelming and that religion is the necessary foundation for civil society and the rule of law.</meta>

-># REFLECTIONS.<-

<margin>
An atheist <note>original: *ongodist*. A 17th-century term for someone who denies God or lives as if God does not exist.</note>, convinced by so many proofs, cannot excuse himself.
</margin>
When neither death nor the grave could hold God’s body,
When He ascended to heaven, clearer than the sun.
If an atheist still desires to extinguish God’s service,
To smother it in his heart; how can he meanwhile
Deafen God’s trumpet, the sound of the eternal Word,
That from the east to the west, through all ages, was heard,
In the creation of the universe, in the heavenly revelation
Of angels, and messengers, who declare heaven’s will,
Sealing it with power, through many a miracle,
Revealed more widely than the firmament hangs with darkness
And covered with clouds, known by Jew and Heathen?
How can he reject God the Son, for whom the patriarchs waited,
So many centuries before, where He speaks
From the Father’s mouth and name, and entreats all souls,
And invites them into His service, with the promise to crown
Those who, under this banner of the cross, show themselves brave?
Or is the comfort of rank, an unstable fate,
Worth more than standing well with one’s conscience, and God?

<margin>
Civil jurisdiction <term>Civil jurisdiction: *Het burgerlijck gebied*, referring to the power of the state and secular law.</term> looks only to the welfare of the civil state, which is better secured by religion and shaken by irreligiousness.
</margin>
Civil jurisdiction looks no higher than this life,
Supported by earthly power, and thus raised to the peak:
Religion supports that power with tribute, and reverence,
And punishes the conscience, where no executioner <note>original: *scherprecht*. Literally "sharp justice," referring to the official who carries out corporal or capital punishment.</note> can reach.
Where God is denied, there is neither civil right nor order,
Nor virtue, nor religion, which are founded upon God.
Thus the world, where religion sinks, drifts wildly,
Like a rudderless ship: no bridle of laws stills
The threatening tyrant, nor the uprising of rebels,
Like a wild sea, where all the waves swell,
And rage according to the wind, which drives the water about.
Overthrow religion, and piety: what remains
Resting in its place? one cannot rely on edicts <note>original: *plackaeten*. Official public decrees or ordinances posted by authorities.</note>,
Nor weapons, and arms, and oaths,
Since they would ever alter the nature of the mind,
Which, God-forgotten, pants after robbery, and holy blood,

<meta>catchword: And,</meta>

<vocab>God, atheist, civil jurisdiction, religion, Jew, Heathen, patriarchs, cross, conscience, piety</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that the historical Resurrection and universal revelation leave the atheist without excuse, while positing that religion is the essential foundation for a stable and moral civil society.</summary>
<keywords>atheism, civil authority, social order, Resurrection, conscience, divine revelation, secular law, piety</keywords>

--- Page 255 ---
<meta>This page serves as the concluding section of the work. It transitions from the discussion of civil jurisdiction and the necessity of religion for a stable society to a final warning against Machiavellian hypocrisy and a poetic benediction for the reader's spiritual journey.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

And, resting by day, at evening he lurks in wait,
Deceit and night-treachery, and does not fear to venture
In shadows that which the bright day forbids.
Thus an atheist <note>original: *ongodist*. A 17th-century term for someone who denies God or lives as if God does not exist.</note> does not know his own political welfare <note>original: *staetheil*. The stability and well-being of the state.</note>.

<margin>The Christian religion is too holy to be misused for political ambition.</margin>
Yet he finds it good, for the sake of political welfare, to feign piety,
Like Machiavelli <note>Refers to Niccolò Machiavelli, whose political theories were often interpreted in the 17th century as advocating for the cynical use of religion as a tool for state control.</note>, to cover his thoughts
With the foliage and shadows of religion before the people,
Who are easily deceived by a hired interpreter;
Surely that hypocrite shall not, with religion and the knowledge of God,
Play unpunished, and without supreme desecration
Of the Deity, who did not bring the service of God’s crucified Son
Into the world to serve as a mere spectacle,
A Moloch <note>An ancient deity associated with child sacrifice; here used to signify a hollow, monstrous, or life-devouring idol.</note>, without spirit or life,
But to draw humanity to Himself through this service.
The Christian faith <note>original: *Christdienst*</note>, hallowed on the cross by God’s blood,
With such bitterness, out of such a fire and glow
Of love, to wipe away the stain of Adam and his descendants,
Suffers no one’s mockery or scorn; no worldly-wise pleasure
Dims the radiance of the cross—a scandal to the Hebrews,
Foolishness to the Greek, and a laughed-at language <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 1:23: "But we preach Christ crucified, unto the Jews a stumblingblock, and unto the Greeks foolishness."</note>,
But wisdom and God’s power to all who trust
To behold the countenance of God’s Son in eternal light.

<margin>Conclusion and wish of the poet.</margin>
Happy is the man who, from the wild sea
Of so many errors and miseries and woe,
Through all the reefs, at times buried in foam,
Overwhelmed by surges, finally sails into the quiet harbor
Of religion, and lands safely
With God, the salvation of mankind, in the heavenly fatherland,
And God’s immortality, separated from the wicked:
Thither my sacred poem wishes to lead everyone,
To steer them, without fear of shipwreck or danger.
Thus may it be consecrated to God, upon His consecrated altar.

->GLORY TO GOD ALONE.<-
<note>original: *Soli Deo Gloria*</note>

<summary>This concluding page warns against using religion as a cynical political tool (Machiavellianism) and offers a final poetic blessing, comparing the search for religious truth to a perilous sea voyage ending in a safe harbor.</summary>
<keywords>Machiavelli, political welfare, atheism, the Cross, salvation, seafaring metaphor, Soli Deo Gloria</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the work, following a discussion on the dangers of using religion as a political tool. The text transitions from a critique of Machiavellian hypocrisy to a poetic exhortation on true humility, the redemptive power of Christ’s sacrifice, and a final benediction for the faithful soul.</meta>

# THE FOURTH BOOK.

<margin>The proud</margin>
The proud man thinks he wishes to shine from without,
Deceives and overpowers; and outwardly displays
In shadows the essence of the fruit of promise.
<margin>For the Jews <unclear/></margin>
Thus everyone judges their own actions.
<margin>To deny</margin>
Yet diligently listen for counsel, so that you may understand,
<margin>Rethoric</margin>
As did Mary, and likewise her Son.
<margin>out of the</margin>
With God the Father you are no longer in joy
<margin>suffers and</margin>
But learn humility of heart, which is full virtue;
<margin>the spirit</margin>
The Lord does not deceive him who wishes to hope in Him.
<margin>gives him</margin>
Surely he shall fall <note>The context suggests "falling" here refers to prostrating oneself in worship or humble surrender.</note> with godliness and good knowledge
<margin>God</margin>
Seeking not the slightest bit of friendship [with the world].
<margin>Lord</margin>
Of the Divinity, which serves that which is opened by God’s wisdom,
<margin>in the</margin>
Fearing nothing in the world, to be displayed everywhere
<margin>all God</margin>
As one humble, good, and upright in understanding,
<margin>God with</margin>
But to make oneself one with the other.
Christianity is not an empty appearance,
But such a wedding feast, where one partakes of the radiance
Of love, to redeem Adam in his nakedness.
Let no one [mock] this good book, nor let worldly pleasure
Lead the flaxen wig <note>original: *vlassen-pruijck*. A 17th-century metaphor for vanity, artificiality, or the disguises of the fashionable elite.</note> astray, O Lord.
One who errs in their deeds: if not so;
At least in God’s power, among all who fear Him
The sight of God’s eye shall be an eternal love.
Happy is the man who, in the fear [of the Lord],
Constrains God’s laws in his heart more and more
Through all the reefs, at times in their cycle
Of remembrance, he helps against the enemy’s thick ranks.
Theology focused on God surely brings aid.
<margin>flows from</margin>
By God, who grants us His salvation and His mercy
<margin>the free</margin>
In God’s compassion, guided from above,
My godly works are set against you,
To pour out the pure sweetness of the fruit of love and spirits,
As He proves the Good upon His beloved spirit.

->TO GOD ALONE THE GLORY<-
<note>original: *SOLI DEO GLORIA*</note>

<summary>This concluding page contrasts the outward "shining" of the proud with the inner humility of Mary and Christ, ending with a poetic blessing on the soul that internalizes God's law. It characterizes true Christianity as a "wedding" of love that restores the fallen state of humanity.</summary>
